# R-INST-2 — TRADE, COMMERCE AND CRAFTS INSTITUTIONS (DW program P1b, tranche 2 — the largest)

# ⚠ PARTIAL marker RETAINED for six residual measured-figure gaps (status map rows B′, F, J, O, P, S carry a PARTIAL clause); all 20 families otherwise AT DEPTH; 124 entries researched. Nothing below is grammar yet — the owner taste-gates (CT-0).

**Lane.** TC-R-INST-2, started 2026-08-23T03:45Z (UTC); the first session died at the owner's usage-window reset during research round 3 (mills) and the lane was RESUMED at 05:25Z by session 695a70c5, which transcribed rounds 1–2, fanned the remaining research out to nine sub-lanes and integrated their verbatim reports (method disclosure at the end). Read-only on the repo; scratchpad-only deliverable; the receipt `laneTCRINST2-receipt.md` holds the round-by-round log and the resume points. Owner taste-gates this dossier (CT-0 pattern). Charter: `docs/DESIGN_DWELLINGS_PROGRAM.md` §2 (nine laws), §3 (13-system weave), §4 S6 (circulation classes, ODQ §452) and S6's storage/service classes (ODQ §453), §5 data contracts, §8 (every entry reports CIRCULATION and STORAGE typology), §15 P1a–P1d. Sibling dossiers: `draft-R-INST-1-CIVIC-DEFENSE.md` (style template; its §0 roster is the exclusion cross-check) and `draft-DWR1A-CONSTRUCTION-HISTORY.md` (construction substrate — cited by section, NOT restated: §1 bay module, §6 burgage plots/party walls/1667 Act, §7 smithy/warehouse/watermill/granary/inn imperatives, §9 undercrofts/garrets).

**Labeling discipline.** CONFIRMED = a source fetched or returned in a search digest THIS lane (URL inline; fetch date 2026-08-23 unless stated). CONFIRMED-digest = the claim came back in a search-engine digest quoting the page; the page itself was not opened. PLAUSIBLE = synthesis or training knowledge — the grammar may NOT consume a PLAUSIBLE figure until P1c verifies it. Contested points carry both sides. Morphology is `EUROPEAN_FANTASY_BASE`: every cited building is a bounded possibility or a counterexample, never a prevalence prior; this dossier mints no probabilities. Conventions-never-expression: tropes and functions are research; no protected text or names are copied. Deity doctrine: guild chapels/altars appear only as liturgical-functional cells. Finite semantics: every proposal is a closed enum the clerk composes from. Dates are absolute.

**Catalog source of record.** `src/data/institutionalCatalog.js` in the clean read-only tree `chair-baseproof-b10ed1a1` (2,526 lines, 311 entries; flattened by `RINST2-flatten.cjs` → `RINST2-catalog-flat.tsv`, roster by `RINST2-roster.cjs` → `RINST2-roster.md`). Line numbers are that file's. Selection is by SHELF (Economy, Crafts) + a name/desc keyword sweep over the other nine shelves (market, shop, warehouse, smith, mill, forge, guild, merchant, bank, exchange, trader, stable, wharf, dock, quay, fair, craft, workshop, foundry, kiln, brew, bake, tan, weave, glass, print, trade, stall, store, granary, salt, quarry, mine, livery, caravan, counting, money, pawn, auction, shipyard, rope, dye, saw, pot, jewel, fur, tailor, cobbler, chandler, cooper, carpenter, butcher, fishmonger, apothecary) — the sweep added Merchant warehouses (Adventuring shelf), Dungeon delving supply district (Adventuring) and Communal root cellar (Infrastructure). As in R-INST-1, `priorityCategory` is the faction-role axis and is ignored for selection: a Brewery whose faction role is 'government' is a brewery.

**Engine recon (read-only, this lane).** (1) An existing bounded interior grammar ALREADY EXISTS at `src/domain/interior/` (DOOR 3, "THE KEYED SCALE"): `INTERIOR_KINDS` = faith/security/trade/craft/learning/vice/civic/generic; `ROOM_KINDS` includes hall, counting, strongroom, stall, workfloor, store, kiln, common, kitchen, cellar, lodging, chamber, records, dais, main, back, evidence, concealed; `FURNISHING_KINDS` includes counter, strongbox, ledger, workbench, hearth, rack, crate, barrel, bar, cauldron; the `trade` template = hall(front) + counting(back, tier≥1) + strongroom(back, tier≥2); the `craft` template = workfloor(front) + store(back) + kiln(back, tier≥2). This dossier's typed proposals mark which enums already have a home there ("HOME: ROOM_KINDS.x") versus NO TYPED HOME. (2) `src/data/resourceChains.js` `INDUSTRY_WATER_NEEDS` is a name-keyed water-requirement table for exactly six names (Tannery, "Dyers' guild", "Fulling mill", Mill, Brewery, "Paper mill") — three of which are not catalog names; it is the nearest thing to a `wetTrade` flag and is incomplete. (3) District siting hints exist at `src/domain/townMap/asymmetrySources.js` `EXPORT_RULES` (leather/hide → industrial/downstream; quarry/mine → edge; timber/mill → water; salt/fish → merchant/water; cloth → craft/core; grain/wine/ale → merchant/gate) and `townLayoutV2.js` `locationBias` (regex over quarter prose: downwind/downstream/tannery/noxious → ring-push + water pull; dock/wharf/quay → water pull). These are DISTRICT-level pulls, not per-building flags. (4) `src/domain/townMap/fabric/frontage.js` carries four axes only (emptinessQ, gridChaosQ, sizeFloorQ, sizeVariationQ) — there is NO water-edge frontage kind. (5) `resourceSites` (mines/quarries) is a PREDICTED path (`src/domain/resourceSites.js`, producer train T2N — not landed; undercity draft §) — family J coordinates with it by description only. (6) Supply-chain status vocabulary (`supplyChainState.js`): stable / strained / scarce / blocked / captured / substituted / collapsing — the typed WEAR input for a cold forge or an empty warehouse bay.

### §0.2 THE FAMILIES (research units; per-tier distinctions inside each)

| Family | Members (tier) | Historical analogue to research |
|---|---|---|
| A markets & fairs | Periodic market (hamlet) · Weekly market (village, town REQ) · Fish market (village) · Market square (town REQ) · Annual fair (town) · Multiple market squares (city REQ) · Daily markets (city REQ; metropolis superseded) · Major annual fairs (city) · District markets (metropolis) | the open market place with its fixed fixtures (cross, stocks, pillory, conduit, weigh-beam), temporary stalls, the shambles/butchers' row, the market hall (open arcade + room over), the fish house, fair booths and the fair's timber halls |
| B the shop-house | Cobbler (village) · Cobbler's guild (town) · Tailor (village) · Tailor's guild (town) · Chandler (town) · Jeweller (town) · Luxury goods quarter (city) · Furrier's district (city) · Dungeon delving supply district (city) | the burgage-plot shop-house: shop at the frontage with shutter/stall-board, workshop behind or in the shop, hall/kitchen behind, solar above, through-passage to the yard; Italian bottega; German Handwerkerhaus; the Chester Rows two-level shop |
| B′ apothecary | Apothecary (village) · Apothecary (established) (town) · Apothecary district (city) | the apothecary's shop: counter + drawer-wall + jar shelving, a back room (consultation/preparation), a still, a drying loft, a herb garden |
| C maps, paper & print | Cartographer's workshop (village, town) · Cartographer's guild (city) · Printing house (city) | the scrivener/stationer's shop; the printing house (press room, composing room, paper store, drying loft, bookshop front) |

## §1 · THE TRADE SPINE — three findings before the families

**1.1 "A shop was a workshop, not a store."** Pantin, quoting H. E. Salter: "a shop in those days was a workshop, not a store; and though a spicer must keep a stock of goods, a hatter, a glover, a tailor, or a shoemaker kept nothing ready made, but would manufacture in his shop the shoes, gloves, and hats that were required" (W. A. Pantin, "Medieval English Town-House Plans", *Medieval Archaeology* 6–7 (1962–3), p. 211; ADS PDF fetched 2026-08-23 → `RINST2-pantin.txt`; CONFIRMED-primary). Consequence: for the retail crafts (family B, B′, most of D, the dry half of K) the engine's existing `trade` template (hall + counting + strongroom) and `craft` template (workfloor + store + kiln) are NOT two buildings — they are ONE frontage cell whose function is `SHOP_WORKSHOP`, with the counter at the window and the bench behind it. The split into a show-shop and a back workshop is a PROSPERITY rung (Bucklersbury 1405's "sotelhouse (possibly a showroom)" beside the shop — Pantin p. 223), not the floor.

**1.2 The frontage is a commercial asset that can be let separately from the house.** Pantin: "it was certainly very common for shops to be let off separately, or even to form separate freeholds" (p. 211); at Tackley's Inn the five street shops with their solars were a "commercial or lay unit" distinct from the hall behind, reached "by an entry between two of the shops" (p. 217). Consequence for law 6 (frontage from the parcel): the shop cells on the street are a TENANCY LAYER, not necessarily rooms of the household behind — the grammar needs a `tenancy` field on frontage cells (OWNER_USED | LET_SEPARATELY) so that a merchant's house can carry 2–5 street shops that are other people's businesses. This is the medieval origin of the "luxury goods quarter" and every "district" entry: a street of let shop-cells in front of a few large houses.

**1.3 The trade interior has three rooms the dwelling does not.** "There are certain rooms which would be peculiar to the town house of a merchant or craftsman. One is a counting-house, rather like what we should call an office, another is a warehouse, another a shop" (Pantin p. 211). Counting-house = `ROOM_KINDS.counting` (HOME); warehouse = `ROOM_KINDS.store` at bay scale (HOME, but see family P for the STACKED warehouse, which has no home); shop = `ROOM_KINDS.stall` (HOME, but the name is wrong — a stall is the market fixture, a shop is a room; flagged in §Σ). Everything else in this tranche's interiors is either the dwelling substrate (DWR1A) or a trade-specific FIXTURE set in one of these three.

## §2 · FAMILY A — markets & fairs: Periodic market (hamlet L397) · Weekly market (village L468; town L907 REQ) · Fish market (village L501) · Market square (town L900 REQ) · Annual fair (town L915) · Multiple market squares (city L1548 REQ) · Daily markets (city L1555 REQ) · Major annual fairs (city L1569) · District markets (5-10) (metropolis L2410)

*Researched by sub-lane AR (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. Catalog rows read from RINST2-catalog-flat.tsv.*

**(a) Analogue.**

The open market place is an interior-less institution whose "rooms" are zones of ground assigned by commodity and policed from a few fixed fixtures. The best-documented small English case is Newmarket, whose 1472/3 account rolls let the editors reconstruct the plan: a market cross out on the King's Way announcing the market to travellers; butchers and the second-hand-clothes dealers of the "Shraggery" clustered round the tollbooth "where they could be closely monitored"; barkers (tanners) and cordwainers placed nearer the Guildhall and the watercourse because they needed water; and a broad cloth zone behind the main tenements divided into Drapery, Mercery and Old Drapery rows (CONFIRMED, Boydell blog by James Davis and Joanne Sear on *Records of Medieval Newmarket*, https://boydellandbrewer.com/blog/british-local-history/reconstructing-a-local-medieval-marketplace/ fetched 2026-08-23 via curl after a 403 to WebFetch). The same zoning law at city scale is Oxford c. 1370: fishmongers, drapers, glovers on the north side of High Street; butchers, ale-sellers, timber on the south; coarse bread, earthenware and coal sold "in the middle of the road"; rush and broom sellers, tanners, poulterers, corn and hay each assigned a stretch of Northgate Street; cheese, eggs, butter, peas in Queen Street (CONFIRMED, VCH Oxford iv, https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/oxon/vol4/pp305-312 fetched 2026-08-23). Salisbury shows the same with named cross-fixtures: the Poultry Cross (a cross by 1307, present hexagonal stone structure 15th c.), a Cheese/Milk Cross of c. 1416 to which sellers of cheese, milk and fruit were "ordered to keep", a yarn-market stone cross mentioned 1525, corn in the NW corner, hemp, linen thread, wool, yarn and coal at the eastern end (CONFIRMED, VCH Wilts vi, https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/wilts/vol6/pp85-87 fetched 2026-08-23).

Fixed fixtures carried by the market place: the cross (Chichester: Perpendicular, octagonal, 50 ft tall, a pointed-arch entrance on each face, a central column whose base "forms a seat", built by Bishop Story 1477–1503 explicitly "so that the poor people should have somewhere to sell their wares" — CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chichester_Cross fetched 2026-08-23); the tollbooth (Newmarket); the weigh-house (Dutch waag — Amsterdam's 1617 weigh-house was a converted 1488 city gate with the courtyard between front and main gate roofed over in 1617–18, scales below, four guilds each with its own entrance on the upper floors, walls almost 2 m thick, cellars beneath; Gouda's 1668 Waag by Pieter Post had its balances hung in May 1670 and an upper floor that "was not important for the weighing function" and served the militia as an armoury — both CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waag,_Amsterdam and https://nl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waag_(Gouda) fetched 2026-08-23); the lock-up or "cage" (Titchfield's market hall: a space behind the stair "with mortices for perpendicular bars and a separate access door" used as a temporary lock-up, later part of the arcade enclosed as cells — CONFIRMED, https://www.wealddown.co.uk/buildings/market-hall-from-titchfield/ fetched 2026-08-23). Stocks and pillory: PLAUSIBLE as standard market-place fixtures adjoining the cross or tollbooth; no measured example fetched this session.

The shambles/encroachment law: rows of fixed butchers' and fishmongers' boards became shops and then buildings. Salisbury's Oatmeal Row, Ox Row, Butcher Row and Fish Row "show every sign of being encroachments of permanent shops built to replace the temporary stalls"; Butcher Row by 1455 "consisted chiefly of holdings described as shops"; Fish Row was "fysschamels" by 1314 (CONFIRMED, VCH Wilts vi, above). Oxford: permanent "fishboards" against properties by the late 14th c.; freemen's open-fronted shops supplementing street stalls c. 1370; a new shambles built in Queen Street 1556, burned 1644, rebuilt 1656 with shops "arranged by seniority"; a lean-to covering five fish-boards under the guildhall replaced in 1712 by a structure of "five fishboards, two chambers above them, and three other rooms or shops" (CONFIRMED, VCH Oxford iv, above). Haarlem: a meat hall stood from 1386; from 1592 26 butchers' stalls were allowed to stand outside it "but the meat spoiled faster outside", hence the new Vleeshal of 1602–04 (Lieven de Key), with rules forbidding slaughter inside, walking about, dogs, and children's games (CONFIRMED, https://nl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vleeshal_(Haarlem) fetched 2026-08-23).

The market hall (open arcade below, room over) is the one true interior of the family. Ledbury (begun c. 1617, attributed to John Abel, completed after 1655): seven bays to the High Street, open ground floor for the market, a grain storeroom on the jettied first floor carried on 16 posts (the listing digest gives 7 posts per long side and 3 at the ends with curved braces, i.e. the same 16-post perimeter; materials disputed — chestnut in the listing digest, oak in Wikipedia), reached by a wooden stair at the north end (CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ledbury_Market_Hall fetched 2026-08-23; post counts CONFIRMED-digest from the Historic England listing 1349392, which returned a Cloudflare challenge to both WebFetch and curl). Titchfield (c. 1620, re-erected at the Weald & Downland Museum 1974): open arcade on posts, council chamber above reached by stairs, lock-up behind the stair, close-studded upper storey with brick infill, gabled roof (CONFIRMED, Weald & Downland page above). Chipping Campden (1627, Sir Baptist Hicks, total cost £90): a "double arcaded loggia" of ashlar, 2 × 5 arches, cobbled floor on which each slender pillar stands on a chamfered plinth, three gables each long side and two each end, NO upper room; built for cheese, butter and poultry sellers, "not, as you might think, wool" (CONFIRMED, https://www.britainexpress.com/counties/glouces/buildings/chipping-campden-market-hall.htm fetched 2026-08-23 via curl; chippingcampdenhistory.org.uk returned 403 both ways). Oxford's 1536 corn market was "a lead roof supported by stone pillars, in the middle of the street" (CONFIRMED, VCH Oxford iv). The French halle is the same parti without the upper room and at a far larger span: Milly-la-Forêt (licensed 1479 by Louis XI with 3 fairs a year and a weekly market) is 46 m long, 16 m wide, 13 m high, 730 m², on 48 oak posts on stone cube bases, gabled at the north end and hipped ("pavillon") at the south (CONFIRMED, https://www.fondation-patrimoine.org/les-projets/la-halle-medievale-de-milly-la-foret/78891 fetched 2026-08-23). The Flemish vleeshuis is the walled, guild-owned version: Ghent's Groot Vleeshuis (1407–17, Gillis de Suttere) gave each member of the great craft his own meat bench, was extended 1446–48 by three bays for a St Hubert chapel-cum-meeting-room, acquired sixteen external tripe-and-poultry stalls (penshuisjes) in 1542–43, and had four masonry channels beneath so flat fish-boats could reach the fish market directly from the Leie (CONFIRMED, https://nl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groot_Vleeshuis fetched 2026-08-23).

The fair: a temporary town of booths on a field, not a building. Defoe on Stourbridge (1724): "all the fair is kept in tents and booths"; shops "placed in rows like streets, whereof one is called Cheapside"; the Duddery, a wholesale square "formed by the largest booths", about 80 to 100 yards across; traders "eat, drink, and sleep in their booths"; coffee-houses, taverns, brandy-shops "all in tents"; a court of pie-powder "held every day in a shed built on purpose"; the whole on a corn-field "about half a mile square" (CONFIRMED, https://www.buildinghistory.org/primary/defoe/cambs.shtml fetched 2026-08-23 — an 18th-century witness; the 1561 row names Duddrye, Birchin Lane, Chepesyde, Hadley Rowe, Back Boothes, Bury Boothes are CONFIRMED-digest). Oxford's St Frideswide fair standings fell from 31 in 1570 to 4 in 1613 and the fair was moved into the guildhall, its courtyard and the street outside after 1549 (CONFIRMED, VCH Oxford iv) — a fair can shrink into a building. The permanent fair building is the cloth hall: Ypres (1200–1304) is 125 m broad with a 70 m belfry, "the main market and warehouse" of the cloth trade, rebuilt 1933–67 as a replica from pre-war surveys (CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ypres_Cloth_Hall fetched 2026-08-23 — a reconstruction).

Structural imperatives: water (tanners and cordwainers placed at the watercourse; Ghent's boat channels; Haarlem's spoilage argument puts meat under a roof); fire (Oxford's shambles and corn market both burned 1644; Haarlem's hall bans slaughter); smell and policing (butchers and old-clothes dealers next to the tollbooth); weight (grain stored over the Ledbury arcade — a heavy-floor upper store). Siting: the cross at the approach road, the tollbooth at the centre, meat and fish on the side nearest water or drainage, cloth in the broadest sheltered zone. Prosperity/wear: floor = bare ground with piccage stakes and a wooden cross; ladder = stone cross, tollbooth, fixed boards, a weigh-beam; ceiling = arcaded market hall with chamber over, walled guild hall with chapel. Decline sheds the hall's roof first (Titchfield "lost most of its tiles" in the 1960s), then the upper room's use (Ledbury's grain store became a town hall), then the fixed rows, which fossilise as housing. Live/work: at the fair the trader sleeps in the booth; in the town the shambles row becomes a shop-house row; the market hall itself houses nobody. Tier distinctions: hamlet L397 = ground + custom, no fixture; village L468 = charter + cross + tolls; town L907/L900 = zoned place with rows, cross, tollbooth, hall; city L1548/L1555 = several commodity places with walled halls (vleeshuis, waag, corn hall); metropolis L2410 = named commodity districts each with its own hall, the Oxford/Istanbul pattern of one street per trade.

**(b) Measured.**
- Ledbury Market House: 7 bays; 16 posts (7 + 3 per side); begun c. 1617; raised 600 mm for restoration 2006 (CONFIRMED Wikipedia; post layout CONFIRMED-digest HE 1349392).
- Chipping Campden 1627: 2 × 5 arches; cost £90; no upper floor (CONFIRMED britainexpress).
- Milly-la-Forêt halle 1479: 46 × 16 m, 13 m high, 730 m², 48 oak posts (CONFIRMED fondation-patrimoine).
- Ghent Groot Vleeshuis: c. 70/71 × 12.75 m exterior (CONFIRMED-digest); 3-bay chapel extension 1446–48; 16 penshuisjes 1542–43; 4 boat channels (CONFIRMED nl.wikipedia).
- Haarlem: 26 outdoor butchers' stalls licensed 1592; hall rent 30 guilders/yr 1604, fixed at 16 in 1619 (CONFIRMED nl.wikipedia).
- Chichester Cross: 50 ft tall, octagonal, 8 flying buttresses (CONFIRMED).
- Amsterdam Waag: tower walls almost 2 m thick; courtyard roofed 1617–18 (CONFIRMED).
- Newmarket 1472/3: Drapery at least 23 shops/stalls, Mercery 9.5, Old Drapery 2; one butcher renting 3 shops in Butchery row 1411–37 (CONFIRMED Boydell).
- Oxford: 56 victual sites 1320 paying 4s 4½d each; 18 fishmongers' stalls for Winchelsea fish 1360; 12 shops in Butcher Row 1623; 1712 fish structure = 5 fishboards + 2 chambers over + 3 shops; stallage 1428 = 1d/day a standing, 2d a cart space; 1751 town hall lower storey a corn market "opening upon the street through nine archways"; 1774 covered market 40 butchers' shops (CONFIRMED VCH).
- Stourbridge (Defoe 1724): Duddery 80–100 yards square; field c. half a mile square (CONFIRMED; 18th-century witness).
- Ypres Cloth Hall: 125 m breadth; belfry 70 m (CONFIRMED; reconstruction).
- Stall pitch: the only measured stall found is Istanbul's pre-1894 dolap, 6–8 ft wide × 3–4 ft deep, the merchant seated on a divan in front of his shelves (CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Bazaar,_Istanbul fetched 2026-08-23 — a 19th-century observation). No measured English medieval stall frontage found (searched: "medieval market stall width feet regulations stallage pitch", "fish market / fish shambles fish boards"); PLAUSIBLE range for a board or booth frontage 6–12 ft, depth 3–6 ft.
- Market-place area: no measured figures found (searched: "market place size acres hectares Salisbury Norwich Newark Market Harborough"); Salisbury's VCH says only "probably larger than at present". PLAUSIBLE: English chartered market places of c. 0.3–2 ha, i.e. roughly 50–150 yards a side, which brackets the catalog's "50–100 yards per side".

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**
- The market is frequently a STREET, not a square: Oxford's market "centred on Carfax and extended into the surrounding streets", spreading down High Street by 1331, with sellers in the middle of the road (CONFIRMED VCH). The catalog's "central plaza" for L900 is one of two morphologies; the search digest's "triangular footprint where two roads meet" is the other (CONFIRMED-digest).
- The fish market is a FIXTURE, not a building, through most of the register: fish "boards" against house fronts and under the guildhall; the first built fish house at Oxford is 1712 (CONFIRMED VCH). Ghent's fish market was reached by boat channels under the meat hall, i.e. it is the waterfront edge of the vleeshuis, not a hall of its own (CONFIRMED). The catalog L501 "open-air stall or small covered market" is the right floor; "covered" is the ceiling, not the default.
- The market hall with a room over is LATE: the three English examples are c. 1617, c. 1620 and 1627; the lead-roofed Oxford corn market is 1536; Milly 1479 is the earliest measured open halle fetched. For the 13th–15th-century register the hall is PLAUSIBLE only in large towns; the cross and tollbooth are the fixtures.
- Fairs can live inside buildings and towns can outgrow them: Oxford's fair moved into the guildhall courtyard and the street (CONFIRMED); the Champagne fairs' halles and Provins' grange were searched (fr.wikipedia "Grange aux dîmes de Provins" returned no extract) and no measured plan was obtained — open question.
- Negation search result: "middle row market place infill encroachment permanent stalls became houses" returned only generic aggregators confirming that "stalls... became permanent shops" (CONFIRMED-digest, weak); the primary confirmation came instead from VCH Salisbury and Oxford. Null result: no source found that denies encroachment in any English market town; the counter-case is the French halle, where the covered hall pre-empts infill and the square stays open (PLAUSIBLE).

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**
- Periodic market L397 / Weekly market L468: no enclosed circulation; the market ground is a zoned exterior. Nothing to license.
- Weekly market L907 / Market square L900: exterior zones + fixtures; where a market hall exists: `GALLERY` (the open arcade, 5–7 bays, c. 3–5 m between posts PLAUSIBLE from Ledbury/Campden bay counts against plausible 15–25 m lengths; Milly's 48 posts over 46 × 16 m give c. 4 m post centres — CONFIRMED arithmetic on confirmed figures) → external straight timber stair at one gable end (Ledbury "wooden staircase at the north end"; Titchfield stair with lock-up beneath) → upper `THROUGH_ROOM` single chamber. Stair type: straight flight / winder; never spiral in the timber halls. Licensed at town tier, prosperity ≥ middling, era ≥ late-medieval (1479 earliest measured), English form c. 1600+.
- Fish market L501: exterior; a lean-to pentice (`GALLERY`) against the guildhall or row is the only covered form until the 18th c.
- Annual fair L915 / Major annual fairs L1569: the booth streets themselves are the circulation — named rows (Cheapside, Garlick Row) and a wholesale square of c. 80–100 yards; a pie-powder shed; no stairs.
- Multiple market squares L1548 / Daily markets L1555 / District markets L2410: add the walled commodity hall: a single long `THROUGH_ROOM` hall lined with benches (Ghent: one nave c. 70 × 12.75 m; chapel/meeting room off it), the weigh-house (scales hall below; guild rooms above each with its own entrance and stair — Amsterdam); the bedesten-type covered street (see Family R). Stair: winder/spiral in stone halls; the Waag's guild stairs are separate per guild.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**
- Market place proper: no cells. Fixtures only: cross (with seat), tollbooth, stocks/pillory (PLAUSIBLE), weigh-beam/tron, conduit, fixed boards. Piccage stakes = zero storage.
- Market hall: upper room = `STORE` / `GARRET_STORE` (Ledbury's grain store — heavyFloor, lightReq ANY, adjacency: over the arcade, reached only by the end stair); the lock-up is a fixture-sized cell (`concealed`/`evidence` host) under the stair. Campden has no storage at all.
- Fish market: the fishboard is a fixture; a `STORE` of salt and barrels is PLAUSIBLE behind the row; no cellar.
- Commodity halls: Ghent — benches as fixtures, the chapel/meeting room as the guild's `records` room; boat channels = a wet `UNDERCROFT`. Waag — cellars (Amsterdam's were reopened on restoration) = `CELLAR`; upper floors = guild `records`/armoury (Gouda). Cloth hall (Ypres) = `STORE` warehouse bays over a market floor (CONFIRMED function; no measured bays).
- Fair: the booth IS the store, the shop and the bed (Defoe); a `STORE` class at booth size, lightReq NONE, adjacency: the booth back.

**(f) Typed proposal.**
- `MARKET_GROUND_ZONED` (L397, L468, L501 floor, L900): functions floor = bare ground + wooden cross; rungs = stone cross / tollbooth / fixed boards / weigh-beam / conduit; ceiling = paved, rows fossilised as shop-houses. fixtures[]: cross(seat), tollbooth, stocks+pillory, tron, conduit, fishboards, piccage stakes. structural: none (`wetTrade` only on the fish/meat edge, `noxious` on the shambles edge). licensing: hamlet×any×any; village×charter; town×REQ. HOME: ROOM_KINDS.stall for the fixed board; otherwise NO TYPED HOME (exterior zone — the engine has no exterior-zone room kind).
- `MARKET_HALL_ARCADE_OVER` (L907, L900 ceiling, L1548): `GALLERY` arcade 5–7 bays + end stair + one upper `THROUGH_ROOM`. fixtures[]: posts on stone bases, cobbled floor, external stair, cage under stair, grain store (heavyFloor), bell/clock (PLAUSIBLE). structural: `heavyFloor`. licensing: town×middling+×c.1480+ (continental) / c.1600+ (English). HOME: ROOM_KINDS.hall (arcade), ROOM_KINDS.store (upper), ROOM_KINDS.concealed (cage).
- `HALLE_OPEN_TIMBER` (continental variant, L900/L1548): 48-post 730 m² single volume, no upper room. HOME: ROOM_KINDS.hall.
- `COMMODITY_HALL_WALLED` (L1548, L1555, L2410): one long bench-lined nave, guild chapel/meeting room, external tripe stalls, water access. fixtures[]: benches, hooks, channels/boat dock, chapel. structural: `wetTrade`, `noxious` (meat), `waterAccess`. licensing: city×prosperous×1400+. HOME: ROOM_KINDS.hall + ROOM_KINDS.records (chapel/meeting).
- `WEIGH_HOUSE` (L1548/L2410): scales hall + per-guild upper rooms + cellar. fixtures[]: balance beams, weights chest, guild doors. HOME: ROOM_KINDS.hall, ROOM_KINDS.cellar, ROOM_KINDS.records.
- `FAIR_BOOTH_TOWN` (L915, L1569): named booth streets, wholesale square 80–100 yd, pie-powder shed, tent taverns; booths double as lodging. fixtures[]: board front, straw bed, pack-opening apron. licensing: town×any×any (seasonal). HOME: ROOM_KINDS.stall + ROOM_KINDS.lodging (same cell — the engine must allow a dual-kind cell or a booth kind).
- `CLOTH_HALL` (L1569 ceiling): Ypres-type warehouse-over-market with belfry. HOME: ROOM_KINDS.store + ROOM_KINDS.hall.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**
The engine needs an EXTERIOR ZONED PARTI: a bounded open polygon subdivided into commodity zones with point fixtures (cross, tollbooth, tron, stocks) and linear fixtures (rows of boards) that can harden over time into shop-house rows — i.e. an encroachment rule that converts a row fixture into a terrace of `stall`+`lodging` cells. Nothing in ROOM_KINDS expresses an outdoor zone or a fixture-only institution; the catalog's REQ market square currently has no building to instantiate. Second, a dual-kind cell (booth = stall + lodging) or a `booth` kind for fairs. Third, an "arcade-under, room-over" parti with an external end stair, which is the market hall, the Chester Row and the inn gallery all at once — this lands on the `GALLERY` pull. Fourth, a seasonal flag: fairs exist only on their days, and a fair can collapse into a guildhall courtyard (Oxford) — the grammar should let L915 point at an existing hall rather than always minting a field.

**(h) Continental vs English.**
The English register is parochial in making the market hall a small timber-and-room affair of c. 1600 and in treating the guild as an owner of stalls rather than of buildings; the continental mechanism is the French halle (a vast posted roof of the 1470s licensed with the fairs themselves), the Flemish/Dutch guild hall (vleeshuis, lakenhalle, waag — walled, stone, with chapel and meeting room, water channels and per-guild stairs, and a city rent-roll that charges butchers 30 guilders for a prestigious bench), and further east the Ottoman covered street with its fixed 6–8 ft dolap stalls and iron gates. The English cross-with-seat and tollbooth are closer to the Italian loggia's civic function than to any hall.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.

**Parent-lane review note.** The sub-lane flags a catalog data slip: L1670 Auction house carries priorityCategory "military" in the flat TSV (ledger item). The exterior-zoned market parti it proposes is the single largest NO TYPED HOME in this tranche — every REQ market entry at town/city tier currently has no building to instantiate.

## §3 · FAMILY B — the shop-house: Cobbler (L689) · Cobbler's guild (L1177) · Tailor (L696) · Tailor's guild (L1184) · Chandler (L1191) · Jeweller (L1051) · Luxury goods quarter (L1724) · Furrier's district (L1685) · Dungeon delving supply district (L2125)

*(English register written by the parent lane from rounds 1–2; the continental register and the trade-specific fixture sets — cobbler/cordwainer, tailor, tallow vs wax chandler, goldsmith, skinner — are sub-lane BE's and follow as §3-bis below.)*

**(a) Analogue.** The burgage-plot shop-house. Pantin's 1962–3 typology (CONFIRMED-primary, `RINST2-pantin.txt` pp. 203–5) is the parti catalog for every town-tier entry in this family: the hall PARALLEL to the street in four forms — (i) 'extended' (two- or three-part hall house strung along the street, "needs a fairly large frontage, of say 30 to 50 feet", so found "in the side streets of large towns, or in small towns"); (ii) 'contracted' (hall across the full tenement width, solar perched over the screens-passage = 'Beam Hall' type, or solar behind the hall = 'Broad Street' type; Coventry examples converted from the first to the second by adding a two-storey rear wing); (iii) 'double-range' = the 'Tackley's Inn' type ("two parallel ranges, the hall occupying the back range and a row of shops occupying the front range; this had the advantage of allowing commercial use of the frontage, and so was specially suitable in the main streets of a town"); (iv) 'courtyard' (hall range parallel to the street behind a court "separated from the street by a row of shops, the courtyard being reached by an entry between the shops" — the large merchant's form, "entre cour et jardin"); and the hall at RIGHT ANGLES to the street ("economical of street frontage and thus suitable for comparatively narrow tenements; it must have been common from the earliest times"), in 'narrow' and 'broad' variants, the broad one with the warehouse/courtyard behind (Hampton Court, King's Lynn). The SMALLEST houses — "only one or two rooms to each floor, where the hall plan… is applied vertically, the solar being piled on top of the hall, or the hall or solar piled on top of the shop" — Pantin excludes (p. 202); they are exactly the village Cobbler and Tailor, and the row-house evidence covers them: Lady Row, Goodramgate, York (1316; a row of two-storey jettied units, c. 128 ft × 18 ft overall, 11 bays; shop below, chamber above; built on the churchyard edge by the parish as rent — Wikipedia fetched 2026-08-23, CONFIRMED-aggregator), Tewkesbury's Church Street row (c. 1410, 23 units built by the abbey as a speculative rent row; John Moore Museum page fetched 2026-08-23, CONFIRMED), and the late-15th-c. Horsham shop re-erected at the Weald & Downland Museum: two units, each a ground-floor shop with a smoke bay behind, a rear stair to the chamber, an unglazed window closed by a shutter, timbers sooted from an open hearth — a shop-house with NO hall (W&D page fetched 2026-08-23, CONFIRMED). The shopfront mechanism: a window opening closed by two horizontal shutters, the upper swinging up as a pentice/awning and the lower dropping down as a STALL-BOARD (the counter projecting into the street) — buildingconservation.com and buildinghistory.org pages fetched 2026-08-23, CONFIRMED; so the medieval shop's counter is an EXTERIOR fixture in the open position and the wall in the closed one. Two-level shopping is the Chester Rows: continuous first-floor walkways with shops off them above street-level undercroft shops, origin c. 1200–1350 (VCH Chester fetched 2026-08-23, CONFIRMED; figures in (b)). Live/work: the master lives over or behind the shop at every grade (the solar over the shop at Tackley's; the chamber over the Horsham shop; the parlour/chambers over the Bucklersbury shop); the prosperous merchant separates the household (hall range behind the court) from the let shops (1.2). Prosperity grades: FLOOR = one shop-room + one chamber over (the row unit); RUNG 1 = shop + hall behind + solar (Pantin's contracted/right-angle narrow plans); RUNG 2 = shop + 'sotelhouse' (showroom) + hall + parlour + counting-house + warehouse + cellar (Bucklersbury 1405; Hampton Court); CEILING = the courtyard merchant house with a row of LET shops on the street, warehouse range with open arcade to the quay, and a solar 44 × 22 ft (Hampton Court, see (b)). Decline sheds in reverse: the counting-house becomes a parlour, the warehouse a stable, the shops are let, the hall is floored over and the house becomes cottages (Hampton Court's 18th-c. fate — Pantin p. 235; a law-5 fossil sequence). Tier distinctions: village Cobbler/Tailor = the row unit or a bay of the craftsman's own house; town guild entries (L1177/L1184) = a STREET of such shop-houses (the guild is the institution, the guild HALL is family S); Jeweller = the goldsmith's shop-house with a strongroom (sub-lane BE); Luxury goods quarter / Furrier's district = let-shop rows on the principal street (1.2).

**(b) Measured (all CONFIRMED unless marked).** Shops "sometimes only 6 or 10 ft. wide" (Pantin p. 211). Tackley's Inn, Oxford, c. 1291–1300: five shops "each about 8–12 ft. wide, with solars belonging to them above, and a vaulted cellar of five bays below, having two entrances from the street"; "possibly… each shop should have the use of one bay, but by 1363 the whole cellar was let as a wine tavern"; hall behind 33 × 20 ft, open roof of three bays; two-storey block east of the hall with rooms "about 21 or 25 ft. by 17 ft."; detached kitchen probable in the court (Pantin pp. 217–18). Bucklersbury, London, contract of 1405: street range with entry + 'sotelhouse' + shop 22 × 18 ft "(large as medieval shops went)", cellar below, two storeys of parlour and chambers above; hall range at the back with a warehouse (cellar below), a latrine pit 16 × 10 ft, a wood house and a stable; open stair with an 'oriel' up to a first-floor hall 33 × 20 ft of two bays with a bay window; screens-passage at the hall's east end opening to kitchen, buttery and pantry; a 'tresaunce' gallery along the west side of the court giving access to the chambers (Pantin pp. 223–5; "We cannot be certain that the building was actually erected"). Hampton Court, King's Lynn: quadrangle c. 90 × 40 ft internally; 14th-c. hall 35 × 22 ft, open roof of two bays, fireplace in the south wall, three arched doorways (two to buttery and pantry, one to the stair); a two-storey solar block with a ground-floor parlour and a second ground-floor room "with an external arched doorway, and perhaps served as a counting-house, being close to the adjoining warehouse and quay"; solar above c. 44 × 22 ft, three bays; a c. 1500 west range with "an open arcade of eight bays towards the adjacent quay… for receiving merchandize, while the upper story was probably a warehouse"; shops on the street side, rebuilt early 16th c. (Pantin pp. 233–5). Screens-passages 3–4 ft wide in the small Coventry houses; a "broad screens-passage or entry, 10 ft. wide" in a large Oxford house (Pantin pp. 213–16). Chester Rows: undercrofts 3.7–9 m wide × 10–41 m deep; Row-level shops as small as c. 2 m × 3 m; stallboards c. 2.3 m deep; walkway c. 2 m (VCH Chester fetched 2026-08-23; the per-figure attributions are from the search digest of the same page — CONFIRMED-digest for the individual numbers). Lady Row c. 128 × 18 ft, 11 bays (CONFIRMED-aggregator).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** (1) Salter's law (1.1) is the dry-retail case; the spicer/grocer who "must keep a stock" is the counterexample inside the same sentence — a STORE-shop with a warehouse behind is lawful at rung 1 for the stock trades (chandler, spicer, mercer), so the `SHOP_WORKSHOP` cell has a `stockBias` (MADE_TO_ORDER | STOCKED). (2) The poverty inversion is real and documented: Lady Row and the Tewkesbury row are landlord-built rent rows — the poorest shop-house is not the craftsman's own house at all but a unit he rents; below that, the craftsman sells from a market stall (family A) and works at home (charter law 6's "market-stall poverty inversion"). (3) Pantin's houses are "middle-sized and fairly large" by his own selection (p. 202); the typology under-represents the one-room-deep unit — the Horsham and Lady Row evidence is the corrective, not Pantin. (4) The Chester Rows are a unique morphology (VCH), not a parti the grammar should draw anywhere but as a named culture variant. Negation searches this lane: "medieval shop separate from house" and "shops let separately" — found the Pantin/Salter primary confirming both the letting and the workshop-not-store claim; no credible source found claiming a free-standing medieval retail 'shop building' separate from a dwelling in the English register.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** Village Cobbler/Tailor (row unit): `THROUGH_ROOM` only — the shop is entered from the street, the chamber by a `LADDER`/`WINDER` stair at the rear (Horsham: "a rear stair"); no passage. Town shop-house (Pantin contracted/right-angle narrow): `SCREENS_PASSAGE` 3–4 ft wide between shop and hall, two doors (street and yard), service doors off it; `THROUGH_ROOM` hall→parlour; `WINDER` stair. Double-range/courtyard (Tackley's, Bucklersbury, Hampton Court): `CROSS_PASSAGE` as a 10-ft ENTRY between the shops to the court (a carriage-width passage — the same cell as the inn's yard entry); `GALLERY` = the 'tresaunce' along the court giving access to chambers (Bucklersbury 1405 — CONFIRMED; this is the earliest dated domestic long-hallway chamber in this tranche and it is a MERCHANT'S, which licenses `GALLERY` at town tier for the courtyard parti from c. 1400); an external `STAIR_HALL`-in-the-open (the open stair with its oriel landing to a first-floor hall — a stair as an exterior cell); `LONG_GALLERY` = Hampton Court's eight-bay open arcade to the quay (a loggia, ground floor, commercial). Era grade: all pre-1500 — the `CORRIDOR` never appears in this family; the shop-house's only corridor-like cell is the entry/passage. Stair type by grade: ladder (row unit) → winder (rung 1) → open external stair with oriel (rung 2, courtyard) — no grand stair in this family.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** Row unit: NONE as a cell — a hanging shelf and a chest are FIXTURES of the shop/chamber. Rung 1: `BUTTERY` + `PANTRY` as a pair off the screens-passage (Pantin p. 212: "buttery and pantry, for wet and dry stores respectively; of the two, the buttery would probably be the larger"; where there is only one store-room "it would probably be called the buttery, or… the dispence"); `CELLAR` where an undercroft exists (the 5-bay vault at Tackley's — a §311 joint: the cellar has its OWN street doors and can be a separate tenancy, a wine tavern). Rung 2: `STORE` = the warehouse bay (Bucklersbury: warehouse with cellar below; Hampton Court: the arcade range's upper storey), `CELLAR` under both shop and warehouse, the 'sotelhouse' as a show-store, a wood house (`STORE`, fuel). LightReq: the buttery/pantry pair sits at the hall's low end, unlit or north; the warehouse wants the QUAY door, not light. Adjacency law: counting-house "close to the adjoining warehouse and quay" with its own EXTERNAL door (Hampton Court) — the counting-house is a frontage-of-the-yard cell, not a frontage-of-the-street one.

**(f) Typed proposal.**
- `parti`: `ROW_UNIT_SHOP_UNDER_CHAMBER` (floor; 1 bay, 2 storeys) · `PARALLEL_CONTRACTED_BEAM_HALL` · `PARALLEL_CONTRACTED_BROAD_STREET` · `PARALLEL_EXTENDED` (frontage ≥ 30 ft) · `DOUBLE_RANGE_SHOPS_BEFORE_HALL` (Tackley's) · `COURTYARD_SHOPS_ENTRY_HALL_BEHIND` (Bucklersbury) · `RIGHT_ANGLE_NARROW` · `RIGHT_ANGLE_BROAD_WITH_WAREHOUSE_COURT` (Hampton Court) · `ROWS_TWO_LEVEL` (Chester; culture-gated, off by default).
- `functions[]`: floor = `SHOP_WORKSHOP` (frontage; HOME: ROOM_KINDS.stall — misnamed, see §Σ), `SLEEPING` (over); rung 1 adds `HALL`, `SOLAR`, `BUTTERY`+`PANTRY`, `KITCHEN`; rung 2 adds `SHOWROOM` (NO TYPED HOME), `COUNTING_HOUSE` (HOME: counting), `WAREHOUSE_BAY` (HOME: store), `CELLAR` (HOME: cellar, law-7 gated), `PARLOUR`, `STABLE`, `LATRINE_PIT`; ceiling adds `LET_SHOPS[]` (tenancy layer), `QUAY_ARCADE`, `GALLERY`.
- `fixtures[]`: `STALL_BOARD_SHUTTER_PAIR` (floor; exterior in the open state), `WORKBENCH` (HOME), `COUNTER` (HOME; = the stall-board at the floor, a fixed counter at rung 1+), `TOOL_RACK` (HOME: rack), `CHEST`, `HANGING_SHELF`, `SMOKE_BAY_HEARTH` (floor; pre-chimney), `WALL_FIREPLACE` (rung 1+), `BAY_WINDOW` (rung 2, hall), `ORIEL_LANDING`, `TRESAUNCE_GALLERY`, `ARCADE_OPEN` (quay), `VAULT_RIBBED` (cellar), `STRONGBOX` (HOME; jeweller/goldsmith — sub-lane BE).
- buckets: `frontageBucket` {≤10 ft | 10–20 | 20–30 | ≥30} drives the parti (Pantin's 30–50 ft threshold is the one measured parti-switch in this family); `tenancy` {OWNER_USED | LET_SEPARATELY} per frontage cell; `stockBias` {MADE_TO_ORDER | STOCKED}.
- licensing: `ROW_UNIT` at village+; `DOUBLE_RANGE`/`COURTYARD` at town+ (needs ≥ 3 shop bays of frontage); `RIGHT_ANGLE_BROAD_WITH_WAREHOUSE_COURT` needs a water or back-lane edge (NO TYPED HOME — `frontage.js` has no water-edge kind; §Σ).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) The `trade` and `craft` interior templates must merge at the floor into one `SHOP_WORKSHOP` frontage cell and diverge only by fixtures; the present split forces a village cobbler to choose between 'hall+counting' and 'workfloor+store', neither of which is his one room. (2) A `tenancy` layer on frontage cells is new capability: the grammar can then derive a 'district' entry as N let-cells across the frontages of M houses (this is how Luxury goods quarter, Furrier's district, Apothecary district and the guild-street entries should land — as a STREET composition, not a building). (3) `frontageBucket` is readable from the parcel today (law 6) — the parti switch at ~30 ft is the first measured consumer of it. (4) The cellar-as-separate-tenancy (Tackley's wine tavern) is a law-7 joint with its own street door — the undercity projection must allow a cellar whose surface joint is a STREET hatch/stair, not the house's stair.

**(h) Continental vs English.** Pantin himself ends Bucklersbury with "the combined merchants' houses and warehouses of the Hanseatic towns" (p. 225) — the continental mechanism stacks the warehouse OVER the dwelling (the Lübeck Diele house) where the English ranges it round a court; and the Italian bottega / Bernese Laube / Bolognese portico put the shop under an ARCADE that the English row lacks. Sub-lane BE carries the measured continental cases in §3-bis.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None for B proper; the Dungeon delving supply district's note is in §3-bis (i).

## §3-bis · FAMILY B — the shop-house (retail crafts & luxury), CONTINENTAL register + unresearched members: Cobbler L689 · Cobbler's guild L1177 · Tailor L696 · Tailor's guild L1184 · Chandler L1191 · Jeweller L1051 · Luxury goods quarter L1724 · Furrier's district L1685 · Dungeon delving supply district L2125

*Researched by sub-lane BE (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. Continental register and the trade-specific fixture sets for family B; the English register is §3 above.*

(Scope note: Pantin, Tackley's Inn, Bucklersbury 1405, Chester Rows, Lady Row, Horsham, the English shopfront mechanics are the parent's rounds 1–2 and are not restated. Everything below is NEW material.)

**(a) Analogue.**

*The continental shop-house is the same parti as Pantin's shop-with-solar, but with a covered or arched FRONT that the English register lacks.* Four mechanisms:

1. **French boutique/ouvroir/étal.** Viollet-le-Duc's *Dictionnaire* entry 'Boutique' (fr.wikisource.org, fetched 2026-08-23, **CONFIRMED**) describes the medieval French shop as a room opening on the street by a "grand arc prenant toute la largeur de la pièce", closed below by a "mur d'appui pour poser les marchandises" (the étal — a low stone or wood sill that IS the counter) interrupted on one side for the door; the ouvroir behind is where "ouvriers et apprentis travaillaient"; a private stair goes up to the first floor and down to a cellar; the shutters are two-leaved, the lower folding out as a display shelf, the upper raised like a skylight. Examples cited at Cluny (12th c.), Cordes, Saint-Yriex, Périgueux, Albi, Saint-Antonin, Montferrand, Riom, Reims, Beauvais, Chartres, Dol (12th–15th c.). Room program = ARCH-FRONT SHOP (étal sill) → OUVROIR (workroom, same floor, behind) → private stair → solar above / cellar below. Live/work: yes, master above. This is the Pantin right-angle narrow type with an ARCHED instead of a shuttered window-front.

2. **Swiss/German Laube (arcaded frontage).** Bern: after the 1405 fire (about a third of the city, c. 600 houses) the wooden houses were re-fronted in stone, the stone façade set roughly "2 meter vor der holzfassade", the ground storey of that extension arched through to make the arcade; the word *Laden* (shop) is derived from folding the shutter down (*runterklappen*) to trade under cover (stadtwanderer.net/?p=2291, fetched 2026-08-23, **CONFIRMED**, a local-history blog; de.wikipedia Berner Altstadt, fetched, **CONFIRMED**: 1479 Bonstetten — "vaults on both sides, under which one can walk with dry feet"; total length "well over six kilometres"). Under the arcade floor the *Kellerhals* — the cellar stair/hatch entered from the arcade — so the cellar is let or used SEPARATELY from the house (digest, bern.fandom / panorama pages, **CONFIRMED-digest**). The Cambridge *Urban History* paper on arcaded houses (cambridge.org, fetched 2026-08-23, **CONFIRMED**): arcades are a 13th–14th-c. cluster across N. Italy, S. Germany, Switzerland, Silesia, S. France, Spain; Freiburg's 1249 municipal law let citizens "construct brick arches in front of their houses and build upon them"; arcades were "introduced subsequently", not planned; weavers were "permitted to trade while standing under an arcade"; some houses had "independent access to the upper floors" by "steep stairs from the arcade" — i.e. the upper dwelling could be tenanted separately from the shop. So the Laube shop-house is: ARCADE WALK (public, covered) → shop with stall-shutter → Werkstatt/Stube behind; cellar by Kellerhals from the arcade; solar by its own stair. **Counterexample (important):** the Nuremberg *Handwerkerhof* is NOT a survival — it opened 1 April 1971 for the Dürer year, built by the fair company AFAG inside the Waffenhof at a cost of 800,000 DM, with "imitated half-timbered façades" (de.wikipedia Handwerkerhof Nürnberg, **CONFIRMED-digest**). Do not cite it as evidence of anything medieval.

3. **Italian bottega + portico.** Bologna's 1288 statute made the portico mandatory on new houses and retro-fitted to old ones, minimum seven Bolognese feet (2.66 m) high "and equally wide", to pass a man on horseback; shopkeepers occupied the portico for shelter and light (bolognadavedere.it / Bologna Welcome, **CONFIRMED-digest**). Florentine botteghe: houses with ground-floor shops were recorded in the 15th-c. tax returns (catasto 1427, 1480); the bottega was at once place of production, trade and training; a ground-floor salesroom opened on the street where finished work was displayed and the master received clients (digests of the academia.edu 'Home shopping' paper and of thecultural.me / thecollector, **CONFIRMED-digest**; the paper itself returned 403). The *sportello* is the tiny wine-window of rich houses, and the "botteghe a sportello" category was formalised in 1759 (en.wikipedia Buchetta shop, **CONFIRMED-digest**) — so the *sportello* is NOT a medieval artisan's fixture; treat it as a palace-service hatch. The 'sporto' (jettied overhang) and the 'bancone' (counter) returned nothing on the web this session: **PLAUSIBLE** only (training: the bancone is the sill-counter under the arch, the same object as the French étal). **Live/work in Florence is CONTESTED** — see (c).

4. **Dutch voorhuis.** Amsterdam canal houses were narrow (digest: "not more than 30 ft wide (9 m)", whatsupwithamsterdam.com, aggregator, **CONFIRMED-digest**); the *voorhuis* is entered directly from the street, undivided, high-ceilinged, the full width of the house, and is the trading/reception room; behind it the *binnenhaard* (= binnenkamer/binnenkeuken/winterkeuken) round the central hearth; deep plots have a *voorhuis* + smaller *achterhuis* separated by an inner court (UNESCO atlas + amsterdam.info digests, **CONFIRMED-digest**; the UNESCO page itself 403). The Vingboons 1648 plan of the Daniel Sohier house shows "voorhuis, side room, and inner room, corridor along the inner court to the achterhuis with zaal" (American University Omeka caption, **CONFIRMED-digest**) — note the CORRIDOR along the court, licensed in Dutch merchant houses by 1648. Counting-room (*comptoir*) is a side room off the voorhuis (**PLAUSIBLE**, training).

*Trade-specific fixtures (the unresearched members):*

- **Cobbler vs cordwainer.** The cordwainer works only NEW leather; the cobbler works OLD (repair); in 1395, after a street fight, the Mayor of London banned cobblers from making new shoes and cordwainers from patching old ones; cobblers dodged it by cutting 'new' shoes from salvaged leather; the two bodies merged in the 16th c. (cordwainers.org + thehcc.org digests, **CONFIRMED-digest**). The building consequence: the *cobbler* (village L689) is a STALL trade — a bench, a last, awl and knife, no store and no stair; the *cordwainer* (the guild shop, L1177) is a shop-house with a leather store and a drying/cutting bench. Last rack, cutting board and 'window bench' (the shoemaker works at the light): **PLAUSIBLE** (training; iconographic, not excavated).
- **Tailor.** Apprentices were taught "the tailor's posture", sitting cross-legged on a raised board while sewing; 15th-c. images show the tailor sitting IN HIS WINDOW (seasaltercross.com / thegenealogist digests, **CONFIRMED-digest**); the later (19th-c.) establishment — master measures in a front room, cutting/sewing in adjoining rooms (en.wikipedia Tailor digest) — is a POST-period elaboration. So the medieval tailor's shop is a single window-lit room with the shop-board (a broad raised platform/bench at the window) as its only distinctive fixture; pattern store and pressing irons are **PLAUSIBLE**. No furnace, no water.
- **Chandler — two different buildings.** *Tallow chandler* (L1191's first half): worked "small workshops fronted with little shop fronts" in a poor quarter; transformed butchers' raw fat "into dipped and moulded candles"; rendering was "a disgusting and smelly job" and tallow producers "were often moved about the City, due to complaints made about the stench" (tallowchandlers.org, fetched 2026-08-23, **CONFIRMED**) — the melting is an outhouse/yard function that gets pushed outward. *Wax chandler*: the 1371 London ordinances govern tapers, torches, square funeral candles, torchettes, prickets, perchers; adulteration of wax with tallow/resin fined 6s 8d → 13s 4d → 20s → expulsion; every chandler stamps his own mark; hire of candles plus iron stands ("hearses") at 9d/lb — and the ordinances contain "no mention of shop premises" (waxchandlers.org.uk, fetched 2026-08-23, **CONFIRMED**). So the wax chandler is a luxury RETAIL shop (church and funeral trade) with a small melting hearth; the tallow chandler's defining fixture is the rendering copper and it lives at the noxious edge. Soap and rope (the catalog's "similar raw materials") belong to the tallow side and, for rope, to a ropewalk — a different building family.
- **Jeweller/goldsmith.** Goldsmiths' Row, south side of Cheapside between Bread St and Friday St: "ten houses and fourteen shops" (MoEML GOLD6, fetched 2026-08-23, **CONFIRMED**, citing Stow 1:296, 1:345–6); built 1491 by Thomas Wood, goldsmith and sheriff; "all in one frame, builded foure stories high" (Stow, via Mark Patton blog digest, **CONFIRMED-digest**); in the painting of Edward VI's coronation procession (1547) "the windows of the shops in Goldsmiths' Row are stocked with standing cups and other plate" (MoEML, **CONFIRMED**); the Goldsmiths' Company held the freehold and tried through the 16th–17th c. to keep the shops let to goldsmiths, "often difficult to enforce" (digest, **CONFIRMED-digest**; Reddaway 1963 is the primary for that). 14 shops under 10 houses means shops NARROWER than houses (some houses carry two shops). Interior: a show-window of plate + a small forge/bench + a strongroom: **PLAUSIBLE** (no interior primary fetched; the Met essay and Reddaway would carry it).
- **Furrier/skinner.** BHO *London Record Society* 38 ch. III (fetched 2026-08-23, **CONFIRMED**): the Peltry in West Cheap first mentioned 1274; "the shops and homes of skinners extended from Cheap southwards, near the banks of the River Walbrook, to the Thames"; Walbrook ward held 22 skinners in 1292, 24 in 1319, 31 in 1332; TWO business models — the small skinner "carried no stock of materials and … needed little equipment; his customers would purchase the raw skins they required and then bring them to him to be dressed and made up", vs. the prosperous skinner who "bought raw skins, dressed them, made them up, and sold them in their shops"; at Beverley (1359) skinners "paid ¼d. a week for a 'window' in which they worked in full gaze of their customers"; the 1300 skinner–tawyer agreement fixed piece rates (5s–6s per 1,000 greywerk) and made trading in dressed skins a skinner monopoly; the 1288 ordinance fixed skins-per-fur and banned mixing old and new skins. The 1345 ordinances ordered freemen of the trade to "dwell in Walbrook, Cornhill, and Budge Row" and that "no man shall cause his furs to be scoured in the high streets in the day-time" (pepysdiary encyclopedia / skinners.org digests, **CONFIRMED-digest**). The Hanse Steelyard stood opposite Skinners' Hall and was the Baltic/Novgorod fur source (skinners.org digest, **CONFIRMED-digest**). Building consequence: the SKINNER is a clean sewing/retail shop-house (the wet work — tawing — is the TAWYER's, by the Walbrook, a Family G/H building); drying loft **PLAUSIBLE** (no primary found naming one).
- **The luxury street as a ROW.** Goldsmiths' Row (above). Ponte Vecchio: in 1593 Grand Duke Ferdinando I expelled the butchers, fishmongers and tanners from the bridge shops and replaced them with goldsmiths and jewellers; the decree still governs the tenancies (italiani.it / florenceitaly.org digests, **CONFIRMED-digest**, aggregator grade, consistent across five pages but untraced to the decree). Pont au Change, Paris: returned NOTHING this session — **PLAUSIBLE** only (training: money-changers and goldsmiths on the rebuilt bridge, 17th c.). Bologna Mercato di Mezzo: not searched separately; the portico statute covers the mechanism.
- **Ship's chandler / outfitter (the L2125 analogue).** Ship chandlery as a trade is documented by the mid-17th c. (Pepys 1663, offshoresupply.co.uk digest, **CONFIRMED-digest**); stock = rope, tar, canvas, lanterns, ironmongery, navigational instruments, salt provisions, rum, tobacco (Mystic Seaport — a 19th-c. American example, **CONFIRMED-digest**); contracting was via the ship's agent, and a chandler could himself act as agent. The building is a WAREHOUSE-SHOP: deep store bays behind a counter, a loft, a yard, a quay-side siting — the Dutch *pakhuis* parti, not the shop-house.

Prosperity/wear: floor = a single arch-front room with the étal sill and a ladder to a loft (the French 12th-c. type); rungs = ouvroir behind, private stair, cellar, arcade/portico frontage; ceiling = a four-storey framed row of identical units under one company freehold (Goldsmiths' Row 1491). Decline sheds first the company control of tenancy (Goldsmiths' Row), then the specialised show-window (plate → mixed goods), then the arcade becomes an infilled shop.

**(b) Measured.**
- Saint-Antonin, 15th-c. boutique, "sept mètres" wide; ground storeys "trois ou quatre mètres" high (Viollet-le-Duc, **CONFIRMED**).
- Bern: stone façade c. 2 m in front of the wooden façade = arcade depth c. 2 m (stadtwanderer, **CONFIRMED**; inference that depth ≈ extension is mine); arcades >6 km total (de.wikipedia, **CONFIRMED**); 1405 fire c. 600 houses (stadtwanderer, **CONFIRMED**).
- Bologna 1288: portico min 7 Bolognese ft = 2.66 m high and equally wide (**CONFIRMED-digest**).
- Arcaded-house paper: arcade extension "about half the width of the arcade" or "by their full width" (cambridge.org, **CONFIRMED**) — a ratio, not a metre figure.
- Amsterdam: houses ≤ 30 ft (9 m) wide (**CONFIRMED-digest**, aggregator).
- Goldsmiths' Row: 10 houses / 14 shops / four storeys (**CONFIRMED** + **CONFIRMED-digest**); no frontage widths found (searched: "Goldsmiths' Row … widths", "Reddaway").
- Skinners: 22/24/31 skinners in Walbrook 1292/1319/1332; Beverley window ¼d/week 1359; 5s–6s per 1,000 greywerk 1300 (**CONFIRMED**).
- Wax chandler fines 6s 8d / 13s 4d / 20s; hearse hire 9d/lb (**CONFIRMED**).
- No measured figures found for: the Florentine bottega (searched: "bottega catasto dimensions sporto", "Goldthwaite bottega rented separately braccia"), the Venetian mezzanine shop (searched: "Venice shop mezzanine mezzà Welch"), Paris shop widths from the taille (searched: "taille 1292 1313 boutique ouvroir étal largeur" — only modern costume shops returned), Bern Lauben widths (searched twice, German and English).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**
- *Live/work is not universal.* The Florentine bottega was commonly a RENTED ground-floor unit in someone else's palace or house, with the artisan's dwelling elsewhere (**PLAUSIBLE**, training — Goldthwaite/Nevola; the web search for this exact claim returned null, and the 'Home shopping' paper 403'd). The Cambridge arcade paper's "independent access to the upper floors … steep stairs from the arcade" (**CONFIRMED**) shows the same separability in the Laube house. The engine should NOT hard-wire master-above-shop for the continental bottega.
- *Sold from a window, not a shop.* Beverley skinners rented a 'window' by the week (**CONFIRMED**) and the small London skinner carried no stock (**CONFIRMED**) — the stall/window, not the shop-house, is the floor of the furrier trade.
- *The sportello is not a shop.* The Florentine buchetta/sportello is an 18th-c. category (1759) grafted on palace wine-sales (**CONFIRMED-digest**).
- *The Handwerkerhof is a 1971 pastiche* (**CONFIRMED-digest**).
- *Ponte Vecchio goldsmiths are post-period* (1593) and the bridge was a BUTCHERS' row before — the luxury street can be a NOXIOUS street one decree earlier.
- *Tallow vs wax*: the catalog's single 'Chandler' fuses a noxious rendering trade (pushed out) with a luxury church-supply shop (1371 ordinances silent on premises) — two buildings.
- Negation searches run: "tallow chandler … removed from town" (found the relocation practice, no specific ordinance text); "bottega rented separately from house" (null on the web); "Nuremberg Handwerkerhof 1971" (confirmed it is a reconstruction). Null result worth recording: no source this session located a medieval Paris shop width from the taille rolls.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**
- Cobbler L689 / Tailor L696 (village): no circulation class — one room; ladder to loft at best. Tailor: window-board room; ladder.
- Cobbler's guild L1177 / Tailor's guild L1184 (town): `THROUGH_ROOM` (shop → ouvroir/back workroom, Viollet-le-Duc) at all grades; private stair (winder) to solar licensed from the floor; `CELLAR` stair down from the shop (French) OR from the street/arcade (Bern Kellerhals) — the latter is an `LOBBY`-less external entry, a distinct indication; `GALLERY` class for the Laube/portico walk in any settlement carrying the `ARCADE_FRONT` flag (Bern 2 m deep; Bologna ≥ 2.66 m wide), era ≥ 13th c. continental, ≥ 15th c. Bern.
- Chandler L1191: tallow — `THROUGH_ROOM` shop → yard melting house (external); wax — one-room shop, stair to solar.
- Jeweller L1051: `THROUGH_ROOM` shop (show-window) → workroom → strongroom, stair (winder → framed) to four storeys at the Goldsmiths' Row ceiling.
- Furrier's district L1685 / Luxury goods quarter L1724: the STREET is the circulation — a `GALLERY`-class covered walk (portico/Laube) or a bridge deck; units repeat; `CORRIDOR` only in the Dutch merchant-house variant (Vingboons 1648 corridor along the court) at city-prosperous grade.
- Dungeon delving supply district L2125: warehouse-shop: counter room → deep `STORE` bays through-connected; external hoist (Dutch), loft ladder.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**
- Cobbler: no cell; a leather bin is a FIXTURE. Cordwainer/guild: `STORE` (leather, NORTH/NONE light, dry), `CELLAR` where the plot has one.
- Tailor: `CLOSET` (pattern/cloth press — a fixture below a size); `STORE` for the guild shop (cloth bolts, dry, NONE light).
- Chandler: tallow — `STORE` for raw fat (cool, noxious → adjacency: rear/yard, never street), the melting house is a service cell outside the house; wax — `STORE` small (wax is valuable: adjacency to the counting/strong function, lockable).
- Jeweller: `STRONGROOM` (HOME: ROOM_KINDS.strongroom) mandatory from the floor upward — the defining storage law of this entry; `CLOSET` for the touch/assay kit (fixture).
- Furrier: `STORE` for dressed skins (dry, NONE/NORTH light, vermin-proof) and a `GARRET_STORE`/drying loft (**PLAUSIBLE**); the small skinner has NO store at all (customers bring skins — **CONFIRMED**).
- Luxury quarter: `CELLAR`/`UNDERCROFT` let separately (Bern Kellerhals, Tackley's Inn parallel from the parent) — storage that is NOT the shop's.
- Supply district (outfitter): `STORE` as the dominant cell (multiple bays: rope, tar, ironmongery, provisions), `CELLAR` for tar/oil, `GARRET_STORE` for canvas; adjacency law = quay/yard access and a hoist.

**(f) Typed proposal.**
- parti: `ARCH_FRONT_SHOP` (French/Italian: étal sill + full-width arch), `ARCADE_FRONT_SHOP` (Laube/portico; public covered walk in front, depth bucket 2–3 m), `VOORHUIS_SEQUENCE` (Dutch: trading hall → inner hearth room → court → achterhuis), `SHOP_ROW_FRAME` (identical units under one frame, 10 houses/14 shops), `BRIDGE_ROW` (shops on a bridge deck), `WINDOW_STALL` (the Beverley window — rented frontage, no building of its own), `WAREHOUSE_SHOP` (outfitter).
- functions[]: floor = `WINDOW_STALL` | one-room `ARCH_FRONT_SHOP` + ladder loft; rungs = back ouvroir, private winder stair, cellar (own or arcade-entered), strongroom (goldsmith), melting outhouse (tallow), drying loft (skinner); ceiling = `SHOP_ROW_FRAME` 4 storeys with company tenancy control, or `VOORHUIS_SEQUENCE` with corridor and comptoir.
- fixtures[]: étal/bancone sill-counter (HOME: FURNISHING_KINDS.counter), stall-shutter pair (parent), shop-board (tailor; NO TYPED HOME — closest workbench), last rack + cutting board (cordwainer; workbench + rack), tallow copper (cauldron), dipping frame/moulds (NO TYPED HOME), wax-mark stamp (fixture, no home), show-window of plate (counter), goldsmith's forge bench (hearth + workbench), strongbox (strongbox), fur press/drying rails (rack), hoist beam (NO TYPED HOME), Kellerhals cellar hatch (NO TYPED HOME).
- structural buckets: `noxious` (tallow rendering; also butchers/tanners on the pre-1593 bridge), `fireTrade` minor (wax/tallow/goldsmith hearths), `heavyFloor` none, `wetTrade` none (tawing is another family), `secureStore` (goldsmith — a NEW bucket), `coveredFrontage` (arcade/portico — a NEW frontage axis).
- licensing: `ARCADE_FRONT_SHOP` — tier ≥ town, continental morphology, era ≥ 1249 (Freiburg law) / 1288 (Bologna statute) / post-1405 (Bern); `SHOP_ROW_FRAME` — tier city, prosperity high, era ≥ 1491 English ceiling example; `BRIDGE_ROW` — city with a bridge, luxury tenancy only at era ≥ 1593 (before that the bridge row is butchers — a SITING INVERSION the engine can play); tallow `noxious` siting = edge/yard, relocatable under complaint; jeweller requires `STRONGROOM` at every prosperity.
- HOME tags: ROOM_KINDS.stall (window stall, cobbler), .workfloor (ouvroir), .counting (comptoir), .strongroom (goldsmith), .store/.cellar, .chamber/.lodging (solar); NO TYPED HOME for: arcade walk (a frontage, not a room), melting outhouse, drying loft, Kellerhals.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**
1. The frontage model (parent's recon: frontage.js four axes, no water-edge kind) has no COVERED-FRONTAGE axis; arcade/portico must be expressible as a public walk INSIDE the building line (depth 2–3 m) — this lands on the district/frontage pull, not on the interior template.
2. Cellar tenure separate from shop tenure (Kellerhals; Tackley's undercroft let as a tavern, parent) — the interior grammar assumes one occupier per plot; a `separatelyLet` flag on CELLAR/UNDERCROFT is needed.
3. `Chandler` L1191 must split into two archetypes or carry a `waxOrTallow` selector that flips siting (`noxious` edge vs luxury street).
4. `Jeweller` needs STRONGROOM as a required cell (it exists in ROOM_KINDS — the template just has to require it).
5. The luxury quarter is a ROW grammar (repeat N identical units under one frame) with a tenancy-control rule — the existing district pull handles adjacency, not repetition.
6. Live/work must be a per-entry dial, not a global default (Florentine rented bottega; Laube upper-floor stairs).

**(h) Continental vs English.** The English shop-house is parochial in its OPEN shuttered window under a pentice; the continental mechanism is the covered or arched front — the French arc-and-étal, the Bolognese portico (a public right of way enforced by statute from 1288), the Bernese Laube (a 2 m stone re-fronting after 1405 that created a 6 km covered street), with the cellar entered from the walk and the upper floors sometimes reached from it. The Italian bottega adds the separability of shop from dwelling (a palace-owned rental unit), and the Dutch merchant house replaces the shop with the voorhuis — a full-width trading hall with the counting room beside it and, by 1648, a corridor along the court. The Hanseatic Speicher/Kontor and the Dutch pakhuis are the outfitter's true parents, not the shop-house.

**(i) Fantasy note.** `Dungeon delving supply district` L2125: model it on the ship's chandler/outfitter — a quay-or-gate-side WAREHOUSE-SHOP (counter room, deep store bays, loft with hoist, yard), where the chandler also acts as the expedition's agent (the documented ship's-agent role). The "+1 weapons, silver weapons" line is a RETAIL sub-counter (the goldsmith's show-window mechanism: plate in the window, strongroom behind); keep the magical stock in a `STRONGROOM`, not the open store. Siting: at the edge facing the road out (the chandler sits where the ships are). The other eight entries are mundane: None.

**Parent-lane review note.** The sub-lane's `coveredFrontage` axis (arcade/portico/Laube) and the Kellerhals separately-let cellar are the two findings the parent carries into §Σ; its live/work caution (the rented Florentine bottega) is PLAUSIBLE only and is recorded as such in the ledger.
