## §4 · FAMILY B′ — apothecary: Apothecary (village L567) · Apothecary (established) (town L1134) · Apothecary district (city L1656)

*Researched by sub-lane BCQ (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

**(a) Analogue.**
The historical apothecary is a *shop-house with a fire room behind it*, and its interior program is remarkably stable from the 14th to the 18th century across the whole register. The German-language building literature gives the cleanest closed vocabulary, and it matches the English and Italian survivals room for room: the **Offizin** (the sales/dispensing room, with a central recipe table, the apothecary's scales and "Arzneischränke" — medicine cabinets with drawers below and glazed shelving above, arranged alphabetically by container size), behind or beside it a **Materialkammer** (bulk store), a **Laboratorium** (stove/furnace, distillation apparatus, mortars, cutting boards, sieves, tincture press), a **Kräuterboden** (herb-drying loft under the roof) and an **Arzneikeller** (medicine cellar) — CONFIRMED, Wien Geschichte Wiki "Apotheke", https://www.geschichtewiki.wien.gv.at/Apotheke, fetched 2026-08-23. The Schweinfurt Stadt-Apotheke (municipal, founded 1412, housed in/at the old town hall of 1570–72) is described as an Offizin with laboratory rooms, storerooms, a Kräuterboden and a dwelling of "three rooms and three chambers", staffed by a provisor, two journeymen, an apprentice and a maid — CONFIRMED, https://www.schweinfurtfuehrer.de/geschichte/600-jahre-stadt-apotheke/, fetched 2026-08-23. That is the town-tier program in one sentence: sales room, fire room, three stores at three heights, and the master's household over it.

Live/work is the rule, not the exception. The Tallinn Raeapteek has been "in business in the same house" since at least 1422 (on its third owner that year; some scholars propose 1415), was *owned by the city council* and leased until Johann Burchart IV bought it in 1688 for 600 thalers; a pillar on an upper floor carries the carved date 1663 and the Burchart arms — CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raeapteek, fetched 2026-08-23; the building reached its present size in the mid-18th century (CONFIRMED-digest, visitestonia/visittallinn). The English probate evidence is the same: James Preston, "Chirurgeon Barber" of Wrexham (d. 1681), "lived above his shop", with "another room over the shop" holding feather beds and linen, while the shop held six chairs, a case of razors and combs, "a douzen and a halfe of washboales", fifteen razors and scissors, looking glasses, pewter and brass basins and "instruments of music"; he is also owed money for over twenty cures (dressing a leg, a quinsy) — CONFIRMED, Withey (inventory NLW MS SA/1681/216), https://dralun.wordpress.com/2012/05/18/inside-a-seventeenth-century-welsh-barber-surgeons-shop/, curl-fetched 2026-08-23. This licenses the catalog's "some double as chirurgeons": the chirurgeon-apothecary's shop adds *chairs and basins* (a bleeding/shaving station) to the drawer wall, and the shop reads as semi-social (Pelling's argument, cited by Withey).

Fixtures by grade (floor → ceiling). Floor: a counter/recipe table, a mortar on a block, a hanging balance, a few shelves of pots and a locked box for poisons — the Nantwich apothecary Raphe Walley's whole stock inventoried at only £23 15s (CONFIRMED, Chemistry World, https://www.chemistryworld.com/news/apothecary-inventory-offers-glimpse-into-medicines-from-the-17th-century/4020974.article, fetched 2026-08-23). Middle: the full drawer-wall with glazed upper shelving and labelled jars (albarelli, Italian tin-glaze 15th–18th c. — CONFIRMED-digest), a separate back room, a furnace with alembic. Ceiling: the monastic-institutional form — the Officina of Santa Maria Novella, Florence, where the Dominicans (from 1221) prepared remedies; the Green Room (built 1335–37) housed "the pyramid-shaped distillation ovens, the stoves and the glassware" from 1542; the public pharmacy was voted in 1612 with access from the Great Cloister through Matteo Nigetti's portal; the "Antica Spezieria" was the sales room 1612–1848; the sacristy of the chapel of St Nicholas served from the 17th c. as the *aromataria* where distilled waters were kept; the deconsecrated chapel itself became the grand sales hall in 1848, with cellars for product storage and a herb garden near the piazza — CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Officina_Profumo-Farmaceutica_di_Santa_Maria_Novella, fetched 2026-08-23. The Dubrovnik Franciscan pharmacy (1317, the 1317 inventory survives in the library) originally sat *inside the cloister* and was moved in the early 19th century to the atrium "so that women could enter" — CONFIRMED for date/inventory (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franciscan_friary,_Dubrovnik, fetched 2026-08-23); CONFIRMED-digest for the location and move. Decline sheds in the reverse order: the still and furnace go cold first (fuel), the glazed cabinet decays to open shelving, the back room is let, and the trade retreats to the kitchen hearth.

Structural imperatives: **fire** (furnace/still in the laboratory; a masonry hearth and flue, and the Kräuterboden must be kept away from it), **water** (distillation, washing — a well or cistern nearby), **dry air and ventilation** for the drying loft, **cool and dark** for the cellar, **smell** (mild — spices, not tannery-grade), **light** for the Offizin (a glazed street front for display by the 17th c.). Siting law: a central street position next to barbers and grocers (the Bucklersbury pattern below); a monastery dispensary sits off the cloister beside the infirmary and herb garden (the Plan of St Gall places the physician's house next to the bloodletting house, the herb garden, the infirmary and the kitchens, with the physician controlling the medical cabinet — CONFIRMED-digest). The spicer/grocer overlap is constitutive in England: the Pepperers (1180) and Spicers (joined by 1316) became the Grocers' Company (1428); the word comes from *apotheca*, "a place where wine, spices and herbs were stored"; the spicer-apothecaries kept their shops in Bucklersbury selling spices, confectionery, perfumes and compounded drugs; the Society of Apothecaries separated by charter on 6 December 1617 — CONFIRMED, https://www.apothecaries.org/heritage/history/origins/, fetched 2026-08-23; Stow's late-16th-c. Bucklersbury "on both the sides throughout possessed of Grocers and Apothecaries" — CONFIRMED-digest.

Tier distinctions. *Village L567*: the shop is at most the front room of a house, and more often is not a shop at all (see c). *Town L1134*: the full Offizin/back-room/laboratory/three-store/dwelling program, with the chirurgeon's chairs and basins optional. *City L1656*: the "district" is a street of such shop-houses (Bucklersbury) plus up to two anchors — a monastic dispensary off a cloister (SMN, Dubrovnik) and a company hall with its own manufacturing laboratory. Apothecaries' Hall, Blackfriars: the Society bought the former Dominican priory guesthouse (Cobham House) in 1632, lost it in 1666, rebuilt by 1672 to Edward Jerman's design with an "Elaboratory" for the first large-scale manufacture of drugs (manufacture continued to 1922), and ran a retail shop on Water Lane (now Blackfriars Lane); a 9 m-high stretch of 13th-c. priory wall survives in the north range; Great Hall, Court Room and Parlour are on the first floor of the east wing — CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apothecaries'_Hall,_London and apothecaries.org, fetched 2026-08-23; the listing describes a courtyard building with 17th-c. north and east ranges of two storeys, late-18th-c. south and west ranges of three storeys, the hall on the first floor of the east side with an *external stair in the south-east corner* — CONFIRMED-digest (britishlistedbuildings 101359133; Historic England 1002055 returned a Cloudflare challenge to both WebFetch and curl). The laboratory "on ground level along the eastern side of the courtyard, underneath the Great Hall" is digest-only and possibly describes the 1780s arrangement.

**(b) Measured.**
- Knaresborough "Oldest Chemist Shop": 17th-c. building, *two storeys with attics*, timber-framed core encased in brick, pantile roof, *two gabled bays*; apothecary from 1720, 18th-c. shop fittings, 17th-c. panelling and stairs; pharmacy until 1998 — CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oldest_Chemist_Shop_in_England, fetched 2026-08-23.
- Schweinfurt Stadt-Apotheke dwelling: three rooms + three chambers; staff of five — CONFIRMED (above). Founding capital 5,000 gulden (1412).
- Apothecaries' Hall: two-storey 17th-c. ranges, three-storey 18th-c. ranges; 9 m of medieval wall — CONFIRMED / CONFIRMED-digest (above).
- SMN Green Room built 1335–37; distillation ovens there from 1542 — CONFIRMED.
- Preston's shop, Wrexham 1681: 6 chairs, 18 wash-bowls, 15 razors/scissors — CONFIRMED (counts are a proxy for the number of simultaneous customers, roughly 4–6).
- Walley, Nantwich: stock value £23 15s — CONFIRMED.
- Raeapteek: purchase price 600 thalers (1688); carved date 1663 — CONFIRMED.
- No measured figures found for: Offizin room dimensions, counter length, drawer counts, furnace/still size, drying-loft area, herb-garden area, Dubrovnik room sizes, Tallinn room sizes (searched: Dubrovnik "interior rooms laboratory measured"; Tallinn "building interior rooms plan"; "apothecary probate inventory rooms shop still house warehouse"; Apothecaries' Hall "courtyard plan Survey of London"). PLAUSIBLE ranges for the grammar: Offizin 15–35 m²; laboratory 10–25 m²; town shop frontage one to two bays (4–8 m); a Kräuterboden is the whole roof void.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**
- *The village "apothecary" is usually not a shop.* The negation search ("village herbalist wise woman no shop … monastic infirmary dispensary") returned only the cunning-folk literature: herbal healers were "simply householders whose knowledge had travelled through generations", midwives and cunning men/women who dispensed from their own houses, with no shop and no drawer-wall — CONFIRMED-digest (UAB research guide; Wikipedia "Cunning folk"). The monastic infirmary's dispensary is a *cabinet under the physician's control* inside the infirmary complex, not a room with a street door (St Gall, CONFIRMED-digest). Both mean the village entry at baseChance 0.45 is a house with a "physic" room and a garden, not the town program shrunk.
- *The apothecary was often a grocer first.* Until 1617 the London apothecary was a species of grocer; the same shop sold spices, sugar, wax and drugs; "apothecary" is a stock-in-trade, not a building type (CONFIRMED, apothecaries.org).
- *Survival dates are late.* Tallinn's present building is mid-18th c.; Knaresborough's fittings are 18th c.; the SMN sales hall is an 1848 conversion of a chapel; Dubrovnik's pharmacy room is a 19th-c. relocation. Every "medieval pharmacy interior" a visitor sees is early modern or later; the *program* is medieval, the *fabric* is not.
- *Apothecary vs. alchemist.* The catalog's own line ("Distinct from the alchemist. No magical compounds") holds historically in the shop, not in the laboratory: the apothecary's still, furnace and glassware are physically the alchemist's kit (SMN's "pyramid-shaped distillation ovens"). The distinction is in what comes out, not in the room.
- Null results: no excavated apothecary shop plan found; no Survey of London plan fetched for Apothecaries' Hall.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**
- *Apothecary (village L567)*: `THROUGH_ROOM` only — street door into the physic/front room, rear door to kitchen and garden; stair type **ladder → winder** to a loft. No passage. Era ANY, prosperity low–mid.
- *Apothecary (established) (town L1134)*: `THROUGH_ROOM` enfilade shop → back room (consultation) → laboratory, the back room acting as the hinge; a `CROSS_PASSAGE` is licensed when the shop occupies the front of a hall-house or when a side entry serves the dwelling and yard independently of the shop (the town shop-house norm; PLAUSIBLE). Stair **winder** (Knaresborough's 17th-c. stair, CONFIRMED the stair exists, type inferred) rising from the back room or passage to the dwelling; the Kräuterboden is reached by a **ladder** from the upper floor. Widths: no primary; PLAUSIBLE passage 1.0–1.5 m.
- *Apothecary district (city L1656)*: the street itself is the circulation (Bucklersbury); each shop repeats the town unit. Anchors add `GALLERY` (the cloister walk from which the SMN and Dubrovnik pharmacies are entered; SMN's late-18th-c. gallery to the sales hall — CONFIRMED) and, for the hall, a courtyard with an external `STAIR_HALL` to the first-floor Great Hall and a `LOBBY` at the street archway (CONFIRMED-digest). Stair type **spiral/newel (external)** for the hall; grand stair only post-1780.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**
- Village: `CLOSET` (locked poison box — fixture of the front room), `ATTIC/GARRET_STORE` for drying bunches (the loft), the kitchen hearth as the laboratory. No cellar required.
- Town: `STORE` (Materialkammer, lightReq NONE, small–medium, adjacent to the Offizin and laboratory), `STILL_ROOM` (the laboratory *is* the still room: hearth + furnace + alembic + water; lightReq ANY; adjacency: rear, with a flue; never beneath the loft stair), `CELLAR` (Arzneikeller, NONE, cool; syrups, waters, oils), `ATTIC/GARRET_STORE` (Kräuterboden, dry and ventilated, ANY light, no hearth beneath it), `CLOSET` (poison cupboard; a fixture). Fixtures-vs-cells: the drawer wall, jar shelving, scales and counter are fixtures of the Offizin; anything under ~6 m² (the poison aumbry, the spice chest) is a fixture, not a cell.
- City district: as town per shop, plus the institutional `STORE` as an *aromataria* (the SMN sacristy for distilled waters, CONFIRMED), `CELLAR` stores, and the hall's `STORE`/warehouse feeding a street shop (the Water Lane shop, CONFIRMED).

**(f) Typed proposal.**
- `parti`: `APOTHECARY_HOUSE_ROOM` (village) · `APOTHECARY_SHOPHOUSE` (town) · `APOTHECARY_STREET_CLUSTER` (city; N × shophouse + 0–2 anchors: `MONASTIC_DISPENSARY_OFF_CLOISTER`, `COMPANY_HALL_WITH_ELABORATORY`).
- `functions[]`: floor = `dispense_simple`, `consult`; rungs = `compound`, `distil`, `dry_herbs`, `dress_wounds/bleed` (chirurgeon variant), `retail_spice` (grocer overlap); ceiling = `manufacture_bulk` (hall elaboratory), `dispense_to_infirmary`.
- `fixtures[]` with grades: `counter` (all), `scales` (all; NO TYPED HOME — fold into counter), `mortar_block` (all; HOME: workbench), `drawer_wall` (mid+; HOME: rack), `jar_shelving_albarelli` (mid+; HOME: rack), `poison_aumbry` (all; HOME: strongbox, locked), `furnace_and_still` (mid+; hearth + NEW fixture `STILL` — NO TYPED HOME), `tincture_press` (high; NO TYPED HOME), `barber_chairs_and_basins` (chirurgeon variant; NO TYPED HOME), `herb_garden_plot` (exterior; NO TYPED HOME).
- Structural buckets: `fireTrade` (laboratory flue), `wetTrade` (minor: distillation, washing), `noxious` = false (spice-grade smell only), `dryLoft` (new: needs a ventilated roof void without a hearth beneath).
- Licensing: village × any prosperity × any era × siting "house on the street or green, garden behind"; town × mid+ × 1300+ (pharmacies documented from the early 14th c. in Vienna, 1412 Schweinfurt, 1422 Tallinn) × central street, barber/grocer adjacency; city district × high × 1500+ for the company hall (1617/1672 in London), 1300+ for the monastic anchor × a named street.
- HOME tags: Offizin → `ROOM_KINDS.stall` (front sales) or `main`; back/consultation → `ROOM_KINDS.back`; laboratory → `ROOM_KINDS.workfloor` + `hearth`; Materialkammer → `store`; Arzneikeller → `cellar`; Kräuterboden → `store` (attic); dwelling → `lodging`/`chamber`; company hall → `hall`, `records`; sacristy-aromataria → `store`. NO TYPED HOME: still/alembic, drawer-wall as a *wall* (rack approximates), herb garden, barber station.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**
1. A *district* entry must compose as a street cluster of repeated shop-house units plus optional anchors, not as a single footprint; this lands on whatever "cluster/row" pull the grammar has for market rows (the Chester Row / Bucklersbury case), and if none exists it is the first new capability this family demands.
2. The apothecary needs a **three-height storage law** (cellar cool / back-store / loft dry) plus a **fire room that is also a still room**; §453 has the classes but the engine must be able to place a `STILL_ROOM` with a flue at the rear and forbid a hearth under the `ATTIC` store.
3. A **hosted-institution relation**: Schweinfurt's and Tallinn's pharmacies were council property inside or beside the town hall; the grammar needs "entry occupies rooms of host building X" (see Family Q, where it is the default), which no current flag expresses.
4. The chirurgeon variant needs a fixture (chairs + basins) flag on the same room, not a second room.

**(h) Continental vs English.** The English register is parochial in two ways: the apothecary is a grocer until 1617, so the shop is a spice shop with a drug corner, and the surviving English fabric is 17th–18th c. The continental mechanism is older and more institutional: the Italian *spezieria* attached to a convent and entered from the cloister (SMN 1612, Dubrovnik 1317), the German *Ratsapotheke* as a municipal monopoly with a provisor living in the house (Schweinfurt 1412; Tallinn leased by the council until 1688), with the Offizin/Materialkammer/Laboratorium/Kräuterboden/Keller program as a fixed room list; the Italian shop adds the tin-glazed albarello wall as the display grammar. Where an English town has one apothecary-grocer, a German Reichsstadt has a council-licensed pharmacy with a privilege and a pharmacopoeia.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None (the catalog explicitly excludes magical compounds).

## §5 · FAMILY C — maps, paper & print: Cartographer's workshop (village L738; town L1008) · Cartographer's guild (city L1649) · Printing house (city L1731)

*Researched by sub-lane BCQ (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

**(a) Analogue.**
Three different buildings hide under these four entries, and one of them did not exist. Say it plainly: **there is no free-standing medieval "cartographer's workshop"**. Before c. 1500 a map is made by a scribe or illuminator at a writing desk — in a monastic scriptorium, in a stationer's shop, or in a portolan *atelier* that is a house in a port city. The only commercial map workshops of the period are the portolan-chart ateliers concentrated in Genoa, Venice and Majorca from the last third of the 13th c. to the end of the 15th, producing thousands of charts exported as far as Flanders and Alexandria; their production line is described as one hand lining the vellum, a second tracing the template, a third writing, then decoration — CONFIRMED-digest (Archivo de la Corona de Aragón; maphistory.info). Some scholars argue the form emerged from monastic establishments before ateliers formed (CONFIRMED-digest). Institutionally, the map-maker's room is a **chartroom inside a trading/state house**: the Casa de Contratación (Seville, 1503), housed first in the Royal Shipyards and soon in the Alcázar, employed a chief pilot (1508), a cosmographer and chart-making master (1523) and a professor of cosmography (1552), and kept the *Padrón Real*, the secret master chart, probably hung as a large wall chart in the old Alcázar — CONFIRMED-digest (Wikipedia "Padrón Real"; MUCAIN). So the "cartographer" in this register is either (i) a scrivener/illuminator's desk in a shop-house, (ii) a port-city atelier, or (iii) a chartroom inside someone else's building.

The scrivener/stationer's shop, which is the real village-and-town analogue, is a desk trade: the book streets around St Paul's (Paternoster Row, Amen Corner, Ave Maria Lane, Creed Lane) and the Rue Neuve Notre-Dame in Paris were lined with stationers who *hired* the scribes, illuminators and binders living nearby rather than housing them — CONFIRMED-digest (Toni Mount; medievalbooks.nl). The fixed structural imperative is **light**: the scriptorium "shall have large windows" (CONFIRMED-digest, Rawlings via Gutenberg.org); north light is the illuminator's preference in the later workshop literature (PLAUSIBLE — I found no primary stating "north" for a medieval scriptorium). Fixtures: a sloped writing desk with a book rest, a pumice/knife/pen tray, a parchment press or chest, ink horns, a drying line; parchment and pumice are stored dry in a chest or a small store (PLAUSIBLE). Live/work is total — the desk is in the house's best-lit room.

The printing house is the measured building. The Plantin-Moretus complex on the Vrijdagmarkt (the family moved into "De Gulden Passer" in 1576; CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantin%E2%80%93Moretus_Museum, fetched 2026-08-23) is a **courtyard plan of wings built for the trade**: the south wing, nine bays and two storeys, was built in 1579 *for the printing works on its ground floor* and heightened in 1637–40; the west wing, fourteen bays and two storeys (1579 southern section, 1620–22 and 1637–40 northern section, with a construction seam between the 7th and 8th bays), contains the proofreaders' chamber; the north wing, five bays and three storeys (1620–22), carries the type foundry on its upper floor over five blue-limestone arcades at ground; the east wing (1620–22, twelve bays, two storeys) and the ten-bay, two-and-a-half-storey Louis XV entrance building on the Vrijdagmarkt (1761–63, one room deep) close the court; the property zone is 2,172 m²; seven late-16th-c. stepped-gable houses on the Heilige Geeststraat (the "Coperen/IJzeren/Houten/Zilveren Passer") belong to the complex and carried the street shop — CONFIRMED, Inventaris Onroerend Erfgoed 4688, https://inventaris.onroerenderfgoed.be/erfgoedobjecten/4688, fetched 2026-08-23. Around 1575 there were "certainly 16 presses"; seven stand in the room today (five working), the two oldest c. 1600; a press printed up to 1,250 sheets both sides a day in 14-hour shifts; the workforce exceeded 50 — CONFIRMED, https://museumplantinmoretus.be/nl/pagina/de-twee-oudste-drukpersen-ter-wereld and /en/seeing-doing/museum/printing-workshop, fetched 2026-08-23. A secondary digest breaks the 56 men into 32 printers, 20 compositors, 3 proof-readers and one labourer (CONFIRMED-digest) — which, against 16 presses, gives the two-man press crew (puller and beater) as the staffing unit (PLAUSIBLE-derived). The room program is therefore: **press hall** (ground, long, lit along one wall, presses in a row), **composing** (type cases beside or in the press hall), **foundry** (fire; upstairs at Plantin, which is unusual and only possible because the melt is small), **proofreaders' room** (quiet, lit, off the press hall), **paper store** and **drying loft** (sheets hung on cords/poles; PLAUSIBLE location in the roof), **counting house and archive** (the business archive from 1555 survives — CONFIRMED-digest), the **shop on the street**, and the **dwelling** around the court. Prosperity grades: floor = Caxton, who from 1476 *rented a shop* at the sign of the Red Pale in Westminster and paid the Prior rent "for tenements and a loft over the gate to the Almonry" — a press in rented rooms, ~100 books 1473–92 — CONFIRMED, https://www.westminster-abbey.org/abbey-commemorations/commemorations/william-caxton, fetched 2026-08-23; 10s rent for the shop in 1476 per the sacrist's rolls (CONFIRMED-digest). Middle = Gutenberg and Fust in the rented Humbrechthof c. 1450 (Schöffer's from 1470/71, a printing house from 1481; only the 1584 stair tower survives, site now Schusterstrasse 22/24) — CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humbrechthof, fetched 2026-08-23; Aldus in Campo Sant'Agostin, early 1490s–1506 (CONFIRMED-digest). Ceiling = Koberger in Nuremberg (first press 1470): 24 presses, 100 workers, two paper mills and agents across Europe — CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anton_Koberger, fetched 2026-08-23; and Plantin. What decline sheds first: presses are sold (a press is the capital), then the foundry goes cold, then the shop closes and the printer works to order in one room.

The paper mill is a **water-power building with a louvred loft**: at Amalfi the Nicola Milano mill (mid-13th c.) keeps a 14th-c. vat lined with majolica tiles, a 16th-c. stamper, a Hollander dated 1745 scratched in the plaster, water in underground canals, and a drying loft of drilled poles threaded with wire; the Cartiera Amatruda's lower storey is original with stone vats of c. 1400 and a *later* drying loft above; the Cavaliere mill's drying loft is "in a tall building across the street" — CONFIRMED, https://www.handpapermaking.org/magazine-articles/living-museums-of-papermaking-in-italy-part-i, fetched 2026-08-23. Basel's Gallician mill (a Klingental grain mill to 1428, then a hammer forge, converted to paper in 1453 on the 13th-c. St Alban-Teich canal) was rebuilt in 1788 as a two-storey building with a *three-storey roof structure* — the drying lofts — CONFIRMED, https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basler_Papierm%C3%BChle, fetched 2026-08-23. Fabriano's 1280s innovations (hydraulic stampers, gelatine sizing, watermark) are CONFIRMED-digest.

Tier distinctions. *Village L738*: a scrivener's desk in a house; sells copies of road itineraries and surveys; no workshop. *Town L1008*: a stationer's shop-house with a lit workroom and a *reference collection* of older maps (the catalog's own line) — the atelier/shop form. *City guild L1649*: a portolan-style atelier cluster or a chartroom in a trading house; "guild" is a licence to make the institutional anchor a *hall with a chartroom*. *Printing house L1731*: the courtyard works; era-gated post-1450.

**(b) Measured.**
- Plantin-Moretus: south wing 9 bays × 2 storeys (1579; heightened 1637–40), printing works on its ground floor; west wing 14 bays × 2 storeys with proofreaders' chamber; north wing 5 bays × 3 storeys, foundry upstairs, 5 limestone arches at ground; east wing 12 bays × 2 storeys; entrance house 10 bays × 2.5 storeys, one room deep; site 2,172 m²; 16 presses c. 1575; 7 presses today; 1,250 sheets/day/press; 14-hour shift; >50 staff (56 = 32+20+3+1, digest) — CONFIRMED as labelled above.
- English common press c. 1750 (Smithsonian): height 78 in (1.98 m), width at cheeks 30.5 in (0.77 m), length 57 in (1.45 m), platen 12 × 18.5 in — CONFIRMED-digest (the SI page is JS-rendered; curl returned a shell). Steve Pratt's full-size Gutenberg-era replica (1999): 8 ft H × 5 ft W × 7 ft 3.5 in D — CONFIRMED-digest. Moxon (1683) sets the cheek gap "some four inches wider than" the carriage — CONFIRMED-digest. Working footprint per press with pull-room and paper bank: PLAUSIBLE 6–9 m².
- Koberger: 24 presses, 100 workers (1470s–1500s) — CONFIRMED. Venice: >200 establishments and >400 presses by the late 15th c. (aggregate) — CONFIRMED-digest.
- Caxton: shop rent 10s (1476) — CONFIRMED-digest; shop + tenements + loft over the Almonry gate — CONFIRMED.
- Humbrechthof: 1584 stair tower is the only survival — CONFIRMED.
- Paper mills: 14th-c. tiled vat, 16th-c. stamper, 1745 Hollander (Amalfi); stone vats c. 1400 (Amatruda); Basel 1453 conversion, 1788 rebuild 2 storeys + 3-storey roof — CONFIRMED. No vat counts per mill and no loft dimensions found.
- Casa de Contratación posts: 1508, 1523, 1552 — CONFIRMED-digest.
- No measured figures found for: any medieval scrivener's or illuminator's shop (searched "scrivener stationer shop London Paternoster Row scriptorium desk north light illuminator workshop"); portolan atelier rooms; Plantin room dimensions in metres (searched in English and Dutch; the inventaris gives bays, not metres — PLAUSIBLE bay pitch 3.5–4.5 m gives a press hall of roughly 30–40 m × 6–8 m). Gutenberg's press count: not stated anywhere fetched.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**
- *No medieval cartographer's workshop* — the negation search confirmed it by returning only scriptoria, stationers and portolan ateliers. The village entry (0.15) and even the town entry are, strictly, scriveners; keep the catalog's names but let the grammar instantiate a stationer's shop-house.
- *The atelier is real but is a house.* Majorcan and Genoese chart-makers worked in ordinary town houses; the "workshop" is a lit room and a vellum store, not a building type (CONFIRMED-digest).
- *Map-making as a state chartroom* — the Casa de Contratación's cosmographers sat in the Alcázar; the Padrón Real hung on a wall. This is the correct "guild" analogue for a city with a crown or trading company: a chartroom inside a *hall*, not a guild-hall of its own.
- *Era*: printing is post-1450; the Printing house entry at city tier is a late-era licence, and the catalog says so ("if technology exists"). The press "house" before Plantin is rented domestic space (Caxton, Gutenberg, Aldus — all in someone else's building); the purpose-built printing wing is a 1579 Antwerp event.
- *Reconstruction caveat*: no original Gutenberg press survives; every replica is inferred from later presses and period woodcuts (CONFIRMED-digest).
- *Paper mill siting is outside the settlement* (canal, fall of water, rag supply) — a printing house in a city has no mill; Koberger *owned* two mills elsewhere.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**
- *Cartographer's workshop (village L738)*: `THROUGH_ROOM`; the desk in the front or upper room; **ladder/winder**. No passage.
- *Cartographer's workshop (town L1008)*: `THROUGH_ROOM` shop → workroom (lit) → store; a `CROSS_PASSAGE` where the house is a hall-house with the shop in the front bay; the reference collection in a locked upper chamber (records). Stair **winder**.
- *Cartographer's guild (city L1649)*: institutional: a `STAIR_HALL` and `GALLERY` (the arcaded loggia of a trading house; the Casa's rooms in the Alcázar are reached through courtyards — PLAUSIBLE) with the chartroom on an upper floor for light and security; era 1500+ at high prosperity; at mid prosperity it is an atelier row on a harbour street (portolan cluster), `THROUGH_ROOM` per house.
- *Printing house (city L1731)*: the Plantin plan is `COURTYARD`-mediated — wings communicate via the court and the north wing's five-arch **arcade = `GALLERY`** (CONFIRMED: five semicircular limestone arcades at ground), the press hall being a `THROUGH_ROOM` run of nine bays; the street shop is reached from the street, separately from the works; stairs **winder/newel** in the wings (the Humbrechthof's 1584 *stair tower* is the continental marker for a Hof with a newel stair — CONFIRMED it survives). Caxton-grade: one rented shop + a loft over a gate, **ladder** to the loft (CONFIRMED the loft existed). Widths/lengths: bays only (above).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**
- Village/town scrivener: `CLOSET` or chest for parchment, pumice, pens (fixture: `parchment_chest`); `STORE` small, lightReq NONE, *dry* (parchment cockles), adjacent to the workroom; the reference map collection as `records` (locked chamber or press).
- Guild/chartroom: `STORE` for vellum and instruments; `records` strongly — the master chart is a *secret* document kept under lock (Padrón Real); a `CLOSET`-grade map press is a fixture of the chartroom.
- Printing house: `STORE` for paper in reams (lightReq NONE, dry, **heavyFloor** — reams are dense; ground floor or vaulted), `ATTIC/GARRET_STORE` as the drying loft (ventilated, lines/poles; at Plantin location is PLAUSIBLE in the heightened south/west wings), `CELLAR` for oil, lampblack and ink-making (noxious smoke belongs in a yard shed), type in cases = fixture (`type_cases`), the foundry's metal store = fixture; the business `records` (archive from 1555) as a counting-house cell. Paper-mill (if ever instantiated): `STORE` rag store (wet, smelly — `noxious` bucket), vat room, and a louvred `ATTIC` drying loft that can be a separate tall building (Cavaliere, CONFIRMED).

**(f) Typed proposal.**
- `parti`: `SCRIVENER_DESK_ROOM` (village) · `STATIONER_SHOPHOUSE_WITH_MAPROOM` (town) · `CHARTROOM_IN_TRADING_HALL` and `PORTOLAN_ATELIER_ROW` (city guild, alternative partis by siting: inland/institutional vs harbour) · `PRINTING_COURT` (city printing house; wings around a court) with a floor-grade alternate `PRESS_IN_RENTED_SHOP`.
- `functions[]`: floor = `copy_and_draw`, `sell_maps`; rungs = `survey_commission`, `reference_collection`, `engrave_plates` (post-1500), `compose_and_press`, `cast_type`, `proofread`, `dry_sheets`, `retail_books`; ceiling = `master_chart_keep` (state), `multi_press_manufactory` (16–24 presses).
- `fixtures[]`: `writing_desk_sloped` (all; HOME: workbench), `map_press/chest` (mid; NO TYPED HOME — chest ≈ crate), `wall_chart` (ceiling; NO TYPED HOME), `common_press` (press house; NO TYPED HOME — 0.77 × 1.45 × 1.98 m, braced to the ceiling beam, PLAUSIBLE bracing), `type_cases` (HOME: rack), `imposing_stone` (HOME: workbench), `casting_furnace` (HOME: hearth / ROOM_KINDS.kiln), `drying_lines` (HOME: rack), `ream_stacks` (HOME: crate), `ledger` (counting house).
- Structural buckets: `heavyFloor` (presses, type, reams), `fireTrade` (foundry only), `lightCritical` (new: long lit wall, north preferred), `dryLoft` (ventilated roof void); paper mill adds `waterPower`, `wetTrade`, `noxious` (rag rot).
- Licensing: village × any × any era × house; town × mid+ × any era × market street; city guild × high × 1300+ for harbour ateliers, 1500+ for the state chartroom × port or capital; printing house × mid+ × **1450+ (hard)** × city; purpose-built court form 1575+ and high prosperity only; rented-shop form 1450+ any.
- HOME: press hall/composing → `ROOM_KINDS.workfloor`; foundry → `kiln`; paper store → `store`; drying loft → `store`; shop → `stall`; proofreaders' room → NO TYPED HOME (nearest `chamber`); counting house/archive → `counting`, `records`; chartroom → `records` + `workfloor`; dwelling → `lodging`/`chamber`.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**
1. **Era gating with a fabric split**: the same entry must instantiate as "press in rented rooms" (1450–1575) or "printing court" (1575+, high prosperity); the grammar needs an era × prosperity switch on the parti, not just on the entry's existence.
2. **Press count as the size driver**: a printing house scales by presses (1 → 16 → 24) with ~2 men and ~6–9 m² per press; no other trade in the dossier scales so linearly, so the grammar wants a `unitCount` field that drives bay count.
3. **Light as a hard constraint** on the workroom/chartroom (long window wall, upper floor preferred) — the current vocabulary has lightReq on storage but, as far as the brief states, not on work rooms.
4. **Hosted chartroom**: the guild entry's best analogue is rooms *inside* a trading hall or palace — the same hosted relation Family Q needs.
5. The scrivener entries are renames, not new rooms: the grammar should treat "cartographer" as a tag on a stationer's shop-house so the village/town forms do not invent a workshop that never existed.

**(h) Continental vs English.** The English register is parochial on both ends: the English book trade is a stationers' street (Paternoster Row) of shops that hire out work, and England's first press sat in rented Abbey property. The continental mechanisms are the Mediterranean *atelier* (Majorca, Genoa, Venice) for charts; the German *Hof* (Humbrechthof) as a rented aristocratic courtyard house taken over by a trade; the Italian *bottega* in a campo (Aldus); and the Flemish purpose-built *court of wings* (Plantin, 1579) whose street shop, press hall, foundry, proofreaders' room and dwelling are distinct wings around a garden — the nearest thing to an industrial plan before 1700. The paper mill is everywhere a canal-side mill outside the walls (Fabriano, Amalfi, Basel's St Alban-Tal).

**(i) Fantasy note.** None for the cartographers; the catalog's "dungeon sketching" is a commission type, not a room.

**Parent-lane review note.** The parent endorses the sub-lane's plainest finding: there is no free-standing medieval cartographer's workshop; the village and town entries compile to a stationer's shop-house with a `cartographer` tag (§Σ).

---

## §6 · FAMILY D — wood & fibre workshops: Carpenter (part-time) (hamlet L164) · Carpenter (village L546) · Carpenters (5-15) (town L1098) · Cooper (village L560) · Woodcarver (village L731) · Thatcher (village L553) · Bowyer & fletcher (village L574) · Bowyers & fletchers (guild) (town L1141) · Ropemaker (town L1219)

*Researched by sub-lane DF (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

**(a) Analogue.**

*Carpenter (all three tiers).* The medieval carpenter's "workshop" is not a building where buildings are made; it is a **yard** ("timber-haw" / framing place) plus a **tool store and bench**. Framing happened flat on the ground in the open, members jointed, test-assembled, scribed with Roman-numeral carpenters' marks, dismantled and carted to the erection site. **CONFIRMED** (D. S. Leach, *Carpenters in Medieval London c.1240–c.1540*, Royal Holloway PhD 2017, pp. 175–177, https://pure.royalholloway.ac.uk/ws/files/27817538/2017leachdsphd.pdf, fetched 2026-08-23): in 1410 John Wolfey of St Giles without Cripplegate bequeathed a "tymberhawe" containing his timber, nails and carpentry; the Mercers paid for "making of a pytte for sawyers in ye Tymber Hawe in Colmanstrete"; in 1487 the Carpenters' Company rented its own "timber hawe in Lime Street" to members; the Westminster Hall roof (1394–1401) was framed at a yard called "The Frame" at Farnham, Surrey, and carted to the Thames in 1395; the inventory of Simon Byrlyngham (d. c.1499) lists timber at his Bishopsgate Street house worth £27 13s 6d plus stores at Barnet, Deptford and Walthamstow. The thesis states the timber-haw sat in "a less congested area outside the walls". So the program is: (1) OPEN FRAMING GROUND (the dominant area, unroofed, level, drained), (2) TIMBER STORE (racked in the open or under a lean-to; seasoning by years), (3) SAWPIT (a fixture of the yard; in a craftsman's yard it is permanent, stone/brick-lined, with niches and footholds, "usually under cover, often beneath a purpose-built open-sided shed or pent-house" with "two or three sides of the building ... left open" — **CONFIRMED**, L. Maskill, *The Farnham Park Sawpit*, 2011 monograph PDF, pp. 5–6, 20, http://oxfordshirewoodlandgroup.co.uk/images/Pits/Sawpits/L%20Maskill%20-%20Sawpits%20monograph.pdf, fetched 2026-08-23), (4) the SHOP proper, a single bench room with a chest/rack of tools and a glazed wall toward the light. The surviving open-air-museum example — the Carpenters' Shop from Windlesham at the Weald & Downland Living Museum — is a **late 19th/early 20th-century survival** (dismantled 1978, rebuilt 1980): posts dug into the ground, vertical boarding, tarred, glazed end to the street "to provide adequate light for the workbenches", a shuttered gable aperture through which ladders were pushed for storage across the tie beams; benches and tool racks in situ (**CONFIRMED** https://www.wealddown.co.uk/buildings/carpenters-shop/, fetched 2026-08-23; no dimensions published). Structural imperatives: level ground and cart access (weight), dry racks (seasoning), daylight at the bench, shavings = fire load but no hearth in the shop (glue heated on the house hearth). Live/work: the medieval carpenter sleeps in his own house; the yard may be at the plot tail or elsewhere in the town (Byrlyngham's timber was at several addresses). Prosperity/wear: floor = a bench in the house's outshut and an open plot; ceiling = walled yard with permanent roofed sawpit, timber racks, crane/tripod, separate shop with glazing; decline sheds the sawpit roof first, then the rack stock (sold off), leaving bench and chest. Tier distinctions: hamlet part-time = NO yard, bench in the cross-passage/outshut of his own house, sawing on trestles; village = house + yard with an unlined or timber-shored pit under a pent-house; town 5–15 = the count is households on burgage plots, each with a bench and some with a timber-haw on the margins, plus hired company space (Lime Street) — not a guild building.

*Cooper.* A bench-and-block trade with one fire fixture: staves are dressed on the shaving horse/block, jointed on a long upturned jointer plane, raised inside a truss hoop, and the cask is bent over a **cresset** (an iron basket fire lit inside the cask) — **CONFIRMED-digest** (search digest quoting colonialsense.com / Colonial Williamsburg RR0316; the CW report itself returned 403 to both WebFetch and curl). Coopers in London organised from 1298; in the 16th century the Company "won the right for coopers to be independent from breweries" (**CONFIRMED-digest**, Wikipedia *Cooper (profession)* via search digest) — i.e., the medieval default for a large share of coopers was a tied workplace in the brewer's or vintner's yard, not an independent shop. Program: one shop room (block, horse, jointer, hoop rack, cresset hearth on a flag/earth patch), a stave store outdoors or in a lean-to (staves season one to three years, **PLAUSIBLE**), a yard for finished casks. Fire: a contained small fire on an earth floor, chimney-less, needing a high or open roof; smell is wood-smoke only. Live/work: master over or beside the shop in the village; tied coopers lodge at the brewhouse.

*Woodcarver / image-maker.* The continental Bildschnitzer worked in a town house. Riemenschneider's Hof zum Wolfmannszichlein (Franziskanergasse 1, Würzburg), which his wife brought to the marriage, was a **three-winged courtyard house** (dreiflügeliger Hof) with a 13th-century vaulted cellar; the local tradition labels the cellar "the former Riemenschneider workshop" — he was admitted to the Lukasbruderschaft (painters, sculptors, glaziers) on 7 Dec 1483 and became master/citizen in 1485; inner wings destroyed 16 Mar 1945, only the ruin and vaulted cellar survive (**CONFIRMED** https://wuerzburgwiki.de/wiki/Hof_zum_Wolfmannszichlein, fetched 2026-08-23). The cellar-as-workshop attribution is a hospitality-era label, not an excavated fact; a carving shop wants north or even light and a dry floor, so the realistic program is a ground-floor shop on the courtyard with cellar store (**PLAUSIBLE**). English analogue: the Nottingham "alabastermen" (Nicholas Hill selling fifty-eight "heads of St John" in 1491 — **PLAUSIBLE**, training knowledge) worked in ordinary town houses; no dedicated building form is known. Program for the village entry: one bench room with a window wall, a chest of gouges, a glue pot on the house hearth, a store for blanks (lime, oak, walnut), and for the "sacred images" product a painting/gilding table that is a FIXTURE not a room.

*Thatcher.* **NO WORKSHOP.** Thatching is performed entirely on the roof; the thatcher's premises are a yard with a reed or straw rick (or the client's straw), a store for spars/liggers (split hazel, cut green in winter), leggetts and needles kept in the house. Stated plainly: the catalog entry should license a YARD + RICK fixture on the thatcher's own house plot, not a building. Negation search (see (c)) returned nothing contradicting this.

*Bowyer & fletcher.* Two bench trades that London separated in 1371 by petition, "no man of one trade should do the other" under penalty of £4; first Fletchers' ordinance 16 June 1403; by 1423 fletchers were forbidden "from opening shops on Sundays and High Feast Days" (so they had street-opening shops); by the 16th century the Company had "a hall in St Mary Axe" (**CONFIRMED** https://www.fletchers.org.uk/index.php/history/item/47-fletchershistory-cat, fetched 2026-08-23). The bowyer's program: a stave store (imported yew staves seasoning in racks, two or more years — the 1472 statute tying bowstaves to wine imports is **PLAUSIBLE**, training knowledge), a bench with tiller and drawknife/float, a horn/nock and string corner, and a tillering wall; the fletcher's: shafts racked, a small glue hearth or pot, feather store, a heading bench, and sheaf store (arrows sold by the sheaf of 24). Both sell from a shop window. The Mary Rose (1545) yielded roughly 137 complete longbows and some 3,500 arrows stored in chests of fifty bows — **PLAUSIBLE** (not fetched this session); this is ship-stowage evidence for how finished stock was crated, not workshop evidence.

*Ropemaker / ropewalk.* The analogue is LINEAR GROUND, not a room. Hamburg distinguishes the Seilerbahn (ropes up to 50 m) from the Reeperbahn (c. 400 m for ship cables), ropemakers working in the Neustadt until the 16th century, then from 1626 outside the walls because cable lengths grew toward 1,000 m (**CONFIRMED** https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reeperbahn, fetched 2026-08-23). Venice's state ropery: the Caxa del Canevo founded 1303, rebuilt as the Corderie della Tana in 1579 by Antonio da Ponte, 316 m long, 21 m wide, three naves on 84 pillars (**CONFIRMED** https://venezianews.it/en/stories/le-vie-della-canapa/, fetched 2026-08-23). Bridport: King John ordered ropes "night and day" in 1213; the town's houses have unusually long rear strips used as walks with a "turn house" at the far end where the jack was hoisted (**CONFIRMED-digest**, search digest of localhistories.org / Wikipedia Bridport); the best-preserved Bridport walk building (MWX200, 40 St Michael's Lane) is a late-19th-century two-storey, 15-bay gabled twine walk "built on the site of an earlier open rope walk" that formerly extended further west toward the River Brit (**CONFIRMED** https://heritage.dorsetcouncil.gov.uk/Monument/MWX200, fetched 2026-08-23). Smaller operators "used the streets of the port", sometimes under "simple roofs" (**CONFIRMED** https://www.platformspace.net/home/the-age-of-rope-part-2-building-ropewalks, fetched 2026-08-23). So the medieval register is an open walk — a line of posts/trestles on a strip of ground with a wheel at the head and a sledge at the tail — and the covered walk is late-17th-century naval (Chatham, Rochefort) and 19th-century provincial. Program: SPINNING/WHEEL HEAD (a small roofed hut with the wheel and hackling board), the WALK (open strip), TAR HOUSE (for naval work, a fire hazard kept apart), hemp/yarn STORE (dry loft). Siting: outside the walls, along a street or ditch, beside the harbour.

**(b) Measured.**
- Sawpit generic: "between 4 and 6 feet wide, 5 to 6 feet deep and 14 to 15 feet long"; pit saws "usually between 1.8m and 2.0m" long; sawpits "begin to appear in the historical and archaeological record of the British Isles around the middle of the 15th Century", in common use by the 16th (Goodburn 1992) — **CONFIRMED** (Maskill 2011, pp. 5, 14, 10).
- Marston Moretaine, Beds (post-medieval, excavated sawpits): 3.55 m × 1.45 m × 1.16 m deep; second pit 3.55 × 1.45 × 0.73 m, both judged too shallow without a superstructure — **CONFIRMED** (Maskill pp. 31–32, citing Edmondson & Steadman 2001).
- Farnham Park sawpit (late-17th-century brick over reused medieval clunch, graffito "July 21 1764"): c. 1.5 m × 4 m, floor at c. 1.6 m, recessed footholds, arched recess, possible post base for a roof — **CONFIRMED** (Maskill pp. 37–38). One author (Shepherd 2006) suggests a stone-sawing pit instead — qualifier preserved.
- Byrlyngham inventory c.1499: timber £27 13s 6d at Bishopsgate; wainscot and boards 39s 10d; Mercers' sawpit cost 12s 1½d — **CONFIRMED** (Leach pp. 175–176).
- Corderie della Tana: 316 m × 21 m, 3 naves, 84 pillars; 1303 / 1579 — **CONFIRMED** (venezianews).
- Hamburg: Seilerbahn ≤ 50 m; Reeperbahn c. 400 m; demand up to 1,000 m — **CONFIRMED** (de.wikipedia Reeperbahn).
- Bridport MWX200: 15 bays, two storeys, red brick; HER footprint 55 m × 13 m; late 19th century — **CONFIRMED** (Dorset HER).
- Chatham double ropehouse 1,135 ft (c. 346 m), 1790s — **PLAUSIBLE** (brief's figure; not fetched).
- Carpenters' shop (Windlesham, W&D): no measured figures published on the museum page — **CONFIRMED** null.
- No measured figures found for the cooperage (searched: "medieval cooperage workshop cresset jointer", "cooper probate inventory 16th 17th century in the shop"); for the carver's shop (searched: Riemenschneider Werkstatt Haus Gesellen); for the pole lathe footprint (searched: Coppergate pole lathe bed length — finds are a tool-rest fragment and hundreds of turning cores, no lathe dimensions); for the bowyer's stave store (searched: bowyer workshop Mary Rose staves).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**
- Negation search "medieval carpenters 'no workshop' OR 'on site' ... framing place" returned the opposite corrective: London carpenters DID hold yards (timber-haws) and the Company let yard space; but the yard is open ground, and big frames were cut at the timber source (Farnham 1395; Hadleigh 1405; Kingston 1510; Charlwood 1515) — the "workshop" as a roofed room is the minor part (**CONFIRMED** Leach pp. 175–176).
- Sawpit date: pit-sawing is a 15th-century arrival in Britain; a 13th-century carpenter's yard has no pit — timber was hewn and riven, sawn on trestles (**CONFIRMED** Maskill p. 10). Any pre-1450 plan with a lined sawpit is anachronistic.
- The W&D carpenter's shop is a c.1900 building; treat as a wear-grade ceiling, not a medieval plan.
- Coopers: the tied cooper in the brewhouse (pre-16th-century London) has no shop of his own — the catalog's village cooper with an independent shop is the post-medieval and rural form (**CONFIRMED-digest**).
- Riemenschneider's "workshop cellar" is a modern attribution on a tourist site; no primary evidence places carving in the cellar.
- Thatcher negation: no source describes a thatcher's workshop; null result stands as the finding.
- Ropewalk: the covered multi-bay walk is 1579 (Venice state), late-17th-century (naval), 19th-century (Bridport trade); medieval walks were open strips or streets. Chatham's figure is 18th-century.
- Bowyer/fletcher guild: the town entry says "guild"; the London hall is 16th-century — a 14th/15th-century town guild meets in a church or tavern, not its own building (**PLAUSIBLE**).

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**
- Carpenter (part-time, hamlet): host house only — `CROSS_PASSAGE` of the hall house, bench in the passage/outshut; ladder stair. Carpenter (village): `THROUGH_ROOM` shop → yard door; yard is open ground with a pent-house over the pit (no circulation class — a fixture). Ladder/winder to a loft over the shop at ceiling grade. Carpenters (5–15, town): burgage plot — front shop (`THROUGH_ROOM` to hall), side passage/cart entry to the rear timber-haw (`CROSS_PASSAGE` widened to cart width c. 2.4–3 m, **PLAUSIBLE**); winder stair in the house.
- Cooper: `THROUGH_ROOM` shop with a wide cart door to the yard; no stair in the shop; house winder.
- Woodcarver: continental Hof form = `GALLERY`-less courtyard with `THROUGH_ROOM` ground-floor shop; English village = one shop room off the `CROSS_PASSAGE`. Winder stair.
- Thatcher: none (no building).
- Bowyer & fletcher (village): `THROUGH_ROOM` shop-to-hall with shop window to street; ladder to stave loft. Guild (town): each member a burgage shop; guild hall (if any, 16th c.+) = `SCREENS_PASSAGE` hall.
- Ropemaker: the walk is a `GALLERY`-class linear element by geometry but it is OPEN GROUND at era grades before c.1580 (Venice) / 1690s (English naval); covered walk width 5–6 m per nave (Tana 21 m / 3 naves = 7 m), length bucket 50 m (cordage) / 300–400 m (cable) / 1,000 m (ceiling). Wheel hut is a `LOBBY`-sized cell at the head.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**
- Carpenter: `STORE` = timber rack (open, roofed lean-to, lightReq NONE, adjacency: yard, cart access); `ATTIC/GARRET_STORE` over the shop for ladders/boards (the Windlesham gable aperture is the fixture that licenses it); tool chest = FIXTURE. No cellar (weight goes to ground).
- Cooper: `STORE` for staves (dry, airy, NONE light, adjacency yard) and hoops (iron or hazel) — hoop rack is a FIXTURE; finished-cask `STORE` in the yard.
- Woodcarver: `CLOSET` for blanks and finished images (dry; adjacency shop); `CELLAR` only in the continental Hof form.
- Thatcher: rick = outdoor FIXTURE; spar store = `STORE` (lean-to).
- Bowyer/fletcher: stave `STORE`/loft (NONE light, dry, adjacency shop, long span 2 m+ racks); feather/glue `CLOSET`; sheaf `STORE` at guild grade (`crate` furnishing).
- Ropemaker: hemp `STORE` (dry loft, fire-risk separation from tar); tar house = separate `STORE` cell with hearth, `fireTrade`.

**(f) Typed proposal.**
- `CARPENTER_YARD_SHOP` — functions: floor `bench_in_host_house` → `open_framing_ground` + `timber_rack` → `roofed_sawpit` (era ≥ 1450) → `glazed_bench_shop` + `garret_ladder_store` (ceiling). fixtures[]: `workbench`(floor), `rack`(timber; floor+), `sawpit`(new FIXTURE; era-gated ≥1450; wear: unlined → timber-shored → stone/brick with niches), `pent_house`(open-sided roof, 2–3 sides open), `crate`(tool chest). Buckets: `heavyFloor`(yard only), `cartAccess`. Licensing: hamlet=host-house only; village=yard+pit; town=burgage households ×N, NO guild building. HOME: ROOM_KINDS.workfloor (shop), ROOM_KINDS.store (rack); the open framing ground has NO TYPED HOME (it is a yard, not a room).
- `COOPERAGE` — functions: `shop_with_cresset` → `stave_store` → `cask_yard`. fixtures: `workbench`(block/horse), `jointer`(new; 1.5–2 m long plane on legs, **PLAUSIBLE**), `cresset_hearth`(new; contained open fire, no chimney, needs high roof or open bay), `rack`(hoops), `barrel`. Buckets: `fireTrade`(minor, contained), `cartAccess`. Licensing: village independent (era ≥ c.1500 in the English register; earlier = tied to a brewhouse yard, which is a pull on the BREWER entry). HOME: workfloor, store.
- `CARVERS_SHOP` — single cell; fixtures: `workbench`, `rack`(gouges), `gilding_table`(fixture, prosperity ≥ mid), `glue_pot`(on host hearth). Buckets: `northLight`. Continental variant `HANDWERKERHOF` (three-wing court, cellar store). HOME: workfloor; NO TYPED HOME for courtyard.
- `THATCHER_YARD` — NO building; fixtures: `rick`(new outdoor fixture), `spar_store`(lean-to). HOME: NO TYPED HOME (yard-only entry class — see (g)).
- `BOWYER_SHOP` / `FLETCHER_SHOP` (one cell each; may share a house at village grade, which London forbade from 1371 — a continental/rural allowance): fixtures: `workbench`, `tiller_wall`(new), `rack`(staves, long-span), `glue_hearth`(fletcher), `counter`(shop window). Guild grade adds `crate`(sheaves), `strongbox`(ordinances), hall only at era ≥ 1500. HOME: workfloor, stall (window), store.
- `ROPEWALK` — functions: `open_walk_with_posts` (floor; length buckets 50 / 150 / 300–400 / 1,000 m; width 3–7 m) → `wheel_hut` → `covered_walk` (era ≥ 1580 state / ≥ 1690 naval / 19th c. trade) → `tar_house`. fixtures: `spinning_wheel`(new), `posts`, `sledge`, `top`. Buckets: `linearGround`(new), `extraMural`, `fireTrade`(tar house only). HOME: NO TYPED HOME (no ROOM_KIND for a 300 m unroofed strip).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**
1. The engine needs a **yard-as-primary** building class: entries whose main "room" is unroofed ground with fixtures (framing ground, ropewalk, thatcher's rick, cask yard). ROOM_KINDS has no `yard`; the finding lands on the plot/curtilage pull rather than the interior pull.
2. A **linear-footprint** license (ropewalk) that can exceed the plot depth and run along a street or wall outside the gate — currently unexpressible; lands on the siting flag (`extraMural`) plus a new length bucket.
3. **Era-gated fixtures** (sawpit ≥ 1450; covered walk ≥ 1580/1690; glazed shop front late) — the grammar needs fixture-level era gates, not only room-level.
4. The town "Carpenters (5–15)" must compile to N households on burgage plots sharing a hired timber-haw, not one big workshop; the "guild" entry for bowyers/fletchers likewise compiles to shops + a meeting place (church/tavern) before 1500.
5. `cresset_hearth` and `glue_hearth` are sub-hearth fire fixtures without chimneys — a `fireTrade=minor` grade is needed so they do not pull the full forge/kiln siting law.

**(h) Continental vs English.** The English record is parochial in three ways: pit-sawing is late (mid-15th c.) where the continent had water-powered sawmills from the 14th–16th centuries (Maskill p. 10, citing Erfurt 1490 and Dutch wind sawmills by the 16th century); the ropewalk as a state-built covered hall is Venetian (1303/1579) and French (Rochefort) long before the English naval ropehouses; and the carver's establishment is the German Handwerkerhof — a multi-wing courtyard house where master, journeymen and apprentices lodged with the shop on the court and a vaulted cellar store (Riemenschneider's Hof) — versus the English alabasterman's ordinary town house. The Hanseatic Reeperbahn/Seilerbahn distinction (50 m vs 400 m) gives the engine a two-bucket length law the English sources do not articulate.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.
