## §7 · FAMILY E — hot metal: Resident smith (part-time) (L157) · Blacksmith (L539) · Blacksmiths (3-10) (L1091) · Specialized metalworkers (L1717) · Smelter (L1148)

*(English/Irish register from round 2; the continental Schmiede, the specialized city trades — armourer, cutler, pewterer, bell-founder — the smelter site and the part-time hamlet smith are sub-lane BE's and follow as §7-bis below.)*

**(a) Analogue.** The rural smithy is a ONE-ROOM building organised around three fixed points — hearth, anvil, quench — with a fuel store partitioned off by a light screen. The primary is Ballykillaboy, Co. Kilkenny (15th–17th c., excavated 2006; G. Laidlaw, "A Medieval Smithy in South Kilkenny", NRA monograph chapter, PDF fetched 2026-08-23 → `RINST2-kilkenny-smithy.txt`, CONFIRMED-primary): "a small cob and stake built smithy structure with several exterior hearths and rubbish pits"; inside, "a hook-shaped arrangement of eight stakeholes… acted as a barrier between the charcoal rich deposits and those which contained large amounts of anvil residue… may have formed a wattle and daub barrier which separated the charcoal storage area from the smithy proper"; the bellows "located to the east of the internal barrier", a post-pit beside the hearth "may mark the location of a post which secured the bellows"; hearth, anvil-block pit and quench-tank pit (figures in (b)); three hearths OUTSIDE to the west "unlikely to have been smithing hearths but used instead to produce charcoal"; the fuel "almost entirely charcoal… almost exclusively oak". The discussion compares Ballinvinny South, Co. Cork (late 17th–18th c.): "the same distinction between charcoal-enriched soil on one side of the smithy, and deposits of trampled anvil residues on the other… may indicate that the layout of smithies was becoming somewhat standardised by the late medieval period." The Scottish burgh register (ScARF Highland regional framework page fetched 2026-08-23, CONFIRMED): a Cromarty burgh smithy of posts and daub with "a hearth of three upright stones"; Portmahomack's 15th–16th-c. smithy "roofed and open-fronted"; Eilean Donan's castle smithy 1450–1640 serving an armoury function. The WATER-POWERED tier: Wealden iron (Wealden Iron Research Group page fetched 2026-08-23, CONFIRMED) — the blast furnace arrives 1490 (Newbridge), c. 1 tonne of pig a day, a POND for the water-powered bellows and a separate finery forge with its own pond; Ifield finery forge excavation (digest: forge building + wheel pit + dam; CONFIRMED-digest); Shepherd Wheel, Sheffield (fetched 2026-08-23, CONFIRMED): first referenced 1584, present buildings c. 1780, a 5.5 m overshot wheel driving two grinding 'hulls' — the cutler GRINDS at the water mill and forges in town (sub-lane BE carries the cutler's dry shop); Finch Foundry, Sticklepath (digest, CONFIRMED-digest; 19th c.): tilt, drop and shear hammers, four hearths, two furnaces, a leat and three overshot wheels — the late-era ceiling of the family. Siting: the town's "Ironmonger Lane" (London; digest) is the metal MERCHANTS' street, named by the 12th c., the trade having moved by the late 16th c. — a reminder that a 'Blacksmiths (3–10)' entry is a dispersal of smithies at the gates and the market edge, and the metal STREET is merchants' shops (family B). Live/work: the rural smith's house is separate from the smithy (Ballykillaboy is a free-standing shed with no domestic floor deposits reported); the town smith lives over/behind a shop-smithy on the burgage plot (PLAUSIBLE; the town smithy on a plot is not measured this lane — queued). Prosperity: FLOOR = the one-room cob smithy (hearth + anvil + quench + bellows + screened charcoal store); RUNG 1 = open-fronted or enclosed timber smithy with a stone-built hearth and chimney hood, a tool/iron store, a shoeing area outside; RUNG 2 = two hearths, a separate fuel house, a water-powered hammer or grinding wheel if there is a leat (the 'fireTrade' + 'waterPower' pair); CEILING = the multi-hearth forge with tilt hammer and wheels. Decline: a cold forge (the supply-chain `blocked`/`scarce` state) keeps its fixtures — the hearth cold, the anvil in place, the charcoal bay empty — which is the wear-grade rendering law for this family.

**(b) Measured.** Ballykillaboy (CONFIRMED-primary): site 300 m² in total; hearth pit 0.55 × 0.50 × 0.08 m deep "with large areas of scorching"; anvil-block pit 0.80 × 0.60 × 0.30 m deep (held "a section of tree-trunk on which the anvil was set"); quench pit sub-circular 1.0 × 0.90 × 0.40 m deep; barrier stakeholes c. 0.1 m diameter × 0.1 m deep, eight of them; c. 30 kg of metallurgical residues (receipt round 2); Martincamp pottery and radiocarbon place use in the 15th–17th c. The building's own footprint is NOT recoverable from the cob survival ("very little of the cob structure survived… the remainder of the structure was defined by the limits of the internal floor deposits") — no wall-to-wall dimension is quotable; PLAUSIBLE bucket from the three pits' spread: a single cell of order 4–6 m. Wealden blast furnace c. 1 tonne/day (CONFIRMED). Shepherd Wheel: 5.5 m overshot wheel, two hulls (CONFIRMED). Forge-bench figures from modern smithing sources (hearth 2–3 ft square, c. 30 in high) are PLAUSIBLE-grade for the medieval case and are NOT to be consumed by the grammar.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.** Open-fronted vs enclosed: Portmahomack "roofed and open-fronted" against Ballykillaboy's cob walls and Cromarty's post-and-daub — both forms are lawful and the choice is climate/culture, not prosperity (flag for the culture weights). "Standardised by the late medieval period" rests on TWO Irish sites (Laidlaw's own hedge: "may indicate"); the grammar may treat the hearth/anvil/quench triangle + screened fuel store as the floor pattern but must not claim a standard plan. The 'Blacksmiths (3–10)' town entry is NOT a building; the catalog desc is a count of households. The 'Smelter' at town tier is anachronistic for the English register before 1490 (blast furnace) but the BLOOMERY is earlier everywhere — sub-lane BE dates both. Negation search this lane ("smithy inside the house medieval", "no separate smithy") returned nothing credible for a smithy inside the dwelling in the English/Irish register; the part-time hamlet smith's outshut is PLAUSIBLE only — queued to sub-lane BE.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).** Floor: NONE — a single cell with one door (or an open front); `THROUGH_ROOM` is the whole building. Rung 1–2: the smithy is a yard building; circulation is EXTERIOR (the shoeing area, the fuel house door, the leat). The town shop-smithy on a plot takes the family-B `SCREENS_PASSAGE`/entry to the yard where the forge sits at the plot tail (PLAUSIBLE siting — fire ordinances push the hearth off the street; not measured this lane). No stair at any rung except the dwelling's. The water-powered forge adds a `LOBBY`-like wheel-house cell beside the hammer floor (Finch Foundry — digest).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).** Floor: `STORE` (fuel) as a SCREENED BAY of the single cell — Ballykillaboy's wattle barrier is the measured precedent for a storage cell that is a partition, not a room: in the vocabulary it is a `STORE{sizeBucket: BAY, lightReq: NONE, fixtures: [WATTLE_SCREEN]}` adjacent to the bellows side. Rung 1: iron/bar store as a rack (`FIXTURE: rack`), a separate `STORE` (fuel house) outside, a water butt. Rung 2: the charcoal BARN of a furnace site is a large-volume `STORE` (sub-lane BE measures it). No pantry/buttery/larder at any rung — this family's storage is fuel and stock only.

**(f) Typed proposal.**
- `parti`: `ONE_CELL_SMITHY_SCREENED_FUEL` (floor) · `OPEN_FRONT_SMITHY` (culture variant) · `SMITHY_AT_PLOT_TAIL` (town; hosted on a family-B plot) · `WATER_FORGE_ON_LEAT` (rung 2; needs `waterPower`) · `FURNACE_SITE_ON_POND` (ceiling; the smelter — a SITE of furnace + bellows house + charcoal barn + finery, sub-lane BE).
- `functions[]`: floor = `FORGE_HEARTH`, `ANVIL_STATION`, `QUENCH`, `BELLOWS`, `FUEL_STORE_BAY`; rung 1 adds `SHOEING_AREA` (exterior), `IRON_STORE`, `CHIMNEY_HOOD`; rung 2 adds `SECOND_HEARTH`, `HAMMER_FLOOR`, `WHEEL_HOUSE`, `GRINDING_HULL`; ceiling adds `CASTING_FLOOR`, `CHARCOAL_BARN`.
- `fixtures[]`: `HEARTH` (HOME; grade: pit-hearth → stone-kerbed → hooded), `ANVIL_ON_BLOCK` (NO TYPED HOME), `QUENCH_TROUGH` (NO TYPED HOME), `BELLOWS_ON_POST` (NO TYPED HOME), `WATTLE_SCREEN`, `TOOL_RACK` (HOME: rack), `TILT_HAMMER`, `OVERSHOT_WHEEL`; wear states: `COLD_FORGE` (the `blocked`/`scarce` supply render — hearth cold, fuel bay empty).
- buckets: `fireTrade: true` (siting away from thatch; at the gate/market edge), `waterPower` {NONE | LEAT | POND} (rung 2+), `fuel` {CHARCOAL | COAL} (charcoal at the floor — Ballykillaboy; coal is a late/regional variant, PLAUSIBLE).
- licensing: `ONE_CELL_SMITHY` at hamlet+ (the part-time smith); `WATER_FORGE` needs the map's water (`INDUSTRY_WATER_NEEDS` has no smith row — §Σ); `FURNACE_SITE` at town+ with `resourceSites` ore (PREDICTED path, T2N — description-only coordination).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.** (1) The existing `craft` template is RIGHT at the floor and wrong one rung up: its tier-0 `workfloor` already carries the `hearth` furnishing (verified in `interiorTemplates.js` L115 of the clean tree, this session), which satisfies law 3 ("no forge, no smithy"); but its tier-1 addition is a separate `store` ROOM, whereas the measured floor smithy has the fuel store as a screened BAY of the one cell and a separate store only at rung 1 — and its tier-2 `kiln` room is a second FIRE cell the smithy never has (a second hearth is the same room; the kiln belongs to family F). The requirement roster for E should be: floor = workfloor{hearth, anvil, quench, bellows, screened fuel bay}; rung 1 = + iron store + shoeing area; rung 2 = + hammer floor/wheel house (water). (2) The screened fuel bay is the first instance in this tranche of a §453 `STORE` that is a PARTITION of its host cell — the StorageCell contract needs a `sizeBucket: BAY` that renders as a screen line, not a wall. (3) `fireTrade` is a new siting flag the town map must read (the existing `locationBias` regex pulls on 'noxious/tannery', not on fire) — §Σ. (4) The cold-forge wear state is the cleanest demonstration of S7's supply-state variants and should be the pilot.

**(h) Continental vs English.** See §7-bis (h) (the Alpine/German Esse-and-chimney smithy, the French travail shoeing frame, the Liège/Siegerland and Lapphyttan blast furnaces that predate the Weald's 1490 by a century or more).

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.

---

## §7-bis · FAMILY E — hot metal, NEW members: Resident smith (part-time) L157 · Blacksmith L539 · Blacksmiths (3-10) L1091 · Specialized metalworkers L1717 · Smelter L1148

*Researched by sub-lane BE (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. Continental smithy, the specialized city metal trades, the smelter site and the part-time hamlet smith; the Irish/English one-cell smithy is §7 above.*

(Parent's rounds 1–2 — Ballykillaboy, Cromarty, Portmahomack, Eilean Donan, Wealden 1490 furnace, Ifield, Shepherd Wheel, Finch Foundry, Ironmonger Lane — not restated.)

**(a) Analogue.**

1. **Continental village smithy.** French: the forge is a hearth with chain-worked bellows, anvil and quenching trough; the *travail à ferrer* (shoeing frame, a heavy post-frame fixed to the ground, for lifting and restraining horses and oxen) stands OUTSIDE in the forge yard; at Liorac (Dordogne) "a small building near the forge housed this tramail" (liorac.info + champcevinel / ecomusée digests, **CONFIRMED-digest**, 18th–19th-c. survivals). So the continental forge has a second fixture in the yard or a second shed, which the English register lacks. The German/Alpine Schmiede with its *Esse* (hearth+hood+chimney): NO source found this session (searched once, German terms) — **PLAUSIBLE** only: a hearth c. 0.8–1.2 m square under a masonry hood.

2. **Specialised metalworkers (city L1717) — five different buildings, sorted by their defining fixture:**
- *Bell-founder — the PIT trade.* Blaylock, 'Bell and Cauldron Founding in Exeter', *Historical Metallurgy* 30/2 (1996), PDF downloaded and read (hmsjournal.org article 416/398, fetched 2026-08-23, **CONFIRMED**, PRIMARY): four sites, late 12th to early 18th c. (i) St Mary Major, Cathedral Close: a one-off late-12th-c. pit "at the west end of the church, between the responds of the tower arch, in a classic position from which the finished casting could be hoisted directly into the tower"; a long narrow trench, the Theophilus lost-wax mould resting "on two parallel flank walls"; the same form at Winchester, 10th–13th c. (Davies & Ovenden 1990) and St Oswald's Gloucester (10th c.). (ii) Mermaid Yard, Preston Street ('Billeter Lane' from the late Middle Ages to the early 19th c. "presumably from the presence of this foundry"): 13th to late-15th-c. waste dumps in quarry pits, "no structural remains"; the core of the medieval foundry "was never located". (iii) Cowick Street, across the river by the bridge end, Birdall family c. 1520/1550–1624: "a two-roomed house with cob walls on the street frontage" and behind it "a large area of some 650 square metres" of yard, pitted for mould clay ("the ready supply of riverine clay … surely one of the reasons for moving the foundry to this site"); casting pit and furnace base "laid out on one long axis"; the pit "trefoil-shaped in plan, permitting the simultaneous casting of up to three bells"; "some 18 individual pit cuts … at least 23 bells cast"; "larger bells required deeper pits"; mould bases on a ring of stone (later brick) with 3–4 vent gaps; a second furnace implies a second pit. (iv) Paul Street, Penningtons c. 1625–1721: a tenement "running back from the street to the city wall"; house on the frontage (destroyed by later cellars); workshop behind; bell casting itself "probably took place at another site, outside the North Gate" until the mid-1650s; after the Civil War a permanent pit and a REVERBERATORY furnace "dug into the clay rampart behind the city wall", fire-box turned 90° to the pit for want of space; the earliest cut "large enough to contain four moulds for a simultaneous casting of a ring of bells" (the 1656 ring of four for St Mary Steps, still hanging); 20 casting operations in situ plus up to 12 more as spindle stakeholes; the rear "accommodated workshops and storerooms, arranged around a small courtyard". Also: founders travelled and set up TEMPORARY workshops — Thomas Birdall at Honeychurch, 1570. Plus Chalbury, Dorset (HER MDO5426, fetched 2026-08-23, **CONFIRMED**): two 14th/15th-c. pits "beneath the floor of the nave", one 0.75 m diameter × 0.7 m deep, the other 0.7 m deep with a 0.5 m unfired-clay ring in situ, an adjacent heat-reddened clay area 1.25 × 0.75 m "probably a furnace zone", cut through the 12th-c. nave floor so the founding happened "within the shell of the newly-constructed" nave; excavated 1989 by the East Dorset Antiquarian Society (Proc. DNHAS 1991). Cologne Cathedral: a pit of shortly before 1450 for one of the two great bells (koelner-dom.de digest, **CONFIRMED-digest**). Room program of the URBAN foundry: street house → rear yard workshop with pit+furnace on one axis → storerooms round a court → clay quarry pits/waste dumps (off-plot at the cramped site). It is the one trade whose defining fixture is BELOW the floor.
- *Pewterer — a low-heat trade that sits in the street.* First London ordinances 1348 (pewterers' history digests, **CONFIRMED-digest**); 'A London Pewterer's Workshop in 1551' (*Antiquaries Journal*, Cambridge Core — PDF fetch failed with an HTML interstitial) is an inventory describing "the alloying and casting of over a ton of tin" into flatware (**CONFIRMED-digest**). Fixtures: stone/bronze moulds, a small bellows hearth, sometimes a double hearth (melt + mould-warming) (hobbyist reconstruction sites, **CONFIRMED-digest**, low grade). Building: an ordinary shop-house with a small furnace in the workroom — no pit, no pond, no noxious bucket.
- *Armourer.* Helmschmied of Augsburg: Lorenz (fl. 1467–1515), Kolman (1471–1532), Desiderius (1513–1579), working for the Habsburgs; Nuremberg's Grünwald, Siebenbürger, Lochner (Met essay + wikipedia digests, **CONFIRMED-digest**). Henry VIII "employed first Italians and then, from 1515, Germans in his Greenwich workshops" (Met digest, **CONFIRMED-digest**) — the royal counterexample: a workshop owned by the crown, outside the guild town, with a water-powered mill (**PLAUSIBLE** for the mill). NO measured armourer's workshop found (searched once); the academia.edu paper 'Armourers and their workshops' exists but was not fetched (403 class). **PLAUSIBLE**: hearth, stake-anvils, polishing wheel, a quiet shop for finishing — a large smithy with a grinding/polishing annexe.
- *Cutler.* Solingen: the *Kotten* is "a small, independent business facility, usually located on a watercourse" for hammers and grindstones; the trade split across the town into "manual forges, cottage workshops with waterwheels, steam-powered grinding workshops … small workshops of the quenchers or fitters" (LVR-Industriemuseum digest, **CONFIRMED-digest**) — same split as Sheffield (parent): the cutler's SHOP is dry; the grinding is at the mill. Wipperkotten/Balkhauser Kotten survive (museums).
- *Nailer/chain-maker — the domestic forge.* Nailer's Cottage, Quinton (qlhs.org.uk, fetched 2026-08-23, **CONFIRMED**): single-storey workshop 10'4" × 10'6" (3.15 × 3.20 m) on the corner, parallel to the road, with NO original internal access to the two-storey cottage; walls c. 2'6" thick; the forge chimney rising against the west end; stone foundation possibly 16th c., brick 17th c., "apparently C18 but probably C17 in origin"; nailmaking documented there to 1890–1900. The typical nail shop: "about ten or twelve feet square with one door and one or two unglazed windows, with a central hearth or fire so that all the family could work independently" — up to six round one fire; the chain shop differs: hearths around the walls; the 1843 Midland Mining Commission: "little brick shops of about 15 feet by 12 feet in which seven or eight individuals constantly work" (sedgleymanor / rootsweb digests, **CONFIRMED-digest**). All 17th–19th-c. survivals; the MEDIEVAL nailer is documentary only (**PLAUSIBLE**).

3. **Smelter (town L1148) — a SITE of several buildings on a pond.** Dates: Lapphyttan, Norberg, Sweden — excavated 1978–83, water-powered, "probably between 1150 and 1350", producing pig iron refined to blooms that were "cut into lumps for trade" (= the 'osmonds' in English customs from the 1250s) (en.wikipedia, fetched 2026-08-23, **CONFIRMED**; ekomuseum digest for the excavation years, **CONFIRMED-digest**); Weald blast furnace 1490 (parent); Panningridge, Sussex: the only 16th-c. blast furnace whose building (1542) AND operating accounts (1542–1563) survive; earth dam, furnace, wheel-pit, water system, slag heaps, a second furnace superimposed; excavated 1964–69 (ancientmonuments.uk digest, **CONFIRMED-digest**; the page and the *PMA* 1972 abstract both 403'd). Liège/Siegerland 14th–15th c.: not searched — **PLAUSIBLE**. Finery forge: Chingley Forge, Kent HER MKE1074 (fetched 2026-08-23, **CONFIRMED**): 14th-c. bloomery deposits under the 16th-c. structure; finery hearth with water-powered bellows, chafery hearth, hammer frame, wheel-pit, "a timber anvil base in a pit 7 ft deep"; a lower wheel-pit "probably housed two wheels" (16th-c. pottery, possibly working 1588); pond bay c. 100 m long; documented 1626–1726; Crossley 1968–72; evidence of tool and cutlery making alongside refining. Charcoal barn: NO measured figure found (searched "charcoal barn 'coal house' bridge house casting house Weald dimensions" — returned the furnace sites only) — **PLAUSIBLE**: a barn-sized timber shed, because charcoal is bulky (several tonnes per tonne of iron, parent's ~1 t/day). **The counterexample with NO building:** the lead bole. Bole Hill, Totley (Historic England 1009711 / ancientmonuments.uk, both 403; digest **CONFIRMED-digest**): on the ridge crest, "orientated north-south and open to the west", 7 × 3 m externally, casting channels to an oval casting area c. 2 × 1.5 m; a medieval slag hearth ("blackwork oven") c. 50 m north, c. 5 × 3 m; a bole was "a wind-blown smelting fire located on an exposed hilltop", stone-walled, open on one side, wood and ore interleaved, "normally facing south west"; medieval to later 16th c.

4. **Resident smith (part-time), hamlet L157.** Wharram Percy's smithy is SAXON (Blakelock et al., *Historical Metallurgy* 2021, hmsjournal article 89, fetched 2026-08-23, **CONFIRMED**: the assemblage "fitted the most complex" of three ironworking-landscape models; nothing on farming) — it does NOT evidence the medieval peasant smith. Feltham Farm, Silton, Dorset (HER MDO4705, fetched, **CONFIRMED**): "a thirteenth or fourteenth century smithy" recorded 1991 on the A303 (Jones 1991, unpublished interim — no plan or dimensions on the record). Houghton (A14, Internet Archaeology 67): a smithy whose "separate northern section … may have served as a domestic space for the smiths and their families, or storage for iron stock", with "a beam-slot along the south-eastern edge" read as "a lean-to shed used for storing fuel outside the smithy to mitigate fire risk" (digest, **CONFIRMED-digest**; page 403; the section is titled post-medieval, so DATE UNKNOWN — flag). Goltho: nothing returned. So the part-time smith's building rests on: (i) the late-medieval standardised small smithy (parent's Ballykillaboy), (ii) the nailer's detached 10 × 10 ft shop with no door into the house (**CONFIRMED**, 17th c.), (iii) the Houghton fuel lean-to. **PLAUSIBLE** synthesis: a detached shed 3–4 m square with one hearth, OR an outshut bay of the house with its own door — the no-internal-door rule (fire) is the one CONFIRMED trait.

5. **Open-fronted vs enclosed.** Open-fronted: Portmahomack (parent, **CONFIRMED**); the bole (no building at all). Enclosed: Ballykillaboy (cob walls, wattle barrier — parent), the nailshop ("one door and one or two unglazed windows"), the Paul Street foundry (walled yard, court). The continental forge adds the shoeing frame OUTSIDE — a hybrid: enclosed hearth, open yard fixture. No continental open-fronted smithy primary found — **PLAUSIBLE**.

Prosperity/wear: floor = the bole (no building) / the one-off church-floor bell pit / the 3 m shed; rungs = yard fixtures (travail, fuel lean-to, quench), the two-roomed frontage house + 650 m² yard (Cowick St), the permanent pit + reverberatory furnace; ceiling = the pond site (dam, wheel-pits, furnace, finery, chafery, hammer) and the royal Greenwich armoury. Decline sheds first the water power (back to hand bellows), then the permanent pit (back to travelling founders casting in the churchyard — Honeychurch 1570), then the building (bole).

**(b) Measured.**
- Chalbury pits: 0.75 m dia × 0.7 m deep; 0.7 m deep + 0.5 m clay ring; furnace zone 1.25 × 0.75 m; 14th/15th c. (**CONFIRMED**).
- Exeter Cowick St: two-roomed cob house on the frontage + c. 650 m² rear yard; trefoil pit, up to 3 bells at once, 18 cuts/≥ 23 bells; Paul St: 4-mould sub-rectangular pit (1656), 20 castings + 12 stakeholes; occupation dates c. 1520/1550–1624 and c. 1625–1721 (**CONFIRMED**). No pit diameters in the text (plans at 1 m scale only).
- Bole Hill: bole 7 × 3 m, casting area 2 × 1.5 m, slag hearth 5 × 3 m, 50 m apart (**CONFIRMED-digest**).
- Chingley: anvil base pit 7 ft (2.1 m) deep; pond bay c. 100 m; two-wheel pit; dates 14th c. / 1588 / 1626–1726 (**CONFIRMED**).
- Nailer's Cottage workshop 10'4" × 10'6"; walls 2'6" (**CONFIRMED**); typical nailshop 10–12 ft square; 1843 report 15 × 12 ft, 7–8 workers (**CONFIRMED-digest**).
- Lapphyttan 1150–1350 (**CONFIRMED**); no furnace dimensions found (searched: "Lapphyttan … dimensions").
- Panningridge 1542 / accounts 1542–63 / dug 1964–69 (**CONFIRMED-digest**); no dimensions.
- Walbrook-style counts not applicable; Helmschmied dates 1467–1579 (**CONFIRMED-digest**).
- No measured figures found for: the Alpine Esse, the armourer's workshop, the pewterer's furnace, the charcoal barn, Feltham Farm's plan.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**
- *Bells were NOT always cast in a foundry*: three of the four Exeter sites show the pit under the church floor or a travelling founder (St Mary Major; Honeychurch 1570; the Penningtons casting "outside the North Gate" until the 1650s even while holding a town tenement) — the fixed urban foundry is a 16th–17th-c. consolidation (**CONFIRMED**). The hobbyist claim that monks cast all medieval bells in churchyards is the OPPOSITE over-statement (distillatio.wordpress digest) — Mermaid Yard shows a fixed 13th–15th-c. town foundry too.
- *The smelter can have no building at all* (bole) — the catalog's "charcoal-fuelled furnaces" description is the iron case only; lead smelting in the 13th–16th c. is wind and a hillside.
- *Wharram is Saxon*; the one named 13th/14th-c. village smithy (Feltham Farm) is an unpublished interim — the "hamlet smith" is the weakest-evidenced member of the family.
- *The nailer's forge is an 18th-c. survival* with 17th-c. origins; projecting it to 1200–1500 is inference.
- *Specialized metalworkers* as "separate guilds" (catalog) is right for the town, but the cutler's production is SPLIT across two building types (dry shop + water-powered Kotten/hull), so the entry is a pair, not one building.
- Negation searches run: "bole hill … wind-blown" (found the no-building smelter); "village smithy … peasant farmstead" (found Feltham/Houghton, weak); "Greenwich armoury 1515" (found the royal counterexample, no building detail). Null: no German Esse measurement; no Goltho smithing page.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**
- Resident smith L157: none; a door to the yard; no stair. If an outshut: the house's own `CROSS_PASSAGE` is NOT shared (no internal door — the nailer rule).
- Blacksmith L539: single cell with a yard; the continental variant adds the travail yard/shed — `LOBBY`-less external circulation between hearth and shoeing frame.
- Blacksmiths (3-10) L1091: a street of such cells; no interior class; Kotten variant = detached mill cell on the watercourse.
- Specialized metalworkers L1717: bell-founder = `THROUGH_ROOM` house → rear yard → workshop/storerooms "arranged around a small courtyard" (a COURTYARD parti, Paul St, **CONFIRMED**); pewterer/cutler (dry)/goldsmith = the Family-B shop-house; armourer = large workshop + finishing room (**PLAUSIBLE**).
- Smelter L1148: a SITE, not a building: charging bridge from the dam bank to the furnace top (a `GALLERY`-class external walk, **PLAUSIBLE** — not measured this session), casting floor, bellows house, wheel-pit, finery and chafery hearths, hammer, charcoal barn, with the pond as the organising element (Chingley pond bay 100 m, **CONFIRMED**).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**
- Smith (all tiers): `STORE` for iron stock (Houghton northern section, **CONFIRMED-digest**) and a FUEL store that is a separate lean-to OUTSIDE for fire risk (**CONFIRMED-digest**; parent's Ballykillaboy wattle barrier is the inside version); quench trough = fixture.
- Bell-founder: `STORE` rooms round the court (**CONFIRMED**); the clay is QUARRIED on-plot (650 m² yard) or off-plot; mould waste backfills the quarry pits — storage/waste share one yard.
- Pewterer: `STORE` for tin ingots and moulds (valuable, lockable — ≥ a ton of tin in 1551, **CONFIRMED-digest**); adjacency to the street shop.
- Armourer: `STORE` for plate and a finishing room; `STRONGROOM` for finished harness (**PLAUSIBLE**).
- Smelter: the charcoal barn is the largest cell on the site (`STORE`, volume bucket XL, dry, NONE light, away from the furnace mouth — fire adjacency); ore/mine store by the roasting place; cast-iron sows on the casting floor.
- Nailer: iron rod store = fixture in a 10 ft shop.

**(f) Typed proposal.**
- parti: `DETACHED_SMITHY_SHED` (3–4 m sq, one hearth, no door into the house), `SMITHY_OUTSHUT` (bay of the house, own door), `FORGE_WITH_TRAVAIL_YARD` (continental: enclosed hearth + external shoeing frame or frame-shed), `OPEN_FRONTED_SMITHY` (parent), `NAILSHOP_CENTRAL_HEARTH` (family forge, hearth central, 10–15 × 10–12 ft), `CHAIN_SHOP_WALL_HEARTHS`, `BELL_FOUNDRY_PIT_YARD` (frontage house → yard → pit + furnace on one axis → court of stores; pit depth scales with bell size), `CHURCH_FLOOR_BELL_PIT` (one-off, inside the nave/tower arch — an EVENT in a church, not a building), `PEWTERER_STREET_SHOP`, `CUTLER_DRY_SHOP` + `GRINDING_KOTTEN` (pair), `ARMOURER_WORKSHOP`, `BLAST_FURNACE_POND_SITE` (dam, wheel-pit, furnace, casting floor, bellows house, charcoal barn, bridge), `FINERY_FORGE_SITE` (finery + chafery + hammer + anvil pit), `BOLE_HILL` (no building; ridge-crest, open to the prevailing wind).
- functions[]: floor = bole / church-floor pit / detached shed; rungs = fuel lean-to, travail, quench pit, second hearth, permanent pit, reverberatory furnace, storerooms round a court; ceiling = pond site with two wheels, or the royal armoury.
- fixtures[]: hearth (HOME: FURNISHING_KINDS.hearth), anvil block (workbench), bellows (NO TYPED HOME), quench trough (barrel), travail/shoeing frame (NO TYPED HOME), casting pit (NO TYPED HOME — sub-floor), reverberatory furnace (kiln room kind is the nearest: ROOM_KINDS.kiln), mould rings/strickle spindle (NO TYPED HOME), pewter moulds (rack), grindstone (NO TYPED HOME), charging bridge (NO TYPED HOME), hammer frame (NO TYPED HOME), charcoal barn (ROOM_KINDS.store).
- structural buckets: `fireTrade` (all), `waterPower` (Kotten, furnace, finery, Greenwich mill), `heavyFloor`/`subFloorPit` (bell pit, anvil pit 2.1 m — a NEW bucket), `clayQuarry` (bell-founder — a NEW siting need), `windSited` (bole — a NEW bucket: ridge crest, no building), `noxious` (smoke; the Exeter foundries sat across the river / against the wall / outside the gate).
- licensing: `CHURCH_FLOOR_BELL_PIT` any tier with a church, era 10th–15th c.; `BELL_FOUNDRY_PIT_YARD` tier ≥ town, era ≥ 13th c. (Mermaid Yard), permanent pit + reverberatory era ≥ c. 1650 English; `BLAST_FURNACE_POND_SITE` era ≥ 1150 Swedish / ≥ 1490 English / 14th–15th c. Low Countries-Rhineland (**PLAUSIBLE**), needs `INDUSTRY_WATER_NEEDS` row + pond; `BOLE_HILL` lead-ore hinterland, era ≤ late 16th c., prosperity any; `NAILSHOP` era ≥ 17th c. as a building (medieval nailer = `DETACHED_SMITHY_SHED`); `FORGE_WITH_TRAVAIL_YARD` continental morphology, any tier ≥ village; `SMITHY_OUTSHUT` hamlet only, prosperity low.
- HOME tags: ROOM_KINDS.workfloor (smithy), .kiln (furnace/reverberatory), .store (charcoal barn, iron store), .hall/.chamber (frontage house); NO TYPED HOME for: pit, yard, bridge, pond, bole.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**
1. A sub-floor cell: the bell pit (and the 2.1 m anvil pit) is a room-kind BELOW the workfloor with no walls — the grammar has no negative-space cell.
2. A building-less production site (bole) and a multi-building site on a pond (furnace + finery) both exceed "a building with rooms"; they land on resourceSites/INDUSTRY_WATER_NEEDS (parent recon: 6 names; smelter is presumably one — the lead bole needs a `wind` not `water` need).
3. The no-internal-door fire rule (smithy never opens into the dwelling; fuel store outside) is an ADJACENCY PROHIBITION, which the adjacency law set (§453) currently expresses only as attractions.
4. `Specialized metalworkers` must fan out into ≥ 5 building archetypes, two of which (cutler, goldsmith) are Family-B shop-houses and one (bell-founder) is a yard parti.
5. A one-off EVENT parti (bell cast inside the church) is a building-modifying happening, not a building — the news/event layer, not the plan layer.
6. The continental yard fixture (travail) needs a yard-furnishing slot outside the room envelope.

**(h) Continental vs English.** The English village smithy is a one-cell shed with the shoeing done at the door; the continental forge externalises the shoeing into a permanent travail in the yard or its own shed, and the Alpine/German Schmiede roofs the hearth under a masonry Esse hood (unmeasured this session). On the smelter the English register is LATE: the water-powered blast furnace is at Lapphyttan c. 1150–1350, three centuries before the Weald's 1490, and the Rhineland/Liège furnaces sit between (**PLAUSIBLE** for the latter). On specialised metal the continental mechanism is the guild-town specialisation (Augsburg/Nuremberg armourers for the Habsburgs, Solingen's Kotten split) against which Greenwich 1515 is a crown import; on bells, Exeter shows the English town foundry doing exactly what Cologne did (a pit before 1450) — no parochialism there.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None (all five entries are mundane).

**Parent-lane review note.** The bell-founder's sub-floor casting pit and the Exeter Cowick Street two-roomed house + 650 m² yard are the parent's picks for §Σ; the Feltham Farm 13th/14th-c. smithy remains the weakest-evidenced member of the family (unpublished interim).

## §8 · FAMILY F — kiln trades: Potter (village L646) · Brickmaker (village L653) · Glassblower (town L1198) · Glassmakers (city L1738)

*Researched by sub-lane DF (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

**(a) Analogue.**

*Potter.* The excavated medieval pottery is a **production yard**, not a shop: clay pit and weathering heap, a wheel shed (a light timber building; wheel emplacements rarely survive), drying space (shelves/racks under cover or a heated drying feature), the kiln(s) in the open or under a temporary roof, waster dumps and stoke pits. At Temple Street, Brill (early 16th century, excavated 1983) the complex comprised an earlier brick-built twin-flue pedestal kiln (kiln 1) replaced by a rectangular triple-flued kiln (kiln 2) "with a raised floor and no permanent roof", firing both roof tile and pottery, with sand quarries, stoke pits, a possible drying trench and a waster dump, all "probably associated with workshop/storage structures" (**CONFIRMED** P. A. Yeoman, *Records of Buckinghamshire* 30 (1988) pp. 123–155, http://www.bucksas.org.uk/rob/Rob_30_0_123.pdf, fetched 2026-08-23). Brill is a hilltop village whose potters worked within the village; the kiln lay on a plot beside the street. At Potterspury (Northants) a late-14th/early-15th-century single-flue updraught kiln lay on the plot at 75 High Street, "built over by a building soon after production had ceased" (**CONFIRMED** Northants HER MNN20085, https://her.northamptonshire.gov.uk/Monument/MNN20085, fetched 2026-08-23, citing Mynard 1972) — village potters fired at the tail or side of their house plots. Laverstock, outside Salisbury: trial trenches over 1.5 acres yielded nine kilns, two buildings (one a potters' workshop), and eighteen pits, 12th–13th century (**CONFIRMED-digest**, Musty, Algar & Ewence, *Archaeologia* 102 (1969); the ADS record returned 403/bot-check). Continental: Siegburg's potters' settlement, the Aulgasse, lay "immediately before the protective city wall" with clay and firewood nearby and the navigable Sieg; production from c.1400, guild before 1429, masters limited to nine to sixteen firings a year between Ash Wednesday and Martinmas, winter firing forbidden (**CONFIRMED** https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siegburger_Steinzeug, fetched 2026-08-23). Raeren had c. 50 master potters in the second half of the 16th century (**CONFIRMED** https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raerener_Steinzeug, fetched 2026-08-23). Structural imperatives: fuel (wood, furze) in bulk; water; a clay pit within cart reach; smoke and fire risk → village edge, suburb, or forest; the wheel shed wants daylight; drying needs shelter. Live/work: the potter lives in his house on the same plot; the kiln is 10–30 m from the dwelling. Prosperity/wear: floor = a single clamp/updraught kiln 1–1.5 m, wheel under the house eaves; mid = a 2–2.5 m pedestal kiln with stone-lined stokehole and a wheel shed; ceiling = several kilns, a drying kiln, tile production added (Brill), a guild-regulated firing calendar (Siegburg). Decline sheds the second kiln and the drying shed first; the last thing standing is a kiln base under a later house.

*Brickmaker.* No building at all at floor grade: clay pit (the "brick-hole"), weathering/tempering heap, pug (trodden by men or animals before the horse pug-mill), a **moulding bench under a thatched shed** (the only roof), **hacks** (long low drying rows under straw or board caps), and the **clamp** — green bricks stacked into their own kiln with fuel channels, turfed over, fired for days to weeks. Clamps in Clare/Limerick: "an average sized example might have been stacked up to 5m high and produced 20,000 to 30,000 bricks"; the best-preserved had six benches "4.5m to 4.7m long, 0.35m to 0.45m wide and two or three bricks high"; another clamp "approximately 7m by 9m", rows 0.42–0.46 m wide spaced 0.6–0.7 m apart (**CONFIRMED** G. Hull, *Brick kilns in Counties Limerick and Clare*, unpublished article PDF, https://www.limerick.ie/sites/default/files/atoms/files/brick_kilns_in_counties_limerick_and_clare_by_graham_h.pdf, fetched 2026-08-23 — these are post-medieval Irish examples; qualifier preserved). The Low Countries veldoven: c. 50,000 bricks per firing, "ongeveer drie tot vier weken" per cycle, about five firings April–November, used "from the Middle Ages through the 20th century" (**CONFIRMED** https://nl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veldoven, fetched 2026-08-23). The German Feldbrandofen fired at 700–800 °C, sealed with clay and earth (**CONFIRMED** https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziegelei, fetched 2026-08-23). Medieval English: two clamps less than 400 m from Leiston Abbey, Suffolk, tied to the abbey's c.1365 relocation or post-1380 repairs (**CONFIRMED** https://oxfordcotswoldarchaeology.org.uk/news/brick-clamps-and-kilns/, fetched 2026-08-23); Boston, Lincs: a coal-fired kiln of 1400–1450 "with an associated workshop and yard" (Mayes 1965) and a York Street tile kiln with triple parallel flues of 1270–1350; Danbury, Essex tile factory 1275–1335 with "workshops, drying sheds, possible accommodation for workers, and two well-preserved tile kilns" (**CONFIRMED-digest**, search digest of Lincolnshire HER / Danbury PMA 1975). Siting law: the works go to the clay, beside the building they serve (abbey, castle, town wall), and are often temporary; the permanent "Scotch" updraught kiln with permanent walls is a late-medieval/early-modern upgrade (Boston 1400–1450 is the early end).

*Glassblower (town) / Glassmakers (city).* The English medieval glasshouse is a **forest** establishment: Chiddingfold made window glass "in the 1350s for St Stephen's Chapel, Westminster and St George's Chapel, Windsor" (**CONFIRMED** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiddingfold, fetched 2026-08-23). The forest-glass furnace (Theophilus; the c.1380 Bohemian Mandeville miniature) put "all the high temperature processes ... in the one structure containing several ovens", housed "in a wooden building" with "wood ... stored and dried above the furnace", batch mixed "at a pit nearby"; fritting c. 900 °C, melting up to 1400 °C in crucibles, annealing in a separate oven; "a large glasshouse typically might use 67 tonnes of wood a week operating for 40 weeks a year" (**CONFIRMED** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_glass, fetched 2026-08-23). Bishop's Wood, Eccleshall (Staffs), scheduled 1006087: "a rectangular glasshouse ... built from sandstone and glacial erratic boulders on a raised plinth", furnace with central flue and four crucibles and an adjacent working area, excavated 1931–32, worked 1580–1615 by glassworkers Bishop Overton brought from Hampshire, ending with the 1615 legislation forbidding wood fuel (**CONFIRMED** Historic England list entry, read in browser 2026-08-23). Continental Waldglas: three-storey round furnaces "3 m Durchmesser und bis zu 3 m Höhe", firing chamber below, melting with crucibles in the middle, annealing above at c. 400 °C; c. 1 Raummeter of wood per kg of glass, 2,000–3,000 Festmeter a year; Spessart Zunftordnung of 1406; forest glasshouses were mobile and "ab dem 17. Jahrhundert wurden die Glashütten sesshaft" (**CONFIRMED** https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waldglas and https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glash%C3%BCtte, fetched 2026-08-23). Venice confined its furnaces to Murano by law of 8 November 1291 because they were "a fire hazard, especially in cities with wooden structures nearby" (**CONFIRMED** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murano_glass, fetched 2026-08-23). Program: one FURNACE HALL (timber shed, open sides or high louvred roof; furnace central; sieges; working holes on two or more sides), ANNEALING/FRITTING ovens (wings of the furnace or separate small ovens), BATCH/POTASH STORE, POT (crucible) STORE and clay preparation, FUEL STACK (enormous; above the furnace in the Mandeville image), sand/ash pits. Live/work: the forest crew lodged in cabins at the site (the Hüttenmeister's house at the continental ceiling). The English cone is a late-17th-century coal-era form (Bridgwater's Chandos cone is 1720s) — **PLAUSIBLE**, supported by the HE entry's "hitherto conical structures over circular furnaces" as the coal-era norm. Urban English glasshouses begin with the 1567 Lorraine/Carré immigration and London's Crutched Friars (1570s, Verzelini) — **PLAUSIBLE** (not fetched; the HE entry independently confirms the wood-to-coal shift "from about 1610").

**(b) Measured.**
- Brill kiln 2 (early 16th c.): rectangular pit 3 m N–S × 4.4 m × ≥ 0.5 m deep; west wall just over 2.5 m; bricks 45 × 100 × 220 mm; south wall 5 courses to 0.34 m. Kiln 1 flue mouth max 1.4 m wide, stoke-hole 0.32 m wide, 0.08 m ash deposit, stoke pit 0.37 m deep, possible arched flue "over one metre" internal height. Waster dump 1.5 m diameter, 9 m W of kiln 1; pits 0.45–1.6 m deep; drying/drain trench average 1 m wide, 0.5–1.4 m deep; clamp base A 2.4 × 1.8 m, B 2 × 2.6 m — **CONFIRMED** (Yeoman 1988).
- Potterspury 75 High Street: circular oven 2.52 m diameter; stokehole 3.18 × 1.644 m widening to 2.256 m; late 14th–early 15th c. (archaeomagnetic) — **CONFIRMED** (Northants HER MNN20085). Other Potterspury ovens 2.44 × 1.83 m (tile pedestal) and 1.10 × 1.65 m (oval); Yardley Gobion kilns 2.8 × 1.7 m and 1.6 × 1.0 m sharing a 4.7 × 2.2 m stokehole; Lyveden possible clamp c. 9 ft × 7 ft, 13th–14th c. — **CONFIRMED-digest** (Northants HER digests).
- Laverstock: 9 kilns, 2 buildings, 18 pits over 1.5 acres — **CONFIRMED-digest**. Laverstock kiln stand 146 mm diameter (kiln furniture, not kiln) — **CONFIRMED** (PSAS Rattray paper PDF, https://journals.socantscot.org/index.php/psas/article/download/8984/8952, fetched 2026-08-23).
- Heath End, Leics 18th-c. kiln internal diameter 6 ft (1.83 m) — **CONFIRMED-digest**.
- Siegburg firing calendar: 9–16 kilns (firings) per master per year — **CONFIRMED**.
- Brick clamps: benches 4.5–4.7 m × 0.35–0.45 m; clamp c. 7 × 9 m; rows 0.42–0.46 m at 0.6–0.7 m spacing; height to 5 m; 20,000–30,000 bricks — **CONFIRMED** (Hull, post-medieval Ireland). Veldoven c. 50,000 bricks, 3–4 weeks, c. 5 firings/season — **CONFIRMED**. Feldbrandofen 700–800 °C — **CONFIRMED**. Leiston clamps < 400 m from the abbey — **CONFIRMED**.
- Waldglas furnace 3 m diameter × up to 3 m high; 1 Raummeter wood/kg; 2,000–3,000 Festmeter/yr — **CONFIRMED**. Forest glasshouse 67 tonnes wood/week × 40 weeks — **CONFIRMED**. Bishop's Wood: 4 crucibles, central flue (no dimensions in the entry) — **CONFIRMED**.
- Knightons, Alfold (1550s) and Blunden's Wood (c.1330): no measured figures obtained this session (searched: Knightons furnace dimensions — search budget refused; HE list search for "Knightons glasshouse"/"glasshouse Alfold" returned no scheduled entry; Surrey HER site returned null). Training recollection puts the main Wealden furnaces in the 2.5–4 m long bucket with wing ovens — **PLAUSIBLE** range only.
- No measured figures found for a medieval moulding shed or hack length (searched: brick kiln excavation dimensions moulding shed hacks; Ziegelei).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**
- Urban potters are the exception: Siegburg's potters sat outside the wall; Laverstock outside Salisbury; Brill and Potterspury are villages. Negation result: the search on kiln dimensions surfaced Potterspury "High Street" — a kiln ON a village street plot, which cuts against "always at the edge"; the village street-plot tail is a licensed position, the walled-town interior is not (**CONFIRMED**).
- Kiln roofing: Brill kiln 2 had "no permanent roof" (**CONFIRMED**); the "kiln shed" is a fixture of prosperity, not a default.
- Clamp vs kiln: the brickmaker's "kiln" at floor grade is the clamp itself, which leaves no building; the permanent updraught brick kiln (Boston 1400–1450, coal-fired) is the ceiling and early for England (**CONFIRMED-digest**).
- The Glassblower (town) entry is an anachronism in the English register before 1567 and ambiguous until c.1610–1615 when coal moved the trade into towns (**CONFIRMED** HE: "From about 1610, production shifted to the coalfields"; 1615 wood ban); continental Hessian/Bohemian/Spessart forest glass is earlier (1406 Zunftordnung) and the Venetian urban glasshouse is a 1291 island quarantine — urban in the sense of "inside the city's jurisdiction", not "inside the city". The city "Glassmakers (luxury)" entry is sound only as Murano (Italy) or post-1570 London; in a 13th–15th-century English city it is a counterfactual.
- The English glass cone is late 17th century; any "cone" silhouette before c.1690 is wrong.
- Danbury's "possible accommodation for workers" and the Waldglas crew hierarchy support live-at-the-works for tile/glass; the potter lives at home.
- Null results: no source describing a medieval potter selling from a shop on the works (pottery went to market/fair — the catalog's "trade" tag is a market pull, not a shopfront).

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**
- Potter: dwelling = hall-house `CROSS_PASSAGE`; the works has NO interior circulation — wheel shed is a one-cell open-fronted shed (`THROUGH_ROOM` degenerate); kiln yard is open ground with a stokehole pit 3–4.7 m long that is a fixture. Ladder to a drying loft at ceiling grade only.
- Brickmaker: none — moulding shed is a roof on posts; hacks are open rows; clamp is a stack. Living in a hut at the pit (seasonal) or in the village.
- Glassblower/Glassmakers: FURNACE HALL = a single large `THROUGH_ROOM` (timber, open sides at floor grade; stone-walled rectangular house on a plinth at Bishop's Wood grade) with the furnace central and working positions around it; continental three-storey round furnace implies a hall c. 8–12 m square (**PLAUSIBLE**); annealing/fritting ovens in wings reached from the same floor; no stair (the "upper storey" is the furnace's own annealing chamber). Potash house and pot house are separate sheds. At Murano/Venetian ceiling, the fornace sits on a canal-side plot with a warehouse front (`STORE`) — **PLAUSIBLE**.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**
- Potter: clay `STORE` = outdoor weathering heap (fixture, no light, adjacency pit + wheel shed); drying shelves = FIXTURE of the wheel shed (`rack`); fired-ware `STORE` (dry shed, NONE light) at mid grade; waster dump = landscape fixture; fuel stack = outdoor `STORE` kept 10 m+ from the kiln.
- Brickmaker: hacks = outdoor drying `STORE` rows (capped), 10–20 m long bucket (**PLAUSIBLE**); fuel stack; no cells.
- Glass: batch/potash `STORE` (dry, NONE light, adjacency furnace hall, separated for fire); pot/crucible `STORE` with clay tempering floor; fuel `STORE` = the largest element, dried above the furnace in the Mandeville image (a `GARRET_STORE` over the hall — licensed by that source); finished-glass `STORE` with straw crates at the urban ceiling; cullet bin = fixture.

**(f) Typed proposal.**
- `POTTERY_YARD` — functions: `clay_pit` + `weathering_heap` → `wheel_shed` → `updraught_kiln` (single-flue, 1–1.5 m; wear up to 2.5 m pedestal with lined stokehole) → `second_kiln` + `drying_feature` + `kiln_roof` (ceiling). fixtures: `kiln`(HOME: ROOM_KINDS.kiln — note the engine treats kiln as a ROOM; here it is an outdoor structure 1.1–3 × 1.65–4.4 m with a 3–4.7 m stokehole), `wheel`(new), `rack`(drying), `stoke_pit`(new). Buckets: `fireTrade`(major, open-air), `smoke`, `waterNeed`, `extraMural OR village_plot_tail`. Licensing: village (L646) at plot tail or village edge; town only as suburb; city never inside the wall before 1600. Era: pedestal/multi-flue kilns from 13th c.; brick-built kilns 15th–16th c.
- `BRICK_CLAMP_YARD` — functions: `brick_hole` → `pug_patch` → `moulding_shed`(posts + thatch, bench 2–3 m long, **PLAUSIBLE**) → `hacks` → `clamp`(7 × 9 m bucket, to 5 m high) → `permanent_kiln`(ceiling, era ≥ 1400, coal). fixtures: `workbench`(moulding), `rack`(hacks), `clamp`(new), `kiln`. Buckets: `fireTrade`(episodic), `temporary`, `nearClay`, `nearClient`. Licensing: village L653 (requires clay deposit flag — the catalog says so); typically seasonal Apr–Nov. HOME: kiln, workfloor (moulding shed); NO TYPED HOME for hacks.
- `FOREST_GLASSHOUSE` (Glassblower L1198 re-sited) — functions: `furnace_hall`(timber, open-sided) with `working_furnace`(3 m diameter round, continental; rectangular English with central flue and 4–6 crucibles) → `annealing_oven` + `fritting_oven` → `potash_house` + `pot_house` → `stone_glasshouse_on_plinth`(Bishop's Wood grade) → `coal_furnace + cone`(era ≥ 1615/1690). fixtures: `furnace`(new; not `hearth`), `siege`(new), `crucible_rack`, `cauldron`(potash), `rack`(blowing irons). Buckets: `fireTrade`(extreme), `fuelHunger`(67 t/week), `forestSited`, `mobile`(pre-17th c.). Licensing: NOT a town building in the English register before 1567; town from 1567–1615 as immigrant enterprise; coalfield towns after 1615. HOME: kiln (closest), workfloor; NO TYPED HOME for furnace hall as a typed cell.
- `URBAN_GLASSHOUSE` (Glassmakers L1738) — Murano model: canal-side `fornace` hall + warehouse front + workers' lodging on the island; English model only post-1570 (Crutched Friars) and post-1615 coal cones. Adds `strongbox`/`ledger` (luxury trade), `counter`. Licensing: city × era ≥ 1291 (Italy) / ≥ 1570 (England) × siting = quarantined quarter/island.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**
1. ROOM_KINDS.kiln is a room enum, but every excavated medieval kiln is an **outdoor structure in a yard**; the grammar needs a yard-fixture class for kiln/clamp/furnace with its stokehole footprint (the stokehole is as big as the oven) and a 10–30 m separation from the dwelling.
2. A **temporary/episodic** flag for the brickworks (clamp fired, then removed; works moved to the next clay) — nothing in the engine expresses a building that exists for one season.
3. An **era × region gate** on the town Glassblower: refuse English placement before 1567, allow continental forest placement from the 12th century, convert to coal-town form after 1615; the cone is a silhouette gate at ≥ 1690.
4. `fuelHunger` as a siting pull (forest or coalfield) distinct from `fireTrade`; the Waldglas figures (1 Raummeter per kg) make fuel, not clay or sand, the siting variable.
5. The catalog's "trade" tag on Potter/Brickmaker must compile to **market-sold**, not a shop cell; the only shop-fronted member is the luxury city Glassmakers.

**(h) Continental vs English.** The English register is parochial on glass: the continent had a regulated forest-glass industry (Spessart Zunftordnung 1406, Hessian/Bohemian huts, three-storey 3 m furnaces) and an urban-luxury quarantine (Murano 1291) centuries before England's 1567 immigrants and 1615 coal shift; English medieval glass is a Wealden forest sideline for royal chapels. On pottery, the Rhenish stoneware settlements (Siegburg Aulgasse, Raeren's c.50 masters) are guild-regulated extramural potters' suburbs with firing calendars — a mechanism the English village pottery never acquires. On brick, the Low Countries' veldoven (50,000 bricks, five firings a season) and the Hanseatic Backstein towns ran municipal brickyards (Ziegelhöfe) at scale while England's 14th-century clamps served single abbeys and halls; the Dutch/Flemish yard is the prosperity ceiling the English village brickmaker never reaches before 1500.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.
