## §9 · FAMILY G — leather (wet/noxious): Tannery (village L625) · Tanners (town L1112) · Tanner (established) (town L1169)

*Researched by sub-lane GH (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

Catalog text being described: L625 "Converts hides into leather using oak bark. Foul-smelling. Placed downstream."; L1112 "Leather production. MUST be downstream and downwind." (tag `tanneryScale`); L1169 "Full-scale tannery with multiple vats and a guild-trained workforce… shoes, armour, and saddlery" (tag `tanneryScale`).

**(a) Analogue.**

The historical tanyard is not a building but a *yard with buildings at its edges*, and the excavated record is dominated by the pits, because the sheds rarely survive. The program, in process order: (1) a **water frontage** (river, stream, or a purpose-cut canal) for soaking and washing raw hides; (2) the **lime pits / lime house** for de-hairing (smaller, often barrel-lined for small skins); (3) the **beam house** — a roofed shed with a sloped beam over which the limed hide is scraped (scudding/fleshing); (4) the **tan pits** proper (larger, rectangular, timber-lined, later stone/brick), where hides lie in layered bark liquor for months; (5) the **bark store** and, in the post-medieval yard, the **bark mill** (horse-driven edge-runner); (6) the **drying loft** — a ventilated upper storey with louvred or slatted walls; (7) the **house**.

Excavated program, English: at The Green, Northampton, up to three tanneries of c. 1470–1550 and c. 1550–1700, of circular and rectangular pits, the largest (the Western Tannery) 36–37 pits, many with lime-and-ash fills from de-hairing; cattle and horse hides and sheepskins were all processed "despite contemporary prohibitions" — **CONFIRMED** (Shaw 1996, *Post-Medieval Archaeology* 30, abstract read at https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1179/pma.1996.002, fetched 2026-08-23). The adjacent St Peter's Street northern complex (later 16th to mid-17th c.) is the clearest plan of the *shed-plus-yard* parti: a stone wall divided the building into a **roofed area with a clay floor and a stone-lined pit in mid-floor (interpreted as the beam house)** and an **uncovered western area containing the pits**; the HER adds that the small scale "might indicate tawing rather than tanning" — **CONFIRMED** (Northamptonshire HER MNN33286, https://her.northamptonshire.gov.uk/Monument/MNN33286, fetched 2026-08-23). The generic identification criterion in the literature: "large tanning pits of rectangular or rounded shape, lined with wood and, from the 16th century onwards, more commonly with stone or brick," sited by streams for water and at the settlement edge for smell — **CONFIRMED** (Albarella et al., Greenwich High Road horncore paper, White Rose eprint PDF, fetched and text-extracted 2026-08-23, citing Gomersall 2000).

Small-skin vs hide: the Bermondsey excavations found "sunken barrels used for liming smaller hides and large wooden tanks for tanning," horncores used to line pit edges, and drains to nearby ditches — **CONFIRMED** (Bermondsey Street Back Stories no. 12, PDF fetched and extracted 2026-08-23). This is the archaeological face of the **whittawer/tawyer (alum, white leather, small skins) vs tanner (oak bark, cattle hides)** split; curriers (dressing tanned leather with oil/tallow — *dry*, indoor, bench work) were a distinct craft, first recorded as a London association in 1272 — **CONFIRMED-digest** (Wikipedia/Curriers' Company digest). Who is wet: tanner and tawyer (pits, water). Who is dry: currier (shop/bench), cordwainer (shop). The Strasbourg "Maison des Tanneurs" was possibly built by a *corroyeur* (Rothgerber), and the tanners' guild room (poêle) was actually in rue des Drapiers — the house name "Gerwerstub" is modern — **CONFIRMED** (Archi-Wiki, https://www.archi-wiki.org/Adresse:Maison_des_Tanneurs_(Strasbourg), read via browser 2026-08-23).

The drying loft as a structural imperative with exterior consequence: at Strasbourg the 1572 house's larger block has two jettied storeys and its gable wall is "entièrement ajouré" from the first floor up; the second floor formerly carried **open galleries** for drying tanned skins, and the tanners also used ventilated attics — **CONFIRMED** (Archi-Wiki, as above). At Colmar the tanners' houses (17th–18th c.) are tall for their date, with steep gable roofs ventilated through slatted shutters; the attics were **interconnected** and served for drying; a wastewater drain ran along the Rue des Tanneurs; the quarter sits at the city edge on the Lauch — **CONFIRMED** (https://www.erlebe-elsass.de/colmar/gerberviertel/, fetched 2026-08-23); 33 parcels, 27 buildings restored 1968–74 — **CONFIRMED-digest** (Colmar tourism page; the direct fetch 404'd). English survival of the louvred drying house: The Old Tannery, Winsley (Wilts), late 18th/early 19th c., three storeys and attic built into the hillside, "a row of four upper windows in two pairs, possibly originally louvred," part of an extensive tannery with Turleigh Mill — **CONFIRMED** (Historic England 1021910, read via browser 2026-08-23). An 1819 English tanyard sale listed "first and second workhouse, bark chamber over the bark mill, drying chamber, limehouse and tanpits" — **CONFIRMED-digest** (RH7 History Group PDF digest) — which is the late, fully articulated program; for 1200–1700 treat bark mill as *post-1600 and optional* (PLAUSIBLE: hand-pounded or water-milled bark earlier).

Siting law and water: Freiburg cut the Gewerbekanal off the Dreisam *deeper* than the Bächle because the tanners of the Gerberau needed more water — **CONFIRMED-digest**. Fountains Abbey's monastic tannery (12th–13th c. peak, to 1539) sits by the River Skell — **CONFIRMED** (https://www.medievalists.net/2021/10/medieval-tannery-discovered-at-fountains-abbey/, fetched 2026-08-23). Tickhill's 14th–15th c. pits lie on the town's south-western periphery with a probable limekiln — **CONFIRMED** (Burgess & Andrews 2017, *YAJ* 89 abstract, read via browser 2026-08-23).

Prosperity/wear grades (floor → ceiling). Floor (village L625): pits by the stream, barrel-lined or clay-lined (the St Peter's Way 15th-c. pits are 1.4 m wide, barrel-lined), one open shed, drying on poles/in the house loft; the "tannery or skin-dressing workshop" of MNN33286 is this grade. Middle (town L1112): a named yard of 10–40 pits, a roofed beam house, a bark store, hides dried in a loft; tanner's house fronting the lane. Ceiling (L1169): the Colmar/Strasbourg type — street-fronting house with the tanyard behind, two or three storeys of dwelling and **a whole roof storey given to drying**, open galleries, a drain to the street, a bark mill, and a small-skin (barrel) range as well as the hide tanks; at Fountains the monastic ceiling reached two stone buildings 16 m wide, one at least 32 m long, "more than a single storey" (GPR, unexcavated — **CONFIRMED**, medievalists.net as above). What decline sheds first (PLAUSIBLE): the bark mill (revert to hand-pounding), then the dedicated drying loft (revert to the house attic or the yard), then pit lining quality (stone → timber → clay/barrel); the pits themselves are the last to go, which is why they are what archaeology finds.

Live/work: yes — the master lives on the site at every grade; at Colmar/Strasbourg the same building holds dwelling, drying loft, and (by the canal) the wet work — **CONFIRMED** (sources above). Northampton's tanner John Buckby "was of some importance in the town" while his pits sat on derelict ground — **CONFIRMED** (Shaw 1996 abstract) — i.e. the established tanner's house need not be *at* the pits.

**(b) Measured.**

| Figure | Source | Label |
|---|---|---|
| Six circular pits, 15th c.; best-preserved 1.4 m wide × 0.4 m deep; clay lining with wooden (barrel?) lining pressed to it; cow hair and lime | Northants HER MNN19965, fetched 2026-08-23 | CONFIRMED |
| Up to 3 tanneries at The Green + 2 at St Peter's St; largest 36–37 pits; phases c. 1470–1550 and c. 1550–1700 | Shaw 1996 abstract (tandfonline, browser) | CONFIRMED |
| Northern complex (late 16th–mid 17th c.): roofed area with clay floor + stone-lined pit; uncovered pit yard; two pits timber-lined, rest clay-lined; one clay trough, one small stone trough | HER MNN33286 | CONFIRMED |
| Fountains: two stone buildings 16 m wide, one ≥ 32 m long, >1 storey; lined pits and tanks; 12th–13th c. peak; GPR only | medievalists.net 2021 | CONFIRMED (unexcavated) |
| Tanners Hall, Gloucester: 13th-c. merchant house 10.3 × 5.25 m internally, taken by tanners 1540 to 18th c.; 17th–18th-c. rectangular and circular pits | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanners_Hall, fetched 2026-08-23 | CONFIRMED (tertiary) |
| Greenwich: pits dug 1778–1830; horncore-walled drain 5.25 m × 0.62 m | Greenwich PDF | CONFIRMED (18th–19th c.) |
| Coquet St, Newcastle: pits c. 2.35 m square, ≥ 1.44 m deep; puddled-clay bed c. 0.5 m; horizontal tongue-and-groove plank lining; brick floor | https://sitelines.newcastle.gov.uk/index.php/node/53700, fetched 2026-08-23 | CONFIRMED (VICTORIAN — later-date caveat) |
| Bermondsey Long Lane: >300 horncores set in clay in an 18th-c. pit | Bermondsey PDF | CONFIRMED |
| Strasbourg 1572: two timber houses joined at the gable; larger has two jettied storeys; open drying galleries on 2nd floor; ventilated attics | Archi-Wiki (browser) | CONFIRMED |
| Colmar: 33 parcels / 27 buildings restored 1968–74; houses "tall" for 17th–18th c. | Colmar tourism digest; erlebe-elsass | CONFIRMED-digest / CONFIRMED |
| Winsley: 3 storeys + attic; row of 4 upper windows possibly louvred | HE 1021910 (browser) | CONFIRMED (c. 1800) |
| Tickhill: 14th–15th-c. pit group + probable limekiln; five tanners recorded late 14th c. | YAJ 89 abstract (browser) | CONFIRMED |
| Nottingham: rock-cut vats, tannery 1500–1640 in a cave first cut c. 1250; small rectangular clay-lined vats for sheep/goat | search digests | CONFIRMED-digest |

No measured figures found for: medieval **yard areas** (the Northampton report surely has them — the full paper is paywalled; searched: "Northampton The Green pit dimensions", HER records), medieval **timber-lined tan-pit dimensions** (only the Victorian Coquet St set and the 15th-c. barrel pits), **bark-store/bark-mill footprint**, **beam dimensions**, **loft storey counts** beyond "attic(s)/second-floor galleries" (Strasbourg), and **York Tanner Row/Skeldergate pit sizes** (Skeldergate 11th–12th-c. pits are cited via Addyman 1984 in the Greenwich paper — CONFIRMED as a citation, not as a figure).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**

The "banished outside the walls" law is weaker than the catalog's "MUST be downstream and downwind" implies. Evidence *for* in-town tanning: Northampton's St Peter's Way record states the "area inside defences given over to tanning in late C13th" — **CONFIRMED** (HER MNN19965); Norwich's late-13th-c. tannery was "within the city walls," serving vellum for two friaries — **CONFIRMED** (https://archaeology.org/news/2015/01/07/150107-england-norwich-tannery/, fetched 2026-08-23); Gloucester's tanners took over a 13th-c. merchant's town house in 1540 — **CONFIRMED** (Tanners Hall); Nottingham's tannery was *underground*, in the Broadmarsh caves (1500–1640) — **CONFIRMED-digest**; York's Tanner Row ("Barker Rawe" by 1524) and Skeldergate 11th–12th-c. pits are inside the walls — **CONFIRMED-digest** / **CONFIRMED** (citation in Greenwich PDF). The Strasbourg and Colmar quarters are *inside* the ring, at its watery edge, not outside it. Conversely, Tickhill's pits are on the periphery and Fountains' tannery is by the river below the precinct.

Negation search result (ordinances): I searched for an actual dated English ordinance *expelling* tanners ("Leet Book OR Liber Albus OR York Memorandum Book tanners ordinance"; "tanners ordinance banished outside town walls"; "medieval tanners within the walls … not banished"). **Null for tanners specifically.** The Coventry Mayor's Proclamation of 1421 forbids slaughter within the walls and dung in the Sherbourne and *does not mention tanners* — **CONFIRMED** (https://www.environmentandsociety.org/arcadia/city-sanitation-regulations-coventry-mayors-proclamation-1421, fetched 2026-08-23). York 1371 concerns butchers — **CONFIRMED-digest**. The one tanner-specific complaint surfaced is a digest line that "John de Bristoll, tanner, had erected two tanneries and a latrine, contaminating the water" (undated, unlocated in the digest) — **CONFIRMED-digest, single-sourced**. Conclusion for the grammar: the smell/water law is a *siting pull toward water and toward the edge*, not a prohibition; the engine should not hard-fail a town tanner inside the walls. Also contested: Shaw's "primitive form of tanning" and the HER's "tawing rather than tanning" show that pit groups are not reliably tanneries (skin-dressing, dyeing, and tanning pits overlap archaeologically — the Skeldergate pits "may have been associated with tanning or textile dyeing" — **CONFIRMED-digest**). The multi-storey open-loft house is a **16th–18th-c. continental survival**; no English medieval tanner's house with a purpose-built louvred loft was found (Winsley is c. 1800).

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**

- L625 Tannery (village): no interior circulation class — the yard *is* the circulation; one open-sided shed (`THROUGH_ROOM` degenerate: single cell). Stair: none or **ladder** to a house loft used for drying. Licensed by the St Peter's Way barrel-pit grade and MNN33286's shed-plus-yard.
- L1112 Tanners (town): house on the lane + yard behind: house carries the ordinary `CROSS_PASSAGE` (front door to yard door is the hide route — PLAUSIBLE, no measured example); beam house and bark store are `THROUGH_ROOM` sheds opening on the yard. Stair: **winder** to the loft (PLAUSIBLE, vernacular norm).
- L1169 Tanner (established): the Strasbourg/Colmar type adds `GALLERY` — external open timber galleries on an upper floor used as drying decks (**CONFIRMED**, Archi-Wiki; 1572) and interconnected attics (**CONFIRMED**, Colmar) — i.e. the `GALLERY` class licensed here is a *drying* gallery, not a circulation luxury; a `STAIR_HALL` is not licensed at any grade (PLAUSIBLE). Stair: winder → straight flight in the stone ground storey (Strasbourg houses have "massive stone ground floors" — CONFIRMED-digest). Measured widths/lengths: none found.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**

- `STORE` (bark store): every grade; dry, any light, adjacent to the pits; at L1169 it becomes a "bark chamber over the bark mill" (CONFIRMED-digest, 1819 — late). Fixture, not cell, at L625 (a bark stack under a lean-to).
- `ATTIC`/`GARRET_STORE` → re-typed as **DRYING LOFT**: lightReq ANY but *ventilation* required (louvres/slats/open gallery); size bucket = whole roof storey at L1169 (CONFIRMED, Strasbourg/Colmar); adjacency: over the dwelling, not over the pits.
- `CELLAR`/`UNDERCROFT`: not characteristic, except the Nottingham cave tannery where the *whole works* is subterranean (CONFIRMED-digest) — a bounded possibility, not a prior.
- Lime house: a `STORE` variant at L1112/L1169 (CONFIRMED-digest, 1819 list); at L625 lime is a fixture (a covered barrel).
- No `PANTRY`/`BUTTERY`/`LARDER` beyond the dwelling's own.
- Fixtures vs cells: pits and barrels are **fixtures of the yard**; the beam is a fixture of the beam house; the horse-walk of the bark mill is a fixture that forces a curved or square room (CONFIRMED-digest, US bark-mill house with curved walls for the horse path — late and American; treat as PLAUSIBLE for Europe).

**(f) Typed proposal.**

`parti`: `TANYARD_PITS_BY_STREAM` (L625) · `TANYARD_HOUSE_FRONT_PITS_BEHIND` (L1112) · `TANNER_HOUSE_DRYING_LOFT` (L1169; Colmar/Strasbourg; era ≥ 1550 continental, ≥ 1650 English as PLAUSIBLE) · `CAVE_TANNERY` (bounded possibility; rock-cut sites only).
`functions[]`: floor = {soak/wash at water, lime, beam/scud, tan, dry, dwell}; ladder rungs = {+bark store → +lime house → +roofed beam house → +drying loft → +open gallery deck → +bark mill + horse walk → +small-skin barrel range → +drain to street/ditch}; ceiling = {stone ground storey, two jettied storeys, full roof storey drying with interconnected attics, guild-trained workforce}.
`fixtures[]`: tan pit (timber-lined; stone/brick ≥ 1550 CONFIRMED) · lime barrel/pit (small-skin) · beam · bark stack · bark mill edge-runner (≥ 1600, PLAUSIBLE) · horncore kerb/floor (prosperity-neutral waste reuse, CONFIRMED Bermondsey) · drying poles/louvres · drain. Wear: pit lining degrades stone → timber → clay.
Structural buckets: `wetTrade`, `noxious`, `waterAdjacent` (river/stream/canal; Freiburg shows a *purpose-cut* canal), `ventilatedLoft` (new), `openYardProgram` (new: the parti is yard-dominant; built area < pit area), `heavyFloor` NO, `fireTrade` NO (limekiln optional at L1112+, CONFIRMED Tickhill).
Licensing: L625 × any prosperity × any era × stream-adjacent; L1112 × ≥ modest × any era × water-adjacent, edge-pull soft, inside-walls allowed; L1169 × ≥ prosperous × era ≥ 1500 × water frontage or canal; `tanneryScale` tag on L1112/L1169 maps to pit count bucket (≤ 10 / 10–40 / > 40, with 36–37 the only CONFIRMED upper figure for a single yard).
HOME: the beam house and bark store = ROOM_KINDS.workfloor / ROOM_KINDS.store; dwelling = hall/chamber; **drying loft = NO TYPED HOME** (attic-as-process-space is absent from ROOM_KINDS); pits = FURNISHING_KINDS.barrel is the nearest (a *sunken barrel* is literally CONFIRMED at Bermondsey), but a lined pit is **NO TYPED HOME**.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**

The engine must express (1) a **yard-dominant parti** where the open yard carries typed fixtures (pits) and the buildings are subordinate sheds; (2) a **ventilated roof storey** as a process room with exterior consequence (louvres, open gallery, extra height — the Colmar houses are "tall for their date"); (3) a **soft edge/water pull**, not a hard "MUST be downstream" — the catalog's L1112 text overstates the law and should become a siting weight; (4) the **tanner/tawyer/currier split** as three fixture sets (hide tanks; small-skin barrels; dry bench) rather than one "tannery." The finding lands on the existing `tanneryScale` tag (re-type as pit-count bucket) and on the §452 `GALLERY` class (licence a *drying* gallery sub-use).

**(h) Continental vs English.** The English register is parochial in treating the tannery as a pit field with sheds; the Alsatian/German *Gerberhaus* integrates dwelling, canal frontage, and a multi-storey drying loft into one tall house on a purpose-cut *Gewerbekanal* (Freiburg, Strasbourg, Colmar), and guild identity sits in a separate *Zunftstube* (the Strasbourg tanners' poêle in rue des Drapiers). In Italy the *conciatori* quarters (Florence's Via dei Conciatori by the Arno) follow the same river-edge logic (PLAUSIBLE; not verified this session). In the Ottoman register the *debbağhane* was a walled compound outside the city proper (Istanbul's Yedikule tanneries — PLAUSIBLE, unsearched), which is the one register where the banishment law is close to absolute.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.

---

**Parent-lane review note.** The negation result matters for the catalog: no dated English ordinance EXPELLING tanners was found in three rounds (Coventry 1421 is about butchers); the catalog's L1112 "MUST be downstream and downwind" is therefore a siting WEIGHT, not a hard rule, and the engine should not hard-fail a town tanner inside the walls (York Tanner Row, Norwich, Gloucester are all intramural).

---

## §10 · FAMILY H — cloth: Weavers/Textile workers (town L1105) · Fuller (village L632) · Dyer (village L639)

*Researched by sub-lane GH (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

Catalog text being described: L1105 "Cloth production. Often guild-organized."; L632 "Finishes woven cloth by cleaning and thickening it. Often water-powered."; L639 "Applies colour to raw or woven cloth."

**(a) Analogue.**

*Weaver (L1105).* In 1200–1700 the loom stands **in the house** — in the hall or a ground-floor shop — not in a purpose-built loomshop. The dedicated loomshop with the long "weaver's window" is an 18th-century form: Spitalfields houses of the 1720s–40s have "mansard garrets" and "weather-boarded fronts containing wide weavers' windows," and at 14 Fournier Street (1743) silk waste was packed between the joists "to deaden the sound of looms in the garret storey" — **CONFIRMED** (Survey of London vol. 27, https://www.british-history.ac.uk/survey-london/vol27/pp199-225, fetched 2026-08-23). The Kilbarchan Weaver's Cottage (1723) has its loomshop on the lower ground floor — **CONFIRMED** (https://www.aocarchaeology.com/key-projects/weavers-cottage, fetched 2026-08-23). Most extant weavers' cottages are late 18th–early 19th c.; loomshops are top-floor/garret for wool and silk, cellar/ground floor for Lancashire cotton (humidity), with "a long window of up to ten lights" and typically three or four looms per shop — **CONFIRMED** (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weavers%27_cottage, fetched 2026-08-23). For the medieval town weaver the analogue is the **hall house or shop-house with the loom in the principal ground-floor room**, lit by the street window: Salisbury in 1421 had 81 master weavers with 207 *valetti* (journeymen), and the weaver William Woderove (d. 1405) owned his home in Endless Street plus shops elsewhere — **CONFIRMED** (VCH Wiltshire vol. 4, https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/wilts/vol4/pp115-147, fetched 2026-08-23). The broadloom is a two-man loom ("double looms worked by two weavers seated side by side" — CONFIRMED, VCH Wilts), so its footprint governs the room.

*Clothier's house as merchant-manufacturer (the ceiling of L1105).* The putting-out system means no factory: the clothier's house is a hall house with a wool store, a shop, and tenters in the close. Margery Haynes of Castle Combe by 1454 held "a grain-mill, a fulling-mill, two houses which she herself had built, a shop to which a stall had been added"; Thomas Bailey of Trowbridge (d. 1543) had "four [fulling mills] under one roof" at Stowford — **CONFIRMED** (VCH Wilts). Little Hall, Lavenham, built in the 1390s for the Causton clothiers, enlarged 1425–50, with a Tudor inserted floor and fireplace — **CONFIRMED** (https://www.littlehall.org.uk/introduction/, fetched 2026-08-23) — is a domestic hall house; its site gives no wool-loft evidence (the cloth rooms are PLAUSIBLE). Strangers' Hall, Norwich: undercroft c. 1320 (three bays), first-floor hall 10.3 × 6 m, courtyard house of merchant-mayors; Thomas Sotherton (mayor 1565) obtained the licence for the Dutch/Walloon "Strangers" and may have lodged some, but **no weaving rooms are documented** — **CONFIRMED** (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strangers%27_Hall, fetched 2026-08-23). So the "strangers' halls" are merchant houses, not weaving houses.

*Fuller (L632).* Two analogues, and the catalog's "often water-powered" is right for the *mill* but not for the *village fuller*. (i) The **fulling mill**: earliest English record at Stanley Abbey (Wilts), 1189, "molendinum monachorum fullericum"; Marlborough's royal mill 1215, rebuilt 1237 with "flagella et baterella" (beaters/hammers); by 1454 at least five mills on a three-mile stretch of the Box Brook at Castle Combe; Heytesbury's new mill of 1421–22 bought **2,500 tenterhooks** — **CONFIRMED** (VCH Wilts). A fulling mill is a conventional watermill in which stocks (paired wooden hammers off a tappet wheel; "falling" vertical stocks or "driving" pivoted stocks) replace the stones — **CONFIRMED** (Jaggers, Welsh woollen industry PDF, fetched and extracted 2026-08-23; cross-reference to the MILLS family). Barrowburn (Northumberland) is a 13th-c. excavated fulling mill with wheel pit and river-bed timber — **CONFIRMED** (https://co-curate.ncl.ac.uk/resources/view/104799, fetched 2026-08-23). The fulling mill "was a separate entity" long before the integrated 19th-c. woollen mill — **CONFIRMED** (Jaggers PDF). (ii) The **walker's trough** (foot fulling): "old-fashioned foot-fulling continued, particularly in the boroughs, which clung conservatively to old-established customs" — **CONFIRMED** (VCH Wilts); manual fulling persisted in Scotland into the 1700s ("waulking") — **CONFIRMED** (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fulling, fetched 2026-08-23). The Welsh *pandy* sat "close to the manorial corn-mill" and farm families sold the finished goods "at the local market" — **CONFIRMED** (Jaggers PDF). The fuller's room program: a trough/stocks room (wet), a fuller's-earth/urine store, a press and shears (the Salisbury tucker William Hanleygh had "a press, a pair of shears, and twelve pairs of … hand-frames for setting the teasles" — **CONFIRMED**, VCH Wilts), and **tenters outside**: Salisbury 1350 "rekkis ibidem constructis"; Trowbridge, a strip 26 perches × 6 ft rented "pro uno tentorio superponendo" — **CONFIRMED** (VCH Wilts).

*Dyer (L639).* The dye house is a **furnace room**: Marlborough c. 1460, a dyer's house lease requiring "a furnace and vats provided"; John Wyke (1460) left "one 'great furnace' in his dye-house by the west mill"; William Webbe (1553) "dye howse by the water lane" — **CONFIRMED** (VCH Wilts). A late-medieval Norwich dyehouse (Norfolk Archaeology 1973) had drainage, furnaces, and large leaden vessels heated over the furnace; early-modern coppers of 60–70 gallons set in a brick furnace — **CONFIRMED-digest** (elizabethancostume.net digest; the page itself failed on a certificate error). Woad, madder, alum and oil moved by cart from Southampton to Salisbury, Warminster, Devizes and Bradford — **CONFIRMED** (VCH Wilts). Siting: by water and (PLAUSIBLE) downstream; the Skeldergate (York) timber- and clay-lined pits "may have been associated with tanning or textile dyeing" — **CONFIRMED-digest** — so dyer and tanner pits are archaeologically confusable, and the engine can share the pit fixture.

*Drying as an exterior fixture.* The tenter ground is the cloth trade's outdoor room: Spitalfields' 17th-c. tenterground was about 150 yards square, bordered by weavers' houses — **CONFIRMED** (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenterground, fetched 2026-08-23); Castle Combe's "Rack Hill" and "Tenterfield" (1454) — **CONFIRMED** (VCH Wilts). The Florentine *tiratoio* is the roofed version: two-level buildings with a masonry ground floor (spinning, dyeing, weaving) and an **upper storey entirely of timber** used for stretching and drying; six listed in the Arte della Lana's 1498 inventory — **CONFIRMED** (https://www.florencecity.it/i-tiratoi-a-firenze/, fetched 2026-08-23); the Tiratoio delle Grazie had "alti palchi in legno" and a roofed pier along the Arno, demolished c. 1860; the Pergola was destroyed c. 1650 — **CONFIRMED** (it.wikipedia pages, fetched 2026-08-23).

Prosperity/wear grades. Weaver: floor = one narrow loom in the hall; rung = broadloom + journeymen (two-man loom); ceiling = clothier's hall house with shop + stall, wool store, dye house and fulling mill *owned but separate* (Haynes, Bailey — CONFIRMED). Decline sheds the journeymen first, then the broadloom for a narrow loom (PLAUSIBLE). Fuller: floor = walker's trough in a shed; rung = water stocks in a mill; ceiling = "four fulling mills under one roof" (CONFIRMED, 1543) with a rack close. Dyer: floor = a single vat and a hearth in the yard; rung = a furnace with a lead/copper and several wooden vats; ceiling = "dye howse by the water lane with all tenementes" (CONFIRMED, 1553). Live/work: weaver yes (loom in the dwelling); dyer yes (dyer's *house* leased with furnace, CONFIRMED); fuller at the mill — the miller-fuller lives at the mill (PLAUSIBLE), the village walker in an ordinary house.

**(b) Measured.**

| Figure | Source | Label |
|---|---|---|

| Salisbury 1412: ray cloths 28 yd × 6 quarters (1.37 m) | VCH Wilts | CONFIRMED |
| Two weavers side by side on the double loom | VCH Wilts | CONFIRMED (loom width itself NOT measured: PLAUSIBLE 2.3–3.0 m overall for a 190-cm web) |
| Salisbury 1421: 81 master weavers, 207 valetti (≈ 2.6 journeymen per master) | VCH Wilts | CONFIRMED |
| Heytesbury mill 1421–22: 2,500 tenterhooks bought | VCH Wilts | CONFIRMED |
| Trowbridge tenter strip: 26 perches (≈ 131 m) × 6 ft (≈ 1.8 m) | VCH Wilts | CONFIRMED (14th c.) |

| Stanley Abbey fulling mill 1189; Marlborough 1215/1237; Downton 1215 | VCH Wilts | CONFIRMED |
| Coppers 60–70 gallons in a brick furnace (early-modern dyehouse advice) | digest | CONFIRMED-digest |
| Strangers' Hall hall 10.3 × 6 m; undercroft c. 1320, 3 bays | Wikipedia | CONFIRMED (tertiary) |
| Weavers' window "up to ten lights"; 3–4 looms per loomshop | Wikipedia Weavers' cottage | CONFIRMED (18th–19th c.) |
| Macclesfield: > 600 weavers with looms at home, 1825 | same | CONFIRMED (19th c.) |
| Esgair Moel mill c. 1760: internal wheel on ground floor beside the stocks; two handlooms upstairs; tenter frame outside | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esgair_Moel and museum.wales, fetched 2026-08-23; Jaggers PDF | CONFIRMED (18th c.) |

No measured figures found for: **medieval loom footprint** (searched: "broadloom width metres medieval horizontal treadle loom size"); **loomshop window widths** (only light counts); **fulling-stock footprint** (searched "fulling stocks dimensions"; milldrawings.com failed on certificate); **dye-vat counts and diameters** per dye house (Norwich 1973 report not reachable; Marlborough lease gives "vats," uncounted); **tenter-frame lengths** other than the Trowbridge strip; **tiratoio dimensions** (it.wikipedia gives none).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**

The loomshop-with-long-window is **not** medieval: every dated example found is 1720 or later (Kilbarchan 1723, Fournier Street 1720s–40s, Lancashire cottages late 18th–19th c.) — **CONFIRMED** as above; the medieval weaver's loom sits in a multi-purpose room. The "strangers' halls" of Norwich are merchant houses with no documented weaving space — **CONFIRMED**. "Fuller = water mill" fails at village tier: foot-fulling survived *in the boroughs* (VCH Wilts) and in Scotland into the 1700s; the village walker is a trough, and the mill is a **mill-family** building (cross-referenced). Negation search ("sold at market, not shop"): the Welsh upland evidence has farm families selling cloth "at the local market" with the only fixed plant being the pandy — **CONFIRMED** (Jaggers PDF) — supporting a *no-shop* weaver at village/hamlet tier; the Salisbury weaver with "shops elsewhere" (1405) is the town counter-case. The tiratoio is a **guild-owned communal** drying building (1498 inventory, six of them), which has no English equivalent: English tenters are private fixtures in closes — so an engine "drying shed" must be licensed by register. Date disputes: the Domesday 1086 Temple Guiting fulling mill is asserted by Wikipedia (CONFIRMED as a claim) but VCH Wilts gives 1189 Stanley as the earliest *known* English mill — treat "earliest English fulling mill" as **contested between 1086 and 1189** (PLAUSIBLE resolution: the scholarly consensus follows Carus-Wilson's late-12th-c. date; her "industrial revolution of the thirteenth century" was searched but only reached as a name in digests, not read).

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**

- L1105 Weavers (town): the shop-house/hall-house set — `CROSS_PASSAGE` in the hall-house variant; `THROUGH_ROOM` shop → hall → chamber in the street-front variant; no `CORRIDOR`. Era ≥ 1700 (English) licenses a **garret loomshop** reached by a winder — the Spitalfields form (CONFIRMED) — and `GALLERY` is *not* licensed. Stair: ladder (floor) → winder (rung) → framed newel in the clothier's house (ceiling; PLAUSIBLE). Measured: Strangers' Hall hall 10.3 × 6 m as a clothier-merchant ceiling (CONFIRMED, tertiary).
- L632 Fuller (village): trough shed = single `THROUGH_ROOM`; the mill variant inherits the MILL family's circulation (wheel bay + stocks bay, ladder to a loft). Stair: ladder.
- L639 Dyer (village): dye house = single-cell furnace room opening to a yard and water; `LOBBY` not licensed; attached dwelling by `THROUGH_ROOM`. Stair: ladder/winder.
- Continental ceiling (tiratoio, Florentine Arte della Lana): `GALLERY`-class open timber upper storey over a masonry ground floor (CONFIRMED) — licensed only in the Italian register, any era 1300–1650.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**

- Weaver: `STORE` = wool/yarn store (dry, lightReq NONE, adjacent to the loom room; at clothier grade a **wool loft** = `ATTIC`/`GARRET_STORE`, PLAUSIBLE); `CLOSET` for the finished cloth/press at master grade; `CELLAR`/`UNDERCROFT` at merchant-clothier grade (Strangers' Hall undercroft c. 1320 "storage and display," CONFIRMED). Fixtures not cells: the warping board, the press, the shears (CONFIRMED as chattels, VCH Wilts).
- Fuller: `STORE` for fuller's earth and soap (dry, NONE); urine/lye in barrels (fixture); at the mill, a cloth store over the stocks (PLAUSIBLE). The **tenter/rack close** is an exterior `STORE`-analogue with *no* closed class — it needs one (see (g)).
- Dyer: `STORE` for woad balls/madder/alum (dry, NONE, locked — these were high-value carted imports, CONFIRMED); the furnace room itself is a `SCULLERY`-class wet room (hearth + vessels + drain), lightReq ANY, adjacency: water side. Fixtures: the lead/copper set in a brick furnace, wooden vats, a drain.

**(f) Typed proposal.**

`parti`: `LOOM_IN_HALL` (L1105 floor; era 1200–1700) · `SHOP_HOUSE_LOOM_FRONT` (L1105 rung; town) · `CLOTHIER_HALL_HOUSE` (L1105 ceiling; hall + shop/stall + wool store + rack close; CONFIRMED Haynes 1454) · `GARRET_LOOMSHOP_LONG_LIGHT` (L1105; era ≥ 1700 only) · `WALKERS_TROUGH_SHED` (L632 floor) · `FULLING_MILL` (L632 rung/ceiling; delegates to MILL family with `stocksBay`) · `DYE_HOUSE_FURNACE` (L639) · `TIRATOIO_DRYING_LOGGIA` (Italian register only).
`functions[]`: weaver floor = {weave, dwell}; rungs = {+wool store → +second loom/journeymen → +shop front with stall → +press/shears → +wool loft → +rack close → +garret loomshop (≥ 1700)}; ceiling = {owned fulling mill and dye house as separate buildings}. Fuller floor = {trough, tenters}; rungs = {+earth store → +water stocks → +rack close with hooks}; ceiling = {four stocks under one roof}. Dyer floor = {one vat, open fire}; rungs = {+furnace with lead/copper → +several vats → +dyestuff store → +drain to water}; ceiling = {"dye house by the water lane with all tenements"}.
`fixtures[]`: broadloom (two-man; width PLAUSIBLE 2.3–3 m) · narrow loom · warping board · press · shears · teasel frames · trough · fulling stocks (pair) · tenter frame (CONFIRMED strip 131 × 1.8 m, outdoor) · furnace + lead/copper (60–70 gal, CONFIRMED-digest) · vats · drain. Wear: vats rot → replaced by pits (PLAUSIBLE); stocks → trough.
Structural buckets: weaver `lightHungry` (new: long glazed/unglazed light on the loom wall; pre-1700 = the biggest ordinary window), `vibration` (CONFIRMED 1743 joist-packing — post-1700 only), not wet; fuller `wetTrade`, `waterPower` (mill variant), `exteriorFixtureField` (tenters — new); dyer `fireTrade` + `wetTrade` + `waterAdjacent` + `noxious` (soft).
Licensing: L1105 × tier town × any prosperity × era any (garret form era ≥ 1700); L632 village × modest × any era × stream (trough) or mill-site (stocks); L639 village × modest+ × any era × water-adjacent; tiratoio × Italian register × guild-owned × 1300–1650.
HOME: loom room = ROOM_KINDS.workfloor; shop = ROOM_KINDS.stall; wool store = ROOM_KINDS.store; dye furnace room = ROOM_KINDS.kiln is the nearest (a furnace with vessels) but semantically wrong — **NO TYPED HOME** for a *furnace-and-vat wet room*; stocks bay = **NO TYPED HOME** (mill bays are not in ROOM_KINDS); tenter ground = **NO TYPED HOME** (exterior fixture field). FURNISHING_KINDS.cauldron covers the dyer's copper; FURNISHING_KINDS.workbench covers the loom only loosely (the loom should be its own kind); FURNISHING_KINDS.rack covers tenters and teasel frames.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**

(1) An **exterior fixture field** (tenter ground / rack close / drying yard) that belongs to a building but lies outside its footprint and scales with output (2,500 hooks per mill; 150 yd square per quarter) — the engine has no class for "outdoor room with typed fixtures." (2) A **light-hungry room** rule: the loom room takes the longest light in the house, and from 1700 forces the garret long-window elevation — an era-gated exterior consequence like the tanner's loft. (3) **Mill delegation**: L632's "often water-powered" must resolve to either `WALKERS_TROUGH_SHED` or a MILL-family `FULLING_MILL` with a `stocksBay`; the catalog entry currently conflates the two. (4) A **guild-owned communal process building** (tiratoio) that belongs to no household — lands on the existing "Often guild-organized" flag of L1105 but needs an ownership field (household / guild / lord). (5) Dyer and tanner share the `pit` fixture and the `waterAdjacent` pull; the grammar should let the two trades co-locate downstream (Skeldergate confusion, CONFIRMED-digest).

**(h) Continental vs English.** The English register is parochial in keeping every cloth process domestic and private until the 18th-c. loomshop; the Flemish and Italian registers had **guild or communal plant** — Florence's six tiratoi (1498) as two-storey timber-topped drying halls along the Arno, and the *Arte della Lana* as owner of process buildings, while the Flemish *lakenhalle* (Ypres, Bruges) housed *sale and inspection*, not production (PLAUSIBLE; not fetched this session). The Italian *bottega* of the *lanaiuolo* is a counting-house-plus-store, the weaving being put out to households — the same putting-out logic as the Suffolk clothier, so the ceiling parti converges. The Welsh *pandy* and the Scottish waulking trough are the surviving floor of the fuller across all registers.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.

## §11 · FAMILY I — mills: Mill (village L532 REQ) · Mills (2-5) (town L1084 REQ) · Sawmill (village L581) · Sawmill (commercial) (town L1155)

*Researched by sub-lane IK (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

Catalog text read (RINST2-catalog-flat.tsv): L532 "Water or windmill with monopoly milling rights… wealthiest and most resented figure"; L1084 "Multiple mills for grain, fulling cloth"; L581 "Water- or ox-powered saw… planks and beams"; L1155 "water- or ox-powered mill… permanent crew".

**(a) Analogue.**

The mill is the one building in the family whose program is a *vertical stack fixed by the machine*, not a room sequence. Three analogues, in ascending complexity:

1. *The horizontal-wheeled mill (early floor).* A two-level box: wheelhouse/undercroft below with the paddle wheel on a vertical shaft, millhouse above with the single pair of stones driven directly, no gearing. Nendrum (Co. Down) is dendro-dated AD 619–621 with a second mill of AD 789; both horizontal-wheeled, "a two-storey structure" with wheelhouse below and millhouse above; the dam is c. 110 m long and originally 6 m wide, the penstock inclined c. 15 degrees (**CONFIRMED**, Current Archaeology, https://archaeology.co.uk/articles/features/harnessing-the-tides-excavating-the-earliest-mills-in-ireland.htm, fetched 2026-08-23). Tamworth (Staffs) is the English instance: horizontal-wheeled, dendro AD 855–9, wheelhouse of upright posts with planked walls and a thick plank floor, "the millhouse, in effect a first storey" extending over wheelhouse and pool end (**CONFIRMED-digest**, Rahtz & Meeson CBA RR 83 via Google Books/ADS digests; the report PDF was 403/404 on both hosts tried). Rynne notes Tamworth is the only chronological parallel to the Irish series outside Ireland (**CONFIRMED**, Rynne 1992, corkhist.ie PDF, fetched 2026-08-23). Register note: these are pre-1200, so they enter the dossier as the *floor* of the mill stack, not as a dated form.

2. *The vertical-wheeled grain mill (the medieval default).* External or internal wheel in a wheel pit; pit wheel on the axle drives a wallower on an upright shaft; the stones sit on a timber **hurst frame** on the stone floor; spouts drop meal to the floor below; later a great spur wheel runs several stone nuts and a sack hoist lifts grain to a bin/garner floor above. Munro (citing Reynolds) gives undershot efficiency 15–30 per cent; overshot "more than twice as powerful", 50–70 per cent, on a quarter of the water, but needing head, dam, pond and race and "a much larger capital investment"; Usher and Reynolds conclude overshot wheels were "much less common than undershot wheels until the early sixteenth century" (**CONFIRMED**, Munro 2002, MPRA 11027 PDF, fetched 2026-08-23). The earliest English overshot depiction is the Luttrell Psalter 1338 and Munro reads the Batsford (E. Sussex) mid-14th-century excavation as overshot (**CONFIRMED**, same); Bedwin's Batsford report (Medieval Archaeology 24, 1980, pp. 187–201) found "almost half the water-wheel surviving in situ" with 14th-century pottery in the wheel-pit, probably a corn mill (**CONFIRMED-digest**, tandfonline abstract; full text 403). The three-floor program (stone / meal / bin) is a post-medieval flour-mill arrangement; the medieval and post-mill floor is TWO levels (the Nutley buck is two-floored on a 15 ft x 10 ft plan — see (b)).

3. *Siting law and the institutional wrapper.* The leat, pond, sluice, race and weir are site fixtures that precede the building (Loudfoot, **CONFIRMED**, Waterloo WHR PDF, fetched 2026-08-23). Water is a negotiated commodity: in c. 1255 the Abbot of Battle agreed to let water flow to Michelham Priory's millpond "up to a height of two feet and nine inches" for 10s a year (**CONFIRMED**, Loudfoot). The monopoly is the *soke / suit of mill*: at Chester "all corn and malt, except the abbot's, was ground at the Dee Mills", toll levied "as a sixteenth of the grain"; a horse mill set up at Boughton was suppressed in 1623 (**CONFIRMED**, VCH Cheshire v/2, https://www.british-history.ac.uk/vch/ches/vol5/pt2/pp104-114, fetched 2026-08-23). The St Albans abbot "confiscated querns from his tenants, smashed them and formed them into a pavement" (**CONFIRMED**, Cambridge Archaeological Journal open-access PDF, fetched 2026-08-23). This is why the village Mill is REQ in the catalog: it is a lordship artefact, not a technological necessity — see (c).

*Town "Mills (2–5)" = the mill row.* Chester's Dee Mills in 1298 were "housed under two roofs in groups of two and three"; by 1600 Gamull had five corn mills and a malt mill "all housed under two roofs"; fulling mills were added in 1355 at Handbridge on the causeway, the causeway itself raised in 1355 "to provide extra power"; by 1811 the complex had "18 pair of stones, suitable warehouses, drying kilns" and 22 pairs by 1830 (**CONFIRMED**, VCH Cheshire). Tewkesbury's 1694 lease names "three water corn mills being under one roof" (**CONFIRMED-digest**, tewkesburyhistory.org). Dulverton's single leat drove four mills in series (**CONFIRMED-digest**, Exmoor HER). On the Tillingbourne (Surrey) Downward & Skinner counted 22 watermills on a 19 km stretch (**CONFIRMED**, Loudfoot). So the catalog's "2–5 mills" is best read as *one leat, several wheels, one or two roofs*, plus the fulling mill and malt mill as additional wheels, not as five detached buildings.

*Miller's house.* Both attached and detached survive. Fiddleford Mill (Dorset) is two storeys with attics, Greensand/limestone, with the 1566 inscription on reset stones in the millhouse ("Miller be true…"), the mill being in outbuildings NW of the 14th-century manor range (**CONFIRMED**, RCHME Dorset III via BHO, fetched 2026-08-23 — note the brief's "Sturminster Newton 1566" is a misattribution: the Sturminster Newton mill entry is late-17th-century, L-shaped, N wing projecting into the river, "two-storied with a basement and attics", flood mark 1756; same page). Nether Alderley (Cheshire): earliest reference 1391 (Wikipedia) versus 1290 (Wikishire digest — unresolved), rebuilt 1595–97, two working overshot wheels of 12 ft and 13 ft in tandem "water passing from the upper wheel to the lower one", stone-slate roof c. 200 tons on an Elizabethan oak frame, rear wall doubling as the dam (**CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia, fetched 2026-08-23). Mapledurham (Oxon): Domesday mill, 15th-century core, enlarged 1670s with a leat for a second wheel (**CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia). Eling (Hants): Domesday mention, present mill post-1770s storm, two independent wheels each driving two pairs of stones, productive 5–7 hours per tide (**CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia). Woodbridge: first tide-mill record 1170, present three-storey building 1793 (**CONFIRMED-digest**, woodbridgetidemill.org.uk; page fetched but truncated). Live/work: the miller sleeps in a house beside, not over, the stones — the stone floor is a machine room; where the house is attached it is an L (Sturminster Newton).

*Windmill.* Earliest certain reference 1185, Weedley (Yorks); "more than twenty sites of 'sunk' post mills with buried trestles have been excavated in England" (**CONFIRMED-digest**, tandfonline dendro article 2023; full text 403). The buck is a two-floor box on a main post over crosstrees and quarterbars; the roundhouse enclosing the trestle is a later addition "creating a covered storage area" (**CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia Post mill, fetched 2026-08-23). Oldest survivors: Bourn main post felled shortly after 1515, Nutley and Pitstone posts mid-16th century (**CONFIRMED-digest**); Great Gransden 1612, Outwood 1665 (**CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia). Dutch standerdmolen from the 1200s; hollow-post (wipmolen) giving way to the octagonal smock around 1526 (**CONFIRMED-digest**, iamexpat/Wikipedia digests).

*Horse mill.* A counter-example to water: the Chester horse mill of 1623 (above); the 1386 Mountnessing (Essex) inventory lists "horse-mill quern stones" beside steeping vats (**CONFIRMED**, The Past malting feature, curl 200, fetched 2026-08-23). The round house form with a c. 10 ft central axle is documented only in post-medieval survivals (Beamish four-horse mill, **CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia Horse mill). In the register a horse mill is an indoor machine in a rectangular or polygonal bay, licensed for malt in towns and for castles/inns.

*Sawmill — the continental-vs-English crux, dated.* Continental: "Normandy provides the first documented example of a hydraulic saw mill, in 1204"; Villard de Honnecourt's drawing c. 1235 shows rotary feed plus reciprocating saw (**CONFIRMED**, Munro). Augsburg 1322 was not verified this session (**PLAUSIBLE**, standard secondary claim). Norway: water sawmills "in the late 1400s in southern Norway and around the Oslo Fjord", the oppgangssag in use in the 16th century (**CONFIRMED-digest**, Norsk Teknisk Museum / Norsk Folkemuseum). Netherlands: Cornelis Corneliszoon van Uitgeest patented the crankshaft December 1593 (improvements December 1597), built a raft-mounted mill in 1594, sold to Alkmaar by 1595 (remains found 2004), the form developing into the paltrok, "30 times faster" (**CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia, fetched 2026-08-23); paltrok mills arose c. 1600 with the whole body turning on a roller rim on a brick base (**CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia Post mill). England: Bowett (Regional Furniture XXII, 2008) — "In England sawmills were virtually unknown; indigenous timber was converted by pit saw"; "a solitary, Dutch-built sawmill built on the south bank of the Thames near present-day Waterloo Bridge in the mid-17th century, but this was short lived"; Neve 1726 believed "Parliament thought fit to prohibit them"; Scotland had sawmills "since the mid-17th century"; Stansfield converted a Bingley fulling mill to saw in 1760; Dingley's Limehouse mill of 1767 was "built on similar lines to the Dutch wainscot mills, initially with twenty blades, and powered by wind", cost £4,454 2s 2d, "partially destroyed by a mob led by sawyers" in May 1768, the 1769 Act making damage a felony (**CONFIRMED**, Bowett PDF, curl 200, fetched 2026-08-23). The "1663" date for the Thames mill is digest-only (**CONFIRMED-digest**). Two corrections to the brief: (i) the English riot of 1768 was against a *wind* sawmill, not water; (ii) the 1663 mill's power source is not stated in the primary I read. So for an English-register settlement c. 1200–1700 the village "Sawmill" should resolve to a SAW PIT + sawyers' yard; a water/wind frame sawmill is licensed continental (1204+), Scandinavian (1450s+), Dutch wind (1594+), Scottish (1650+), and English only as an anomaly before 1760.

Prosperity/wear: floor = one pair of stones, undershot or horizontal, timber box; rungs = second pair, overshot with pond, stone walls and stone roof (Nether Alderley), sack hoist and garner floor, attached house; ceiling = multi-wheel row with malt mill, fulling stocks, kilns and warehouses (Chester). Decline sheds the garner floor and hoist first, then the second pair, then the pond (silting), leaving an undershot single-pair mill; a windmill sheds its roundhouse.

**(b) Measured.**

- Nendrum dam c. 110 m long x 6 m wide; penstock incline c. 15 degrees; two-storey (CA, **CONFIRMED**).
- Irish horizontal-mill penstocks: "around 3–4m long", central section c. 36 cm² [sic — the text reads cm2; likely a transcription of a larger unit], from oak balks "4m long and at least 70cm in diameter"; Little Island (c. AD 630) flumes 2.10 m and 2.03 m long, sides c. 81 cm high (**CONFIRMED**, Rynne 1992).
- Tamworth: dendro AD 855–9; plank-walled wheelhouse (**CONFIRMED-digest**). No wheel diameter retrievable this session (the report says the paddle spacing and wheel diameter were "agreed by F. W. B. Charles" — digest only).
- Domesday 1086: 6,082 mills at over 3,000 locations (Darby/Holt) against the older figure of 5,624 (Hodgen/Reynolds); Holt: over 10,000 by 1300 (**CONFIRMED**, Munro); Holt & Langdon 10,000–15,000 c. 1300 (**CONFIRMED**, Loudfoot). Symonson's 1596 map of Kent: 39 windmills (**CONFIRMED**, Cambridge PDF).
- Wheel efficiencies: undershot 15–30 %, overshot 50–70 %, overshot needing c. one-quarter the water (**CONFIRMED**, Munro). Venafro Roman undershot 1–2 hp (3 hp by others) (**CONFIRMED**, Munro).
- Michelham 1255 water head 2 ft 9 in; Tillingbourne 22 mills / 19 km (**CONFIRMED**, Loudfoot).
- Chester: 1298 mills in groups of 2 and 3 under two roofs; 1600 five corn + one malt mill under two roofs; toll 1/16; 1811 18 pairs of stones, 1830 22 pairs (**CONFIRMED**, VCH).
- Nether Alderley: wheels 12 ft (3.7 m) and 13 ft (4.0 m) overshot in tandem; roof c. 200 tons; rebuilt 1595–97 (**CONFIRMED**).
- Eling: two wheels, two pairs of stones each, 5–7 working hours per tide (**CONFIRMED**). A 20 ft x 5 ft 10 in wheel on a 22-in square oak shaft appears in the digest for a tide mill (site not certain — see open questions; **CONFIRMED-digest**).
- Post mills: main posts mostly 16 ft 6 in – 19 ft (5–5.8 m); Pitstone post c. 17 ft long, 33 in diameter at base (**CONFIRMED-digest**); Nutley buck 15 ft (4.57 m) x 10 ft (3.05 m), head and tail wheels 7 ft diameter, two pairs of stones head-and-tail, open trestle, post dendro 1533–70 (**CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia Nutley, fetched 2026-08-23).
- Fiddleford Mill 2 storeys + attics; Sturminster Newton 2 storeys + basement + attics, L-plan (**CONFIRMED**, RCHME).
- Millstone diameter c. 4 ft / 1.2 m (**PLAUSIBLE**, 3.5–4.5 ft range; not measured this session).
- Sawmill: Dingley 1767, 20 blades, wind, £4,454 (**CONFIRMED**, Bowett). No frame dimensions found for Klopfsäge/oppgangssag/paltrok (searched: "Klopfsäge oppgangssag … frame dimensions", "Cornelis … paltrok dimensions"; null).
- Beamish four-horse mill: 10 ft oak central axle (**CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia; post-medieval).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**

- *Wheel type at Batsford*: Munro reads it as overshot; Loudfoot cites Bedwin p. 190 when defining the undershot wheel-pit. Single-sourced either way; treat the Batsford wheel as "vertical, type contested".
- *Counts*: 5,624 vs 6,082 Domesday mills is a counting-method difference (fractional mills); both are in print.
- *The mill is not universal*: in Kent suit of mill was absent and "handmilling was particularly persistent"; querns came from a bakehouse complex at Westwood; tenants "were explicitly banned from using handmills" elsewhere (**CONFIRMED**, Cambridge PDF). Windmills were not a Kentish commonplace until the post-Dissolution period (39 in 1596). So a village may have NO mill and a town may have horse mills and querns instead — the REQ flag is a lordship rule the engine should be able to switch off.
- *Overshot is late*: the catalog's pictorial default (big overshot wheel) is a post-1500 prevalence; the medieval default is undershot or horizontal.
- *Sawmill*: the English medieval sawmill is a near-null; the first commercial English mill was wind, 1767; saw pits are the floor for five centuries. Continental water sawmills are real from 1204.
- *Negation search* ("medieval village without mill hand querns prohibited … St Albans"): returned the Ambler/Langdon and Cambridge papers confirming handmill persistence and horse mills; no source claimed every village had a mill. A second negation (Nether Alderley 1290 vs 1391) returned a genuine date conflict, unresolved.
- *The miller's house*: the brief's "Sturminster Newton 1566 attached" collapsed on fetch — the inscription is Fiddleford's, and the Sturminster mill is late-17th-century. Mapledurham and Eling entries do not state the house relation.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**

- *Mill (village)*: the stack is `THROUGH_ROOM` in the vertical — wheel pit (not a cell) → stone floor → (rung) bin floor; stair type **ladder** at floor grade (post-mill buck is reached by an external ladder; Nutley is open-trestle), **winder** at the attached-house rung (Sturminster L-plan, late 17th c.). No `CROSS_PASSAGE`; the mill door opens directly on the stone floor. Widths: Nutley buck 10 ft internal is the minimum cell width for a two-pair stone floor. The horse-mill bay is a single `THROUGH_ROOM` with a circular walk.
- *Mills (2–5) (town)*: `THROUGH_ROOM` laterally along the row under one roof (Chester 2+3 bays), with a `GALLERY`-class walkway only at the ceiling grade (19th-c. Chester warehouses); the leat/causeway is the site axis. Stair: winder; a `STAIR_HALL` only in the ceiling industrial grade.
- *Sawmill (village)*: an open yard — the saw pit is a sunk fixture under a shed roof; no enclosed circulation. *Sawmill (commercial)*: a single open shed `THROUGH_ROOM` with the log carriage on the long axis; the paltrok's turning body is a rotating single cell; stair = ladder.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**

- *Mill*: `ATTIC`/`GARRET_STORE` = bin/garner floor (lightReq NONE; adjacency: directly over the stones; fixture: sack hoist, bins); `STORE` = meal floor sacks and the toll bin (fixture, not a cell); the post-mill roundhouse = `STORE` (NONE light, dry). No `CELLAR` — the wheel pit is wet and is a fixture. Nether Alderley's lake wall doubling as dam shows the storage law of the site: water storage is outside the envelope.
- *Mills (2–5)*: add `STORE` warehouse bays and `kiln` (drying kiln) as cells (Chester 1811); the malt mill's malt store = `STORE` with NONE light.
- *Sawmill*: timber is stored outdoors (log pond or yard) — `STORE` at yard grade; a plank-drying shed is a `STORE` cell with ANY light at the commercial rung. A sawdust pit is a fixture.

**(f) Typed proposal.**

- parti: `HORIZONTAL_MILL_STACK` (2-level, floor; early/regional), `VERTICAL_MILL_STACK` (wheel pit + stone floor; + `GARNER_FLOOR` rung), `POST_MILL_BUCK` (2-floor box on trestle; roundhouse rung), `TOWER_OR_SMOCK_MILL` (ceiling, late), `TIDE_MILL` (siting variant: causeway + pond), `HORSE_MILL_BAY` (indoor round walk; counterexample to water), `MILL_ROW` (2–5 wheels on one leat, 1–2 roofs; + fulling, malt mill, kiln, warehouse), `SAW_PIT_YARD` (English floor), `FRAME_SAWMILL_SHED` (continental/Dutch; water or wind).
- functions: floor = grind grain (one pair); rungs = second pair, fulling stocks, malt mill, sack hoist, bolting; ceiling = multi-pair row with kilns and warehouses. Sawmill floor = pit saw; rung = single frame saw; ceiling = multi-blade wainscot mill (20 blades).
- fixtures: millstone pair (4 ft), hurst frame, hopper/shoe/damsel, tentering screw, pit wheel/wallower/spur wheel, sack hoist, toll dish and bin, sluice gear, wheel (undershot/overshot/breast/horizontal), sails and windshaft, roundhouse, horse walk, saw frame, log carriage, saw pit, trestles.
- structural buckets: `waterPower` (with sub-enum `undershot|overshot|breast|horizontal|tide|wind|horse`), `heavyFloor` (stones and hurst on the first floor), `wetFooting` (wheel pit), `dustNoise`, `siteLinear` (leat/race/pond/causeway as linear site fixtures).
- licensing: Mill REQ only where `suitOfMill=true`; `overshot` gated by head/pond and prosperity ≥ mid, era ≥ 14th c. for England (rare before 1500); `POST_MILL` era ≥ 1185; `SMOCK`/`TOWER` era ≥ 1500s; `FRAME_SAWMILL_SHED` gated by register: continental ≥ 1204, Scandinavian ≥ 1450s, Dutch wind ≥ 1594, English ≥ 1760 (anomaly flag before); `SAW_PIT_YARD` is the English default at every tier.
- HOME: `ROOM_KINDS.workfloor` (stone floor / saw floor), `ROOM_KINDS.store` (garner, meal floor, roundhouse), `ROOM_KINDS.kiln` (drying kiln in the row), `ROOM_KINDS.lodging` (attached miller's house); `FURNISHING_KINDS.rack`, `.crate`. NO TYPED HOME: millstone pair, hurst, wheel, sack hoist, saw frame, saw pit, horse walk.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**

The engine must express (1) a *vertical* program axis where floors are mandated by the machine, not by rooms; (2) external machine fixtures projecting beyond the envelope (wheel, sails, trestle); (3) linear site fixtures (leat, pond, dam, causeway) that precede and constrain the plot; (4) an institutional flag `suitOfMill` that turns REQ on/off and licenses the horse-mill/quern counterexample; (5) a multi-unit-under-one-roof row (2–5 wheels, 1–2 roofs) so "Mills (2–5)" composes as one complex; (6) a power-source enum with era gates, above all the English/continental sawmill gate. The finding lands on the existing `waterPower`/`heavyFloor` buckets and on the "Mill" REQ pull; the sawmill entries need a register gate they currently lack.

**(h) Continental vs English.**

England is parochial in two directions: its mill is overwhelmingly the vertical undershot corn mill under manorial soke, and it has no frame sawmill before 1760. The continent supplies the horizontal mill (Ireland, Scandinavia, Alps, Iberia), the Normandy/Villard water sawmill from 1204, the Norwegian oppgangssag from the late 1400s, the Dutch wind sawmill/paltrok from 1594–c. 1600 with a rotating whole-body mill on a brick ring, and the Dutch smock mill from the 1520s; Dutch and German towns also carried guild-regulated urban mills rather than seigneurial ones. Continental mill complexes likewise interleave horse-powered pumping (Antwerp Brewers' House, 16th c.) with water. The Irish horizontal mill with its 3–4 m oak penstock is a measured form the English register does not have after the 9th century.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.

**Parent-lane review note.** Brief error corrected by the sub-lane (supersession, recorded): the parent's brief attributed the 1566 "Miller be true" inscription to Sturminster Newton; it is Fiddleford Mill's, and the Sturminster Newton mill is late-17th-century (RCHME Dorset III via BHO). The English/continental sawmill crux is the family's one hard era×register gate.

## §12 · FAMILY J — Extraction workplaces (surface buildings only): Woodcutter's camp (thorp L145) · Charcoal burner (hamlet L187; village L602) · Peat cutter (hamlet L201) · Mine (open cast) (hamlet L215) · Mine (village L588) · Stone quarry (hamlet L222; village L595) · Salt works (hamlet L250; village L616)

*Researched by sub-lane JL (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

**(a) Analogue.**

The family splits cleanly into three built grades, and the dossier should say so up front: (1) entries with **no building at all** — the woodcutter's camp, the charcoal burner and the peat cutter are landscapes with a temporary hut and a stack; (2) entries with **one or two single-cell sheds at the working face** — the mine's coe/store hut and smithy, the quarry's lodge; (3) the one entry that is genuinely **built, and built as a street** — the inland brine salt works, whose wich houses/Siedehäuser form rows around a communal brine pit.

*Woodcutter's camp (L145).* The catalog entry is "seasonal felling and timber stacking". The historical analogue has no typed building: the timber stack, the saw pit and a lodge hut. Saw pits were mostly open excavations; a Scottish example at Woolforddyke is an oval pit 2 m x 1.6 m x 0.4 m deep, and the only housed examples cited (Llanaerton Home Farm, a single-storey open-fronted rubble building with pitched slate roof; the Weald and Downland Museum's covered pit) are post-medieval survivals — **CONFIRMED** (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saw_pit fetched 2026-08-23; use in Britain "from medieval times through the 19th century"). Two-man operation (top-sawyer/pit-man). The forester's lodge article states the permanent forester's house "originated with temporary woodcutters' shelters" that became permanent in the High Middle Ages — **CONFIRMED** (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forester%27s_lodge fetched 2026-08-23) — which is the licensing hook: a thorp woodcutter's camp is the pre-permanent state of the Family L hunter's/forester's lodge.

*Charcoal burner (L187/L602).* No permanent building. The working unit is the clamp on a levelled platform (pitstead). A Derbyshire HER record gives a post-medieval platform terraced into the slope, 8 m long x 6–7 m wide x 0.4 m high at the downslope edge with a stone revetment — and notes that no charcoal was found on it, so it was probably a timber-stacking platform — **CONFIRMED** (https://her.derbyscc.gov.uk/Monument/MDR8449 via her.derbyshire.gov.uk fetched 2026-08-23). Barbon Park (Cumbria) platforms were dated post-medieval by radiocarbon modelling — **CONFIRMED** (Historic England Research Report 21/2023 landing page, https://historicengland.org.uk/research/results/reports/21-2023 opened 2026-08-23; the PDF itself is JS-gated and was not obtained); the digest figure for those platforms is "approximately 8 m long, 6 m wide and 0.4 m high at their downslope edge" — **CONFIRMED-digest**. The collier's hut: a low stone ring base with poles meeting at an apex, covered in turf (non-flammable) or thatch, with a hole opposite the door probably for a stove pipe; the only remains are low ruined wall rings — **CONFIRMED** (Haverthwaite huts, https://furnesshiddenheritage.blogspot.com/2017/05/the-remnants-of-lakeland-industry.html fetched 2026-08-23, no dimensions given, 19th-century Furness iron-industry context). The measured hut figure I found is American and 19th-century: conical, base 8 ft across, 10 ft high, interior 6–8 ft — **CONFIRMED-digest** (Iron Allentown wiki "Collier huts"). Wyre Forest: no measured figure found (searched: charcoal burner's hut dimensions Wyre Forest). The dossier must say: no typed building; village tier adds at most a charcoal store at the settlement edge (sacks under cover), which is ROOM_KINDS.store.

*Peat cutter (L201).* Turbary is a right ("to cut turf, or peat, for fuel on a particular area of bog"), seasonal, with peat also used "for evaporating brine" — **CONFIRMED** (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turbary fetched 2026-08-23; in the New Forest the right attaches to hearths, not persons). No building. Drying is in stacks/footings in the open; a roofed turf-house/peat shed at the dwelling is a northern (Irish/Hebridean) outbuilding — **PLAUSIBLE**. No measured figures (searched via turbary page only; budget exhausted before a dedicated search).

*Mine (open cast, hamlet L215; village L588).* The surface program is: the shaft with its **coe** — a limestone-walled cabin where miners kept tools, clothing and food, typically built over the climbing shaft with footholes/stemples — **CONFIRMED** (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derbyshire_lead_mining_history fetched 2026-08-23); the **horse gin/whim** on a gin circle ("a raised, circular mound with a flattened top"); at Magpie Mine the 17th-century climbing and founder shafts are visible but the agent's house, smithy, powder house and chimney are 1840s and the Cornish engine house 1869, and all are undimensioned — **CONFIRMED** (https://her.derbyshire.gov.uk/monument/MDR1306 fetched 2026-08-23). The continental measured reference is the **Göpelhaus/Treibekaue**: Harz whim houses had "a conical construction", Freiberg used open Spießgöpel, the circular work area needs a minimum 11–12 m (36–40 ft) diameter, the oak Göpelbaum is 24–30 in diameter on an octagonal stone base; a horse-powered water-lifter is documented at Schneeberg in 1476, depictions in the Kuttenberg Kanzionale 1490s and Agricola 1556 — **CONFIRMED** (https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%B6pel fetched 2026-08-23). Agricola's plates (Book VI, shafting, pumping by treadmill, animal and water power) are the continental measured-drawing reference — **CONFIRMED-digest** (Wikipedia De re metallica). The Cornish **count house** (Botallack, Grade II listed 21 September 1973) is an 18th–19th-century form; the Wikipedia article gives no date or plan — **CONFIRMED** (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botallack_Mine fetched 2026-08-23); its medieval ancestor is no building but the Barmote court (Wirksworth Moot Hall) and the barmaster's dish — **CONFIRMED** (Derbyshire lead mining page). Siting law: coe at the shaft; smithy within a few metres (tool sharpening is daily); whim circle at the shaft; dressing floor downslope by water; bole smelting was on a windy hillside, not a building. Live/work: nobody sleeps at the shaft; the miner is a smallholder — **PLAUSIBLE**.

*Stone quarry (hamlet L222; village L595).* The building is the **lodge**: "a dedicated covered workshop providing space for tools and working stone", with a tracing house and storage sheds on larger sites; "Whilst it has often been assumed that masons would have lived in the lodge there is little evidence of this in practice" — larger projects built separate masons' houses — **CONFIRMED** (https://www.tracingthepast.org.uk/2021/04/12/making_medieval_vaults_worksite_organisation_craftsmen/ fetched 2026-08-23). Salzman noted that a 15th-century king's master mason marked stone at the quarry (so dressing happened at the quarry, not only at the site) — **CONFIRMED-digest**. Vale Royal 1278 and Westminster lodge dimensions: not obtained (Knoop & Jones PDF unreachable; search budget exhausted before Salzman) — **no measured lodge figures found**. The quarrymen's hut and the stone yard are fixtures; a crane (treadwheel or simple derrick) is a yard fixture — **PLAUSIBLE**.

*Salt works (hamlet L250; village L616).* The most built entry. Inland brine (Droitwich/Cheshire): Saxon-period hearths were "long, stone lined channels with lead pans", Domesday records five brine wells producing over 1000 tons a year, 1500 tons by 1400, iron pans, coal and first brick hearths in the 16th–17th centuries, 3000 tons by the 17th century, the 1695 end of the town monopoly bringing wells over 60 m deep; the 1983–84 Upwich excavations found "salt making houses and a timber lined well" with wicker baskets and implements; deposits over 9 m deep — **CONFIRMED** (https://historicengland.org.uk/listing/the-list/list-entry/1020256 opened in browser 2026-08-23). Cheshire: a 1605 letter records at Northwich "one hundred and thirteen salt houses, every one containing four leads apiece"; Brownrigg's 1748 woodcuts show small lead pans in groups of four with the draught through a small chimney; Camden 1580 describes the brine pit "with stairs about it" from which the wallers carry brine in leather buckets to their troughs; the Nantwich "salt ship" is a hollowed tree-trunk brine trough — **CONFIRMED** (https://thelionsaltworks.blogspot.com/2012/08/salt-in-cheshire-before-lion-salt-works.html fetched 2026-08-23). The continental measured reference is Lüneburg: 54 Siedehäuser each with four pans, fed from the central Sod by wooden conduits, over 20,000 t a year at the 15th/16th-century peak, pans refilled up to 13 times daily, lead pans recast at short intervals, the method persisting to 1792; the surviving pan held "gut 100 l" — **CONFIRMED** (https://museumlueneburg.de/siedepfanne-der-lueneburger-saline/ fetched 2026-08-23); the four pans were roughly 1 m² each on four hearths with a fifth in reserve, the 54 houses documented since 1276 — **CONFIRMED-digest** (Wikipedia Lüneburg Saltworks/German Salt Museum). Coastal salterns (Historic England's class description on the same Droitwich entry): seasonal, brine from sand/silt filtration or tidal pits, "evaporation kiln fuelled by peat or wood", "temporary wooden buildings, wooden or wicker troughs and clay-lined pits", oval/kidney middens of 2 ha or more, "component structures being rebuilt at the beginning of each summer", industry declined by the early 16th century under rock salt mined from the early 17th century — **CONFIRMED** (same HE entry). The French salorge store (Guérande) and Hallein/Hallstatt Alpine works: not fetched (fr.wikipedia "Salorge" 404) — **PLAUSIBLE** only: a salorge is a timber salt barn beside the marsh, and the Alpine Pfannhaus is a large roofed hall over one great pan, a different scale class. Prosperity grade: wich-house rows are prosperity in themselves; decline sheds the house and keeps the pit (the pit is civic infrastructure with stairs). Live/work: the waller's dwelling was in the town street, not at the pan — **PLAUSIBLE**.

**(b) Measured.**

- Saw pit, Woolforddyke: oval 2 m x 1.6 m x 0.4 m deep — CONFIRMED (Wikipedia Saw pit).
- Charcoal platform, Ladybower (MDR8449): 8 m x 6–7 m x 0.4 m downslope, stone revetment, post-medieval — CONFIRMED (Derbyshire HER). Barbon platforms: ~8 m x 6 m x 0.4 m, post-medieval — CONFIRMED-digest.
- Collier hut (Pennsylvania, 19th c.): base 8 ft across, 10 ft high, interior 6–8 ft — CONFIRMED-digest (Iron Allentown wiki). No English measured hut found.
- Göpel circle: minimum 11–12 m diameter; Göpelbaum 24–30 in diameter — CONFIRMED (de.wikipedia Göpel).
- Coe: no measured figures found (searched: "coe stone hut measures metres", Magpie Mine HER); HER says "ruinous", some "to their original height", no dimensions.
- Upwich brine pit 30 ft (9.1 m) deep; Netherwich 18 ft (5.5 m) — CONFIRMED-digest (Wikipedia Droitwich Spa). Droitwich deposits over 9 m deep; post-1695 wells over 60 m — CONFIRMED (HE 1020256).
- Northwich 1605: 113 salt houses x 4 leads — CONFIRMED (Lion Salt Works blog). Northwich 1773 iron pans 36 x 25 ft x 13 in deep (975 cu ft, three furnaces) and 40 x 27 ft x 13 in (1170 cu ft) — CONFIRMED (same; 18th-century, outside era, scale ceiling only).
- Lüneburg: 54 houses x 4 pans (+1 reserve), pan ~1 m² / ~100 l, 20,000+ t/yr, 13 refills/day — CONFIRMED / CONFIRMED-digest as above.
- Coastal saltern midden 2 ha+ — CONFIRMED (HE 1020256 class text).
- Masons' lodge: no measured figures found (searched: Vale Royal 1278 / Westminster 1332 / Knoop & Jones / Salzman; PDF unreachable).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**

- The platform form is not diagnostic: MDR8449 is classed a charcoal platform yet yielded no charcoal and is read as a wood-stacking terrace — CONFIRMED. The Cumbrian platform record is post-medieval by radiocarbon — CONFIRMED. So a measured "charcoal platform" is an 18th–19th-century iron-industry artefact; the medieval clamp is PLAUSIBLE only by continuity.
- The mine as a seasonal pit with no building: Derbyshire free-mining lets a miner work a vein on a smallholding with a coe and nothing more; every dimensioned Magpie building is 1840–1869 — CONFIRMED. The horse whim itself is late in our window (1476 Schneeberg; Agricola 1556) — CONFIRMED.
- The masons' lodge is the cathedral/castle site's building, and the evidence says masons did not live in it — CONFIRMED (tracingthepast). A quarry lodge at a hamlet quarry is therefore a shed, not the "lodge" of the building-accounts.
- Salt is the inverse case: the inland works is a street of hearth houses around a communal pit (Lüneburg 54, Northwich 113), so the village-tier entry must be allowed to generate a row, not one building. Coastal salterns are the opposite again — seasonal and rebuilt each summer (HE class text) — so L616's "along the coast or riverbank" wording straddles two incompatible built forms.
- Negation result: the session search budget (200 shared) was exhausted after my 20th query, before a dedicated negation query ran; the negation checks above were made by fetch (HER no-charcoal platform; post-medieval Barbon dating; "little evidence" masons lived in lodges; HE "temporary wooden buildings" for salterns). No null results to report for Family J beyond the two missing measured items (coe; masons' lodge).

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**

- Woodcutter's camp / Charcoal burner / Peat cutter: no building; circulation is the track between stack, pit/clamp and hut. Hut is a single cell, door only. No stair.
- Mine (hamlet): coe = single cell, THROUGH_ROOM trivially (one door, the shaft inside or beside it); no stair (the shaft's stemples are not a building stair). Mine (village): coe + smithy + whim house as three detached cells in a yard — no internal circulation class; the Göpelhaus is a circular cell 11–12 m across with the horse-walk as its only "corridor". A count house at late/prosperous village grade is a two-cell office: LOBBY entry, winder stair to an upper accounts room — PLAUSIBLE (18th-century form).
- Stone quarry: lodge = one open-sided or enclosed long cell, THROUGH_ROOM; a tracing loft at village/prosperous grade sits "in a roofed space at a high level" (CONFIRMED, tracingthepast) reached by ladder.
- Salt works (inland): each wich house is a single hearth cell with a yard door; the pit sits in the open with stairs down to the brine (Camden 1580, CONFIRMED) — that stair is a civic fixture, not a building stair. Row adjacency is the circulation law: houses open onto a common lane with the pit at its head. Coastal saltern: no building.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**

- Woodcutter: STORE as outdoor timber stack (fixture, ANY light); no cell.
- Charcoal: at hamlet none; at village a charcoal STORE cell (dry, ANY light, adjacent to the lane, sized to a cart) — PLAUSIBLE.
- Peat: peat stack outdoors; a turf-house is a STORE outbuilding of the dwelling, not of the trade — PLAUSIBLE.
- Mine: coe = STORE (tools, food, clothing; CONFIRMED contents) and the ore coe = STORE for dressed ore (Magpie "stone built ore coe", CONFIRMED); powder house is post-1600 and detached — PLAUSIBLE. Records/strongbox belong to the count house (ROOM_KINDS.counting, records, strongroom) only at village-prosperous grade.
- Quarry: lodge doubles as tool STORE; stone yard outdoors; no cellar (weight).
- Salt works: the salt store/"salt barn" next to the wich house (dry, raised floor; salt is hygroscopic so the fixture law is off-ground racks) — PLAUSIBLE; brine store = the trough/"ship" in the house (FURNISHING_KINDS.barrel nearest) — CONFIRMED object; fuel stack outside. Lüneburg's fifth pan "in reserve" is a store fixture — CONFIRMED-digest.

**(f) Typed proposal.**

- `WOODCUTTER_CAMP` (thorp L145): functions floor = timber stack + saw pit (open); rung 2 = roofed saw-pit shed (post-medieval survivals only; license era LATE); ceiling = lodge hut. fixtures: `timber_stack`, `saw_pit` (2 x 1.6 x 0.4 m), `lodge_hut`. buckets: none. NO TYPED HOME (no building); hut has no ROOM_KIND.
- `CHARCOAL_PITSTEAD` (hamlet L187 / village L602): floor = platform 8 x 6 m + clamp + conical turf hut (base ~2.4 m); village rung = charcoal store at settlement edge (HOME: ROOM_KINDS.store). fixtures: `platform`, `clamp`, `turf_hut`, `charcoal_store`. buckets: `fireTrade` (outdoor), `seasonal`. Licensing: woodland siting required; no building in the settlement core.
- `TURBARY` (hamlet L201): floor = cuttings + drying stacks; ceiling = turf-house STORE attached to the dwelling. NO TYPED HOME. buckets: `seasonal`, `wet` (bog siting).
- `MINE_SURFACE_OPENCAST` (hamlet L215): floor = pit + coe (STORE, one cell ~3 x 3 m PLAUSIBLE); rung = smithy (HOME: ROOM_KINDS.workfloor + FURNISHING_KINDS.hearth); no whim. `MINE_SURFACE_SHAFT` (village L588): floor = coe + smithy + gin circle (11–12 m); rung = whim house (conical, Harz; open Spießgöpel variant); ceiling = count house (ROOM_KINDS.counting, records, strongroom; FURNISHING_KINDS.ledger, strongbox) + ore store + dressing floor. buckets: `fireTrade` (smithy), `noxious` (dressing, smelting), `heavyFloor` (ore store), `waterPower` (dressing floor). Licensing: era LATE for whim house (post-1476 continental, later English); prosperity HIGH for count house.
- `QUARRY_LODGE` (hamlet L222 / village L595): floor = working face + stone yard + quarrymen's hut; rung = lodge (HOME: ROOM_KINDS.workfloor; FURNISHING_KINDS.workbench as banker); village ceiling = tracing loft + crane + store. buckets: `heavyFloor`, `noxious` (dust), `dry` roof over bankers. NO lodging in the lodge (CONFIRMED negative).
- `WICH_HOUSE_ROW` (hamlet L250: one house; village L616: a row): floor = brine pit with stairs + trough + one hearth house with 4 pans ~1 m² each (HOME: ROOM_KINDS.kiln; FURNISHING_KINDS.cauldron x4, hearth x4); rung = salt store (STORE, raised racks); village ceiling = N houses on a lane with the pit at its head, plus a communal salt store. buckets: `fireTrade`, `wetTrade`, `noxious` (smoke), `fuelHungry`. Licensing: siting = brine spring/pit mandatory; era: lead pans through 1500, iron pans/coal/brick hearths post-1550. `COASTAL_SALTERN` (alternate parti for L616 coastal wording): no building; midden + kiln + temporary wicker/timber shelters, rebuilt each summer; NO TYPED HOME.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**

The engine must be able to say "this entry has no building" and still emit landscape fixtures (stack, pit, platform, clamp, midden) — three of seven Family J entries need a NO-BUILDING pull with fixtures only. It must be able to emit a **row** for one entry (the wich-house street) where every other family emits one building; that is the same pull the Chester Row / inn-yard gallery already needs, applied to hearth cells. It needs a circular cell (the whim house) and a detached-cell yard (coe + smithy + whim) that is not a courtyard plan. And it needs an era gate that flips the salt parti from lead-pans-in-fours to iron-pan-and-coal after c.1550 — this lands on the existing era flag, not a new one.

**(h) Continental vs English.**

The English register is parochial in treating salt as a smallholder's side-trade; the Lüneburg Saline (54 Siedehäuser, a civic Sülfmeister class, >20,000 t a year) and the Cheshire 113-house Northwich show the built norm is an industrial street under a town or monastic landlord. For the mine, the Saxon/Harz Göpelhaus and Agricola's measured plates are the only measured surface-building tradition in our window; the English coe is an undimensioned stone cabin and the count house is 18th-century. The masons' lodge is a pan-European site building (Bauhütte), and its lodging function is disputed on both sides of the Channel. The Iberian and Ottoman registers add nothing to this family beyond the Alpine Pfannhaus scale class.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.
