## §13 · FAMILY K — food trades: Butchers (3-8) (town L1120) · Bakers (5-15) (town L1127) · Brewer (village L660) · Brewery (town L1162) · Maltster (hamlet L194) · Vintner (town L1058) · Fishmonger (village L681)

*Researched by sub-lane IK (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

Catalog text read: L1120 "Meat processing. Strictly regulated."; L1127 "Bread production. Guild-regulated prices."; L660 "Converts malted grain to ale in quantity… supplies alehouses and households"; L1162 "Commercial-scale ale and beer… regional distribution"; L194 "Converts barley into malt… small operation"; L1058 "Produces and ages wine… importer-blender in a non-wine region"; L681 "Buys, salts, and sells fish… Near water: fresh. Inland: dried or salted."

**(a) Analogue.**

*Butchers — the shambles as a street, the hall as the continental alternative.* York's Shambles: buildings c. 1350–1475, jettied floors overhanging "by several feet", "the rears of the shops were slaughterhouses", offal thrown into street runnels with a natural slope, shops still carrying meat-hooks under the eaves and "shelves on which meat was displayed" (the fleshammels), street 400 ft (120 m) long (**CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia The Shambles, fetched 2026-08-23). The regulatory layer: Coventry butchers "were not permitted to slaughter animals in the street or tie up animals outside"; each butcher to "kepe his durre clene fro bloode"; a 1421 mayoral proclamation required all pig slaughter at the **common scalding house**, sited by the river next to a latrine house, with 1448/1465 leases granting butchers access to draw water; London 1370: a nuisance complaint against a butcher and his wife "for having a scalding house in their tenement"; York 1475 fined three butchers for street slaughter; Charles VI of France ordered the Châtelet meat market demolished for "the filth of the slaughtering" (**CONFIRMED**, Jørgensen 2018 PDF, curl 200, fetched 2026-08-23). Continental hall: the Antwerp Vleeshuis of 1501–04 is the third on its site, with "room for 62 butchers, plus meeting rooms and storage", the interior "divided into two halves, each with a span of 7.5 metres — the maximum length of a structural oak beam", brick fired on site, cellar for meat, ground-floor stalls, chapel (**CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia Vleeshuis, fetched 2026-08-23; 21 m wide / 46 m high / 624 m² market is **CONFIRMED-digest**). Haarlem Vleeshal 1602–03 (Lieven de Key), opened 1 November 1604, 40 meat merchants, salted meat sold separately on Warmoesstraat, fresh-meat sales to the 18th century, ceased 1840, cellar beneath (**CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia Vleeshal, fetched 2026-08-23). So the "3–8 butchers" is either a row of shop-houses with yards behind (English) or stalls inside one hall over a meat cellar (Low Countries), with the killing displaced to a riverside scalding house in either case.

*Bakers — the oven is the building.* Ovens are beehive domes, single or twin. Coventry: earliest named baker 1240–50 with a bakehouse in Cook Street; 1279 castle bakehouse (Bayley Lane, two ovens excavated 1990, one also used for general cooking); Ernsford Grange two ovens of c. 36 in internal diameter, mid-13th–14th century; Kenilworth Castle kitchens two ovens c. 55 in diameter x 30 in high, probably 14th century, "twin ovens were common practice, one being used for baking [steamy] … the other for pies and pastries [dry heat]"; equipment in Coventry bakers' inventories: "Troughs, with hinged lids, in which to mix the dough", "boulting whitches (sieves)"; the 1520 dearth census counted 6,601 citizens, 43 bakers and 68 brewers, the bakers "spread throughout the wards of the city rather than … in any particular ward or street"; from 1513 bakers "were not allowed to operate inns as well as their bakeries" (**CONFIRMED**, Willcox, Bakers' Company of Coventry PDF, curl 200, fetched 2026-08-23). Excavated bakehouses: Mill Cotton (Northants) stone bakehouse 5.5 m wide x at least 7 m long, 35 m south of the manor moat, two circular ovens of 1.4 m and 0.9 m chamber diameter each with a flue to the south, mid-14th to mid-15th century (**CONFIRMED**, Northants HER MNN20959, fetched 2026-08-23); Warrens Yards, Bury St Edmunds: a sunken room 7.5 x 4 m cut 1.2 m into chalk behind Guildhall Street frontages, entered by a chalk ramp, partly timber-roofed, with three circular ovens 1.8–2.7 m in diameter built against its sides, late 13th/early 14th century, "a commercial bakery" (**CONFIRMED**, Suffolk HER ESF20904, fetched 2026-08-23). The shop is the front room of the baker's house selling through the window; the oven sits in the back room or in a detached/sunken bakehouse because of fire. Communal ovens: the French *four banal* and English "bannal ovens" (**CONFIRMED-digest**, oldandinteresting). Willcox's gloss that "in the towns only the wealthier persons could afford … their own ovens" while "smaller houses … came to rely on specialist bakers" (**CONFIRMED** as his statement) gives the floor: most urban households bake nowhere at all.

*Brewers — the domestic floor, the brewhouse ceiling.* Bennett: most households brewed for themselves; commercial brewing was predominantly by married women in their middle years, men taking over after 1350 and dominating by 1600 (**CONFIRMED-digest**, Bennett 1996 via reviews). The village "Brewer" is therefore usually an alewife's kitchen with a lead/copper over a furnace and tubs — no separate building. A purpose-built brewhouse is an estate, monastic or inn feature: Lacock Abbey's brewhouse sits in the courtyard adjacent to the bakehouse, the furnace "accessed" through an opening in the bakehouse wall, brick furnace and copper housing upgraded before the 1721 brewing records begin, with mash tun, coolers and underback, wort moved "by chutes or pails", essentially one room (**CONFIRMED**, tdo-beer.com page, fetched 2026-08-23 — a secondary page; the 16th-century date for the courtyard is National Trust digest); the 1778 Red Lion inventory lists "two coolers and stands, one copper furnace and grates, one mash tub and stand" (**CONFIRMED**, same page) — that is an *inn* brewhouse, the R-INST-4 overlap. Charlecote's brewhouse has mostly 18th-century coppers, pumps and stalls (**CONFIRMED-digest**, National Trust). Monastic commercial scale: Thornholme Priory kiln house and flue "adjacent to a brewhouse"; Castle Acre "malting and brewing on a commercial scale, with several phases of building" (**CONFIRMED**, The Past malting feature, curl 200, fetched 2026-08-23). Hampton Court's Tudor brewhouse was not verified this session (**PLAUSIBLE**). Antwerp's Brewers' House preserves 16th-century horse-powered water pumping (**CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia Horse mill). Boundary with R-INST-4: an inn brews for its own taps in a brewhouse within its yard (Red Lion 1778; Lamb and Fountain Inn, Frome, has a 17th-century stone malt steep); a "Brewery" (L1162) is a brewhouse that sells out — same room program, larger coppers, plus a malt store and cellar, and by the 16th century a horse mill or water mill for malt.

*Maltster.* The process fixes the program: steep 72 h in a cistern; couch up to 72 h in a rectangular frame; grow a fortnight on a low floor; kiln 1–4 days at c. 7 cm depth on a hair cloth over a perforated/tiled floor (**CONFIRMED**, The Past). Medieval evidence: Battle Abbey c. 1190 malt-making "accustomed" with wood brought for the kiln; the 1386 Mountnessing inventory of hair cloths, steeping vats, leaden cisterns and horse-mill querns; Lindisfarne Priory 14th–15th-century stone kiln with a well and steeping cistern; Castle Acre circular stone kilns with stoke holes; Nottingham's caves hold "a complex of steeping cisterns associated with 28 circular malting ovens"; kiln superstructures "probably conical and made of cob or clay"; clay-tiled roofs at Castle Acre, lead and slate at Bridlington's Home Grange (**CONFIRMED**, The Past). Survivals: Boyes Croft, Great Dunmow — "England's earliest surviving malthouse", 16th century, crown-post roof, diamond-mullioned first-floor windows; Chipping Campden late-17th century with grain bins, steeping cistern, couching and germination floors, furnace, drying floor and garners; 17th-century stone steeps at Blacker Hall Farm and the Lamb and Fountain Inn; "a dozen or so" 17th-century survivals with earth, lime or stone floors; ventilation by windows and louvres "in one of the long elevations"; farmyard maltings as independent buildings; cave maltings in barrel-vaulted cellars under a two-storey house at Minchinhampton (**CONFIRMED**, The Past). The hamlet maltster is thus a barn or cellar with a cistern at one end and a cob-cone kiln at the other, giving "no external clue" to its use.

*Vintner.* Stow: the Vintry is where "the Merchants of Burdeaux, craned their Wines out of Lighters … and there landed and made sale of them"; the ward had "many faire and large houses (with Vaults and Cellars for stowage of Wines, and lodging of Burdeaux Merchants)"; Henry Picard's Vintry was "a large house builded of stone and timber, with vaults for the stowage of wines"; three timber cranes on the wharf; later vintners "retayled by the Gallons, Pottell, Quart, and Pint" (**CONFIRMED**, MoEML Stow 1633 Vintry, fetched 2026-08-23). Vintners' charter 1364 with the Gascon monopoly; hall rebuilt by 1671 over a pre-Fire vaulted cellar (**CONFIRMED-digest**). The program is a merchant's house over a stone vault with a street or yard cellar stair, tun room, and a tavern front — the tavern-vintner overlap is Stow's retail line. Measured London undercrofts were not obtained this session (search budget exhausted); **PLAUSIBLE** range from Schofield's corpus: ribbed or barrel vaults c. 4–7 m wide, 2–4 bays, 8–15 m long, entered by a stair from the street.

*Fishmonger.* Old Fish Street with Queenhithe as its landing "formed the western fish-market of London before Billingsgate" took over; the fishmongers' houses there "were at first merely moveable boards or stalls, set out on market-days, but after procuring license to set up sheds, they grew to shops, and by little and little to tall houses of three or four stories"; under Elizabeth a lead and stone cistern fed from the Thames was set against the church wall "for the care and commodity of the Fishmongers"; stockfishmongers and saltfishmongers merged 1537 (**CONFIRMED-digest**, Pepys encyclopedia / BHO digests; pepysdiary 403 on fetch, BHO page fetched but not carrying the passage). The village fishmonger is the first rung of that sequence: a board, a salting trough, a smoke/dry loft, running water nearby.

Live/work: butcher, baker, fishmonger and vintner all sleep over or behind the shop; the brewer sleeps in the house the brewing is in; the maltster's kiln is detached for fire; the Vleeshuis is not a dwelling.

**(b) Measured.**

- Shambles street 400 ft / 120 m; buildings c. 1350–1475; 31 butchers' shops in 1885 (**CONFIRMED**; a late figure, not a prior).
- Antwerp Vleeshuis 1501–04: two halves each 7.5 m span; 62 butchers (earlier cap 52) (**CONFIRMED**); 21 m wide, 46 m high, c. 624 m² market (**CONFIRMED-digest**). Haarlem Vleeshal 1602–03, 40 merchants (**CONFIRMED**).
- Coventry: ovens c. 36 in (0.9 m) internal diameter mid-13th–14th c.; Kenilworth ovens c. 55 in (1.4 m) diameter x 30 in high, 14th c.; 1790 domestic oven 36 in floor, 24 in crown; early-19th-century commercial oven 11 x 9 ft holding 140–150 loaves; 1520: 6,601 citizens, 43 bakers, 68 brewers (**CONFIRMED**).
- Mill Cotton bakehouse 5.5 m x ≥7 m; ovens 1.4 m and 0.9 m; 35 m from moat (**CONFIRMED**). Bury St Edmunds sunken bakery 7.5 x 4 m, 1.2 m deep, three ovens 1.8–2.7 m (**CONFIRMED**).
- Malting: steep 72 h; couch ≤72 h; growth c. 14 days; kiln depth 7 cm, 1–4 days; moisture 43 % → 3 %; Nottingham caves 28 ovens; Worksop 1870s germination floor 22 bays = 104 ft / 31.5 m (late, for the bay module only) (**CONFIRMED**). A steeping cistern 3 m x 2.2 m x 1.05 m, mortared stone (**CONFIRMED-digest**, site not identified in the digest — see open questions).
- Brewhouse: no medieval dimensions found (searched "Lacock … Hampton Court brewhouse … measured survey"; "medieval malthouse … archaeology"); inventories only (two coolers, one copper, one mash tub — 1778).
- Vintner / fishmonger: no measured figures found (searched "Vintry … undercroft measured"; "fishmonger … fish house salting trough"); the Vintry cellar count is qualitative.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**

- *Butchers' slaughterhouse was not always behind the shop*: Coventry's common scalding house (1421) and York's 1475 fines show slaughter both displaced to a municipal riverside facility and still done illegally in the street; the York "slaughterhouse at the rear" is a later-documented arrangement. The Low-Countries hall replaces the row entirely.
- *Bakers were not a row*: Coventry's 43 bakers were dispersed across wards (**CONFIRMED**), so "Bakers (5–15)" must not compose as a Baker Street; the "Bread Street" of London is a market location, not the bakehouses.
- *Brewing was domestic*: Bennett's floor makes "Brewer" a fixture-set in a kitchen, not a building, for most villages; the purpose-built brewhouse is monastic/estate/inn (**CONFIRMED-digest** + The Past).
- *Malting was domestic too*: households "continued to produce beer … until the mid-18th century" but "would increasingly buy their malt" — the hamlet maltster is a post-1400 specialisation with a 1386 inventory as the earliest in-hand evidence.
- *Negation search* ("medieval bakers baked in their own houses … butchers slaughtered in the street ordinance"): returned Willcox (urban reliance on bakers), Jørgensen (street slaughter prohibited but practised), and the tandfonline "Controlling the Butchers" abstract (not opened). Null result: no source was found asserting a dedicated English urban slaughterhouse *building* before the 15th century.
- *Sawn dates and attributions in the brief*: Hampton Court brewhouse unverified; "Sturminster Newton 1566" (Family I) is Fiddleford.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**

- *Butchers (town)*: shop-house `THROUGH_ROOM` front shop → back room → yard/slaughter shed, side `CROSS_PASSAGE` only at the rung where livestock must reach the yard (a 1.2–1.8 m cart/beast passage, **PLAUSIBLE**); jettied solar over, **winder** stair. Vleeshuis: a free-standing `GALLERY`-class hall (two aisles of 7.5 m span) over a `CELLAR`, with stair towers (`STAIR_HALL`, **spiral**) — institutional, prosperity high, era ≥ 1500 continental.
- *Bakers (town)*: `THROUGH_ROOM` shop → bakehouse (back room with ovens in the rear wall, or detached across a yard); at Bury the bakery is a sunken room reached by a **ramp** (floor grade); **ladder/winder** to the flour loft. No corridor at any grade.
- *Brewer (village)*: none beyond the house's own; *Brewery (town)*: `THROUGH_ROOM` brewhouse → cooler loft (ladder) → cellar (steps); a `LOBBY` at the yard door for the dray.
- *Maltster (hamlet)*: one long low `THROUGH_ROOM` (germination floor) with cistern at one end and kiln at the other; **ladder** to the drying floor and garners; cave maltings are `CELLAR` entered by steps.
- *Vintner (town)*: `STAIR_HALL` or street cellar steps to the vault (the vault is the primary cell); tavern front `THROUGH_ROOM`; merchant's hall and lodging over (**spiral/winder**).
- *Fishmonger (village)*: a board at the door; shop → yard with trough; `THROUGH_ROOM` only.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**

- Butchers: `LARDER` (north, no hearth, slate/stone shelves) as the hanging room; `CELLAR` for meat in the Vleeshuis (NONE light); salt tub and hooks are fixtures. Adjacency: larder away from the scalding copper.
- Bakers: `STORE` flour loft = `ATTIC`/`GARRET_STORE` (NONE light, dry, over the bakehouse); bolting room is a cell only at the commercial rung (Coventry "boulting whitches" = fixture at the floor); kneading trough with hinged lid = fixture; wood store = `STORE` at yard.
- Brewer/Brewery: `CELLAR` (ale/beer casks, NONE light) mandatory at the Brewery rung; malt store `STORE` (dry, NONE); cooler trays are a loft fixture needing through-ventilation (ANY light, louvred); `SCULLERY`-class washing for casks at the ceiling.
- Maltster: steeping cistern and couch frame are fixtures; germination floor is the cell (lightReq NORTH/shuttered; long low); kiln is a `kiln` cell (fire-separated, tiled/lead roof); garners = `GARRET_STORE`.
- Vintner: `CELLAR`/`UNDERCROFT` is the primary cell (NONE light, stone vault, street stair); tun room/`STORE`; counting room over.
- Fishmonger: salting trough and `LARDER`-class stone shelves; stockfish loft `GARRET_STORE` (dry, ventilated); running water/cistern adjacency (Old Fish Street cistern).

**(f) Typed proposal.**

- parti: `BUTCHER_SHOPHOUSE_ROW` (shelf front, yard slaughter shed; `COMMON_SCALDING_HOUSE` as a municipal satellite on the river), `MEAT_HALL_OVER_CELLAR` (continental Vleeshuis/Vleeshal), `BAKER_SHOPHOUSE_OVEN_BACK`, `DETACHED_BAKEHOUSE` (manorial/communal, 5.5 x 7 m floor), `SUNKEN_BAKERY` (Bury, urban backland), `KITCHEN_BREWING` (fixture-set, village floor), `COURTYARD_BREWHOUSE` (single room: furnace/copper, mash tun, coolers, underback; + cellar, malt store), `COMMON_BREWHOUSE` (town), `FLOOR_MALTINGS` (cistern–floor–kiln linear), `BARN_KILN_MALTINGS` (hamlet), `CAVE_MALTINGS`, `VINTRY_HOUSE_OVER_VAULT` (+ tavern front, crane wharf), `FISH_BOARD_TO_SHOP` (board → shed → shop-house sequence).
- functions: floor = sell (board/shelf/window), process (oven, copper, trough, cistern); rungs = store (cellar, loft), kill/scald (yard or common house), hall (Vleeshuis); ceiling = guild hall with chapel, multi-copper brewery with horse mill and cellars.
- fixtures with grades: beehive oven 0.9 m (floor) → 1.4 m twin (rung) → 1.8–2.7 m triple (ceiling); copper + furnace (floor) → two coolers + underback (rung); cistern (stone, 17th c.) → leaden cistern (1386 inventory); meat shelf/hooks; salting trough; cranes (wharf).
- structural buckets: `fireTrade` (oven, copper, kiln — detach or back-wall), `wetTrade` (scalding, steeping, fish, washing casks — water adjacency, drains), `noxious` (butchers, fish — runnels, downwind, riverside scalding), `heavyFloor` (grain bins, tuns), `vaultBelow` (vintner, meat hall), `lowLong` (malting floor), `regulatedPrice` (bakers, brewers — assize flag, not a room).
- licensing: Butchers row tier town, all eras; `COMMON_SCALDING_HOUSE` era ≥ 1420 English; `MEAT_HALL_OVER_CELLAR` continental, era ≥ 1500, prosperity high. Bakers: dispersed (not rowed); `SUNKEN_BAKERY` era 1275–1350 urban backland. `KITCHEN_BREWING` every tier; `COURTYARD_BREWHOUSE` monastic/estate/inn all eras, commercial "Brewery" era ≥ 1400 and male-dominated ≥ 1550. `FLOOR_MALTINGS` era ≥ 1386 evidence; survivals ≥ 16th c. `VINTRY_HOUSE_OVER_VAULT` port towns only, era ≥ 1300. `FISH_BOARD_TO_SHOP` grades by prosperity from board to 3–4-storey house.
- HOME: `ROOM_KINDS.stall` (meat shelf, fish board), `.workfloor` (bakehouse, brewhouse, germination floor), `.kiln` (malt kiln), `.cellar` (meat, ale, wine), `.store` (flour loft, malt store), `.hall` (Vleeshuis); `FURNISHING_KINDS.hearth` (oven, furnace — but the oven wants its own kind), `.barrel` (tuns, casks), `.cauldron` (copper), `.counter`, `.rack` (hooks/shelves). NO TYPED HOME: beehive oven, mash tun, cooler tray, steeping cistern, couch frame, salting trough, scalding copper, wharf crane.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**

The engine must express (1) fixture-sets without a building (kitchen brewing; the fishmonger's board) so "Brewer" and "Fishmonger" can resolve to a house tag rather than a plot; (2) a municipal satellite cell (common scalding house, common brewhouse, common oven) that belongs to several trades and sits on the river, not on the trade's plot; (3) detachment-for-fire rules (bakehouse 35 m from the moat; kiln with its own tiled roof); (4) the continental hall-over-cellar as an institutional alternative to the English shop row; (5) a dispersion rule for bakers versus a row rule for butchers and fishmongers; (6) the inn/brewhouse boundary as a shared fixture-set with different selling licences. These land on `fireTrade`/`wetTrade`/`noxious` buckets and on the R-INST-4 inn overlap; the "common house" satellite and the oven fixture have no home yet.

**(h) Continental vs English.**

The English food trades are shop-house trades on a street (Shambles, Fish Street, dispersed bakers) with municipal nuisance law; the Low Countries and Germany collect the butchers into a guild hall with a meat cellar (Antwerp Vleeshuis 1501–04 with 62 stalls and a chapel; Haarlem Vleeshal 1602–03; the German Fleischhalle), sell salted and fresh meat in different places, and run the bakers through the Bäckerzunft's communal ovens and the French *four banal*. Wine is the inversion: the English vintner is an importer with a vault (Gascon monopoly 1364), whereas the continental vintner is a producer with a press house and cellar under the house (German Weingut, Burgundian cuverie) — the catalog's "importer-blender" clause is precisely the English register. Antwerp's brewers pumped with horse engines in the 16th century, a scale the English common brewhouse reached only in the 17th.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.

## §14 · FAMILY L — Primary production and hunting: Fishing community (thorp L127) · Fisher's landing (hamlet L171) · Shepherd collective (thorp L136) · Shepherd (hamlet L243) · Dairy farmer (hamlet L236; village L609) · Beekeeper (village L674) · Wildfowler (village L717) · Hunter's lodge (hamlet L180; village L724)

*Researched by sub-lane JL (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

**(a) Analogue.**

The dossier must state which entries have no typed home: **Beekeeper** (boles and skeps are garden-wall fixtures), **Wildfowler** (the decoy is a landscape; the decoyman's house is a dwelling), **Shepherd/Shepherd collective** (the sheepcote is a stock building, not a workshop; the shepherd's wheeled hut is 19th-century), **Dairy farmer** (the dairy is a room class of the farmhouse, §453 DAIRY), and the thorp **Fishing community** (racks, nousts and a smoke-hut are fixtures of the dwelling). Only the **Fisher's landing** at village-prosperous grade (the fish house/cellar) and the **Hunter's lodge** (a dwelling-with-kennels at hamlet grade; a moated hall complex at lordly grade) are buildings with a room program.

*Fishing community (L127) and Fisher's landing (L171).* The one medieval fish-house survival is Meare, Somerset: a two-storey rectangular stone building 12.4 m x 6.6 m, believed the home and workplace of Glastonbury Abbey's chief fisherman, built 1322–1335; ground floor three rooms with the central room largest, entered from the south-west into the central room and from the north-west into the north room; upper floor two rooms reached by an external staircase on the south-west side (upper storey destroyed by fire in the 19th century); surrounding rectilinear fishponds c. 20–30 m x 5 m; Domesday records three fisheries and ten fishermen at Meare — **CONFIRMED** (https://historicengland.org.uk/listing/the-list/list-entry/1008018 opened in browser 2026-08-23). This is a lordly/monastic fixture, not a village fisher's. The working vernacular is storage-only: the Norwegian naust, one gable to the water with big doors and a ramp, built in grind-frame, log or stone, used for boats and gear and "requiring good ventilation for drying equipment rather than human habitation", with medieval leidang nausts at Kinsarvik 34 m x 13 m and near Steinkjer c. 40 m — **CONFIRMED** (https://no.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naust fetched 2026-08-23). The English net store is the Hastings net shop: originally "eight feet square" (2.45 m), later about 10 ft (3 m) square, up to three storeys, slim frame with diagonal bracing, boarded and tarred, a 2-ft-square ceiling hatch with a ladder on each floor, mackerel nets on one floor and herring on another, trawl gear on the ground floor or cellar, nets coiled and hung from hooks on the ceiling beams; the tallness is explained by plots too small to expand — about 60 shops in the 1820s, 120 in the late 1860s, 39 listed Grade II* in 2010, all but one of two storeys or more; ground rent 2s rising to 5s in 1846 — **CONFIRMED** (https://hastingshistory.net/hastings-net-shops fetched 2026-08-23; the form is documented from 1833–1835, i.e. post-era, so it is a bounded possibility for the CROWDED-frontage case, not a medieval prior). The Cornish fish cellar (pilchard palace): St Endellion, Port Isaac, "circa early C19", irregular courtyard plan, two-storey front range with a wide central cart opening, left wing with stables, salt pits and an open-fronted range "with arcade of timber and granite posts and net lofts above", right wing a later kipper house; an external stone stair to first-floor offices; process — catch layered with salt on the courtyard floor, then into leaky hogsheads on boards over shallow gullies, pressed by stones hung from beams slotted into brick niches, oil collected in the gullies — **CONFIRMED** (https://historicengland.org.uk/listing/the-list/list-entry/1115065 opened in browser 2026-08-23). Liberty Cellars, Port Gaverne, leased 11 May 1803 on a plot 160 ft x 96 ft and built in one summer for the August season — **CONFIRMED** (https://history.gullrock-port-gaverne.co.uk/1803-to-1920-liberty-fish-cellars/ fetched 2026-08-23). Structural imperatives: salt and fish oil (wet, noxious), net drying (ventilated lofts), weight of hogsheads (ground floor only). Live/work: the Meare fisherman lived upstairs over the fish rooms — CONFIRMED; at Hastings and Port Isaac nobody lived in the store — CONFIRMED by absence (offices, not chambers).

*Shepherd collective (L136) and Shepherd (L243).* The medieval building is the sheepcote (bercaria): Dyer's Gloucestershire examples range 23–65 m long by 6–10 m wide, surviving as earthworks, documented as "stone-built, cruck framed tall buildings", one 3.7 m high, housing the whole flock through winter, used for lambing and manure accumulation, costing "sometimes half yearly wool profits" — **CONFIRMED** (Dyer 1995 as quoted at https://whiteadder.aocarchaeology.com/sheepcote/ fetched 2026-08-23; the Dyer article itself (Medieval Archaeology 39, 136–164) was not opened). The Bothwell Water structure: 47 m x 7.5 m, walls 0.6 m of stone and turf surviving to three courses, rounded ends, an annexe about 2 m wide along the west wall, no entrances identifiable, no internal floors or features found, radiocarbon dated to the second half of the 13th century — **CONFIRMED** (same page). Crawley (Hants) bishopric accounts: one sheepcote in 1208–9 with a ditch 60 perches long (just over 300 m) and a fence; at least two by 1219–20, three by 1264–5; 14th-century accounts itemise "bays, doors, groundsills, partitions, roofs and walls"; a barton enclosure around all three by 1305–6; spending about 5s a year before 1300 — **CONFIRMED** (Page, Agricultural History Review 51(2), https://www.bahs.org.uk/AGHR/ARTICLES/51n2a1.pdf fetched 2026-08-23, text extracted). The shepherd's wheeled hut: Wikipedia claims use "since the 14th century", peak late 19th century, stove in one corner, windows each side, stable door, iron wheels and corrugated iron — **CONFIRMED** (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shepherd%27s_hut fetched 2026-08-23); the dossier should treat the wheeled iron form as 19th-century and the "14th century" claim as unsupported manuscript-image inference. Wool house/wool loft: no source fetched — **PLAUSIBLE** as a manorial store, not the shepherd's. Wharram Percy: no sheepcote figure found (searched).

*Dairy farmer (L236/L609).* The dairy is a room, not a building: north side, no hearth, stone or slate shelves, louvred or gauze windows, with a cheese room/loft above or adjacent — **PLAUSIBLE** (Historic England's National Farm Building Types lists "DAIRIES AND CHEESE ROOMS" as a class, CONFIRMED at https://historicengland.org.uk/images-books/publications/national-farm-building-types/ opened 2026-08-23, but the PDF was 403 and the Wikipedia Dairy page has no historical architecture). The Cistercian vaccary (Rossendale, Bowland; 13th–14th century) is the scaled-up form: a cow house plus a dairy at a grange — **PLAUSIBLE** (Wikipedia Vaccary 404). This entry maps to §453 DAIRY directly; the only tier distinction is a detached dairy building at village-prosperous grade.

*Beekeeper (L674).* Bee boles are recesses in garden walls: in Kent brick boles are mostly 9–10 in deep (19 of 29 sets), 10 in or 14–15 in wide; across England most are 9–14 in deep; examples 18 x 12.5 x 10 in (Westerham), 17 x 18 x 19 in (a deep set of six), 25 in wide x 13 in deep holding two skeps; set 2.5–4 ft above ground; almost half face south, 18 per cent east; of 28 dated Kent sets 3 may be 15th-century, 16 16th, 7 17th, 2 18th; 826 sets on the IBRA Register by mid-1988; skeps of 10 in diameter (Wildman) and Purchas 1657 on hive size; Remnant 1637 "be sure to keep them dry"; "Most skeps stood in the open" under a straw hackle and boles were "a less common way of keeping them dry"; boles are in free-standing garden walls and only rarely on buildings (a chimney wall, a brewery, a stable); separate "alcove", "shelter", "winter storage" and "bee house" categories exist — **CONFIRMED** (Walker, Archaeologia Cantiana 106, PDF fetched 2026-08-23 and text-extracted). Stone-wall boles generally 18–30 in high, 15–26 in wide, 14–21 in deep — **CONFIRMED-digest**. The continental Bienenhaus is a wooden (rarely masonry) shed for multiple hives; European adoption is 19th-century with the 1853 frame hive, because "medieval log hives, straw baskets required less protection"; Swiss survival — **CONFIRMED** (https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bienenhaus fetched 2026-08-23). So: NO TYPED HOME; the apiary is a garden fixture; honey and wax are worked in the kitchen.

*Wildfowler (L717).* The duck decoy: a shallow pond of about two acres in about eight acres of wood, four to six pipes (eight known) up to 70 yd long narrowing from 20 ft to 2 ft, reed screens 6 ft high in echelon 3 ft apart with 2-ft dog jumps; introduced by Dutch engineers in the early 17th century (Waxham c. 1620; St James's Park by 1665); a "decoy house" and cottage for the decoyman ("Little Decoy House" occupied to 1881); owners were gentry, with yeoman commercial operators — **CONFIRMED** (https://www.kentarchaeology.org.uk/journal/143/kentish-duck-decoys fetched 2026-08-23). Boarstall: four pipes, each about 50 yd long, 18 in deep, 12 ft narrowing to 1 ft — **CONFIRMED-digest**. The decoy is therefore late (post-1620) and lordly; the medieval fowler works nets and springes from the dwelling with a punt house/shed at the water — **PLAUSIBLE**. The dovecote is a manorial monopoly structure and not the fowler's — **PLAUSIBLE** (HE lists DOVE COTES as a farmstead type, CONFIRMED list only).

*Hunter's lodge (L180/L724).* Two distinct things. The lordly lodge: Writtle (1211) — moat about 50 ft wide and 8 ft deep enclosing 250 ft x 150 ft; first period kitchen, chamber, chapel and gaol; 14th-century hall and chapel moved west; 15th-century cloister and great chamber at the west end with the kitchen still at the east; a 1478 barn — **CONFIRMED** (http://www.gatehouse-gazetteer.info/English%20sites/3955.html fetched 2026-08-23, summarising Rahtz 1969). Queen Bower, New Forest: a moated rectangular platform about 55 m x 45 m, banks up to 7 m wide and 1 m high, moat up to 8 m wide and 1.2 m deep with leats, an internal mound 11 m across and 1.8 m high, Devon slate finds, named in a 1428 document as ruinous, £200 spent 1432–40; Historic England's class text: lodges "usually moated, were built in the forests to provide temporary accommodation for visiting royalty or nobility"; seven lodge sites in the New Forest — **CONFIRMED** (https://historicengland.org.uk/listing/the-list/list-entry/1016524 opened in browser 2026-08-23). Denny: a forester's lodge possibly converted for Edward III's mid-14th-century hunting, excavated 2016 — **CONFIRMED** (https://www.newforestnpa.gov.uk/news/archaeological-excavations-to-uncover-new-forests-medieval-past fetched 2026-08-23; no dimensions). Clarendon is the palace end: 240 m x 80 m, the queen's 1250s apartments a hall, chapel, three chambers and wardrobe on two floors, rooms 40 ft long — **CONFIRMED** (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarendon_Palace fetched 2026-08-23). The realistic hamlet form is the forester's/keeper's house: a dwelling that became an administrative office in the High Middle Ages, with a simpler "forester's hut" for overnight use in remote forest — **CONFIRMED** (Forester's lodge page). Game larder: octagonal, louvred, slate/marble floors, hooks and rails — earliest documented c. 1750 (Farnborough Hall) and "no discussion of medieval equivalents" — **CONFIRMED** (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Game_larder fetched 2026-08-23); the medieval equivalent is the LARDER class inside the lodge. Kennels: a Jagdschloss programme includes stables for horses and dogs, kennels, equipment stores, kitchens, pantries, ice cellars and servants' quarters — **CONFIRMED** (https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagdschloss fetched 2026-08-23).

**(b) Measured.**

- Meare Fish House 12.4 x 6.6 m; 3 + 2 rooms; ponds 20–30 x 5 m — CONFIRMED (HE 1008018).
- Hastings net shop 8 ft (2.45 m) square, later ~10 ft (3 m) square; up to 3 storeys; hatch 2 ft square — CONFIRMED.
- Liberty Cellars plot 160 x 96 ft — CONFIRMED. St Endellion: no dimensions in the entry — CONFIRMED absence.
- Naust: Kinsarvik 34 x 13 m; Steinkjer ~40 m — CONFIRMED (no.wikipedia).
- Sheepcotes: 23–65 x 6–10 m; one 3.7 m high — CONFIRMED (Dyer via Bothwell page). Bothwell 47 x 7.5 m, walls 0.6 m, annexe 2 m — CONFIRMED. Crawley ditch 60 perches (~300 m) — CONFIRMED (AGHR PDF). Digest variant "50 m x 7.5 m" for Bothwell — CONFIRMED-digest, superseded by the fetched 47 m.
- Bee boles: 9–14 in deep (most, England); Kent brick 9–10 in; 10 or 14–15 in wide; 2.5–4 ft high; skep 10 in diameter; stone-wall boles 18–30 x 15–26 x 14–21 in (digest) — CONFIRMED / CONFIRMED-digest.
- Decoy: pond ~2 acres / 8 acres wood; pipes ≤70 yd, 20 ft to 2 ft; screens 6 ft, 3 ft apart, 2 ft dog jumps; 4–6 (8) pipes — CONFIRMED. Boarstall 4 pipes ~50 yd, 18 in deep, 12 ft to 1 ft — CONFIRMED-digest.
- Writtle moat 50 ft wide, 8 ft deep; island 250 x 150 ft — CONFIRMED. Queen Bower platform 55 x 45 m; bank 7 m x 1 m; moat 8 m x 1.2 m; mound 11 m x 1.8 m — CONFIRMED. Clarendon 240 x 80 m; rooms 40 ft — CONFIRMED.
- No measured figures found for: farmhouse dairy rooms (searched: HE farm types PDF 403; Wikipedia Dairy empty); wool house; forester's lodge plan (Denny undimensioned); punt house; medieval smoke house.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**

- Most entries are landscapes with a shed: the beekeeper (boles are less common than open skeps — CONFIRMED), the wildfowler (the decoy is post-1620 and gentry-owned — CONFIRMED), the shepherd (the sheepcote is a stock building and the wheeled hut is 19th-century — CONFIRMED), the thorp fisher (nausts are not dwellings — CONFIRMED).
- The one medieval fish house (Meare) is an abbot's; the net shop and pilchard palace are 19th-century in their surviving form — so the "fish house with a room program" is either lordly or post-era.
- King John's Hunting Lodge, Axbridge, is a c. 1468 wool-merchant's house with ground-floor shops and a name first printed in 1915 — CONFIRMED (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_John%27s_Hunting_Lodge,_Axbridge fetched 2026-08-23): a misnomer, do not cite as a lodge.
- The game larder has no medieval equivalent as a detached building — CONFIRMED; the Bienenhaus is 19th-century in Europe — CONFIRMED; Bothwell sheepcote had no internal features at all — CONFIRMED (so the "shepherd's chamber in the cote" is PLAUSIBLE only).
- Negation result: as for Family J, no dedicated negation query ran (search budget exhausted); the negation checks were made by fetching the specific disconfirming pages listed above, each of which returned a positive counterexample. Null result: no source was found for a medieval separate dairy building in a village context.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**

- Fishing community (thorp): no building; racks, noust and a smoke-hut are fixtures; the naust is a single long cell with a gable door to the water.
- Fisher's landing (hamlet): net store = one cell; at the net-shop form, storeys are stacked cells linked by ladder through a 2-ft hatch (stair type: ladder) — CONFIRMED; at the cellar form (village-prosperous, LATE era) a COURTYARD with an open-fronted arcaded range (GALLERY/loggia class) and an EXTERNAL stone stair to first-floor offices — CONFIRMED. Meare (lordly): ground floor THROUGH_ROOM enfilade of three cells with two external doors; upper floor two chambers by external stair (stair type: straight external) — CONFIRMED.
- Shepherd / collective: sheepcote = a long single-volume building divided into bays with partitions and several doors (Crawley accounts, CONFIRMED), THROUGH_ROOM along its length; no stair; fold yard (barton) enclosure outside.
- Dairy farmer: the DAIRY is a cell off the farmhouse's service end (THROUGH_ROOM from kitchen/cross-passage); the cheese loft above by ladder/winder — PLAUSIBLE.
- Beekeeper: none.
- Wildfowler: decoyman's cottage = ordinary dwelling; pipes and screens are landscape; no stair.
- Hunter's lodge (hamlet, keeper's): a two-cell dwelling, LOBBY or THROUGH_ROOM, with detached kennels and stable; ladder/winder to a loft. Hunter's lodge (village, lordly): moated platform, hall + chamber + chapel + kitchen + gaol as separate or linked ranges (Writtle period 1, CONFIRMED), SCREENS_PASSAGE hall at prosperous grade, and at the 15th-century ceiling a CLOISTER (GALLERY class) linking great chamber and hall (Writtle period 3, CONFIRMED); stair type winder to the great chamber.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**

- Fishing: STORE (net loft: ANY light, ventilated, upper cell or stacked cells; nets hung from beams), salt pit (a floor fixture in the cellar wing — CONFIRMED), smoke house (a cell with a hearth, LATE era — CONFIRMED at St Endellion as mid-19th-century). Boats: the naust is a STORE class of its own (long, water-gable).
- Shepherd: hay in the sheepcote bays (STORE fixture within the stock building); wool house at the manor (STORE, dry, lockable) — PLAUSIBLE.
- Dairy: §453 DAIRY (north, no hearth, slate shelves, small-to-medium bucket) with CHEESE LOFT above (ATTIC/GARRET_STORE variant) — PLAUSIBLE on fixtures; the class itself is CONFIRMED as an HE farmstead type.
- Beekeeper: bee boles = wall FIXTURES (too small to be a cell: 9–14 in deep); winter storage of skeps in "a dark cool building or cellar" — CONFIRMED (Walker §9), i.e. CELLAR/UNDERCROFT adjacency.
- Wildfowler: net/punt STORE shed at the water — PLAUSIBLE; the decoy house (a hide/shelter at the pipes) is a fixture, not a cell.
- Hunter's lodge: LARDER (north, no hearth, hooks and rails — the game-larder fixtures projected back as an interior cell, CONFIRMED fixtures, medieval siting PLAUSIBLE); pelt-drying loft (ATTIC); a gaol cell at Writtle (CONFIRMED — an outlier the grammar may treat as ROOM_KINDS.concealed/strongroom); kennels and stable as detached cells; ice cellar only in the continental Jagdschloss programme (CONFIRMED list, post-1500).

**(f) Typed proposal.**

- `FISHING_SHORE` (thorp L127): no building; fixtures `net_rack`, `noust` (boat pull-up), `smoke_hut`, `fish_drying_flake`. NO TYPED HOME. buckets: `wetTrade`, `noxious` (smell). Licensing: water adjacency mandatory.
- `FISHERS_LANDING` (hamlet L171): floor = landing + racks + salting trough; rung = net store (HOME: ROOM_KINDS.store; FURNISHING_KINDS.rack, barrel); ceiling (village-prosperous, era LATE) = `FISH_CELLAR_COURT` courtyard with arcaded open range, net lofts over, salt pits, pressing niches, external stair to counting room (ROOM_KINDS.counting). Lordly variant `FISH_HOUSE_MONASTIC` (Meare: 12.4 x 6.6 m, 3 + 2 rooms, chief fisherman lodges above — ROOM_KINDS.lodging). buckets: `wetTrade`, `noxious`, `heavyFloor` (hogsheads ground floor only).
- `SHEEPCOTE` (thorp L136 collective; hamlet L243): floor = fold (barton) enclosure + one sheepcote 23–65 x 6–10 m in bays (HOME: ROOM_KINDS.stall is the nearest; strictly a stock building, NO TYPED HOME for a shepherd's chamber); rung = hay bay + shepherd's lodging bay (PLAUSIBLE); ceiling = two or three cotes with a ditched barton (Crawley 1305–6). fixtures: `hurdle_fold`, `hay_bay`, `lambing_pen`. buckets: `seasonal` (winter occupation), `manure`. Licensing: wheeled hut only at era LATE+ (post-1800 in truth, so outside the window; state it).
- `DAIRY_ROOM` (hamlet L236 / village L609): a §453 DAIRY cell of the dwelling (NORTH light, no hearth, slate shelves, adjacency = kitchen/service end) + `CHEESE_LOFT` above; village rung = detached dairy house (`VACCARY_DAIRY`, PLAUSIBLE). HOME: §453 DAIRY; no ROOM_KINDS match (ROOM_KINDS.back is the nearest). buckets: `cool`, `wetTrade` (scalding, washing).
- `APIARY_WALL` (village L674): fixtures only — `bee_bole` (9–14 in deep, 10–15 in wide, 2.5–4 ft up, south-facing, sets of 2–10), `skep_stand`, `skep_winter_store` (cellar adjacency). NO TYPED HOME. Continental `BIENENHAUS` is era POST (19th c.) — refuse in-window.
- `DECOY_AND_FOWLERS_HOUSE` (village L717): floor = fowler's dwelling + `punt_shed` + net store; ceiling (era LATE, post-1620, prosperity HIGH, gentry-owned) = `DUCK_DECOY` landscape (2-acre pond, 8-acre wood, 4–6 pipes 50–70 yd, screens 6 ft) + `decoy_house` hide + decoyman's cottage. NO TYPED HOME. buckets: `wet`, `quiet` (siting law: seclusion). Note: dovecote is a lord's.
- `KEEPERS_LODGE` (hamlet L180): a two-cell dwelling (HOME: ROOM_KINDS.hall, chamber) + detached `kennel`, `stable`, `pelt_loft`, interior LARDER. `HUNTING_LODGE_MOATED` (village L724, lordly): moated platform 55 x 45 m (Queen Bower) / 250 x 150 ft (Writtle); hall, chamber, chapel, kitchen, gaol (HOME: ROOM_KINDS.hall, chamber, kitchen, concealed/strongroom for the gaol); ceiling = cloister link + great chamber (GALLERY). buckets: `moated`, `seasonal` (occupied on royal visits only — CONFIRMED "temporary accommodation"). Licensing: forest/park siting mandatory; era EARLY–MID for the moated form (lodges "ruinous" by 1428).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**

Five of eight entries resolve to fixtures of the dwelling, so the engine needs a first-class "fixture-of-host" pull that attaches bee boles, racks, nousts, folds and a dairy cell to an existing house rather than minting a building. The §453 DAIRY class must be allowed to fire as the *only* output of an economy entry (Dairy farmer). The sheepcote needs a long stock-building class with bays and several doors that is neither hall nor barn. The fish cellar and the lordly lodge both need the COURTYARD-with-GALLERY pull (the same one the inn yard uses), and the lodge additionally needs the moated-platform siting primitive with a seasonal-occupancy flag. The decoy needs an era gate at 1620 and a prosperity gate (gentry), landing on the existing era and prosperity flags; no new flag is needed for it.

**(h) Continental vs English.**

The English register is parochial in two directions: it has almost no vernacular fish building before 1800 (Meare is monastic; net shops and palaces are 19th-century), whereas Scandinavia has the measured naust tradition (34 x 13 m at Kinsarvik) as a normal coastal outbuilding from the Iron Age on. Conversely, the English sheepcote (23–65 m, cruck-framed, documented from 1208) is the best-measured pastoral building in Europe and the Cistercian vaccary its cattle analogue; the continental equivalent is the Alpine Sennhütte/Alm dairy, which I did not fetch (PLAUSIBLE). For hunting, the Jagdschloss programme (stables, kennels, ice cellar, trophy hall; Castel del Monte 1240–50; Fontainebleau 1528) is a palace class the English moated lodge only approaches at Clarendon; the German Forsthaus is the direct analogue of the New Forest keeper's lodge. Bee houses are a German-Swiss-Slovene 19th-century development; in-window the continental apiary is, like the English, skeps and log hives in the open.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.

## §15 · FAMILY M — stables & animal trade: Stable yard (hamlet L208) · Stable master (village L667) · Stable district (town L1036) · Pack animal trader (hamlet L229)

*Researched by sub-lane MNO (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

**(a) Analogue.**

The catalog's four entries map onto four historically distinct things, and the dossier should keep them apart: (1) a farmyard stable of 2–6 stalls (hamlet L208 / L229); (2) a commercial horse-keeping business — the post-horse/livery stable of a road town (village L667); (3) not a building at all but a street of inn-yards with their stabling, plus an open horse market (town L1036).

*The stable range as a measured interior.* The medieval stable was a single-aisle or double-aisle range of tie-stalls (standing stalls): the horse faces the wall, a manger/hay-rack runs along the wall, partitions are "solid ... or half-solid/half-barred", the horse is tied and cannot turn (University of Exeter Warhorse project, "What were medieval stables like?", https://medievalwarhorse.exeter.ac.uk/2022/04/07/what-were-medieval-stables-like/, fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**). The same source is explicit that standing stable buildings are "largely post-medieval in date", so medieval stall form is inferred from excavation, documents, and horse-teeth wear, not from surviving fittings (**CONFIRMED**). Loose boxes are a later fitting: Dunster Castle's stalls "date from the late 18th and early 19th century" even though the building is of the 1660s (**CONFIRMED**, same page); I would date the loose box as a standard fitting to the 18th century at the earliest (**PLAUSIBLE**). Floor: paved stone with a drain channel at Dunster; at Kenilworth a "line of demarcation" suggesting an earlier plank floor (**CONFIRMED**). Above: "hayloft and quarters on a second storey" are likely, with a hay-drop identified (**CONFIRMED**, Exeter). A 13th-century excavated stable at Veselí nad Moravou (Czech, dendro c. 1228) was timber-framed with hurdle-and-daub infill, posts every 2 m, a wooden floor in its second phase, a 1.6 m entrance with raised thresholds, and a 1.6 m-thick dung deposit; the authors estimate c. 7 horses and state that medieval stables "are quite rare in the Central European archaeological record" (PLOS One 2014, https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0089273, fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**; one excavated example).

*Structural imperatives.* Weight: hay loft over (fire load, and a dry loft is the horse's larder); water: a trough and pump/well in the yard; smell/drainage: a paved floor falling to a central or rear channel, muck-heap outside; light: low, small windows on the manger wall; fire: no hearth in the stable range itself — the only fire in the yard is the farrier's forge, which sits in a separate lean-to or across the yard (**PLAUSIBLE** synthesis over the confirmed plan evidence). A horse needs a door of roughly 4 ft+ clear width; the one measured medieval entrance I found is 1.6 m (Veselí, **CONFIRMED**).

*The farrier is not the blacksmith.* London farriers organised in 1356 and were chartered in 1674 (Wikipedia, Worshipful Company of Farriers, fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**); "a village blacksmith was often a farrier but very few farriers were blacksmiths", and before the 1840s the farrier was also the horse-doctor (**CONFIRMED-digest**, exploreyourgenealogy.co.uk / lambourn.org). So the catalog's hamlet "stableman provides basic farriery" is correct in kind: shoeing and doctoring at the stable yard, not general smithing (cross-ref the smithy family).

*Great household stables (the ceiling of the form).* Kenilworth (1553, John Dudley, £4,110): the 1563 survey gives "180 ft long by 21 ft wide" with 30 "rooms" for great horses and 20 for geldings; the modern building is c. 49 m x 10 m, sandstone ground storey, timber-framed upper storey with decorative panelling (Exeter Warhorse, **CONFIRMED**; greatbarns.org.uk, http://www.greatbarns.org.uk/kenilworth_castle_stables.html, fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**, adds the large double door and the later removal of the upper floor). Hampton Court Royal Mews (1537, Christopher Dickinson, £130): "arranged around a square courtyard", hay lofts and tack rooms above, officers of the stable in the attics; Elizabeth's coach house of 1570 was "fourteen bays measuring 16' by 75'" with a great central arch (cuboetexcuboj.blogspot.com, fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED** as fetched, but an unreferenced blog; The National Archives holds a "Ground plan of His Majesty's stables" at Hampton Court, C2016598 — the catalogue page fetched empty). Continental: Munich's Alte Münze was built 1563–67 as Duke Albrecht V's Marstall-and-Kunstkammer — a four-wing block round a three-tier arcaded court, the whole ground floor three-aisled vaulted halls on red marble columns for horses and carriages, library and Kunstkammer above (**CONFIRMED-digest**, de.wikipedia / aviewoncities); the Munich court riding school ran 1580–1945 (**CONFIRMED-digest**). Stuttgart's Marstall: no source surfaced (searched; null).

*Inn-yard stabling (the line to R-INST-4).* The George, Southwark: the 1847 plan shows stabling extending far back towards Guy's Hospital; an 1855 description notes "a round room for the ostlers in the days of pack-horses, and a stable below ground with steps leading down to it"; the 1670 fire burnt "part of the inn and all the barns and stables" (A London Inheritance, https://alondoninheritance.com/londonpubs/george-inn-borough-high-street-southwark/, fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**; Survey of London vol. 22, https://www.british-history.ac.uk/survey-london/vol22/pp9-30, fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**). On Borough High Street the ground floor on three sides of each yard "consisted mainly of stables", with carriers' offices at ground level (**CONFIRMED-digest**, knowyourlondon). Where the line is: the inn's stable belongs to the inn (R-INST-4) — it is a service range of the lodging house; the livery/post stable is a separate business that hires horses out, and it is the latter that is L667.

*The livery and post-horse stable.* OED's first "livery stable" is 1661 (**CONFIRMED-digest**). But the function is Tudor: by the Tudor period stages were "customarily spaced ten or fifteen miles apart and on heavily used routes commercial livery stables were plentiful"; the 1577 Privy Council enumeration gives Devon 120 inns, 40 taverns, 400 alehouses, most inns probably offering horse-hire; the private traveller paid 2d–3d per mile per horse, had to hire a second horse for the guide (statutory fee 4d), and royal riders paid 1d per mile; royal postmasters had to keep "usually a minimum of six" horses, two for the post-boy, a leather bag, a horn, and a bound register of every horse hired; letters were dated in the "post-room" at a principal stage; in 1585 Exeter hired 16 horses for Sidney's party, "seven horses from local stables and another nine from 'strangers'" at 2s 6d each to Ashburton (Brayshay, "Royal post-horse routes in South West England", Trans. Devonshire Assoc. 1991, PDF fetched and text-extracted 2026-08-23, saved as RINST2-MNO-brayshay-posthorse-1991.pdf/.txt — **CONFIRMED**). The post-horse stable therefore has a room program: stable range (6–16 horses), a "post-room" (office with the register — HOME: ROOM_KINDS.records/counting), saddle room, hay loft, and it is usually AT an inn (Brayshay: horses "kept at inns or hostelries" — **CONFIRMED-digest**).

*The horse market.* Smithfield: Fitzstephen c. 1174 — a smooth field where "every Friday there is a celebrated rendezvous of fine horses to be sold" — an open field outside the walls used for races, football, archery and tournaments (**CONFIRMED-digest**, London Museum / Substack). There is no building: the horse fair is an open-market fixture, with the trade's interiors being the inn-yards round it.

*The packhorse trade.* Packhorse bridges of the 14th–18th centuries are 1.25–2 m wide with low or absent parapets (Carrbridge 2.14 m; Beggar's Bridge 1.98 m; Pack Bridge 1.5 m), and a horse carried 50–100 kg in panniers or creels (**CONFIRMED-digest**, Wikipedia pages). The packhorse inn (Crowdecote, bridge 1709) was a drovers'/carriers' house that became a pub (**CONFIRMED-digest**). The George's "round room for the ostlers in the days of pack-horses" is the only interior I found attributable to the packhorse trade (**CONFIRMED**). The "pannier and saddle store" and "muleteers' corral" are continental/Iberian forms — no measured source surfaced; see (h).

*Prosperity/wear grades.* Floor: an earth-floored lean-to with a rail and a rack, no loft (the hamlet L208/L229). Rungs: paved floor with drain; partitions; loft over with pitching holes; a saddle room; a forge lean-to. Ceiling: a courtyard mews with vaulted ground floor, a riding house, lodging for grooms over (Hampton Court 1537; Munich 1563). Decline sheds first the forge and the tack room (sold off / let), then the partitions (a stable reverts to a barn — literally what Kenilworth did: "the owner converted part of it into a barn", **CONFIRMED**, greatbarns). Live/work: the hamlet stableman lives in the adjoining house; at the livery stable the ostler sleeps over the horses (loft lodging); at the great stable the officers of the stable lodge in the attic (Hampton Court, **CONFIRMED** as above).

**(b) Measured.**
- Standing stall (modern reference used by the Exeter project for back-projection): 4–5 ft wide x 9–10 ft long; Ellis's reading of Kenilworth at 2 m stalls gives 25 stalls per side; aisle 6 ft minimum, 10 ft common, Kenilworth possibly 12 ft (**CONFIRMED**, Exeter Warhorse). Medieval "5 by 10 feet" tie stall (**CONFIRMED-digest**, Wikipedia "Stable"). The figures are back-projected modern standards, not measured medieval stalls — say so in the dossier.
- Kenilworth 1553/1563: 180 ft x 21 ft, 30 + 20 "rooms"; surviving c. 49 m x 10 m; 2 storeys (**CONFIRMED**).
- Veselí nad Moravou c. 1228: 14 m x 2.5 m building (as extracted — the 2.5 m is narrow enough that it may be an aisle or trench width; treat as single-sourced), posts every 2 m, entrance 1.6 m wide, deposit 1.6 m thick, c. 7 horses (**CONFIRMED**, one excavated example).
- Hampton Court coach house 1570: 14 bays, 16 ft x 75 ft (**CONFIRMED** from blog, unreferenced). Mews 1537 cost £130 (**CONFIRMED** blog).
- Post stable: minimum 6 horses; a 16-horse hire from 7 local + 9 strangers' stables (**CONFIRMED**, Brayshay).
- Packhorse bridge widths 1.25–2 m; 2.14 m; 1.98 m; 1.5 m (**CONFIRMED-digest**). Packhorse load 50–100 kg (**CONFIRMED-digest**).
- Horse door width 4 ft+ and height c. 7 ft; loft height 6–8 ft to the tie; range depth 16–21 ft for a single row with aisle — the Kenilworth 21 ft and the Hampton Court 16 ft bays bracket this (**PLAUSIBLE** except the two anchors).
- No measured figures found for: a livery stable's plan; Stuttgart Marstall; Ottoman caravan stables as a stand-alone stable (searched: "Marstall Stuttgart Munich Hofmarstall", "Ottoman han stables camel stalls").

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**
- Negation search ("medieval peasant farm horses stabled longhouse byre no separate stable"): the byre-house/longhouse, with animals at one end and humans at the other, was adopted mainly in the 13th century in Wales and northern England and only gradually replaced by separate animal buildings (**CONFIRMED-digest**, Academia.edu paper on the byre-house). So at hamlet tier the honest default is that horses share the byre or a lean-to; a dedicated "stable yard" is the upper rung, and the Central European record has "quite rare" medieval stables because horses grazed out seasonally (**CONFIRMED**, PLOS One).
- The surviving fittings are all late: Dunster stalls late-18th/19th c.; every standing stable the Exeter project could visit is post-medieval (**CONFIRMED**). Loose boxes are not a c.1200–1700 fitting.
- The "stable district" is not a building: Borough High Street had 23 coaching inns (**CONFIRMED-digest**), each a yard with stables on three sides — the district is the street plus the open Smithfield-type horse fair. The catalog's L1036 should read as a street-parti, not a hall.
- "Livery stable" as a word is 1661; the function is at least 1570s (Brayshay). Use the function, date the word.
- Kenilworth's 180 ft claim is documentary (1563) and the dimensions survive; but it is a Master of the Horse's prodigy building — a ceiling, never a prior.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**
- L208 Stable yard (hamlet): `THROUGH_ROOM` only — one range, doors at the end or side, aisle as the circulation; ladder to loft. Licensed at any era; prosperity floor.
- L229 Pack animal trader (hamlet): as L208 plus a yard/corral as the circulation space; animals are the stock, so the "shop" is the yard. Ladder stair.
- L667 Stable master / post stable (village): `THROUGH_ROOM` range + yard; the inn's `GALLERY` over the yard where the post stable sits at an inn (the George's galleries at first and second floor, lower one on cantilever beams — **CONFIRMED**, Survey of London). Winder stair to the ostlers' loft; a "post-room" opening off the yard.
- L1036 Stable district (town): the circulation class is the STREET + repeated inn-yard parti: gate arch (the inn's "high arch" — **CONFIRMED-digest**), yard, stable ranges on three sides, a second and third yard behind (White Hart had three yards — **CONFIRMED-digest**). `GALLERY` on the lodging side, `THROUGH_ROOM` in the stable ranges. Great stables (Hampton Court, Munich) add a square `COURTYARD` with arcaded `GALLERY` (Munich three tiers — **CONFIRMED-digest**) and a winder/spiral stair to the officers' attics. Measured widths: Kenilworth aisle up to 12 ft; coach-house bay 16 ft (**CONFIRMED**).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**
- `ATTIC`/`GARRET_STORE` = hay loft, lightReq NONE, over the stalls, pitching holes/hay drop to the rack (**CONFIRMED** hay-drop at Kenilworth). Adjacency: directly over the stalls; a stair/ladder from the aisle.
- `STORE` = harness/tack/saddle room, lightReq ANY, small, dry, adjacent to the aisle door; at the hamlet this collapses to a FIXTURE (a rack of pegs in the aisle). Hampton Court lists tack rooms above (**CONFIRMED**).
- `STORE` (fodder/corn bin) — a chest or bin in the aisle: FIXTURE at all tiers below the great stable.
- `CELLAR` is exceptional: the George's "stable below ground with steps" is one recorded urban oddity (**CONFIRMED**); do not license generally.
- Records: the post stable's `post-room` = HOME: ROOM_KINDS.records (register of every horse hired — **CONFIRMED**, Brayshay).
- Forge lean-to: not storage; a `workfloor` with hearth (HOME: ROOM_KINDS.workfloor + FURNISHING_KINDS.hearth).

**(f) Typed proposal.**
- `parti`: `STABLE_RANGE_SINGLE` (one row + aisle, 16–21 ft deep), `STABLE_RANGE_DOUBLE` (two rows + central aisle), `STABLE_COURT` (ranges on 3–4 sides of a yard; gate arch), `INN_YARD_STABLING` (stable ranges owned by an inn — a sub-parti of R-INST-4, not a building of this family), `HORSE_FAIR_GROUND` (open field/market fixture, no interior), `CORRAL_YARD` (L229).
- `functions[]`: floor — `stall_row`, `manger_rack`, `aisle`, `muck_door`; rungs — `paved_drain_floor`, `hayloft_over`, `tack_room`, `farrier_forge_leanto`, `post_room`, `ostlers_loft_lodging`; ceiling — `vaulted_ground_floor`, `coach_house_14bay`, `riding_house`, `officers_attic`.
- `fixtures[]`: rack (HOME: FURNISHING_KINDS.rack), manger (NO TYPED HOME — propose `manger`), trough/pump (NO TYPED HOME), hearth (forge; HOME), strongbox/ledger in the post-room (HOME: strongbox, ledger), pannier racks (NO TYPED HOME).
- Structural buckets: `heavyFloor` (loft hay load), `noxious` (dung/drain — muck-heap adjacency law), `fireTrade` only for the forge lean-to, `waterAccess` (trough).
- Licensing: hamlet x any prosperity x any era → `STABLE_RANGE_SINGLE` or byre-share (floor); village x road-siting (catalog: "essential for any settlement on a road") x 1500+ → `post_room` + 6+ stalls; town x prosperous x 1450+ → `STABLE_COURT`/`INN_YARD_STABLING` multiplied along the approach street; great-house ceiling only at court/castle siting.
- Tags: stalls = HOME: ROOM_KINDS.stall; loft = HOME: store; forge = HOME: workfloor; post-room = HOME: records; tack room = NO TYPED HOME (propose `tack`); coach house = NO TYPED HOME.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**
The engine needs (1) a ROW-OF-CELLS-TOO-SMALL-TO-BE-ROOMS primitive — the stall is a 5 x 10 ft bay, below cell size, repeated N times along a wall with one aisle; (2) a multi-yard street parti where the "district" is K repeats of the inn-yard parti on one street, each with a gate arch; (3) an OPEN FIXTURE type (horse fair) that occupies a field with zero interior; (4) a vertical pair (loft over stalls with a hay drop) that the storage typology must express as adjacency-by-stacking, not by door. The forge lean-to lands on the existing fireTrade pull; the post-room lands on `records`.

**(h) Continental vs English.**
The English register's stable is the inn-yard and the farmyard range; its ceiling is the royal mews. The continental ceiling is the Marstall — a palatial four-wing block where the entire ground floor is vaulted stabling under a Kunstkammer or library (Munich 1563–67, **CONFIRMED-digest**), and the Ottoman/Persian mechanism puts the stable INSIDE the han as a vaulted hall or a second arcaded court (Koza Han's "second, irregularly shaped courtyard surrounded by double arcades serving as the stables" — **CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia Koza Han; see family N). The Iberian muleteers' corral and the posada with its patio are the packhorse-trade interiors the English inn only approximates; no measured source surfaced this session.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.

## §16 · FAMILY N — road transport & caravan: Caravanserai (hamlet L389; village L522) · Waystation (village L515) · Carriers' guild (town L963) · Carriers' hiring hall (town L985) · Caravaneer's post (town L1043) · Caravan masters' exchange (city L1619)

*Researched by sub-lane MNO (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

**(a) Analogue.**

*The caravanserai/han as a measured plan.* The canonical parti is "a single entrance wide enough to permit large or heavily laden beasts such as camels", a central open court "surrounded by a number of identical animal stalls, bays, and chambers", with water, often a small mosque and a hammam; rural ones sat a day's journey apart — in Iran 30–40 km on the plain, c. 10 km in mountains; urban ones were lodging AND depot AND transaction venue for foreign merchants (Wikipedia "Caravanserai", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**). Seljuk sultan han, Sultanhanı (Aksaray), 1229 under Kayqubad I, restored and extended 1278 after fire: 4,900 m² total, open summer court 44 x 58 m, marble portal 13 m high on a 50 m front wall, arcades round the court with stables and "accommodation above", a square kiosk-mosque on piers in the middle of the court, and a covered winter hall with a barrel-vaulted central aisle under a short dome-tower with an oculus (Wikipedia "Sultan Han", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**; Archnet returned 429 on two attempts). The Kayseri Sultan Han's winter hall is 30 x 40 m in 5 aisles x 7 vaulted bays (**CONFIRMED-digest**, Archnet). Ottoman urban han, Koza Han, Bursa, 1490–91: courtyard 45.9 x 37.5 m, two-storey gallery on square piers with barrel vaults, 50 vaulted cells below and 54 above, an octagonal mosque raised on 8 pillars so it "was not soiled by pack animals", a second irregular court with double arcades as the stables, a monumental portal; ground floor = pack animals + shops, upper floor = merchants; it was the silk-cocoon market (Wikipedia "Koza Han", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**). Persian Safavid four-iwan type: Gaz (Abbasi) caravanserai, Isfahan road, 83 x 96 m with four corner towers, stables and stores behind the cells with their doors at the corners of the yard (**CONFIRMED-digest**; Iranica returned 403; Wikipedia "Gaz Caravanserai" 404). Khan al-Mirjan, Baghdad 1359, is "the oldest surviving urban caravanserai"; Wikala al-Ghuri, Cairo 1504–05, is the multi-storey Egyptian form with merchant space below and rental apartments above (**CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia Caravanserai).

*The Mediterranean fondaco.* Fondaco dei Tedeschi, Venice: first built 1228 beside the Rialto, burnt 1505, rebuilt 1505–08: one central court formed by the four ranges, "56 rooms" plus storage, housing "over one hundred merchants, and an equal number of servants"; ground floor rental storerooms reached by water through a five-bay canal loggia; living quarters and communal dining above; merchants handed their weapons to the Fontegher, the Venetian room-assigning official (Wikipedia "Fondaco dei Tedeschi", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**). A digest gave "160 separate residential quarters" — conflicts with 56 rooms; treat the count as contested. No measured court dimensions found (searched). The Arabic funduq "appears as fundaco in Venice, fondaco in Genoa" (**CONFIRMED**, Wikipedia Caravanserai).

*The Hanseatic Kontor, Bryggen (Bergen).* Kontor established c. 1350; the present fabric post-dates the 1702 fire; 62 buildings survive on 1.196 ha; the unit is the gård: "rows of two- to three-storey buildings" gabled to the harbour, separated by "narrow passages running parallel to the docks" that were private courtyards; "offices and dwellings at the front, warehouses in the midsection and assembly rooms ('Schøtstuer'), kitchen facilities and fireproof stone cellars at the back"; the stone kjellere protected valuables against fire (Riksantikvaren, https://www.riksantikvaren.no/en/world-heritage/bryggen-in-bergen/, fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**; Wikipedia "Bryggen", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED** for dates/area). Each gård held up to 15 firms and had its own quay, crane, passage and back kitchen; the schøtstue was the only heated room, where the men ate and met (**CONFIRMED-digest**, hanse.org / visitnordic). The Kontor is the North's answer to the fondaco: the same stack (store below, lodging above, communal hall, single controlled passage) stretched into a row.

*The waystation and its European equivalents.* The Roman mansio is the counterexample the brief asked for: Godmanchester's mansio had stables "beyond the main gate", a colonnaded garden court, bedrooms E and W, reception and dining S, a separate bath and temple; at Wall (Letocetum) the stables and tack rooms adjoined the mansio but were "inaccessible from within", and the court well was 8 x 6.5 ft and 25+ ft deep; mansiones were spaced every 20–30 miles (**CONFIRMED-digest**, roman-britain.co.uk / Godmanchester Museum; the digest gave the Godmanchester length as both "95 foot" and "95 metres" — unresolved). The medieval English relay is the post stage at an inn (family M, Brayshay **CONFIRMED**); the road hospice/maison-Dieu is R-INST-3. So the English "waystation" c. 1200–1700 is either an inn or a hospice — never a fortified animal compound.

*Carriers' yard and carriers' inn.* Taylor's Carriers' Cosmographie (1637) is a primary listing of "the Innes, Ordinaries, Hosteries, and other lodgings in, and neere London, where the Carriers, Waggons, Foote-posts and Higglers, doe usually come": St Albans carriers at the Peacock, Aldersgate Street, every Friday; Abingdon at the George, Bread Street, Wednesdays, leaving Thursdays; Braintree at the Tabard, Gracious Street; Colchester at the Cross Keys; Doncaster at the Bell Savage without Ludgate, Fridays; Taylor reports hostlers and porters giving him "harsh and unsavoury answers" (Map of Early Modern London transcription, https://mapoflondon.uvic.ca/CARR1.htm, fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**). Manchester carriers "every second Thursday at the two-necked Swan in Lad Lane" (**CONFIRMED-digest**). The interior is therefore the inn-yard: stabling on three sides, a carrier's office at ground level, warehouse/cart-house in a back yard (Borough High Street — **CONFIRMED-digest**; the George's stables ran back to Guy's — **CONFIRMED**, family M). The Spur Inn in 1720 was "pretty well resorted unto by Waggons" (**CONFIRMED**, Survey of London vol. 22).

*Carriers' guild / hiring hall.* The London carters formed the "Fraternyte of Seynt Katryne the Virgyn and Marter of Carters" on 19 March 1517, with "the right of fellowship, but not of regulation"; they "held standings or 'carrooms', where they plied for hire"; oversight of "Carts, Carres and Carmen" went to Christ's Hospital in 1582; a joint charter with the Woodmongers followed in 1605 and a long fight (the 1649 Carmen's Remonstrance); the Company got "our first permanent City home" only in 2017 (Worshipful Company of Carmen history, https://thecarmen.co.uk/history/, fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**; Fellowship re-authorised 1668, charter 1946 — **CONFIRMED-digest**). That settles the catalog's two entries: the historical "hiring hall" is a STAND in the street (the carroom) plus the inn-yard where the carrier lodges on his named day; and the carriers' guild had no hall at all. The catalog text for L985 ("Transactional, not institutional ... a yard") is the right instinct.

*Prosperity/wear grades.* Floor: a walled yard with a lean-to round the inside and one gate (the hamlet caravanserai = the Persian robat/ the English carrier's yard). Rungs: cells round the court; a second storey of lodging over the cells with a gallery; a covered winter hall; a well/cistern; a kiosk mosque or chapel; a bath. Ceiling: the sultan han (portal, domed hall, bath) or the fondaco (four-storey court block with water loggia). Decline sheds the upper storey first (cells become stores), then the covered hall's roof, leaving the walled court — which is exactly the form of most ruined hans. Live/work: the han-keeper lodges at the gate; merchants sleep over their goods (Koza Han ground/upper split — **CONFIRMED**); at Bryggen the journeymen slept in unheated lodging and gathered to the single heated schøtstue (**CONFIRMED-digest**).

**(b) Measured.**
- Sultanhanı 1229: 4,900 m²; court 44 x 58 m; portal 13 m; front 50 m (**CONFIRMED**). Kayseri winter hall 30 x 40 m, 5 aisles x 7 bays (**CONFIRMED-digest**).
- Koza Han 1490–91: court 45.9 x 37.5 m; 50 + 54 cells; two storeys (**CONFIRMED**).
- Gaz caravanserai (Safavid): 83 x 96 m, four corner towers (**CONFIRMED-digest**).
- Fondaco dei Tedeschi 1508: 56 rooms; >100 merchants + servants; five-bay water loggia; four storeys (**CONFIRMED**; storey count from digest). No metric dimensions found.
- Bryggen: 1.196 ha; 62 buildings; 2–3 storeys (**CONFIRMED**). Gård widths and passage widths: NOT FOUND (searched "Bryggen gård width metres", "Herteig tenement passage width"; Herteig 1959 Medieval Archaeology 3 is paywalled at T&F and the ADS mirror returned 403 to curl; Kulturpunkt page fetched empty; UNESCO page 403).
- Rural spacing: 30–40 km plain / c. 10 km mountain (Iran); mansio every 20–30 miles (**CONFIRMED** / **CONFIRMED-digest**).
- Letocetum well 8 x 6.5 ft, 25+ ft deep (**CONFIRMED-digest**).
- Gate width for a laden camel/wagon: none measured this session; a 1.6 m stable door (Veselí) is the only measured medieval animal door; a han portal passage of 3–4 m is **PLAUSIBLE**.
- No measured figures found for: any English carriers' inn yard, the carroom stands, a cart house/wagon shed (searched Borough inns, Carmen).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**
- Negation search ("caravanserai England medieval equivalent ... no caravanserai"): Constable's *Housing the Stranger in the Mediterranean World* — "fondacos are not synonymous with inns and hotels: in this aspect North-West Europe functioned differently from the Mediterranean"; Italian traders in Bruges/Antwerp met "at particular squares", which "did not have the same function as the Mediterranean fondacos" (**CONFIRMED-digest**, Cambridge chapter abstract / connectedmediterranean). England's organised traveller support was the inn network (**CONFIRMED-digest**, medievalists.net). Verdict: the English register has NO caravanserai; the inn (R-INST-4) plus the carrier's named inn-yard does its job; the catalog's hamlet/village caravanserai is a setting-agnostic entry whose parti is the walled single-gate court.
- The carriers' "guild" had no hall and no regulatory power (**CONFIRMED**); the hiring "hall" was a street stand (**CONFIRMED**). Both catalog entries over-institutionalise if rendered as buildings.
- Fondaco room count is contested (56 vs 160) between two tertiary sources; the plan description is consistent.
- The Roman mansio is a deliberate pre-register counterexample: a state relay with an integrated bath and separately-walled stables — nothing in c.1200–1700 England reproduces it.
- The Bryggen fabric is post-1702; the plan type is medieval by excavation (pier c. 1100, Kontor c. 1350) — preserve that qualifier.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**
- L389/L522 Caravanserai: `LOBBY`/`VESTIBULE` = the single gate passage with a guard/keeper's cell; the court is the circulation; cells open directly off the arcade (`THROUGH_ROOM` at ground, cells are dead-end); `GALLERY` at first floor over the arcade (Koza Han two-storey gallery — **CONFIRMED**); the covered hall is a multi-aisle `THROUGH_ROOM`. Stair: straight flights in the court corners (the Koza mosque has a marble stair — **CONFIRMED**); winder at village prosperity. Era: any (setting-agnostic).
- L515 Waystation: in the English register a `CROSS_PASSAGE` inn or a walled yard with a gate `LOBBY`; the fortified-compound variant is the han floor rung.
- L963 Carriers' guild: no building — if the engine insists, a room hired in an inn or a hall borrowed from another company (the Carmen still dine in other halls — **CONFIRMED**).
- L985 Carriers' hiring hall: the STREET stand (carroom) + inn-yard `GALLERY` and a ground-floor office; circulation = the gate arch and yard.
- L1043 / L1619 Caravaneer's post / Caravan masters' exchange: the urban han parti (Koza) — gate `VESTIBULE`, court, two-tier `GALLERY`, cells; the fondaco adds a water `VESTIBULE` (five-bay loggia) and a communal dining hall reached by a `STAIR_HALL` (grand stair at city prosperity). The Bryggen Kontor offers the alternative: a long private passage (`GALLERY` as alley) with the schøtstue at the back.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**
- Cells = `STORE` (lightReq NONE/ANY, one bay each, lockable door, dead-end off the arcade); at Koza the ground cells are shops/stores and the upper cells lodging (**CONFIRMED**) — the engine needs a cell that is STORE below and `lodging` above on the same footprint.
- Stables as `STORE`-class hall: the Seljuk covered hall / the Koza second court / the Persian stable behind the cells (**CONFIRMED** / **CONFIRMED-digest**); adjacency law: reached from the court, never through a cell.
- Fondaco: `CELLAR`/ground `STORE` "accessible by water" (**CONFIRMED**) — a water-gate storage adjacency the English register lacks.
- Bryggen: fireproof stone `CELLAR` (kjellere) at the BACK of the gård, warehouses in the midsection, the single heated room at the back (**CONFIRMED**); a hanging/drying loft for stockfish is **PLAUSIBLE**.
- Carrier's inn: cart-house/wagon shed and a back warehouse (`STORE`) in a rear yard (**CONFIRMED-digest**, White Hart's third yard "possibly storage areas for carriages or carts").
- Water: the well/cistern is a FIXTURE of the court (Letocetum well 8 x 6.5 ft — **CONFIRMED-digest**; han water fountain — **CONFIRMED**).

**(f) Typed proposal.**
- `parti`: `WALLED_COURT_SINGLE_GATE` (floor; L389/L515/L985-yard), `ARCADED_COURT_CELLS` (rung; L522), `TWO_TIER_GALLERY_HAN` (L1043/L1619), `COVERED_WINTER_HALL` (attachable to any court parti; Seljuk), `FONDACO_WATER_COURT` (city, waterside), `KONTOR_ROW` (Bryggen: passage + stacked firms), `STREET_STAND` (L985/L963: no interior; a fixture on a street segment), `CARRIERS_INN_YARD` (a sub-parti of R-INST-4 with a named-day timetable).
- `functions[]`: floor — `gate_passage`, `keepers_cell`, `court`, `well`, `animal_lines`; rungs — `cell_ring`, `gallery_upper`, `lodging_over_cells`, `stable_hall`, `second_stable_court`, `court_kiosk` (mosque/chapel), `bath`; ceiling — `domed_hall`, `monumental_portal`, `water_loggia`, `communal_hall` (schøtstue / fondaco dining).
- `fixtures[]`: well/cistern (NO TYPED HOME), mangers/tethering rings in the court (NO TYPED HOME), lockable cell doors (FIXTURE), strongbox + ledger in the keeper's cell (HOME), crate/barrel in cells (HOME).
- Structural buckets: `defensible` (blank outer wall, one gate — new bucket), `waterAccess` (court well; fondaco canal), `heavyFloor` (goods over/under), `noxious` (animal court; the raised mosque is the historical answer to it — **CONFIRMED**).
- Licensing: hamlet/village caravanserai only under `EUROPEAN_FANTASY_BASE` setting-agnostic flags (desert/steppe siting); waystation (village, road siting) defaults to the inn/hospice in an English-register world and to `WALLED_COURT_SINGLE_GATE` otherwise; carriers' guild/hiring hall at town x 1500+ → `STREET_STAND` + `CARRIERS_INN_YARD` (no hall); city exchange → `TWO_TIER_GALLERY_HAN` or `FONDACO_WATER_COURT` (waterside) or `KONTOR_ROW` (northern port).
- Tags: cells = HOME: ROOM_KINDS.store / lodging; court = NO TYPED HOME (propose `court`); stable hall = HOME: stall; keeper's cell = HOME: counting; communal hall = HOME: common; gate passage = NO TYPED HOME (propose `gate`).

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**
(1) A RING-OF-IDENTICAL-CELLS-ROUND-A-COURT generator with a single gate, where the ring can be one or two storeys and the upper storey is a gallery — the engine's current room-list model cannot express "N identical bays". (2) A "no building" outcome for an institution: the carriers' guild and hiring hall must be able to resolve to a street fixture plus a dependency on an existing inn entry (a cross-entry reference the catalog lacks). (3) A TIMETABLE attribute on an inn (the named carrier day) — not a room but a load-bearing behaviour. (4) Water-gate storage (fondaco) as a storage adjacency that requires a canal/quay edge — lands on the port/quay pull (family O). (5) A `defensible` bucket distinct from `fortified`: the han is blank-walled, not towered (Gaz has corner towers — the exception).

**(h) Continental vs English.**
The English register is parochial here in the strongest sense: it has no caravanserai, no fondaco and no Kontor, and its carriers have no hall — the inn-yard, the street stand and the named day carry the entire function (Taylor 1637, Carmen 1517 — **CONFIRMED**). The Mediterranean mechanism is the funduq/fondaco (state-supervised court block, stores below and lodging above, Venice 1228/1508), the Ottoman mechanism the urban han (Koza 1491) and the bedesten for valuables, the Persian the four-iwan road caravanserai a day apart, and the Hanseatic mechanism the Kontor row (Bryggen) with its single heated schøtstue. The Iberian lonja (exchange hall) and the French halle belong to the market family rather than here.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None — but the hamlet/village caravanserai must be flagged setting-agnostic: in a European-register world it is expressed as `WALLED_COURT_SINGLE_GATE`, not as a han with a kiosk mosque.
