## §17 · FAMILY O — water: docks, boats, ships: River ferry (village L483) · River boatyard (village L492) · Docks/port facilities (town L955; city L1603) · Barge and river transport company (city L1611) · Shipyard (town L993; city L1627)

*Researched by sub-lane MNO (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

**(a) Analogue.**

*The ferry.* There is no building beyond a cottage: the ferryman's house stands at the hard, with a landing stage (a timber stage or a paved/gravelled hard), a chain or rope post where a rope ferry existed, and the flat-bottomed boat drawn up or moored; the crossing right is a franchise held by the family (catalog L483 says so) — **PLAUSIBLE** (no primary fetched; searched within budget only indirectly). Say so in the dossier: the ferry is a fixture at a river edge with a 1-bay dwelling.

*The boatyard.* The Scandinavian naust is the one measured medieval boat-house type: Kinsarvik's medieval naust, which "likely housed two leidang ships", was 34 m long and 13 m wide; remains near Steinkjer reach 40 m; construction is three stone (or turf) walls with the gable open or wooden-doored to the water, a timber frame above the walls, an earth/sand/stone floor (never planked), a sloped ramp into the water, a turf or tiled roof, and the building is deliberately airy to dry boats and gear (no.wikipedia "Naust", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**; archaeological examples). The English/Thames boatyard was an open riverbank with a slip, a shed, a sawpit and a store; the Thames barge yards and the steam chest for bending planks are 18th–19th-century features and must be dated out of the register (**PLAUSIBLE**; the steam box is later than c.1700). The saw pit is the one universal boatyard/shipyard fixture: earthwork remains at Woolforddyke measure 2 x 1.6 m and 0.4 m deep (a plough-filled relic), Royal Dockyard pits were elm-lined with steps or a ladder, medieval sketches of pit-sawing appear c. 1250, and Corneliszoon's wind sawmill is 1590s (Wikipedia "Saw pit", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**).

*The quay and its crane.* Stationary harbour cranes are a medieval invention: Utrecht 1244, Antwerp 1263, Bruges 1288, Hamburg 1291, none in England before 1331; two types — the Flemish/Dutch gantry crane pivoting on a central axle, and the German tower crane with windlass and treadwheels in a solid tower and only jib and roof rotating; treadwheel diameters "4 m or larger"; capacity 2–3 tons; usually double treadwheels; about fifteen survive; Italian ports kept unloading by ramps (Wikipedia "Treadwheel crane", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**; cites Matheus 1996, Matthies 1992). Gdańsk Krantor: wooden predecessor 1363 (mentioned 1367), rebuilt 1442–44 as two brick towers flanking a gate with the timber crane between; c. 30 m high; two pairs of treadwheels, up to 6.5 m in diameter — "the largest diameter ... of all known treadwheel cranes"; the lower set lifts c. 11 m, the upper c. 27 m, up to 4 t; "four and more" winch-men, for cargo and for stepping masts (de.wikipedia "Krantor (Danzig)", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**; en.wikipedia confirms 30 m and the towers). Lüneburg's Alter Kran: first mentioned 1330, present fabric 1797; a fixed circular base 8 m across, a 9 m king-post, a 5 m treadwheel; it served the salt works' shipments to Lübeck (de.wikipedia, fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**). The crane HOUSE is therefore either a tower (German) or a roofed timber gantry (Flemish); the treadwheel's 4–6.5 m diameter sets the minimum interior height.

*The wharf/staithe and the harbour.* London's Queenhithe is "the only surviving inlet" on the City waterfront, a Saxon hithe (c. 886–899) with timber revetments and wharves, upstream of the bridge so large ships could not reach it (Wikipedia "Queenhithe", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**). The Thames waterfront excavations (Trig Lane, Billingsgate, Custom House) showed reclamation in successive timber revetments, each advancing a few metres into the river — I could not fetch a measured report this session (searched only via Queenhithe; Historic England/MOLA not reached), so the per-stage figure is **PLAUSIBLE** and an open question. The Cobb at Lyme Regis: first mention 1328 "damaged by storms"; built of oak piles and boulders; described in 1685 as "an immense mass of stone, of a shape of a demi-lune" laid without mortar; south arm 1690s; rebuilt 1793 with mortar; 1820 in Portland stone (Wikipedia "The Cobb", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**). The Hanseatic harbour pairs the Kran with the Waage (weigh-house) and the Speicher row — Bryggen's each-gård-has-a-crane (family N, **CONFIRMED-digest**) is the same mechanism at tenement scale. Bristol: the 1240s diversion of the Frome into "Saint Augustine's Reach" created the medieval quays (Wikipedia "Bristol Harbour", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**); the page does NOT mention a 1239 dry dock — see (c).

*The shipyard.* Before the dry dock, ships were careened on a hard-sand beach at low tide, or berthed in soft mud with a "timber, brushwood and clay wall" built round the hull (Grace Dieu, 1434, Southampton) (Wikipedia "Dry dock", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**). Portsmouth 1495 (Henry VII) is "the first recorded dry dock": "a timber-lined excavation, with the seaward end closed off by a temporary revetted bank", emptied by a pump "possibly in the form of a bucket-chain powered by horses"; the first warship built at Portsmouth was the Sweepstake of 1497; a century later the dock was "filled with rubble"; by 1660 a rope house 1,095 ft (334 m) long; a mast pond 1664 and mast house 1669; a double dry dock 1656; Dummer's second dock 1699 (Wikipedia "Dry dock" and "HMNB Portsmouth", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**; dimensions of the 1495 dock not found). Venice, Arsenale: Arsenale Vecchio 12th c.; Nuovo 1325–26 (138,600 m²); Novissimo 1473–1573 to c. 24 ha; Corderie della Tana 1304–22, rebuilt 1579–85 by Antonio da Ponte, 317 m long, 21 m wide, 12.10 m to the eaves, c. 11,430 m²; Gaggiandre (covered wet docks for galleys) 1568–73 with trusses of nearly 25 m span on three rows of arches and piers, 1,750 m² together; Tese Cinquecentesche (covered slips) four buildings of 1566; workforce 1,500–2,000 daily and 4,500–5,000 enrolled (it.wikipedia "Arsenale di Venezia", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**; en.wikipedia "Venetian Arsenal", fetched 2026-08-23, gives 45 ha and 16,000 workers — **CONFIRMED** as fetched but in conflict; see (c)). The Arsenal's program is the city shipyard's ceiling: covered slips, rope walk, mast store, sail loft, smithies/foundry, and "a galley a day" at peak (en.wiki). The Dutch VOC yards (Amsterdam Oostenburg 1660s) — Wikipedia page 404; **PLAUSIBLE** only. The mould loft is late: lofting is "done in large, lightly constructed mezzanines or lofts above the factory floor" (Wikipedia "Lofting", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED** for form, no date found); I would not license a mould loft before the late 17th century (**PLAUSIBLE**). Ropewalks: Christianshavn's early-17th-century walk c. 300 m; Chatham 1790 1,135 ft; many "were in the open air, while others were covered only by roofs" (Wikipedia "Ropewalk", fetched 2026-08-23 — **CONFIRMED**).

*The barge company's wharf and office.* No primary surfaced (searches exhausted before this item); the interior is the quayside warehouse (family P) with a counting-room on the quay and a barge-master's house — **PLAUSIBLE**; in the Hanseatic register the Kontor gård IS this (family N).

*Prosperity/wear grades and live/work.* Ferry: cottage + stage (floor) → stone hard + ferry house with a tap-room rung. Boatyard: open bank + sawpit (floor) → naust/shed 34 x 13 m (rung) → covered slip. Quay: timber staithe (floor) → stone quay + treadwheel crane + weigh-house (rung) → mole/Cobb harbour with crane-tower gate (Gdańsk). Shipyard: beach careening (floor) → timber-lined dry dock with horse pump (1495) → Arsenale (ceiling). Decline sheds the crane's treadwheel crew and the rope walk's roof first; a dry dock silts and is "filled with rubble" within a century (Portsmouth — **CONFIRMED**). Live/work: ferryman and boat-builder live on site; the shipwright master at the town yard lives in the yard house; the arsenalotti live in the town (Venice).

**(b) Measured.**
- Naust Kinsarvik (medieval): 34 x 13 m, two leidang ships; Steinkjer 40 m (**CONFIRMED**).
- Saw pit relic: 2 x 1.6 m, 0.4 m deep (earthwork); parallel hollows 1 m apart (**CONFIRMED**).
- Harbour crane dates: 1244/1263/1288/1291; England ≥1331; wheel ≥4 m; 2–3 t (**CONFIRMED**).
- Gdańsk Krantor 1442–44: c. 30 m high; wheels to 6.5 m; lifts 11 m and 27 m; 4 t; 4+ men (**CONFIRMED**).
- Lüneburg crane: base 8 m; king-post 9 m; wheel 5 m; 1330 first mention; 1797 fabric (**CONFIRMED**).
- Cobb: 1328 first mention; oak piles + boulders; mortar only from 1793 (**CONFIRMED**).
- Portsmouth 1495: timber-lined, earth bank closure, horse bucket-chain pump; rope house 1,095 ft by 1660; mast house 1669; double dock 1656 (**CONFIRMED**). 1495 dock length/width: NOT FOUND.
- Arsenale: Corderie 317 x 21 m, 12.10 m eaves, c. 11,430 m²; Gaggiandre trusses c. 25 m span, 1,750 m²; Nuovo 138,600 m²; total c. 24 ha (it.wiki) vs 45 ha (en.wiki); workers 1,500–2,000 daily / 4,500–5,000 enrolled (it.wiki) vs 16,000 (en.wiki) (**CONFIRMED**, conflicting).
- Ropewalk: c. 300 m (early 17th c. Copenhagen); 346 m Chatham 1790 (**CONFIRMED**).
- Letocetum-type quay well and Thames revetment stage depths: not measured this session.
- No measured figures found for: ferry house, Thames barge yard shed, Bruges/Harlingen crane dimensions, the 1495 dock plan, VOC yard buildings, a barge company office (search budget exhausted at the start of this family; fetches by direct URL only).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**
- "Bristol's dry dock of 1239 (the first documented)" in the brief: the Bristol Harbour page fetched describes the 1240s as the Frome diversion into St Augustine's Reach and says nothing of a dry dock; the Dry dock page names Portsmouth 1495 as the first recorded. I could not find a primary for a 1239 Bristol dry dock; treat the brief's claim as UNVERIFIED and likely a conflation with the 1239–47 harbour works (**CONFIRMED** for what the two pages say; the negative is a null result after two fetches, not an exhaustive search).
- Arsenale figures conflict across two Wikipedias (24 vs 45 ha; 2,000 vs 16,000 workers). The 16,000 is the often-repeated traditional figure; the enrolment-based 4,500–5,000 is the better-grounded one (**PLAUSIBLE** judgement; both cited).
- Harbour cranes are the counterexample to "the medieval port was just a beach": but they are a northern (Flemish/German/Baltic) form; Italian ports used ramps beyond the Middle Ages (**CONFIRMED**). A Mediterranean-register port has no crane house.
- Nearly all standing quay/dock fabric is later: Lüneburg 1797, Cobb 1793/1820, Portsmouth's docks 1656–1699. The 1200–1700 register's port is timber staithes and a beach slip; the stone mole (Cobb) is the exception and is itself dry-laid.
- The Thames barge yard, steam chest, and mould loft are post-register features (**PLAUSIBLE** dating, stated above).
- Negation searched indirectly (the web-search budget ran out at this family); the counterexamples above come from fetched pages, and the fishery/beach-launch default for small ports is the strongest "no building" case: the naust is a roofed beach, not a yard.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**
- L483 River ferry: a cottage (`THROUGH_ROOM`, 1–2 cells, ladder loft) + exterior stage; no interior circulation class beyond the cottage.
- L492 River boatyard: the naust/shed is a single-volume `THROUGH_ROOM` 34 x 13 m at its largest, gable open to the water (**CONFIRMED**); circulation is the ramp; ladder to a gear loft if any.
- L955/L1603 Docks: the quay is a linear `GALLERY`-class open walk along the water; the crane tower (Gdańsk) is a `STAIR_HALL` in effect — four floors, the wheels on two levels (**CONFIRMED**); the crane-gate doubles as a town gate `VESTIBULE`; the weigh-house is a `THROUGH_ROOM` hall.
- L1611 Barge company: quay `GALLERY` + warehouse (family P) + a counting-room `LOBBY` entry (**PLAUSIBLE**).
- L993/L1627 Shipyard: the slip/dock is the organising axis; the covered slip (tese/gaggiandre, 25 m trusses) is a multi-bay `THROUGH_ROOM` open at the water end; the ropewalk is the extreme linear `GALLERY` (300 m+); the sail loft is a long `THROUGH_ROOM` upper floor reached by an external stair; the mould loft a mezzanine (late). Stairs: ladder (boatyard) → straight external stair (lofts) → none grand.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**
- Boatyard: timber store in the open (`STORE`, lightReq ANY, unroofed rack — FIXTURE); gear loft over the shed (`ATTIC`); the naust's airy interior IS the drying store (**CONFIRMED**).
- Docks: the quayside warehouse is family P; the crane house holds its own windlass chamber (FIXTURE, not storage); the weigh-house holds the great beam (FIXTURE).
- Shipyard: mast store/mast pond (`STORE` as a water body — NO TYPED HOME), hemp/tar store (`STORE`, `fireTrade`-adjacent; at Venice the Tana was hemp store + rope walk — **CONFIRMED**), sail loft (`ATTIC`-class, long and dry, lightReq ANY), timber yard (open `STORE`), smithy (`workfloor` with hearth), gunpowder mills at the Arsenal (segregated — en.wiki, **CONFIRMED**).
- Dry dock: not storage — a `workfloor` below grade; its closing bank/gate is a FIXTURE; the pump (horse bucket-chain, 1495) is a FIXTURE (**CONFIRMED**).
- Ferry: none beyond a cottage `CLOSET`.

**(f) Typed proposal.**
- `parti`: `FERRY_HARD` (cottage + stage; no interior beyond 1–2 cells), `NAUST_SHED` (open-gable boathouse 20–40 m), `RIVERBANK_SLIP_YARD` (open bank + sawpit + shed), `TIMBER_STAITHE` (floor quay), `STONE_QUAY_CRANE` (quay + treadwheel crane + weigh-house), `CRANE_GATE_TOWER` (Gdańsk type: crane as town gate), `HARBOUR_MOLE` (Cobb), `DRY_DOCK_TIMBER` (1495 type), `COVERED_SLIP` (tese/gaggiandre), `ROPEWALK` (open or roofed, 300 m+), `ARSENAL_COMPLEX` (city ceiling: docks + slips + ropewalk + sail loft + smithies + mast pond).
- `functions[]`: floor — `beach_careen`, `sawpit`, `timber_rack`, `stage`; rungs — `slip`, `shed`, `crane_treadwheel`, `weigh_house`, `mast_pond`, `rope_walk_open`, `smithy`; ceiling — `dry_dock`, `covered_slip`, `rope_walk_roofed`, `sail_loft`, `mould_loft` (late), `foundry`.
- `fixtures[]`: treadwheel (NO TYPED HOME; 4–6.5 m diameter, 2–4 t), windlass, great beam (weigh-house), sawpit (NO TYPED HOME), steam chest (late; exclude before 1700), dock gate/bank, horse pump, workbench/rack/barrel/crate (HOME).
- Structural buckets: `waterAccess` (mandatory), `heavyFloor` (crane tower, timber stacks), `fireTrade` (smithy, tar/hemp store — segregate), `wetTrade` (dock floor below grade; tidal), `linear` (ropewalk/quay — a new bucket: length-dominant structures the footprint model must permit).
- Licensing: village x river siting → `FERRY_HARD`, `RIVERBANK_SLIP_YARD`/`NAUST_SHED`; town x river/coast → `TIMBER_STAITHE` → `STONE_QUAY_CRANE` (northern register, 1244+; never Mediterranean); town shipyard → `RIVERBANK_SLIP_YARD` + `ROPEWALK`; city x 1495+ → `DRY_DOCK_TIMBER`; city x very prosperous x state → `ARSENAL_COMPLEX`. The catalog's city shipyard text ("multiple dry docks, ropewalk, sail loft, dedicated ironworks") is the Arsenal/1660s-Portsmouth ceiling and should be licensed only at the top of era x prosperity; the town shipyard ("ocean-going merchant vessels") is a riverbank slip yard, not a dock.
- Tags: shed/slip/dock = HOME: ROOM_KINDS.workfloor; smithy = workfloor + hearth; sail loft/mast store/timber yard = HOME: store; counting-room = HOME: counting; ferry cottage = HOME: main/chamber; crane tower, ropewalk, dock = NO TYPED HOME.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**
(1) LENGTH-DOMINANT structures: a 317 x 21 m ropewalk or a 300 m quay cannot be a "room"; the engine needs a linear primitive with a width bucket and an unbounded length. (2) OPEN-AT-ONE-END volumes (naust, covered slip) — a room class with a missing wall facing water. (3) BELOW-GRADE WET workfloors (dry dock) with a tidal flag. (4) A FIXTURE that is a building (the treadwheel crane tower/gate) — the crane sits on the quay pull but carries its own 4-storey section. (5) A "no building" outcome again (ferry, beach careening) — the same zero-interior fixture class family M needs for the horse fair and family N for the street stand. (6) Era gates: crane ≥1244 (≥1331 England), dry dock ≥1495, mould loft/steam chest excluded before 1700.

**(h) Continental vs English.**
The English port of the register is a timber-revetted staithe, a beach slip and (from 1495) one royal dry dock; its craft interiors are a shed, a sawpit and a rope walk. The northern continental mechanism is the Hanseatic quay set — Kran, Waage, Speicher row (Lüneburg 1330, Gdańsk 1444, Bryggen's crane per gård); the Dutch pakhuis/Admiralty magazijn and VOC yard (1650s–60s) industrialise the store and the slip; the Mediterranean state arsenal (Venice 1104/1325/1473, with its 317 m Corderie and 25 m-span covered docks) is the ceiling no English yard approached before Chatham/Portsmouth in the later 17th century, and Mediterranean quays went without cranes entirely.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.

The sub-lane could not issue a formal negation search for this family (session WebSearch cap); its counterexamples rest on fetched pages.

## §18 · FAMILY P — warehouses and granaries: Communal root cellar (thorp L104) · Town granary (town L893 REQ) · City granaries (city L1541 REQ) · State granary complex (metropolis L2443) · Merchant warehouses (town L1380) · Warehouse district (city L1597 REQ)

*Researched by sub-lane PS (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

**(a) Analogue.**

The catalog describes three things that the historical record keeps apart, and the dossier should keep them apart too: the **merchant's warehouse** (a private, vertical, hoist-served store on a quay), the **public granary** (a civic or seigneurial grain reserve, horizontal, floor-stored, vermin-proofed), and the **root cellar** (an earth-cooled pit or vault for perishables). The engine's six entries map onto these as follows.

*Merchant warehouse (town L1380) and Warehouse district (city L1597).* The European analogue is the gable-fronted quay Speicher / pakhuis: a narrow, deep, multi-storey box whose whole room program is one repeated cell, the STORE floor, stacked four to six high, served by a hoist beam or treadwheel in the gable and a column of shuttered taking-in doors directly below it. In Amsterdam the standard pakhuis is 5–8 m wide and about 30 m deep (**CONFIRMED-digest** for width, hollandroute.nl / ensie.nl; **CONFIRMED** for the 30 m depth, nl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakhuis fetched 2026-08-23), with a central hoisting bay carrying, on every storey, a large double-shuttered opening flanked by smaller windows, and the hoist beam or a hoist house (trijshuis) at the top (**CONFIRMED-digest**). The critical date: the free-standing pakhuis as a type appears only around 1600; before that merchants stored goods in the attics (zolders) of their own houses, and the Stadskorenpakhuis at Schoonhoven (1566) is cited as an early exception (**CONFIRMED**, nl.wikipedia Pakhuis, fetched 2026-08-23). The same is true in the Hanseatic world: the Lübeck Salzspeicher, the image everyone reaches for, are dated 1579, 1594, 1599, shortly after 1600, and two of 1743–1745 (**CONFIRMED**, de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salzspeicher fetched 2026-08-23) — i.e. the purpose-built row of private warehouses is a late-16th-century and later form. Before that the Hanseatic merchant's store was the upper floors (Speicherböden) and the ground-floor Diele of his own Dielenhaus (**PLAUSIBLE**; de.wikipedia Dielenhaus fetched 2026-08-23 confirms only the two-storey-high hall used as "main living and working space"). The English end of the same mechanism is the King's Lynn Hanse House: a licence of c.1474 after the Treaty of Utrecht, two long parallel warehouse ranges of 2–3 storeys plus basement running down to the river, joined at the street end by a domestic range and at the river end by a shorter warehouse wing, the street range timber-framed with a deep first-floor jetty later underbuilt in brick (**CONFIRMED-digest** from the Historic England list entry 1195393 as quoted in the search digest; the list page itself returned 403 and Cloudflare-blocked curl). The SPAB account adds that the upper storey of the short west wing "probably served as a dining hall circa 1500" overlooking river and courtyard, that the warehouses stored "wax, pepper, pitch, amber and furs," and that both warehouse ranges became granaries after 1751 (**CONFIRMED**, spab.org.uk building-focus Hanse House, fetched 2026-08-23). That conversion is itself a finding: a warehouse and a granary are the same floor-loaded box, distinguished by fixtures, not by structure. The London Steelyard gives the city-tier "warehouse district" in its Kontor form: a walled site of c. 5,250 m² holding riverside warehouses, a weigh house, chapel, counting houses, a guildhall, cloth halls, wine cellars, kitchens and dwellings; bought outright by the League in 1475, suppressed 1598, burnt 1666 (**CONFIRMED**, en.wikipedia Steelyard fetched 2026-08-23). The Mediterranean form of the same thing is the fondaco/alhóndiga: Granada's Corral del Carbón (before 1336, Yusuf I) was the Alhóndiga Nueva, "lodging for transient merchants, storage, and wholesale market," a quadrangular courtyard ringed by three storeys of galleries, ground floor for goods, upper floors for merchants' lodging, entered through a monumental gateway and a muqarnas-vaulted zaguán (**CONFIRMED**, es.wikipedia Corral del Carbón fetched 2026-08-23).

Structural imperatives: weight (the floor load governs the bay; grain and salt are the heaviest cargoes, so granary floors are the stiffest); water (quay or canal frontage, the hoist bay facing the water; cellars flood, so wet cargo goes up, not down); fire (Danzig's Speicherinsel was built as an island of storehouses with no dwellings — the rule that nobody lived or lit a fire there is **PLAUSIBLE**, not confirmed this session); vermin (a granary's whole design is rat-proofing: staddle stones, raised floors, louvred not glazed ventilation); light (storage floors need little; the counting-house seam needs a window). Siting law: warehouse = the waterfront street behind the quay; granary = "often in the town centre near the market," reachable by both land and water (**CONFIRMED**, de.wikipedia Speicher (Gebäude) fetched 2026-08-23). Prosperity grade: the floor is the merchant's own attic (pre-1600 default everywhere); one rung up is a leased bay in someone else's Speicher; the ceiling is the named, gabled, multi-storey Speicher row or a Kontor compound. Decline sheds the hoist first (the beam rots, the doors are bricked), then the upper floors are let as lodgings. Live/work: the Hanse House and the Corral del Carbón both put lodging in the compound (west-wing dining hall; gallery rooms), but the pure pakhuis row is unoccupied at night — a WATCHMAN'S CELL is the only dwelling.

*Town granary (L893), City granaries (L1541), State granary complex (L2443).* Two analogues, and the English register has only the second. (1) The manorial/monastic granary on staddles: the Weald & Downland granary from Littlehampton (probably 1731, from an incised brick) is 20 feet square with a usable loft, on sixteen Portland-stone staddles "to protect the grain from damp and vermin," elm frame with brick infill, louvred ventilators (not glazed windows), two surviving grain bins re-installed, a ladder stair to the loft by the door, a door rebuilt at its original narrow width (**CONFIRMED**, wealddown.co.uk granary-from-littlehampton fetched 2026-08-23). The South Hams type has an external stone stair on the wall (**CONFIRMED**, en.wikipedia Granary fetched 2026-08-23). This is the rural/thorp/village form and it is NOT a barn: the barn threshes (great doors, threshing floor, open roof); the granary stores threshed grain in bins on a raised floor with a small door, and every feature is about dryness and rats. (2) The civic garner over a market, which is the true "town granary." London's Leadenhall (John Croxton, from c.1440; City acquisition of the site 1411) had an arcaded ground floor that "functioned as part of a common market, while the upper floors were intended to be a granary"; excavation 1984–6 found a fragment of the west wall surviving to its full height of 11.17 m and recovered the plan of quadrangle and chapel (**CONFIRMED**, Samuel 1989, Antiquaries Journal 69, abstract fetched via the Cambridge Core landing page 2026-08-23; full text paywalled). Stow's description has the first and second floors storing grain for the City, spiral stairs at each corner carrying the sacks, four corner towers, the common beam for wool and the scales for meal in the north range, and lofts kept for the pageants (**CONFIRMED**, mapoflondon.uvic.ca LEAD101 fetched 2026-08-23). Florence's Orsanmichele is the same parti: first stone 29 July 1337, a rectangular three-storey block whose ground floor was an open grain-market loggia and whose two upper floors were the commune's granary, with hollow corner piers carrying grain chutes (the "bocche di scarico") from the upper store down to the market, a spiral stair inside the north-west pier, the arcades walled up 1380–1406 to make the church (**CONFIRMED**, it.wikipedia Chiesa di Orsanmichele fetched 2026-08-23). Ghent's Korenstapelhuis on the Graslei (late 12th century, Tournai limestone) was the spijker — the staple granary — until 1734, with a Korenmetershuis (grain-measurers' guild house) beside it of 1435 and a second of 1698 (**CONFIRMED**, nl.wikipedia Korenstapelhuis and Graslei fetched 2026-08-23). The Spanish pósito is the institutional form: a municipal grain deposit whose primary function was lending grain on easy terms to needy neighbours, governed by a board of corregidor, mayor, councillors, deputies and a treasurer with the town clerk; Cardinal Cisneros founded at his own expense the pósitos of Toledo, Alcalá de Henares and Torrelaguna; the institution lasted into the 19th century (**CONFIRMED**, es.wikipedia Pósito fetched 2026-08-23). The building side of the pósito (bins/trojes, the sala, the three-key chest) is **PLAUSIBLE** only.

The state granary complex (L2443) is not an English-register form at all. Its nearest European analogues are Danzig's Speicherinsel (grain stores on the island from the 13th century; "by the 16th century their number grew to three hundred"; the Milchkannentor of 1517–19; the Blaues Lamm the only Speicher with intact old elements — **CONFIRMED**, de.wikipedia Speicherinsel (Danzig) fetched 2026-08-23), but that is a district of private granaries under a staple right, not a state reserve. The state reserve proper is Ottoman (the kapan system — Unkapanı for flour — **PLAUSIBLE**; both Wikipedia targets 404'd), Venetian (the granai at San Marco / Terranova — **PLAUSIBLE**, not fetched), Prussian-Teutonic (Marienburg's grain lofts — **PLAUSIBLE**), or Chinese: the ever-normal granary "elaborate system designed to minimize famine deaths" destroyed in the 1850s (**CONFIRMED** mention, en.wikipedia Granary fetched 2026-08-23). For the engine this means L2443's mechanism must be stated as a LICENSED IMPORT: a network of floor-granaries (each a Leadenhall/Orsanmichele box or a Speicher row) plus an administrative cell (weigh-beam, scales, clerk's counting room, the strong chest) plus a guard — the English metropolis (London) had exactly ONE of these (Leadenhall) and otherwise relied on merchants' lofts and the Bridge House stores (Bridge House **PLAUSIBLE**).

*Communal root cellar (thorp L104).* The cleanest measured analogue is the Scandinavian jordkällare: a free-standing cellar wholly or partly buried, "often on a slope facing north," stone walls and a stone barrel vault under c. 60 cm of earth, a heavy tight door with an inner door or a narrow ante-room (svale) acting as an airlock, a vent on the entry side and one or two roof vents at the back against mould, earth or sand floor; temperature +10 to +12 C in summer and +4 to +6 C in winter; used for roots, potatoes, preserves, juice, cooked food and dairy (**CONFIRMED**, sv.wikipedia Jordkällare fetched 2026-08-23). But its date is the finding: the oldest known examples are mid-1700s and building accelerated in the 1820s with the potato; the predecessor was the stuka, a ground pit (**CONFIRMED**, same page). The catalog's "shared underground store for grain, roots, and preserved food" therefore has no pre-1700 standing building behind it in the north; the in-register form is the pit, the house undercroft, or the lord's/abbey's cellar. The three generic construction modes (pit under a shed with trap door; dug into a hillside for drainage; built at grade and earth-covered) and the target climate (0–2 C, 90–95 % humidity) are **CONFIRMED** (en.wikipedia Root cellar fetched 2026-08-23). Grain does NOT belong in a cellar — damp grain moulds; the catalog line conflates granary and cellar and the dossier should split them: roots and dairy below grade, grain above grade on staddles.

**(b) Measured.**

- Amsterdam pakhuis: width 5–8 m (**CONFIRMED-digest**); depth c. 30 m (**CONFIRMED**, nl.wikipedia Pakhuis). Storey counts not found (**PLAUSIBLE** 4–6 floors plus attic).
- Hanse House, King's Lynn: two warehouse ranges 2–3 storeys plus basement; street range 2 storeys, jettied; licence c.1474, warehouses c.1480 (**CONFIRMED-digest**, HE list entry 1195393); west-wing upper floor dining hall c.1500 (**CONFIRMED**, SPAB). No lengths found (searched: HE listing 403, SPAB, civic society page, britishlistedbuildings 403).
- Steelyard, London: c. 5,250 m² (1.3 acres) walled site; bought 1475; suppressed 1598 (**CONFIRMED**, en.wikipedia).
- Salzspeicher, Lübeck: six buildings dated 1579, 1599, shortly after 1600, 1594, 1743–45, 1743–45 (**CONFIRMED**, de.wikipedia). No storey counts or loads found.
- Leadenhall garner: west wall surviving to full height 11.17 m; two storeys of granary over an arcaded market; quadrangle with chapel and four corner towers; spiral stair at each corner (**CONFIRMED**, Samuel 1989 abstract; MoEML). Completion date: 1449 per search digest, "completed in 1455" per MoEML — discrepancy, both **CONFIRMED-digest/CONFIRMED** respectively, unresolved.
- Orsanmichele: three storeys; first stone 29 July 1337; arcades closed 1380–1406; grain chutes in hollow piers; spiral stair in NW corner pier; Buontalenti bridge 1569 (**CONFIRMED**, it.wikipedia). Plan dimensions not found (**PLAUSIBLE** c. 35 × 18 m, treat as unverified).
- Corral del Carbón: three storeys of galleries round a quadrangular court; before 1336 (**CONFIRMED**, es.wikipedia). Court dimensions not found.
- Littlehampton granary: 20 ft (6.1 m) square; 16 staddles; loft; 1731 (**CONFIRMED**, Weald & Downland).
- Danzig Speicherinsel: c. 300 granaries by the 16th century (**CONFIRMED**); "300–350 at peak" (**CONFIRMED-digest**).
- Korenstapelhuis, Ghent: late 12th century; spijker until 1734 (**CONFIRMED**). Storeys not stated (**PLAUSIBLE** five).
- Jordkällare: 60 cm earth cover; +10–12 C summer / +4–6 C winter; oldest mid-1700s (**CONFIRMED**).
- No measured figures found for: Speicher floor-to-floor heights, floor loads, bay widths, gable-hoist wheel diameters, taking-in door sizes, Wool House Southampton bays (searched: Wikipedia Wool House, search digest: "two-storey stone warehouse, late 14th/early 15th c., arch-braced collar roof, three semicircular buttresses added 16th–17th c." — **CONFIRMED-digest**), Marienburg lofts, Venetian granai, Ottoman kapan (404 ×2), Lueger 1904 Bodenspeicher (connection refused).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**

- The free-standing merchant warehouse is LATE: before c.1600 Dutch merchants used their own attics (**CONFIRMED**); the Lübeck row is 1579+ (**CONFIRMED**); the King's Lynn Hanse warehouses are c.1480 and are the "only extant Hanseatic building in England" (**CONFIRMED**, civic society). So for town tier before c.1550 the honest room is a STORE floor inside the merchant's house, not a separate building; the "Merchant warehouses" entry should be licensed at town tier only as house-attached bays or a quay-side undercroft until the late-16th-century era grade.
- The medieval English town had essentially no public granary: the one built example, Leadenhall, was a one-off financed by a mayor and doubled as market, chapel and pageant store (**CONFIRMED**). Elsewhere grain sat in merchants' lofts, the castle and the monastic granges (**PLAUSIBLE**). The "Town granary REQ" at town tier is therefore a continental/Iberian/Italian mechanism (spijker, pósito, communal garner) imported into the English register — say so in the dossier.
- Warehouse ≠ granary structurally: the Hanse House warehouses became granaries in the 18th century and St George's Guildhall became a warehouse in 1826 (**CONFIRMED**, both) — the box is interchangeable; only the fixtures (bins, louvres, staddles vs hoist, shutters, counting room) type it.
- Root cellar negation: the jordkällare's oldest examples are mid-18th century (**CONFIRMED**) — the form is later than the register. Null result: no medieval communal village cellar was found as a standing measured building this session.
- Granary ≠ barn: the tithe barn (Great Coxwell) is a threshing and sheaf store; the granary is the post-threshing bin house. Not re-fetched this session; **PLAUSIBLE** on the distinction, **CONFIRMED** on the granary's raised-floor/louvre/bin/staddle fixtures (Weald & Downland, en.wikipedia Granary).
- The Roman horreum is excluded as the wrong register (it is the state-warehouse ancestor of L2443, not its analogue); the en.wikipedia Granary article only cross-references it (**CONFIRMED**).
- Negation-search result: the shared WebSearch budget was exhausted (200/200) on the eighth Family-P query, so the explicit negation strings were not issued as searches; the negation evidence above came from fetched pages (pakhuis "before 1600 attics," jordkällare "oldest mid-1700s," Leadenhall "dual-purpose").

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**

- Merchant warehouses (town) / Warehouse district (city): the dominant circulation is VERTICAL and external: the gable hoist (beam or treadwheel) with a column of taking-in doors, one per floor, on the hoist bay; internally each floor is one THROUGH_ROOM cell (no corridor ever) with a ladder or a steep winder stair in one corner, typically against the party wall (**PLAUSIBLE**; stair type ladder → winder). Kontor/fondaco form adds a courtyard as carrier space with a GATE/VESTIBULE (the Corral del Carbón zaguán, the Steelyard's walled enclosure — **CONFIRMED**) and a GALLERY ring round the court on each floor (three storeys of galleries at Granada — **CONFIRMED**). Warehouse district at city tier = a street or island of such boxes with a gate (Milchkannentor 1517–19 — **CONFIRMED**). Era grade: house-attached STORE floors before c.1550; gabled rows after c.1580 at prosperity ≥ merchant.
- Town granary / City granaries: the loggia-garner carries a ground-floor arcaded market open on all sides (GALLERY class, the loggia), with spiral stairs in the corner towers or in a hollow pier (Leadenhall: spiral at each corner; Orsanmichele: spiral in the NW pier — both **CONFIRMED**), and grain chutes as a NON-HUMAN vertical circulation the engine has no class for. The staddle granary has an external ladder or stone stair to a single raised door (**CONFIRMED**) — stair type ladder/external.
- State granary complex: compound circulation — gate, court, a ring of Speicher, the weigh-house as the control cell (**PLAUSIBLE** synthesis over Danzig + Leadenhall + Steelyard).
- Communal root cellar: a single cell entered through an airlock ante-room (the svale — **CONFIRMED**) = LOBBY/VESTIBULE class at its smallest; a trap-door-and-ladder variant under a shed (**CONFIRMED**, en.wikipedia Root cellar).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**

- Warehouse: `STORE` (the bay) × N storeys; `CELLAR/UNDERCROFT` only for wine and dry heavy goods on dry sites (Steelyard wine cellars, Gewandhaus wine cellar 1329 — **CONFIRMED**); `ATTIC/GARRET_STORE` is the pre-1600 default and the Hanseatic Speicherboden. lightReq NONE (shutters only; the Amsterdam hoist bay has small flanking windows). Adjacency law: hoist bay on the water side; counting room (ROOM_KINDS.counting) at the street end or in the domestic range (Hanse House west wing; Steelyard counting houses — **CONFIRMED**). Fixtures: hoist beam, treadwheel, shutters, sack-barrow, the merchant's marks; crates/barrels/racks as FURNISHING_KINDS.crate/barrel/rack. Fire: no hearth on any STORE floor; the only hearth is in the counting/dwelling range.
- Granary: `STORE` with the granary sub-type: raised floor, bins (a FIXTURE — the Littlehampton bins were reinstalled as fittings, **CONFIRMED**), louvred ventilation (lightReq NONE, AIR required), a narrow door, staddles; town-tier garner = two `STORE` floors over a market loggia, chutes, a `CLOSET`-grade clerk's cell with scales and beam (Leadenhall's common beam and meal scales — **CONFIRMED**). Adjacency law: never below grade; never adjoining a hearth; market below.
- Root cellar: `CELLAR` with earth-cover fixture, airlock ante-cell, vents; lightReq NONE; stores roots, dairy, preserves (NOT grain). A cellar too small to enter (the stuka pit) is a FIXTURE of the yard.

**(f) Typed proposal.**

- `parti`: `QUAY_SPEICHER` (gabled multi-storey store, hoist bay waterside), `HOUSE_ATTACHED_STORE` (pre-1600 attic/Diele bays — the town-tier floor), `COURTYARD_KONTOR` (Steelyard/Hanse House: two warehouse ranges + domestic range + court), `FONDACO_ALHONDIGA` (galleried court, goods below, merchants above), `STADDLE_GRANARY` (thorp/village), `LOGGIA_GARNER` (market loggia under two granary floors with chutes and corner spirals), `GRANARY_ISLAND` (Speicherinsel: gated district of stores, no dwellings), `EARTH_CELLAR` (vault + airlock, LATE-era only) and `ROOT_PIT` (the in-register floor).
- `functions[]` (floor → ladder → ceiling): warehouse: attic bays → leased Speicher floor → own gabled Speicher → Kontor compound; granary: pit/loft → staddle granary → loggia-garner → state network; root cellar: pit → house undercroft → earth cellar.
- `fixtures[]` with grade: hoistBeam (merchant) → treadwheel (wealthy/city); takingInDoors per floor; shutters; staddles (16 at 20 ft square — use 4×4 grid); bins; louvres; chutes (hollow pier); weighBeam + mealScales (civic); ironBoundChest (civic garner/pósito — **PLAUSIBLE** for the pósito, **CONFIRMED** as a guild fixture, see S); earthCover 60 cm; airlockDoor; ventPipe. Decline sheds hoist → shutters → upper floors re-let as lodging.
- Structural buckets: `heavyFloor` (all), `waterFront` (warehouse), `noFire` (Speicher floors, granary island), `vermin` (granary: staddles/raised floor), `coldStore` (root cellar: north slope, below grade), `ventilated` (granary louvres, cellar vents).
- Licensing: Merchant warehouses — town × merchant-prosperity × era ≥ late-medieval as `HOUSE_ATTACHED_STORE`; as `QUAY_SPEICHER` only era ≥ c.1580 or continental morphology; siting = quay/canal. Warehouse district — city × any × era ≥ c.1500 × quay; as `GRANARY_ISLAND`/`COURTYARD_KONTOR`. Town granary — town × civic × as `LOGGIA_GARNER` or `STADDLE_GRANARY`-cluster; mark "continental import in the English register." City granaries — city × civic × multiple `LOGGIA_GARNER`. State granary complex — metropolis × state × licensed import (Ottoman/Venetian/Chinese mechanism), `GRANARY_ISLAND` + admin cell. Communal root cellar — thorp × any × `ROOT_PIT` default, `EARTH_CELLAR` only at era ≥ 1700 or alpine/nordic siting.
- HOME: warehouse floor = ROOM_KINDS.store; counting room = ROOM_KINDS.counting; strongroom = ROOM_KINDS.strongroom; cellar = ROOM_KINDS.cellar; watchman = ROOM_KINDS.lodging. Granary floor = ROOM_KINDS.store (no granary kind — NO TYPED HOME for "bin floor"); market loggia = ROOM_KINDS.stall; clerk/weigh cell = ROOM_KINDS.counting. Bins, staddles, chutes, hoist, louvres = NO TYPED HOME among FURNISHING_KINDS (crate/barrel/rack exist; "bin," "hoist," "chute" do not). Root cellar = ROOM_KINDS.cellar; airlock ante-cell NO TYPED HOME.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**

The engine cannot currently express (1) a building that is N identical stacked STORE cells with NO horizontal circulation and an EXTERNAL vertical hoist — the warehouse is a vertical-circulation building and the §452 classes are all horizontal; a `HOIST` class (non-human vertical circulation, paired with a ladder/winder) is needed, and grain chutes are its granary twin; (2) a "no hearth anywhere" bucket at building scope (Speicher, granary) — the fire flag exists per trade but not as a building-wide prohibition; (3) the market-under-store parti (loggia ground floor = stall, upper = store) which the shop-house grammar does not cover because the ground floor is OPEN on all sides; (4) a district-as-building (Speicherinsel, Steelyard): a gated enclosure whose members are buildings, landing on the existing warehouse/trade siting pull rather than on a single plot; (5) the date-gate that the free-standing warehouse and the earth cellar are both later than the default era — the era field must be able to refuse a parti, not just grade it. The granary/warehouse interchangeability means the fixture set, not the parti, should carry the type.

**(h) Continental vs English.**

The English register is parochial in two ways: it has almost no public granary (Leadenhall is the single civic garner, and it was a market with a granary on top), and its surviving medieval warehouses are either monastic or foreign (the Wool House and the Hanse House). The continental mechanism is threefold: the Hanseatic Speicher and Dielenhaus (goods in the merchant's own hall and lofts, then from c.1580 in gabled rows on the quay with a gable hoist), the Dutch pakhuis (5–8 m × 30 m, shuttered hoist bay, c.1600 onward), and the Italian/Iberian civic and commercial granary (Orsanmichele's loggia-garner with chutes, the Ghent spijker, the pósito as a grain bank and the alhóndiga/fondaco as a galleried merchants' court). The state reserve is Ottoman, Venetian or Chinese, not European-vernacular at all.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.

**Parent-lane review note.** The parent carries two findings to §Σ: the free-standing merchant warehouse is a post-c.1580 form (before it, the merchant's own attic/Diele), and the English town had essentially no public granary (Leadenhall is a one-off) — so the catalog's "Town granary REQ" is a continental/Iberian mechanism imported into the English register and must be licensed as such.

---

## §19 · FAMILY Q — finance: Pawnbroker (hamlet L405) · Money changers (town L936) · Banking houses (city L1576) · Banking district (metropolis L2427)

*Researched by sub-lane BCQ (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

**(a) Analogue.**
Before c. 1700 there is **no free-standing bank building**. Banking happens in three kinds of space: the *bench* in the open (the banco on a square or street), the *merchant's house* (a counting room and a strongroom inside a dwelling-warehouse), and *rooms inside a civic or religious building* (the exchange bank in the town hall; the pawnbank as a Monte di Pietà; the treasury as a crypt). The British evidence is blunt: the first bankers "worked from their own houses: the manager lived above the business quarters", purpose-built private banks looked like houses, and the first purpose-built bank is the Bank of England of 1734 — CONFIRMED, https://www.buildinghistory.org/buildings/banks.shtml, fetched 2026-08-23; Asgill's at 70 Lombard Street (1757) is the first purpose-built banking house on that street — CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lombard_Street,_London, fetched 2026-08-23. The oldest surviving purpose-built bank anywhere is Stockholm's Södra Bankohuset, begun 1675 — CONFIRMED-digest — which sets the true edge of the period at "late 17th century, state bank, Baltic".

*The bench.* Lombard Street was granted by Edward I to Lombard goldsmith-bankers who did business on benches (*bancos*); Gresham proposed a "Bursse in Lombert-streete" in 1537 and the Royal Exchange followed in 1565–71; the goldsmith-bankers (Child's, Hoare's, Martins) kept shop-houses there — CONFIRMED, Lombard Street (above). At Bruges the Van der Buerse family ran the Ter Buerse *inn* from the 13th c.; over the 14th c. the square before it became the city's financial centre, with notaries and money-changers setting up there and the nation houses of Genoa, Florence and Venice around it — CONFIRMED-digest (NBB Museum; Visit Bruges). The money-changer's "building" is therefore a *table and a locked chest*, on a square, under a loggia, or in an inn's front room; the civic form is an exchange bank inside the town hall: the Amsterdam Wisselbank (founded 31 January 1609) sat in the old town hall on the Dam — Saenredam and Lingelbach paintings show it "to the right of the tower" — moved into the new town hall from 1655, kept coin in the cellar under civic guarantee, closed twice a year for two weeks to balance its books, and was staffed by commissioners, accountants, clerks, bookkeepers, receivers, ushers and assayers — CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Amsterdam, fetched 2026-08-23 (basement and "roughly 100% vaulted coin" are CONFIRMED-digest).

*The merchant-banker's house.* Francesco Datini's palace at Prato was begun in 1383 (works on the Via Rinaldesca wing), raised from a single storey in 1387–90 when the courtyard with its loggia and well was built over the site of a stable, a small house and the kitchen; the ground-floor *camera delle due letta* has a lowered barrel vault, a fireplace with Datini's arms, a sink, a built-in cupboard and a door to the kitchen; the *fondaco* (warehouse) stood in the building facing the entrance with a painted house and loggia; Datini reckoned palace, fondaco and garden at over 6,000 florins by 1399, and bought a cottage for a stable in 1392 and another in 1407 until he owned the block including a house with an oven — CONFIRMED-digest (comune.prato.it, pratoturismo; the museum and istitutodatini sites timed out at DNS and po-net returned 401); his papers, ~150,000 items, were sealed in the building's walls and rediscovered in the 19th c., and a botanical garden lay in front — CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palazzo_Datini, fetched 2026-08-23. The Medici's Bruges branch occupied Pieter Bladelin's house (built 1435 per the Dutch article, c. 1440 per the tourist digests): Gothic tower, enclosed courtyard, arcaded entrance with carved corbels, barrel-vaulted rooms with arms, two medallions of Lorenzo and Clarice Orsini added 1469; Medici ownership from 1466, Tommaso Portinari managing from 1472; Portinari had a new hall added, the "Bankzaal de Medici" (CONFIRMED-digest); after the 1571 confiscation the house became a *charitable pawn house* — CONFIRMED, https://nl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hof_Bladelin, fetched 2026-08-23. The Fugger firm's seat was the Goldene Schreibstube of 1488 in the family house on the Rindermarkt, Augsburg: a room of about 50 m², maple-panelled with gold strips, the central accounting office where Matthäus Schwarz worked; it does not survive (a bay-window reconstruction exists) — CONFIRMED, https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goldene_Schreibstube, fetched 2026-08-23. Jakob Fugger's 1512–15 Fuggerhäuser on the Via Claudia (Maximilianstrasse) were *two linked houses, a town-house and a warehouse*, with four courtyards (Damenhof, Zofenhof, Serenadenhof, Reiterhof) and the Adlertor gate sized for horse teams; rebuilt 1951 — CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuggerh%C3%A4user, fetched 2026-08-23. So the "banking house" is: a merchant's courtyard house + a warehouse + a ~50 m² counting room + a chest room, and (at Bruges) a hall added for receiving clients.

*The moneylender's stone house.* Lincoln's Jew's House (c. 1170, local limestone, Romanesque): a first-floor hall of approximately 12 × 6 m over ground-floor service and storage; a chimney breast rising over the front-door arch serves the first-floor fireplace; carved doorway and two double-arch windows survive — CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jew's_House, fetched 2026-08-23 (Pevsner & Harris 1989 p. 525; Wood 1974). The neighbouring Norman House, 46–47 Steep Hill, dates 1170–80; its attribution to Aaron of Lincoln is discredited — CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_House, fetched 2026-08-23; it "originally had no windows on the ground floor", for defence — CONFIRMED-digest. The lending form is thus a stone *first-floor hall* over a windowless ground-floor store: stone for fire and theft, hall upstairs for business and living, strongroom below.

*The treasury/strongroom.* Westminster's Pyx Chamber: an undercroft of c. 1070 under the monks' dormitory, walled off in the 12th c., a treasury in the 13th; "two heavy oak entrance doors, with seven locks" of the early 14th c. ("two skins of thick double doors", dendro c. 1300, added after the 1296 and 1303 robberies); a stone table against the east wall with a circular depression for a portable furnace to assay silver; chests of the 13th–14th c. holding Exchequer documents; Trial of the Pyx from 1281 — CONFIRMED, https://www.westminster-abbey.org/history/explore-our-history/pyx-chamber and https://archives.history.ac.uk/richardII/storage.html, both fetched 2026-08-23. The same source gives the Treasury of Receipt on the north side of the Exchequer by Westminster Hall, the moated Jewel Tower of 1365–66, and in the Tower of London chests in basement rooms called the Black Hall and Black Chamber, with Edward III's secret jewel chamber beside them having "walls fifteen feet thick", no windows and a single entrance — CONFIRMED. The iron-bound "domed standard" chests of Kent (16 examples): 96–165 cm long, 50–74 cm high, 44–69 cm deep, clustering 120–150 × 58–67 × 50–56 cm; pine boxes of Baltic origin with lime lids; a single central lock with a butterfly plate, nine with locking bars; one dendro-dated to the early 15th c. from northern Poland; an Ickham deed of 1393 specifies an iron-bound chest with *dual locks* for books, vestments and chalices — CONFIRMED, https://www.kentarchaeology.org.uk/journal/136/study-medieval-iron-bound-chests-domed-standard-chests-kent, fetched 2026-08-23.

*The pawnbank.* The Monte di Pietà (first at Perugia 1462; Florence 1495/96 with eight officials; Padua's seat inaugurated 1533 after Falconetto rebuilt it on a six-arch portico and surviving walls, two storeys with a courtyard and a marble loggia-*fondaco*, internally modified 1607–19) — CONFIRMED, https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palazzo_del_Monte_di_Piet%C3%A0_Nuovo, fetched 2026-08-23; others CONFIRMED-digest. "Part of the building served to preserve money, another part to keep pledges" — CONFIRMED-digest (Wikipedia, Malta). Hof Bladelin's afterlife as a charitable pawn house shows the pawnbank inheriting a merchant-banker's house complete.

Tier distinctions. *Hamlet Pawnbroker L405*: no building — a locked chest and a ledger in the lender's own house (or the miller's/alehouse-keeper's), a hamlet version of "the manager lived above the business"; stone and a strongroom are town marks. *Town Money changers L936*: a bench/table in the market square or under a loggia, a changer's front room in an inn, or rooms in the town hall. *City Banking houses L1576*: the merchant-banker's courtyard house with fondaco, counting room (~50 m²), strongroom/chest room, and at the top a client hall (Bladelin's bankzaal). *Metropolis Banking district L2427*: a square or street of such houses with nation houses and an inn-exchange (Bruges Beursplein; Lombard Street), plus a civic exchange bank in the town hall and, after 1531 (Antwerp) / 1571 (London), a bourse building — PLAUSIBLE for the bourses; never a row of banks.

**(b) Measured.**
- Jew's House, Lincoln: first-floor hall ~12 × 6 m; c. 1170; two storeys; chimney over the door arch — CONFIRMED.
- Norman House: c. 1170–80; no ground-floor windows originally — CONFIRMED / CONFIRMED-digest.
- Goldene Schreibstube: ~50 m², 1488 — CONFIRMED.
- Fuggerhäuser: 1512–15; two houses (dwelling + warehouse); four courtyards; horse-team gate — CONFIRMED.
- Pyx Chamber: built c. 1070; doors with seven locks, double-skinned, c. 1300 — CONFIRMED. Edward III's jewel chamber in the Tower: walls "fifteen feet thick" (~4.6 m), windowless, single door — CONFIRMED (as quoted by the source; the figure is the medieval description, not a modern survey).
- Kent chests: 96–165 × 50–74 × 44–69 cm; cluster 120–150 × 58–67 × 50–56 cm; 1–2 locks — CONFIRMED.
- Datini: begun 1383; raised 1387–90; >6,000 florins by 1399; block consolidated by 1407 — CONFIRMED-digest. Counting-room (scrittoio) dimensions: **not found** (searched English and Italian; both Datini sites unreachable — see receipt).
- Hof Bladelin: 1435 (or c. 1440); Medici 1466; Portinari 1472; medallions 1469; confiscation 1571 — CONFIRMED.
- Padua Monte: six-arch portico, two storeys, 1533; modified 1607–19 — CONFIRMED.
- Bank of Amsterdam: 1609; town hall; new town hall 1655; two-week closures in January and July — CONFIRMED / CONFIRMED-digest.
- Purpose-built banks: Stockholm 1675–1735 (digest); Bank of England 1734; Asgill's 1757 — CONFIRMED.
- No measured figures found for: strongroom door thickness (only "thick double doors" and "seven locks"); the Datini scrittoio; the Bladelin bankzaal; a money-changer's bench; any Monte pledge-store shelving (searched "Monte di Pietà magazzini dei pegni stanze cassiere" in Italian and English — the results describe façades and dates, not the pledge rooms). PLAUSIBLE ranges: a merchant's counting room 20–50 m²; a strongroom 6–15 m², vaulted, one door, wall 0.6–1.2 m in a town house; a pledge store sized to the chest-and-rack wall, 15–40 m².

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**
- *The negation search ("no purpose-built bank before 1700") returned a near-counterexample*: Södra Bankohuset, Stockholm, begun 1675 — a state bank, purpose-built, north European. The law should read "no purpose-built **private** bank before the 18th century; state banks from the last quarter of the 17th". The Monte di Pietà palaces (Padua 1533) are a second counterexample: institutional pawnbanks *did* get purpose-adapted buildings in the 16th century, behind porticoes, with a money side and a pledge side. Genoa's Banco di San Giorgio (1407) sat in the reused 13th-c. Palazzo del Mare — PLAUSIBLE, not fetched.
- *The "banking district" is a street of merchant houses and an inn*, not of banks: Bruges' Beursplein grew around an inn-keeping family's house, and Lombard Street's "banks" were goldsmiths' shop-houses until 1757 — CONFIRMED.
- *The Jew's House attribution is contested*: both Lincoln houses are called "Jews' houses" on tradition; the Aaron link is refuted, and the Jew's House's seizure story is "said to be" — the *form* (stone first-floor hall over windowless store) is certain, the *occupant* is not (CONFIRMED).
- *Pledges vs. coin*: the Monte stored goods, not just money; a pawnbroker's critical room is a store with racks and a ledger, not a vault. The hamlet entry's "no questions" usury is a chest in a house; it generates no architecture.
- *The exchange bank is hosted*: Amsterdam's bank never had its own building until 1814 (Oude Turfmarkt) — CONFIRMED — so the town "Money changers" at 3,000+ is bench-or-town-hall, never a bank house.
- Null result: no excavation or survey plan of a medieval counting room was retrievable this session.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**
- *Pawnbroker (hamlet L405)*: `THROUGH_ROOM` of the lender's house; the chest in the inner room or solar; **ladder**. No dedicated circulation.
- *Money changers (town L936)*: none for the bench (an open-air `stall` on a square or under a market loggia = `GALLERY` of the town); for the hosted form, the town hall's `LOBBY`/`STAIR_HALL` with the bank in a ground-floor room and coin in the `CELLAR` under it (Amsterdam, CONFIRMED-digest for the basement); the inn-exchange form is a front room off the inn's `CROSS_PASSAGE`. Stair **winder** to an upper office; **straight flight down** to the vault.
- *Banking houses (city L1576)*: the courtyard house: street gate → courtyard with `GALLERY` (Datini's loggia, Bladelin's arcaded entrance, the Fugger courtyards — all CONFIRMED) → counting room at ground or first floor → client hall (Bladelin's bankzaal, first floor, PLAUSIBLE level); the Jew's House form gives an external or internal **stair** straight to the first-floor hall with the strongroom below (`STAIR_HALL` at its minimum); `THROUGH_ROOM` within each floor. Widths: the Fugger Adlertor admits a horse team (PLAUSIBLE 3+ m); the Jew's House hall 12 × 6 m.
- *Banking district (metropolis L2427)*: the square/street is the circulation; each house as above; the civic exchange adds the town hall's `STAIR_HALL`; the bourse (post-1531) is a `GALLERY` quadrangle (arcaded court; PLAUSIBLE — not fetched).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**
- Hamlet pawnbroker: `CLOSET` = the iron-bound chest (a fixture; 1.2–1.5 m long, two locks, CONFIRMED range) plus a `STORE` for bulky pledges (a loft or inner room, lightReq NONE, dry; adjacency: the lender's sleeping chamber).
- Town money changer: `STRONGBOX` on the bench; hosted form: `CELLAR` vault under the town hall, lightReq NONE, one door, adjacency directly below the counting room; the assayer's table with a portable furnace is a fixture (Pyx Chamber, CONFIRMED).
- City banking house: `strongroom` = a vaulted `CELLAR/UNDERCROFT` or a windowless upper chamber (Tower "secret jewel chamber", Norman House ground floor — CONFIRMED), walls thick, a single double-skinned multi-lock door (7 locks at Westminster, CONFIRMED; 2–3 locks the parish norm, CONFIRMED); `STORE` = fondaco/warehouse (Datini; the Fugger second house) with the `heavyFloor` and goods-gate law; `records` = the letter-and-ledger archive (Datini's walled-up papers; the Plantin archive) — a small lockable cell next to the counting room; `CLOSET` for keys and seals.
- Metropolis district: as city per house; the Monte adds a **pledge store with numbered racks** (HOME: store + rack) physically separated from the money room ("part... to preserve money, another... to keep pledges", CONFIRMED-digest); the exchange bank adds the civic vault.

**(f) Typed proposal.**
- `parti`: `LENDER_HOUSE_WITH_CHEST` (hamlet) · `CHANGER_BENCH_OPEN` / `CHANGER_ROOM_IN_INN` / `EXCHANGE_BANK_HOSTED_IN_TOWNHALL` (town; three siting-driven alternates) · `MERCHANT_BANKER_COURT_HOUSE` (city; house + fondaco + counting room + strongroom + optional client hall) and `STONE_FIRSTFLOOR_HALL_OVER_STORE` (the Lincoln form, 1150–1300) · `FINANCE_SQUARE_CLUSTER` (metropolis; N merchant houses + nation houses + inn-exchange + hosted civic bank; bourse building only 1530+).
- `functions[]`: floor = `lend_on_pledge`, `change_coin`; rungs = `keep_deposits`, `keep_ledgers`, `issue_letters_of_credit`, `store_goods`, `assay`, `store_pledges_numbered`; ceiling = `civic_exchange_bank`, `bourse`.
- `fixtures[]`: `bench_table` (all; HOME: counter), `iron_bound_chest` (all; HOME: strongbox; 2–7 locks by grade), `ledger_desk` (mid+; HOME: ledger), `cashier_window/grille` (high; NO TYPED HOME), `assay_table_with_furnace` (high/civic; HOME: workbench + hearth), `pledge_racks` (Monte; HOME: rack), `multi_lock_double_door` (strongroom; NO TYPED HOME), `wall_panelling_gilt` (Fugger ceiling grade; decorative only).
- Structural buckets: new `SECURE_SHELL` (stone, windowless ground floor or vaulted cellar, thick walls, single entry, multi-lock door); `heavyFloor` (coin, fondaco goods); `fireTrade` = false except the assay furnace (portable).
- Licensing: hamlet × any × any × house; town × mid+ × 1200+ for the bench, 1600+ for the hosted exchange bank × market square/town hall; city × high × 1150+ (stone hall) / 1380+ (merchant court house) × principal street; metropolis × high × 1300+ × square + street, bourse 1530+, purpose-built state bank 1675+ only.
- HOME: counting room → `ROOM_KINDS.counting`; strongroom → `strongroom`; fondaco → `store`; client hall → `hall`; archive → `records`; pledge store → `store`; bench → `stall`; vault → `cellar` + `strongroom`; dwelling → `lodging`/`chamber`. NO TYPED HOME: cashier's grille, multi-lock door, bourse quadrangle.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**
1. **Hosted entries.** The town-tier bank and the metropolis civic bank live *inside the town hall* (Amsterdam 1609–1814); the grammar needs an "occupies rooms of host" relation that suppresses the entry's own footprint and adds rooms (a ground-floor room + a cellar vault) to the host. This is the same pull Family B′ (Schweinfurt/Tallinn council pharmacies) and Family C (chartroom in the Alcázar) need — three families converge on one missing capability.
2. **A `SECURE_SHELL` bucket** separate from `fireTrade`/`wetTrade`: windowless ground floor, stone even where the settlement is timber, one door with a lock count as a prosperity/wear grade, vault below or windowless chamber above.
3. **District = cluster of houses + one square/inn anchor**, the same street-cluster composition Family B′ needs; bourses and purpose-built banks are era-gated extras, not the default.
4. **Pledge store + ledger adjacency law** (store with racks must adjoin the counting room, not the vault) for the Monte/pawn form.
5. The "banking house" should be generated as a *merchant house parti with a counting room and strongroom flagged*, not as a distinct building — it lands on whatever merchant-house/fondaco parti the Dwellings program already has.

**(h) Continental vs English.** The English register is parochial because England's bankers were goldsmiths in shop-houses and its only medieval "bank houses" are the stone halls of the Lincoln Jewry; the English counting house is a room in a merchant's hall-house. The continental mechanisms are the Italian *banco* on the square with the *fondaco* behind it (Datini's house-and-warehouse; the Medici branch as a rented patrician house with a hall added), the German *Handelshaus* with a panelled *Schreibstube* and a paired warehouse and courtyards (Fugger), the Flemish *nation house* and inn-exchange around a square (Bruges, the Beurs), the Dutch *Wisselbank* hosted in the Stadhuis with the city's guarantee in the cellar, and the Italian *Monte di Pietà* as a porticoed institutional pawnbank with money and pledges on separate sides. The Iberian *lonja* (Valencia 1482–) and the Ottoman *bedesten* are the other vaulted-hall exchange forms (PLAUSIBLE, not fetched).

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.
