## §20 · FAMILY R — exchanges, auction & brokerage: Auction house (city L1670) · Slave market (town L1001; city L1635) · Slave market district (city L1642) · Listening post (town L1073; city L1697) · Chroniclers' exchange (city L1706) · International trade center (metropolis L2435)

*Researched by sub-lane AR (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

**(a) Analogue.**

Bourse/exchange. The form begins as an inn and a square, not a building: the Bruges Huis ter Beurze was a hostelry of the Van der Beurze family operating from 1285; the building had "an interior court, a large staircase and four big windows looking on to the central market place where international merchants customarily assembled", was bought by the city in the 14th century and "used as a covered extension to the square" (CONFIRMED, Calabi, "The Stock Exchange: an innovative commercial structure", https://eusp.org/sites/default/files/archive/RESPUBLICA/RepTradition/14.Calabi_-_The_stock_exchange_senza_note.pdf fetched 2026-08-23, saved; and https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourse_at_Bruges fetched 2026-08-23). Antwerp's Oude Beurs (1515) "resembled a general market rather than a loggia": four arcades round an open court, a tall clock tower with a bell and a chapel reached from the peristyle, an open corridor round a garden "in front of a tribunal and prisons" (CONFIRMED, Calabi). The Nieuwe Beurs of 1531 (Domien de Waghemakere, cost 300,000 gold crowns) fixed the type: a rectangular open court entered through four gates from four narrow streets, surrounded by four vaulted galleries on thirty-six dark-stone columns under star and net vaults, with four upper galleries lit by dormers — the "Pand des peinctures" — let as shops for rich goods and pictures whose rents maintained the building, two clock towers, and "every nation had a more or less permanent location"; burned 1583, rebuilt to the same plan, burned again 1858 (CONFIRMED, Calabi; https://inventaris.onroerenderfgoed.be/erfgoedobjecten/6243 and https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourse_of_Antwerp fetched 2026-08-23; courtyard 40 × 51 m CONFIRMED-digest). London's Royal Exchange (1566–71, Hendryck van Paesschen, Antwerp stone and slates) copied it "even to the number of thirty-six great columns"; a French visitor of 1576 gives the only measured description: three walks one above the other; a central covered alley "six or seven paces wide" paved black and white; 36 stone columns "each 12 feet high, set four paces apart"; a cobbled quadrangle "80 paces long and 60 wide", large enough for 4,000 merchants; two portals north and south flanked by 14 ft jasper columns; a subterranean gallery of drapers' stalls called "New Venice"; and an upper gallery, reached by "25 or 30 steps... in flights of seven", with "150 stalls of rich merchandise" — the Pawn (CONFIRMED, Stephen Alford, Gresham lecture 2019 quoting Saunders ed. *The Royal Exchange*, https://s3-eu-west-1.amazonaws.com/content.gresham.ac.uk/data/binary/3047/2019-05-08_StephenAlford_Gresham'sExchange-T.pdf fetched 2026-08-23, saved). Merchants stood in "walks" by nation and trade; the building was also a postal address and a print district (CONFIRMED, Calabi). Amsterdam (Hendrick de Keyser, first assembly August 1611): open court with a portico of twenty-three arcades on forty-five granite Doric columns, vaulted shops along the outside of the long sides, entrances on the short sides crossing a bridge over the water, a carillon tower, luxury shops and money/share dealing on the floor over the portico, each column assigned a commodity, rules barring beggars, arms and children's games; too small by 1668 (CONFIRMED, Calabi).

Lonja. Valencia's Lonja de la Seda (1482–1533; Sala de Contratación by Pere Compte 1483–98): a columned trading hall of 35 × 21 m on eight free-standing twisted columns 17.4 m high under 24 vault bays, with the Consulado del Mar wing (1498–1548) holding the merchant tribunal on the upper floor over a merchants' prison below, a tower whose base cells still carry merchants' graffiti, and a walled orange courtyard (CONFIRMED, https://www.valencia-trip.com/guides/lonja-seda/ fetched 2026-08-23 — a travel guide, so treat the 35 × 21 m as secondary; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Llotja_de_la_Seda fetched 2026-08-23; the 110-step axis-less spiral stair is CONFIRMED-digest). Its model was Palma's lonja of 1448 (CONFIRMED Wikipedia). The Italian equivalents (loggia dei mercanti / della mercanzia, Rialto money-changers' tables replaced in 1587 by the Banco-Giro "with an open gallery at ground level") are an open loggia with a court room above, and Calabi states that this loggia-below, court-room-above form "had precedents in market halls of the same period" (CONFIRMED, Calabi).

Fondaco. The Fondaco dei Tedeschi (1228; rebuilt 1505–08): four floors round one courtyard, 56 rooms plus storerooms, housing over a hundred merchants and as many servants; ground floor let as storerooms accessible by water, upper floors lodging and communal dining; a five-bay loggia on the Grand Canal for unloading; arriving Germans surrendered their weapons to the Fontegher, the Venetian official who assigned rooms; the Republic took a commission on trades (CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fondaco_dei_Tedeschi fetched 2026-08-23). This is the L2435 "international trade center" in one building: nation-lodging + bonded warehouse + sales under state supervision. The Ottoman bedesten is its lock-up twin: Istanbul's Iç Bedesten (1455/56–1460/61) is 43.30 × 29.50 m, two rows of four piers under fifteen domed bays, with 44 windowless vaulted mahzen cells in the walls, lit only by clerestory windows reached from a wooden ambulatory, open only some hours a day, closed by iron gates, guarded at night by guild-paid watchmen; the Sandal Bedesten is 40.20 × 42.20 m on 12 piers under 20 domes; all other bazaar buildings were one storey and timber until the 1700 fire (CONFIRMED, Grand Bazaar page above).

Auction. In the register the auction is a PROCEDURE held in someone else's room: sale "by inch of candle" is recorded in the Lords in 1641, proposed by Milton's Council in 1652, used by the Admiralty to sell ships in 1660 and 1662 (Pepys), and survived for ships at Lloyd's into the 1820s (CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candle_auction fetched 2026-08-23). Lloyd's coffee house (1686 Tower Street; Lombard Street from 1691) had "a pulpit installed... from which maritime auction prices and shipping news were announced" and held candle auctions of ships (CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lloyd%27s_Coffee_House fetched 2026-08-23). The Dutch fish auction (visafslag/vismijn, French minque) is a descending-price sale by an afslager until a buyer calls "mijn", sited on the water so the catch passes straight to dealers (CONFIRMED as a present-day institution, https://nl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visafslag fetched 2026-08-23; its 16th–17th-c. form PLAUSIBLE). The dedicated sale room is post-register: East India House's rebuild of 1726–29 was "unexpectedly deep, affording large meeting rooms and Directors' offices, as well as a hall, a courtyard and a garden" (CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_House fetched 2026-08-23); its galleried Sale Room and the Christie's/Sotheby's rooms (1766/1744) are 18th-century and PLAUSIBLE only as a ceiling. Istanbul's Sandal Bedesten became a carpet auction house only in 1914 (CONFIRMED).

Slave market — functions and fixtures only. Ottoman towns had dedicated "esir" markets; Mehmed II founded Istanbul's in the 1460s "probably where the former Byzantine slave market had stood", near the Bedesten; the trade was taxed at 1/40 of value under an esirci emini, organised as a guild (esirci esnafı) under an elected sheikh; Nicolas de Nicolay (16th c.) saw persons "displayed naked to be thoroughly checked" (CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_slave_trade and https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avret_Pazarları fetched 2026-08-23). The one building description fetched is 19th-century: the Avret Pazarı near the Burnt Column was "a quadrangular building, with an open court in the middle. Round this are raised platforms... behind is latticed windows lighting apartments, where the white and more costly women are shut up till they are sold" — a han plan (court, platforms, cells), recorded after the 1822 Chios glut and after a pre-1810 fire and rebuild; it closed 1846–47 (CONFIRMED quotation; attribution to Robert Walsh's *Residence at Constantinople* PLAUSIBLE). For the Mediterranean and Iberian side no built venue was fetched: Venetian and Genoese sales went through brokers and notarial deeds at the Rialto and in the notary's shop; Lisbon's Casa dos Escravos (1486) was a royal registry and customs office inside the Casa da Guiné/Índia complex with sales by public almoeda; Seville sold on the cathedral steps before notaries — all PLAUSIBLE (no source opened; the pt.wikipedia "Casa dos Escravos" page fetched 2026-08-23 turned out to be the Gorée house, c. 1776, whose role historians dispute — a reconstruction-disputed flag). The catalog's L1001/L1635/L1642 ladder (public auction → block with holding facilities, registered brokers, provenance documentation → district with halls, compounds, broker offices) maps exactly onto the Ottoman ladder of open pazar → han with platforms and cells → the esir quarter beside the bedesten with its guild and emin; the building is a han, the register is the tax-farm.

Listening post / Chroniclers' exchange. NO historical interior exists for a "licensed house that pays for road news and keeps the register". The nearest analogues: (1) the merchant newsletter — the Fuggerzeitungen are c. 35,000 pages of handwritten news sheets appended to the Fugger house's correspondence 1568–1605, written mostly by employees, friends and agents and "to a lesser extent by special newswriters" (Zeitungsschreiber); three-quarters war and politics (CONFIRMED, https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuggerzeitung fetched 2026-08-23) — the "office" is a counting house's letter desk and press, not a building; (2) the exchange itself, which was "an address where to receive mails" and whose surrounding shops sold newspapers, price bulletins, maps and navigation books, and where Antwerp's merchants in 1632 read a political discourse aloud "in the galleries of our Exchange" and compared letters from Cologne, Worms and other cities the next day (CONFIRMED, Calabi) — this is the chroniclers' exchange in function: cross-checking one road's news against another in a public gallery; (3) the coffee house with pulpit and a printed sheet (Lloyd's News 1696–97, Lloyd's List 1734) (CONFIRMED); (4) the scrivener/notary, a "professional copyist or scribe" drafting deeds and contracts for a fee, who "later developed into notaries" (CONFIRMED, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scrivener fetched 2026-08-23) — the register-room with its protocol books is PLAUSIBLE as a single writing room with presses and a strongbox, shop-fronted. The catalog's "register: who arrived, from where" has its real analogue in the innkeeper's and the gate-warden's obligations and in the Fontegher's room-assignment register at the fondaco (CONFIRMED function) — i.e. the listening post's register is a fondaco/inn fixture, and the building is an inn or a counting house. Say so plainly in the dossier.

Structural imperatives: exchanges need a court large enough for thousands, a bell/clock to open and close trading, portals on several streets, vaults beneath (which at London "proved dark and damp, and were comparatively valueless" — CONFIRMED-digest), and rent-bearing shops above; the fondaco and bedesten need water access and lockable gates; the auction needs a rostrum/pulpit and a long room; the slave han needs a court with platforms and lockable cells. Siting: at the street intersection or "navel" of the city (Antwerp "in ipso fere civitatis umbelico"), at the Rialto, beside the bedesten. Prosperity/wear: floor = the square in front of an inn; ladder = arcades round a court with a bell; ceiling = vaulted galleries, towers, pawn and undercroft. Decline sheds the trading function first while the shell lives on as academy, guild seat, auction hall (Antwerp 1661–1810; Sandal Bedesten 1914). Live/work: the fondaco is the live/work extreme (merchants and servants sleep above the stores); exchange and lonja house no one but jailed debtors.

**(b) Measured.**
- Antwerp 1531: 36 columns; 4 gates; courtyard 40 × 51 m (CONFIRMED-digest); cost 300,000 gold crowns (CONFIRMED Calabi).
- London 1566–71 (1576 description): 36 columns 12 ft high at 4-pace centres; central walk 6–7 paces wide; court 80 × 60 paces (c. 60 × 45 m PLAUSIBLE conversion); capacity 4,000; portal columns 14 ft; 150 upper stalls; 25–30 stair steps in flights of seven; 36 upper jasper columns at 10 ft (CONFIRMED Alford/Saunders). "100 small shops" above and damp vaults (CONFIRMED-digest, a different count from the 150 of 1576 — stall vs shop reckoning).
- Amsterdam 1611: 23 arcades; 45 columns; extension 1668 (CONFIRMED Calabi).
- Valencia Lonja: hall 35 × 21 m; 8 columns 17.4 m; 24 bays; spiral stair 110 steps (CONFIRMED secondary / digest).
- Fondaco dei Tedeschi 1505–08: 4 floors; 56 rooms; >100 merchants; 5-bay canal loggia; clock 1571 (CONFIRMED).
- Iç Bedesten: 43.30 × 29.50 m; 8 piers; 15 domed bays; 44 mahzen. Sandal Bedesten: 40.20 × 42.20 m; 12 piers; 20 domes. Bazaar 1890: 4,399 shops, 2,195 rooms, 24 han, 30.7 ha, 18 gates, 61 streets. Dolap 6–8 ft × 3–4 ft (CONFIRMED).
- Ottoman slave tax: 1/40 of value (CONFIRMED).
- Fuggerzeitungen: c. 35,000 pages, 1568–1605 (CONFIRMED).
- Lloyd's: opened 1686; Lombard St 1691; pulpit; Lloyd's List 1734; moved into the Royal Exchange 1774 (CONFIRMED).
- No measured figures found for: the Oude Beurs 1515 plan, the Avret Pazarı han, any Genoese/Iberian slave venue, any 17th-c. auction room, any notary's office (searched: Antwerp plan, Royal Exchange plan, Lonja dimensions, esir pazarı [search refused — budget], Wikipedia API title searches for Esir Pazarı, Fugger newsletters, Huis ter Beurze, Avrat Pazarı).

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**
- The exchange was NOT where exchange happened: London's stockbrokers "were not allowed into the Royal Exchange because of their rude manners" and worked from Jonathan's coffee house (CONFIRMED Wikipedia); for a century before 1531 the Antwerp, Bruges, Lyon and Toulouse exchanges were squares ("place") and an inn's court (CONFIRMED Calabi). A grammar that insists on a building for L2435 is wrong for most of the register; the building is a 1531+ ceiling.
- Fairs preceded and outlasted exchanges as the venue of international money business, "hosted in temporary wooden shops on empty plots at the boundary of the settlements" (CONFIRMED Calabi) — Family A's booth town is also Family R's floor.
- The lonja/loggia is not an enclosed hall everywhere: the Italian form is an open loggia with a court room above; the Valencia columned hall is the Iberian peak, not the mean (PLAUSIBLE).
- Slave venues: the only described building is 19th-century and post-fire; the 15th–17th-century Istanbul market is known from its officials, tax and a traveller's glance, not a plan. The Gorée "House of Slaves" is a c. 1776 merchant's house whose slave-trade role "historians have minimised since the 1980s" (CONFIRMED pt.wikipedia) — a warning against borrowing reconstructed slave-house interiors from any period.
- Listening post / Chroniclers' exchange: negation result is the finding itself — no dedicated news building exists in the register; the Fugger office is a desk, Lloyd's is a coffee room with a pulpit, the exchange gallery is where letters were compared. Null result for a "news house" as such.
- Negation searches issued: the Bruges/inn question was answered by fetch (hostelry from 1285, CONFIRMED); the Istanbul building search was refused (session WebSearch budget exhausted at 200/200), replaced by Wikipedia API lookups.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**
- Auction house L1670: floor = a coffee-house room (single `THROUGH_ROOM` with boxes and a pulpit/rostrum; stair winder to private rooms PLAUSIBLE); ceiling (18th c., outside register) = galleried sale room reached by a `STAIR_HALL`. Licensed city×prosperous×1640+ (candle auctions), building form 1720+.
- Slave market L1001 (town): exterior `MARKET_GROUND` zone beside the bedesten/market; no interior. L1635 (city): han parti — gated `VESTIBULE` → open court ringed by raised platforms → `GALLERY` on the court side giving onto latticed cells; one stair (stone winder PLAUSIBLE) to an upper gallery of rooms; broker's room at the gate. L1642 (district): several such han + the guild sheikh's and emin's offices, i.e. a `CORRIDOR`-less cluster of courts; circulation is the bazaar street.
- Listening post L1073/L1697: inn parti — `CROSS_PASSAGE` to the yard, register kept at the gate/counter (Fontegher analogue), one writing room; stair winder. Chroniclers' exchange L1706: counting-house parti — street shop-room (counter) → back `THROUGH_ROOM` writing/archive room → upper chamber; or the exchange gallery itself (`GALLERY`) as the public cross-checking floor. Stair winder; no grand stair.
- International trade center L2435: exchange parti — four `VESTIBULE` portals → four-sided `GALLERY` cloister (36 columns, 12 ft high, 4-pace bays; walk 6–7 paces wide) round an open court (80 × 60 paces) → stair in flights of seven to an upper `GALLERY` of 150 stalls → `UNDERCROFT` walk beneath; bell tower. Fondaco variant: water `VESTIBULE` (5-bay loggia) → court with tiered `GALLERY` on four floors → `STAIR_HALL` (monumental, Cairo-type) → lodging rows as `THROUGH_ROOM` chains. Lonja variant: columned hall → `STAIR_HALL` spiral (110 steps) → tribunal over prison. Licensed metropolis×prosperous×1485+ (Antwerp gemeyne borze), full form 1531+.

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**
- Auction house: `STORE` for lots (crates, barrels) behind/below the room; strongbox for deposits. Fixtures: rostrum, candle-stand, hammer, catalogue desk (`counter`, `ledger`, `strongbox`).
- Slave market han: cells = `lodging`-sized locked rooms off the gallery (these are cells, not storage; no storage class applies); a `strongroom` for the emin's tax chest and the brokers' `records`/provenance papers. Fixtures: raised platform, lattice, gate bar.
- Listening post: `records` room with register presses; a `closet`-grade strongbox. Chroniclers' exchange: `records` archive as a full cell (lightReq NORTH/ANY, adjacency: off the writing room, no hearth), `strongroom` for graded claims and cash; fixtures: letter racks (`rack`), presses, `ledger`, `counter`.
- International trade center: exchange = `CELLAR`/`UNDERCROFT` walk of stalls (dark and damp, low value — CONFIRMED-digest), upper pawn = retail cells not storage; fondaco = ground-floor `STORE` bays water-accessed, rental, one per merchant firm, `strongroom` fixtures; bedesten = 44 mahzen = windowless vaulted `strongroom`/`STORE` cells in the wall thickness, lightReq NONE, adjacency: direct onto the hall, gated at night.

**(f) Typed proposal.**
- `EXCHANGE_COURT_CLOISTER` (L2435 ceiling; L1706 public floor): functions floor = square before an inn; rungs = arcades on one side / bell; ceiling = four vaulted galleries, four portals, two towers, pawn over, undercroft beneath. fixtures[]: columns(36), bell/clock, nation-walk markers, statue niches, paving. structural: `heavyFloor` (vaults), `undercroftDamp`. licensing: city+×prosperous×1485+/1531+. HOME: ROOM_KINDS.hall (court+galleries), ROOM_KINDS.stall (pawn), ROOM_KINDS.cellar (New Venice), ROOM_KINDS.records (tribunal, Antwerp/Valencia).
- `FONDACO_NATION_HOUSE` (L2435 metropolis variant): court + 4 tiered galleries + water loggia + 56 lodging rooms + ground stores + Fontegher's register at the gate. structural: `waterAccess`, `gatedNight`. HOME: ROOM_KINDS.lodging, ROOM_KINDS.store, ROOM_KINDS.counting, ROOM_KINDS.records.
- `LONJA_COLUMNED_HALL` (L2435 Iberian variant): 35 × 21 m hall, consulate wing (court over prison), tower with debtor cells, orange court. HOME: ROOM_KINDS.hall, ROOM_KINDS.records, ROOM_KINDS.evidence (prison) — NO TYPED HOME for "prison cell" as such.
- `BEDESTEN_LOCKED_HALL` (L2435/L1642 Ottoman variant): domed hall on piers, 44 wall cells, iron gates, clerestory. HOME: ROOM_KINDS.strongroom (mahzen), ROOM_KINDS.stall.
- `AUCTION_ROOM_CANDLE` (L1670): coffee-room floor with pulpit; sale-room ceiling (18th c.). fixtures[]: rostrum/pulpit, candle, boxes, lot store. HOME: ROOM_KINDS.common (coffee room), ROOM_KINDS.store; NO TYPED HOME for "sale room".
- `SLAVE_HAN_COURT` (L1635) / `SLAVE_PAZAR_GROUND` (L1001) / `SLAVE_QUARTER` (L1642): described above; fixtures[]: platform, lattice, gate, tax chest, brokers' bench. structural: `gatedNight`. licensing: only where the catalog entry fires; era any. HOME: ROOM_KINDS.hall (court), ROOM_KINDS.lodging (cells), ROOM_KINDS.counting (emin), ROOM_KINDS.records.
- `NEWS_COUNTING_HOUSE` (L1073, L1697, L1706): counter + writing room + archive + strongbox; at L1706 add a public gallery or coffee room with pulpit. fixtures[]: `counter`, `ledger`, `rack` (letter rack), `strongbox`, pulpit. HOME: ROOM_KINDS.counting, ROOM_KINDS.records, ROOM_KINDS.common. Flag in the dossier: analogue-only; no historical interior.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**
The exchange needs a COURT-WITH-CLOISTER parti (open quad, four-sided gallery, multiple street portals, rooms over the gallery, optional undercroft) — the same `GALLERY` pull as Family A's market hall and the inn yard, so one cloister primitive serves market hall, exchange, fondaco and han. The grammar must also express "nation walks": named standing zones inside one hall without walls (the Family A exterior-zone primitive applied to an interior floor). The fondaco requires lodging rows stacked over rental stores with a gate register — an `lodging`+`store` dual program under one owner with a `concealed`/`records` gate room. The bedesten requires cells carved in wall thickness (fixtures that are rooms) and a night-gated flag. The auction house and both information entries have NO building of their own in the register and should be licensed as programs that attach to an existing host (coffee house, inn, counting house, exchange gallery) — the catalog's L1073 "appears wherever traffic does" is best realised as a fixture-program on an inn, not a standalone shell. Catalog observation for the parent: L1670 Auction house carries priorityCategory "military" in the flat TSV, which looks like a data slip.

**(h) Continental vs English.**
The English register has exactly one exchange (1566–71) and it is an Antwerp import down to the stone, the mason and the column count; its vaults failed and its brokers were thrown out. The continental mechanisms are richer: the Flemish inn-court that became a square (Bruges), the Brabantine cloister-bourse with rent-paying pand over (Antwerp, copied at Amsterdam with shops in the outer vaults and a bridge entry), the Iberian lonja with consulate tribunal and debtors' tower (Valencia, Palma), the Italian open loggia with court room above and the Venetian fondaco as state-run nation hostel and bonded store, and the Ottoman bedesten/han as gated strongroom-hall and courtyard with cells — the last being the only documented built form for the slave market. For news, the continental desk (Fugger Zeitungen, Antwerp's galleries reading letters aloud) predates the English coffee-house pulpit by a century.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None for any entry; the Listening post and Chroniclers' exchange are not magical, merely ahistorical as buildings.

## §21 · FAMILY S — guildhalls-as-trade: Merchant guilds (3-8) (town L922) · Craft guilds (5-15) (town L929 REQ) · Merchant guilds (15-40) (city L1583) · Craft guilds (30-80) (city L1590) · Merchant guilds (50-100+) (metropolis L2419) · Craft guilds (100-150+) (metropolis L2251)

*Researched by sub-lane PS (a subagent of this lane); every fetch dated 2026-08-23; labels are the sub-lane's, reviewed by the parent. *

**(a) Analogue.**

The first finding is that the catalog entries are COUNTS OF GUILDS, not buildings, and most guilds had no building. The on-disk primary is explicit: in fourteenth-century York craft mysteries used for business meetings "the naves and aisles of parish churches" where their fraternities kept altars; the carpenters met in the Austin Friary, the cordwainers in the Carmelite Friary, others in houses of religious orders; and "the fact that many religious fraternities and craft mysteries never built guildhalls is significant" (**CONFIRMED**, Giles 2000, BAR 315 ch. 6, RINST1-giles-guildhalls-chap6.txt pp. 113–119, read 2026-08-23). Building a hall was "a highly charged act" tied to amalgamation, expansion or crisis (York butchers' and cordwainers' halls in the fifteenth century; the haberdashers' hall only from early-17th-century bequests). A guild had three "modes of expression" — a meeting place, a devotional focus (altar/light in a church), a charitable focus (maison dieu/hospital) — and the hall's purpose was to bring two or three "under one roof" (**CONFIRMED**, Giles p. 113). So per tier: town (5–15 craft guilds) = a church aisle, a friary chamber, a tavern room, or ONE shared "common hall" (York's common hall had a leather seld set up in it in 1428 to settle the cordwainer–tanner dispute — **CONFIRMED**, Giles p. 118); city (30–80 crafts) = a handful of owned halls plus the common hall; metropolis = a street of halls (London: Merchant Taylors' on its site since 1347, Goldsmiths' since 1339, Apothecaries' from 1632 — all **CONFIRMED**, en.wikipedia pages fetched 2026-08-23).

The room program of the owned hall, from the primary: a gatehouse and courtyard as "carrier space" (St John the Baptist's/Taylors' hall, York: a two-bay, two-storey gatehouse with a chamber over the gate, demolished c.1702–3; Trinity hall: "pavyng the entre to the halle warde" 1433–4); the open hall on the tripartite plan — a screens-passage bay narrower than the others and ceiled over with a gallery above it (Trinity, St Anthony's, the Guildhall) or a chamber floor (St John's), a moveable screen (St John's, given to St Crux in 1692) or a fixed partition (St Anthony's, 1611), a longer dais bay with larger windows, the first-floor porch opening into a second carrier space between services and hall; a kitchen (Trinity: a reredos made 1433; brass pots, a long six-footed brandreth and a great stone mortar inventoried 1495; St John's kitchen attested c.1441; St Anthony's kitchen with a chamber over, early 17th c.); a TREASURE HOUSE holding an iron-bound "kyste" bought 1436–7 for 6s 8d and keyed 1438, and by 1554 a second "irynbound kist of Pruce makyng," both "for evydence"; a "counting" or "counsel" house at St John's, possibly fifteenth-century; a hospital/maison dieu in the undercroft under the first-floor hall (Trinity: thirteen poor in 1360, thirty by 1411) or in a separate abutting block; the chapel to the east of the hospital; glass windows commissioned 1477 and 1504 (all **CONFIRMED**, Giles pp. 121–135). Furniture at the dais end in a mid-16th-century Trinity inventory: two boards and five trestles "for the dece," five meat boards with trestles on the south side, four on the north, seven forms, a painted or tapestry "hallyng" behind the high dais (1488), pewter doublers, dishes and saucers (1493), napery and plate in a "greite arke" (**CONFIRMED**, Giles pp. 132–133). Use: the "court day" at "ten of belle"; new masters registered their shop or warehouse before the company in the hall (mercers' ordinances 1495); apprentices sworn "uppon a bouke"; fines for quarrelling "in the maister presence"; the searchers' authority reinforced at these meetings (**CONFIRMED**, Giles pp. 134–136). Guilds copied seigneurial fabric deliberately: London drapers inspected the Celestines at Sheen and the Bishop of Bath's hall before building in St Swithin's Lane in 1425; pewterers viewed four roofs in 1496; the Merchant Taylors' new kitchen roof of 1433/4 was partly modelled on Kennington Palace; and halls ran to the scale of Kenilworth, Dartington and Gainsborough Old Hall, not of town houses (**CONFIRMED**, Giles pp. 119–121, citing Schofield 1995). Undercrofts mattered for hospitals but London's shearmen simply rented theirs out (**CONFIRMED**, Giles p. 120).

The TRADE side specifically: the livery hall is the guild's quality-control and registration office as much as its feasting hall. Merchant Taylors' Hall: site between Threadneedle Street and Cornhill since 1347; first hall built 1347–1392; the Great Kitchen "in continuous use since 1425"; program of great hall, kitchen, parlour, drawing room, court room, library, chapel crypt (late-14th-century chapel); gutted to the walls 1666; the 14th-century beaten clay floor, the 1646 red tile floor and the 1675 marble-and-Purbeck floor all survive in section (**CONFIRMED**, en.wikipedia Merchant Taylors' Hall fetched 2026-08-23). Apothecaries' Hall: site bought 1632, rebuilt 1668–72 "on the same site and footprint of rooms," program Great Hall, Court Room, Parlour, and an "Elaboratory" for drug manufacture which ran 1672–1922 supplying navy, army and East India Company, with a retail shop opening onto Water Lane; a 9 m high run of 13th-century wall survives in the courtyard's north range (**CONFIRMED**, en.wikipedia Apothecaries' Hall, London fetched 2026-08-23) — the one London hall where the trade's WORKFLOOR and SHOP are inside the guildhall. Goldsmiths': on site since 1339; the 1634–36 hall survived 1666 and was demolished in the late 1820s; the assay office's operation inside the hall is not described on the page (**CONFIRMED** for dates; the assay/hallmark mechanism **PLAUSIBLE**). The cloth-search mechanism: alnage (1196 Assize of Measures; Magna Carta art. 35, 1215; an alnager under Edward I; size rules repealed 1353; new-drapery alnager 1665; duties and office abolished 1699 per the page) — an official measured each piece and affixed a seal, faulty cloth "forfeit to the crown" (**CONFIRMED**, en.wikipedia Alnage fetched 2026-08-23). Where it happened (a cloth hall, the guildhall, the market) is **PLAUSIBLE**. King's Lynn's Guildhall of St George: built 1410–1420, 32.6 × 8.8 m (107 × 29 ft), brick, two storeys, hammerbeam roof, undercroft beneath, theatricals from 1445, to the Corporation 1547, used as court house, school and Civil-War munitions store, sold 1814 and converted to a warehouse by the Everards in 1826 (**CONFIRMED**, en.wikipedia St George's Guildhall fetched 2026-08-23). Lavenham: the Guild of Corpus Christi formed 1529 built its hall about then, jettied, with a central gabled porch; four guilds regulated the wool and cloth trade; bridewell by 1689, workhouse 1787 (**CONFIRMED**, en.wikipedia Lavenham Guildhall fetched 2026-08-23).

Continental: Florence's Palazzo dell'Arte della Lana, erected 1308 round an older Compiobbesi tower; the Sala delle Udienze (the guild's audience/tribunal hall) on the ground floor on via Calimala with 14th-century frescoes; three principal floors plus as many mezzanines behind a three-axis stone front; a tabernacle; Buontalenti's covered bridge of 1569 to Orsanmichele's upper floors; the guild ran quality control of cloth and a tribunal until suppressed 1770 (**CONFIRMED**, it.wikipedia Palazzo dell'Arte della Lana fetched 2026-08-23). Braunschweig's Gewandhaus: existed before 1268, first documented 1307, "storage, sales and guild house" of the Gewandschneider, wine cellar added 1329, tavern with spirits privilege 1352, cannon store 1368, later grain store, wine trade and market hall; ground-floor open arcade of three basket arches; the Renaissance east gable 1590–91 (**CONFIRMED**, de.wikipedia Gewandhaus (Braunschweig) fetched 2026-08-23) — the Kaufhaus/Gewandhaus is a warehouse-guildhall hybrid, cloth sold and stored in the same box the guild met in. Zürich: the Zunfthaus zur Waag "completed in 1315" on Münsterhof (**CONFIRMED**, en.wikipedia; the 1636/37 rebuild **PLAUSIBLE**, de.wiki 404); the Zunfthaus zur Meisen, 1752–57 by David Morf for the vintners, tavern-keepers, saddlers and painters, two guild rooms with a balcony hall between, on a site occupied since 1268 (Einsiedlerhof) and rebuilt 1618 (**CONFIRMED**, en.wikipedia). Ghent Graslei: Gildehuis der Vrije Schippers 1531 (Gothic), the masons' house reconstructed 1912 from original plans, the Korenmetershuis 1435 and 1698 (**CONFIRMED**, nl.wikipedia Graslei). Brussels Grand-Place: destroyed 13 August 1695, rebuilt within four years by the guilds under a regime requiring plans to be submitted for approval with fines for non-compliance; Le Roy d'Espagne (bakers), La Brouette (greasers/tallow), Le Sac (carpenters), La Louve (archers), Le Cornet (boatmen), Le Renard (haberdashers), L'Arbre d'Or (brewers), Le Cygne (butchers) (**CONFIRMED**, en.wikipedia Grand-Place fetched 2026-08-23). Frontage widths not found.

Structural imperatives: the hall is a display volume (first-floor hall over an undercroft "physically elevated the hall to a visually prominent position within the courtyard" — **CONFIRMED**, Giles p. 121); the kitchen is a fire cell, detached or at the low end; the treasury is a windowless strong cell; the almshouse is in or beside the undercroft; light concentrates at the dais (bigger windows, oriel). Siting: in the craft's own occupational zone and near its church (**CONFIRMED**, Giles pp. 114–116); the Zunfthaus on the main square or quay; the Arte palazzo beside the grain market it policed. Prosperity: floor = a rented aisle/tavern room + a chest in the church; ladder = shared common hall; ceiling = courtyard hall with gatehouse, hospital, chapel, kitchen, parlour, treasury. Decline sheds the chapel first (Reformation), then lets the hall (theatre, school, court, munitions, warehouse — the St George's sequence), then the undercroft (shearmen). Live/work: nobody lives in the guildhall except the hospital master ("continually and personally to dwell there," Trinity, 1360 — **CONFIRMED**) and, in the Zunfthaus, the tenant tavern-keeper (**PLAUSIBLE**).

**(b) Measured.**

- St George's Guildhall, King's Lynn: 32.6 × 8.8 m; two storeys; 1410–1420 (**CONFIRMED**).
- Apothecaries' Hall: 9 m (30 ft) high surviving 13th-century wall; rebuilt 1668–72 (**CONFIRMED**).
- Merchant Taylors': site 1347; hall 1347–1392; kitchen 1425; chapel crypt late 14th c.; floors 14th c. / 1646 / 1675 (**CONFIRMED**). Hall dimensions not found (searched: en.wikipedia; merchant-taylors.co.uk history page 404).
- York Taylors' gatehouse: two bays, two storeys, chamber over gate (**CONFIRMED**, Giles). Trinity hall bays: screens bay narrower, dais bay longer; crown posts on alternate tie beams (**CONFIRMED**, Giles). No metric dimensions in the extracted chapter (the measured tables are in Appendix 6, not in the file).
- Trinity hospital: 13 poor (1360), 30 (1411) (**CONFIRMED**, Giles).
- Evidence chest: 6s 8d (1436–7) (**CONFIRMED**, Giles).
- Dais furniture: 2 boards + 5 trestles (dais); 5 + 4 meat boards; 7 forms (**CONFIRMED**, Giles).
- Arte della Lana: three principal floors plus mezzanines, three axes; 1308; bridge 1569 (**CONFIRMED**).
- Gewandhaus: ground-floor arcade of three basket arches; dates 1268/1307/1329/1352/1368/1590–91 (**CONFIRMED**).
- Meisen: 1752–57; two guild rooms + balcony hall (**CONFIRMED**). Waag: 1315 (**CONFIRMED**).
- Grand-Place: rebuilt 1696–1700 (**CONFIRMED**); no frontages found.
- No measured figures found for: Merchant Taylors' great hall and kitchen, Vintners', Fishmongers', Skinners' (not fetched — budget), Trinity House Hull (no Wikipedia article resolved), Zürich Zunftstube dimensions, Basel/Strasbourg Zunftstuben, Graslei frontages.

**(c) Contested / counterexamples.**

- Most crafts had no hall: York crafts met in church naves/aisles and friaries through the 14th and 15th centuries; many "never built guildhalls" (**CONFIRMED**, Giles). Taverns as meeting places are **PLAUSIBLE** (not in the extracted text); friaries and church aisles are confirmed. For the town tier this is the DEFAULT, not the exception.
- The guildhall is not derived from the house: Giles argues it parallels seigneurial courtyard houses, collegiate halls and parish churches; first-floor vs ground-floor hall shows "no clear patterns or chronological distinctions" in London (**CONFIRMED**, Giles pp. 119–121).
- The open hall persisted in guildhalls after it "declined" domestically; Grenville's counter that late-medieval halls got LARGER with expanded parlours, services and lodging ranges (**CONFIRMED**, Giles p. 147).
- Continental Zunfthaus dates: the Zürich halls that survive are largely 17th–18th century rebuilds (Meisen 1752–57 — **CONFIRMED**) on medieval sites (1268, 1315 — **CONFIRMED**); the Grand-Place is 1696–1700 (**CONFIRMED**). The famous facades are post-register.
- Apothecaries' is the inversion: a guildhall that IS a factory and shop (1672–1922 — **CONFIRMED**); the Gewandhaus is the other inversion: a cloth warehouse/sales hall that is also the guild's house (**CONFIRMED**).
- Halls were re-let as warehouses and vice versa (St George's 1826 — **CONFIRMED**; Hanse House warehouses → granaries).
- Negation-search result: no WebSearch budget remained for Family S; the negation was run against the on-disk primary (grep for tavern/alehouse/common hall/searcher/counting/treasury) and returned the church-aisle/friary/common-hall evidence above and the 1428 leather seld; "tavern" returned null in the Giles extract.

**(d) CIRCULATION typology (§452).**

- Owned hall (city/metropolis, prosperity ≥ wealthy): gatehouse → courtyard (carrier space, LOBBY/VESTIBULE at the gate with a chamber over) → external stair to a first-floor porch (STAIR_HALL in its medieval form: an external or winder stair landing in a porch) → SCREENS_PASSAGE bay (narrower, ceiled, with a GALLERY over for musicians) → open hall THROUGH_ROOM to the dais → parlour/court room beyond the dais (THROUGH_ROOM); services (buttery/pantry/kitchen) at the low end through the screens; hospital in the undercroft with its own ground-floor entrance "almost directly underneath the first floor entrance to the hall" (**CONFIRMED**, Giles p. 122); chapel east of the hospital, sometimes with a pierced passage wall for sight of the altar (St Anthony's — **CONFIRMED**). Stair type: external stone or timber stair → winder; grand stair only at the Baroque Zunfthaus (Meisen, **PLAUSIBLE**) and the 1835 Goldsmiths' (post-register).
- Shared common hall (town): the same tripartite hall without gatehouse or hospital; the "seld" (stall) inserted in it for a trade (**CONFIRMED**, York 1428).
- No-hall guilds (town floor): circulation is the host's — a church aisle (the nave/aisle as GALLERY-class through-space), a friary chamber, an upper room.
- Zunfthaus (continental city): ground-floor tavern/Trinkstube THROUGH_ROOM, stair to the Zunftstube/Zunftsaal on the first floor, a balcony hall between two guild rooms (Meisen — **CONFIRMED**). Arte palazzo: ground-floor Sala delle Udienze opening to the street, upper floors by internal stair, a covered bridge (GALLERY class) to the granary-church at first-floor level (**CONFIRMED**).
- Gewandhaus/Kaufhaus: open ground-floor arcade (GALLERY/loggia) for sale, halls above, cellar below (**CONFIRMED**).

**(e) STORAGE typology (§453).**

- `BUTTERY` + `PANTRY` at the low end behind the screens (standard tripartite — **CONFIRMED** as the plan Giles documents; named cells not itemised in the extract); `LARDER` **PLAUSIBLE** for the larger London kitchens.
- Kitchen as a fire cell with a reredos (1433 — **CONFIRMED**), brass pots, brandreth, stone mortar (FURNISHING_KINDS.cauldron/hearth); sometimes with a chamber over (St Anthony's — **CONFIRMED**).
- Treasury/strongroom: a "tresour house" holding two iron-bound chests "for evydence" (**CONFIRMED**) — the chest is a FIXTURE (FURNISHING_KINDS.strongbox); the treasure house is a `CLOSET`-grade windowless cell with adjacency to the parlour/counting house, lightReq NONE.
- Counting/counsel house (**CONFIRMED**, St John the Baptist's) = ROOM_KINDS.counting; records/muniments = ROOM_KINDS.records / ROOM_KINDS.evidence.
- Plate and napery in a "greite arke" (**CONFIRMED**) — a fixture in the parlour or treasury.
- `CELLAR/UNDERCROFT`: hospital (York), rented out (London shearmen), wine cellar (Gewandhaus 1329), munitions (St George's, Civil War) — all **CONFIRMED**. lightReq NONE.
- `STORE`: the Gewandhaus cloth store (**CONFIRMED**); the Apothecaries' warehouse for drugs (**PLAUSIBLE** — the page names the elaboratory and the retail outlet, not a warehouse cell).
- Search/assay room: **PLAUSIBLE** only — no fetched page locates the search or the assay furnace in a specific cell; the searchers' authority is confirmed, their room is not.
- Almshouse row: the maison dieu is a cell (undercroft or abutting block), each bed a fixture (a "bed... and fourpence" granted by indenture 1439 — **CONFIRMED**).

**(f) Typed proposal.**

- `parti`: `NO_HALL_CHURCH_AISLE` (guild exists, building does not — meets in host church/friary; the town floor), `SHARED_COMMON_HALL` (one civic hall hired by several crafts, with insertable selds), `TAVERN_ROOM_MEETING` (**PLAUSIBLE**, continental Trinkstube-confirmed via Meisen/Gewandhaus tavern 1352), `COURTYARD_LIVERY_HALL` (gatehouse + court + first-floor hall over undercroft + kitchen + parlour/court room + treasury + hospital + chapel), `ZUNFTSTUBE_OVER_TAVERN` (continental: public tavern below, guild room(s) above), `ARTE_PALAZZO_TRIBUNAL` (ground-floor audience hall, offices above, bridge to the market it polices), `GEWANDHAUS_CLOTH_HALL` (arcade sale floor + cloth store + guild room + wine cellar), `SQUARE_AMBACHTSHUIS` (narrow market-square frontage, one guild per house, a street/square of them — the metropolis "street of halls"), `WORKFLOOR_HALL` (Apothecaries: hall + laboratory + shop).
- `functions[]`: floor = meeting (court day), oath-taking, registration of shops/warehouses, arbitration; ladder = search/assay, feast, chapel/obit, almshouse; ceiling = tribunal, manufacture (elaboratory), cloth sale, library, theatre (St George's from 1445).
- `fixtures[]` by grade: evidenceChest (iron-bound, two keys — poor), treasureHouse (wealthy), dais boards + trestles + forms, hallyng/tapestry behind the dais, screen (moveable → fixed), gallery over screens, oriel at the dais, glass windows (1477/1504 — an upgrade, not a default), kitchen reredos + brandreth + mortar, plate ark, seld (insertable stall), searchers' seal/beam/yardwand (**PLAUSIBLE**), assay furnace (**PLAUSIBLE**), hospital beds. Decline sheds: chapel → gallery/screen (donated away 1692) → hall let → undercroft let.
- Structural buckets: `displayVolume` (open hall), `fireTrade` only for kitchen and elaboratory/assay cells, `strongCell` (treasury), `charityCell` (hospital/almshouse), `publicGround` (Zunfthaus tavern, Gewandhaus arcade).
- Licensing: Merchant guilds (3-8) / Craft guilds (5-15), town: default `NO_HALL_CHURCH_AISLE` + at most ONE `SHARED_COMMON_HALL`; an owned hall only at wealthy prosperity and a crisis/amalgamation trigger (which the engine can express as an event flag). City (15-40 / 30-80): a few `COURTYARD_LIVERY_HALL`s for the richest (mercers/merchants, tailors, drapers, butchers), the rest shared/no-hall. Metropolis (50-100+ / 100-150+): `SQUARE_AMBACHTSHUIS` street or a dispersed set of `COURTYARD_LIVERY_HALL`s in each craft's zone; a `GEWANDHAUS_CLOTH_HALL` or `ARTE_PALAZZO_TRIBUNAL` for the staple trade; `WORKFLOOR_HALL` for apothecaries/goldsmiths. Era: courtyard hall ≥ c.1350; continental square facades ≥ c.1530 (Vrije Schippers 1531) with the famous rebuilt sets 1696–1757 being post-register.
- HOME: hall = ROOM_KINDS.hall; dais = ROOM_KINDS.dais; screens/gallery NO TYPED HOME; parlour/court room = ROOM_KINDS.chamber (a stretch — "court room" NO TYPED HOME); kitchen = ROOM_KINDS.kitchen; treasury = ROOM_KINDS.strongroom; evidence/muniments = ROOM_KINDS.evidence and ROOM_KINDS.records; counting house = ROOM_KINDS.counting; common hall = ROOM_KINDS.common; almshouse = ROOM_KINDS.lodging; chapel NO TYPED HOME; search/assay room NO TYPED HOME (assay furnace could borrow ROOM_KINDS.kiln under `fireTrade`, flagged as a borrow); elaboratory = ROOM_KINDS.workfloor; Gewandhaus sale floor = ROOM_KINDS.stall + store; tavern = ROOM_KINDS.common with FURNISHING_KINDS.bar; seld = FURNISHING_KINDS.counter. Evidence chest = FURNISHING_KINDS.strongbox; ledger = FURNISHING_KINDS.ledger.

**(g) Consequences for the grammar.**

The engine must be able to say that a guild EXISTS without owning a building — i.e. the guild entries need a `seat` field that resolves to a host (church aisle, friary, tavern, common hall) rather than a plot; at present a "Craft guilds (5-15) REQ" at town tier would otherwise force a building that the record says usually did not exist. Second, the shared common hall needs a MULTI-TENANT flag (several guilds, insertable selds, hired by the day) distinct from the livery hall's single owner. Third, the courtyard-hall parti needs a gatehouse-as-lobby and a first-floor-hall-over-undercroft rule with the undercroft typed independently (hospital / let / wine); the existing hall/dais kinds cover the hall but not the vertical split. Fourth, the search/assay function needs either a room kind or an explicit "fixture in the parlour" rule — it is the trade-regulation act that the catalog text ("regulate quality") promises and no current kind holds. Fifth, the guildhall is the only civic building class whose decline path is well-attested (chapel → hall let → undercroft let → warehouse), and that sequence lands on the existing wear/prosperity pull as a typed ladder rather than a random swap.

**(h) Continental vs English.**

The English register is parochial in making the guildhall a first-floor feasting hall over a hospital in a gated court, copied from the seigneurial house, with the trade's policing (search, alnage) as an administrative act done in the parlour; the continental mechanism fuses hall and trade: the German Gewandhaus/Kaufhaus is a cloth warehouse and sale-arcade that is also the guild's house and tavern (Braunschweig 1307/1329/1352); the Zürich Zunfthaus is a public tavern with the Zunftstube above, on the square; the Florentine Arte is a palazzo-tribunal with a ground-floor audience hall and a bridge to the market it regulates; the Flemish ambachtshuis is a narrow gabled facade on the market square or quay, one per craft, producing the "street of halls" the metropolis entry describes (Graslei 1531; Grand-Place 1696–1700). The Iberian lonja and the Ottoman bedesten are exchange halls, not guild seats, and are out of scope here.

**(i) Fantasy note.** None.

**Parent-lane review note.** The sub-lane read the on-disk Giles primary (RINST1-giles-guildhalls-chap6.txt, the sibling lane's extraction) rather than re-fetching; its key result for the catalog — most crafts had NO hall and met in church aisles/friaries/the common hall — means the town-tier "Craft guilds (5-15) REQ" must compile to a `seat` on a host, not a building (§Σ).

## §22 · CONTINENTAL-VS-ENGLISH REGISTER — where this tranche is Anglocentric and what the continental mechanism is

One pattern runs through all twenty families' (h) paragraphs, and it is the tranche's single most important finding for a SETTING-AGNOSTIC engine: **England keeps the trade function DOMESTIC (in the house, at the inn, on the market ground), where the continent BUILDS THE INSTITUTION.** Concretely, by mechanism (each line cites the family sections that ground it; labels as in those sections):

1. **The covered frontage.** The English shop is an open shuttered window under a pentice (§3); the continent puts it under a statutory ARCADE — Bologna 1288 (≥ 2.66 m high and wide), Freiburg 1249, the Bern Lauben re-fronting after 1405 (c. 2 m deep, > 6 km), the French full-width arc-and-étal (§3-bis). The engine's frontage model has no covered-frontage axis (§Σ gap 5).
2. **The courtyard compound with a ring of identical cells.** The caravanserai/han (Sultanhanı 1229: 44 × 58 m court; Koza Han 1491: 45.9 × 37.5 m, 50 + 54 cells), the fondaco (Venice 1508: 56 rooms over water-accessed stores), the Hanseatic Kontor row (Bryggen), the alhóndiga (Granada, three galleried storeys), the exchange cloister (Antwerp 1531 / London 1571: 36 columns round an 80 × 60-pace court), the Monte di Pietà portico (§16, §18, §20, §19). The English register has NONE of these — the inn-yard and the carriers' named day do the work (§15, §16) — so every city/metropolis "exchange", "trade center", "caravan masters'" and "warehouse district" entry is a continental parti the grammar must carry as a licensed import.
3. **Guild-owned process plant.** Florence's six tiratoi (1498) and the Arte della Lana's palazzo-tribunal with a bridge to the granary-church (§10, §21); the Flemish/Dutch vleeshuis, waag and lakenhalle with per-guild stairs and boat channels (§2, §13); the German Gewandhaus that is warehouse, sale-arcade, tavern and guild house in one (§21); the Zürich Zunfthaus with the public tavern below the Zunftstube (§21). The English guild polices in the parlour and feasts in a first-floor hall over an almshouse; most English crafts had no hall at all (Giles, §21).
4. **Civic stores.** The loggia-garner with grain chutes (Orsanmichele 1337), the Ghent spijker, the pósito as a grain bank, Danzig's 300-granary island (§18) versus Leadenhall, London's single one-off; the 54-house Lüneburg Saline under a civic Sülfmeister class versus the English smallholder's salt (§12); the Ratsapotheke leased by the council (Tallinn, Schweinfurt) versus the English apothecary-grocer until 1617 (§4).
5. **Earlier machines.** The water sawmill (Normandy 1204, Villard c. 1235) against England's saw pit until 1760 (§11); the blast furnace at Lapphyttan c. 1150–1350 against the Weald's 1490 (§7-bis); the harbour treadwheel crane (Utrecht 1244, Gdańsk 1444 with 6.5 m wheels) against England's first in 1331 (§17); the Spessart glass guild of 1406 and Murano's 1291 quarantine against England's 1567 immigrants and 1615 coal shift (§8); the Dutch pakhuis and the Lübeck Salzspeicher (both c. 1580–1600) as the first FREE-STANDING merchant warehouses anywhere in the register (§18).
6. **Where England is the better-measured side.** The sheepcote (23–65 × 6–10 m, cruck-framed, documented from 1208) is the best-measured pastoral building in Europe (§14); Pantin's town-house typology (§3) and the Exeter bell-foundry sequence (§7-bis) are primaries the continental literature fetched this lane does not match; the sub-floor trades (bell pit, sawpit ≥ 1450, tan pit, dry dock 1495) are English-excavated.
7. **Where the register is neither.** The Ottoman bedesten (Istanbul 43.3 × 29.5 m, 44 wall cells, iron gates) and esir pazarı/han are the ONLY documented built forms for the catalog's lock-up exchange and slave-market entries (§20); the Scandinavian naust (34 × 13 m) is the only measured boat-house (§17); the Irish horizontal mill (7th–9th c.) is the floor of the mill stack (§11). `EUROPEAN_FANTASY_BASE` must carry these as bounded possibilities with a culture gate, never as the default silhouette.

Consequence for the charter's law 8 (the parti draw weighted by prosperity × institution × CULTURE): in this tranche the culture weight is not a style veneer — it decides whether an institution HAS a building at all (the English "Craft guilds REQ" is a seat in a church aisle; the continental one is a Zunfthaus on the square). The CT-0 taste sitting should rule on the culture axis before the parti catalog is compiled.

---

## §Σ · FINDING → DW-LAW MAP (charter §2 laws and §4 stages → the entries that ground them) and the ENGINE-GAP FLAGS

**Law 1 (functions, not rooms).** Grounded hardest by the one-cell trades: the smithy's hearth/anvil/quench triangle with a SCREENED fuel bay (§7, Ballykillaboy — the partition is a storage cell that is not a room); the shop that IS the workshop (§1.1, Salter); the alewife's kitchen that IS the brewery (§13); the dairy that is a CELL of the farmhouse and the only output of an economy entry (§14). The requirement roster for this tranche must let a FUNCTION land as a fixture-set on a host cell without minting a room.

**Law 2 (three-clamp ceiling).** The economic clamp has measured anchors: Coventry 1520 — 6,601 citizens, 43 bakers, 68 brewers (§13); Salisbury 1421 — 81 master weavers with 207 journeymen (§10); Northwich 1605 — 113 salt houses (§12); Walbrook 1292–1332 — 22→31 skinners (§3-bis). The parcel clamp has Pantin's 30–50 ft frontage threshold (§3). The institutional ceiling is the continental compound (§22.2) and must be culture-gated.

**Law 3 (the floor is existence).** The tranche's floor census is the finding that most changes the grammar: **roughly thirty of the 124 entries have NO BUILDING at the floor** — every market entry (§2), the thatcher (§6), the charcoal burner, peat cutter and woodcutter (§12), the beekeeper, wildfowler, shepherd and thorp fisher (§14), the horse fair and the street-stand carriers (§15, §16), the ferry and the beach-careened shipyard (§17), the town-tier money-changer's bench (§19), the auction and both news entries (§20), and most town-tier guilds (§21). Their existence is a FIXTURE SET on ground or on a host, and four of them are catalog REQ rows (Market square, Weekly market, Town granary, Craft guilds). Law 3 therefore needs a `NO_BUILDING` outcome that still satisfies existence.

**Law 5 (stable anchors / renovation).** Dated use-change sequences to mint fossils from: Hampton Court King's Lynn (hall house → quadrangle → cottages, §3); Tackley's cellar let as a wine tavern 1363 (§3); St George's Guildhall → theatre 1445 → court → munitions → warehouse 1826 and the Hanse House warehouses → granaries 1751 (§18, §21 — the box is interchangeable, the fixtures type it); Kenilworth stables → barn (§15); Ponte Vecchio butchers → goldsmiths 1593 (§3-bis); Orsanmichele loggia → granary → church 1380–1406 (§18); the Portsmouth 1495 dock "filled with rubble" within a century (§17).

**Law 6 (frontage from the parcel).** Pantin's parti switch at 30–50 ft is the first measured consumer of `frontageBucket` (§3); the counting-house faces the YARD/QUAY with its own door, not the street (§3, Hampton Court); the apothecary's Offizin, the tailor's window-board and the weaver's loom take the longest light (§4, §3-bis, §10) — `lightReq` belongs on WORK cells, not only on storage; the market-stall poverty inversion is documented both ways (Lady Row rent units; the Beverley skinners' rented "window"; the Welsh weaver selling at market — §3, §3-bis, §10).

**Law 7 (basements are the undercity's projection).** Joints this tranche adds: the cellar with its OWN street door let separately (Tackley's 1363; the Bern Kellerhals — §3, §3-bis); the vintner's vault as the PRIMARY cell (§13); the Nottingham cave tannery and cave maltings (§9, §13); the bell pit and the 2.1 m anvil pit as sub-floor cells with no walls (§7-bis); the dry dock as a below-grade tidal workfloor (§17); the earth cellar's airlock ante-cell (§18); the Steelyard/Gewandhaus wine cellars (§18, §21).

**Law 8 (the parti draw).** The measured parti catalog this tranche delivers: the nine Pantin town-house forms (§3); ARCH_FRONT / ARCADE_FRONT / VOORHUIS_SEQUENCE / SHOP_ROW_FRAME / BRIDGE_ROW (§3-bis); MARKET_GROUND_ZONED / MARKET_HALL_ARCADE_OVER / HALLE_OPEN_TIMBER / COMMODITY_HALL_WALLED / WEIGH_HOUSE / FAIR_BOOTH_TOWN (§2); the mill stacks and MILL_ROW (§11); TANYARD_* and TANNER_HOUSE_DRYING_LOFT (§9); WICH_HOUSE_ROW (§12); WALLED_COURT_SINGLE_GATE → TWO_TIER_GALLERY_HAN → FONDACO_WATER_COURT / KONTOR_ROW (§16); QUAY_SPEICHER / COURTYARD_KONTOR / LOGGIA_GARNER / GRANARY_ISLAND (§18); EXCHANGE_COURT_CLOISTER / LONJA / BEDESTEN (§20); COURTYARD_LIVERY_HALL / ZUNFTSTUBE_OVER_TAVERN / ARTE_PALAZZO / GEWANDHAUS / SQUARE_AMBACHTSHUIS (§21). Culture weight decides existence (§22).

**Law 9 (vertical honesty).** The mill is a machine-mandated stack (§11); the warehouse is N identical STORE floors with an external hoist (§18); the tanner's and glassmaker's roof storeys are PROCESS rooms with exterior consequence (louvred drying loft; fuel dried above the furnace — §9, §8); the garret loomshop's long window is a post-1700 elevation gate (§10); the Chester Row and the market hall's room-over-arcade are two-level frontages (§3, §2).

**S6 circulation (ODQ §452) — the tranche's typology in one paragraph.** `THROUGH_ROOM` is the floor everywhere; `SCREENS_PASSAGE` 3–4 ft (Coventry) to a 10-ft ENTRY (Oxford) in the shop-house (§3); `CROSS_PASSAGE` widened to cart width for the carpenter's and butcher's yards (§6, §13 — PLAUSIBLE widths); `GALLERY` is the tranche's richest class — the merchant's tresaunce of 1405 (the earliest dated domestic one here), the quay arcade, the Laube/portico walk, the inn and han galleries, the tanner's DRYING gallery, the market hall's arcade, the exchange cloister, the Arte's bridge (§3, §3-bis, §9, §2, §16, §20, §21); `CORRIDOR` appears ONCE in the register, in a Dutch merchant house of 1648 (§3-bis) — its license is era ≥ 1650 and continental; `LOBBY/VESTIBULE` is the han's single gate, the earth cellar's airlock and the waystation's gate (§16, §18); `STAIR_HALL` is the livery hall's external stair to a first-floor porch, the exchange's flights of seven, the Lonja's 110-step spiral and the crane-tower's four floors (§21, §20, §17). Stair type by grade: ladder (row unit, smithy loft, net shop hatches) → winder (rung 1 everywhere) → external stair with oriel/porch (merchant court, guildhall, fish cellar offices) → spiral in stone corner towers (Leadenhall, Orsanmichele) → grand only post-register. Two classes the set LACKS: a VERTICAL non-human class (hoist, grain chute, sack hoist — §18, §11) and an EXTERIOR/OPEN walk (the market zone, the quay, the ropewalk, the court — §2, §17, §6).

**S6 storage (ODQ §453) — the tranche's typology in one paragraph.** `BUTTERY`+`PANTRY` as a pair off the screens (Pantin p. 212: buttery larger; "dispence" when only one — §3) and in every livery hall (§21); `LARDER` for the butcher's hanging room and the lodge's game (§13, §14); `DAIRY` as the whole output of an entry (§14); `STILL_ROOM` = the apothecary's laboratory with flue (§4); `SCULLERY`-class = the dye house furnace room (§10); `STORE` in six sub-forms the vocabulary must distinguish by FIXTURE not by class — the screened bay (§7), the stacked warehouse floor with hoist (§18), the granary floor with bins/staddles/louvres (§18), the han/fondaco cell that is STORE below and lodging above (§16), the drying loft that is a process room (§9, §4, §8), the Speicher with a building-wide NO-FIRE rule (§18); `CELLAR/UNDERCROFT` with the separately-let and street-door variants (law 7 above); `ATTIC/GARRET_STORE` as the pre-1600 merchant's warehouse (§18). Adjacency laws recovered: counting-house beside warehouse and quay (§3); larder away from the scalding copper (§13); fuel store OUTSIDE the smithy with NO internal door (§7-bis — a PROHIBITION, which the adjacency set cannot yet express); hearth never under the herb loft (§4); pledge store beside the counting room, not the vault (§19); bakehouse 35 m from the moat, kiln 10–30 m from the dwelling (§13, §8).

**ENGINE-GAP FLAGS (consolidated; each names the family sections that demand it).**
1. `NO_BUILDING` outcome with a fixture set on ground or host — ~30 entries incl. four REQ rows (§2, §6, §12, §14, §15, §16, §17, §19, §20, §21). Largest gap in the tranche.
2. EXTERIOR ZONED PARTI (market ground with point/linear fixtures, encroachment rule that hardens rows into shop-houses) — §2; the same primitive serves nation-walks inside an exchange (§20) and the yard-as-primary trades (§6, §8, §9, §12).
3. N-IDENTICAL-CELLS generator (let shops, stalls 5 × 10 ft, han cells, Speicher floors, wich houses, ambachtshuis street, presses at 6–9 m² each) — §3, §15, §16, §18, §12, §21, §5.
4. `tenancy` layer on frontage cells + `separatelyLet` on CELLAR (§1.2, §3, §3-bis).
5. HOSTED-INSTITUTION relation ("occupies rooms of host X"): council pharmacy (§4), chartroom in a hall (§5), exchange bank in the town hall (§19), auction/news in coffee house or inn (§20), guild seat in a church aisle (§21), post-room at an inn (§15), tallow melting in a yard outhouse (§3-bis). Seven families converge on one missing capability.
6. `coveredFrontage` axis (arcade/Laube/portico, 2–3 m deep) — `frontage.js` has four axes and none of them (§3-bis).
7. WATER-EDGE frontage kind — the quay arcade, the hoist bay, the fondaco loggia, the boat channels under the Vleeshuis (§3, §18, §16, §2); `frontage.js` has no water edge (engine recon §0).
8. VERTICAL circulation class (hoist/chute/sack hoist) and OPEN-AT-ONE-END volumes (naust, covered slip, open-fronted smithy) — §18, §11, §17, §7.
9. SUB-FLOOR cells without walls (bell pit, anvil pit, sawpit, tan/lime pits, dry dock, casting pit) — §7-bis, §6, §9, §17.
10. LINEAR length-dominant structures (ropewalk 50/300–400/1,000 m; quay; tenter strip 131 × 1.8 m; mill leat/pond/causeway) and an EXTERIOR FIXTURE FIELD class — §6, §17, §10, §11.
11. Structural buckets to add: `fireTrade` with a MINOR grade (cresset, glue pot, wax hearth) and an EXTREME grade (glass, furnace); `noFire` building-wide (Speicher, granary); `fuelHunger` as a siting pull distinct from fire (§8); `SECURE_SHELL` (stone, windowless ground floor, multi-lock door — §19, goldsmith §3-bis); `wetTrade`/`noxious` as SOFT pulls (no expelling ordinance found — §9; butchers inside the walls — §13); `ventilatedLoft`; `defensible` (blank-walled han); `subFloorPit`; `clayQuarry`; `windSited` (bole); `seasonal`/`temporary` (fair, brick clamp, coastal saltern, moated lodge).
12. ERA × REGISTER gates that REFUSE a parti, not merely grade it: sawmill (1204 continental / 1594 Dutch wind / 1760 English); glasshouse (1567/1615 English; 1291 Murano); free-standing warehouse (≥ c. 1580); corridor (≥ 1650); loomshop (≥ 1720); English market hall (≥ c. 1600); harbour crane (≥ 1244; England ≥ 1331); dry dock (≥ 1495); decoy (≥ 1620); earth cellar (≥ 1700); printing (≥ 1450, court form ≥ 1575); loose box (≥ 18th c.) — §11, §8, §18, §3-bis, §10, §2, §17, §14, §5, §15.
13. REQ switches tied to LORDSHIP/CULTURE not technology: `suitOfMill` (Kent had no suit of mill and persistent handmilling — §11); market REQ (§2); granary REQ (an import — §18); craft-guild REQ (a seat, not a hall — §21).
14. `ROOM_KINDS` hygiene: `stall` is the market FIXTURE, the shop is a room (§1.3); `kiln` is a room kind but every excavated kiln is a yard structure with a stokehole as large as the oven (§8); missing kinds surfaced by the trades — yard, court, gate, booth (stall+lodging dual), tack, manger, oven, still, loom, furnace, hoist, bin, pit, sale room, court room, search/assay room, drying loft, proofreaders' room. `FURNISHING_KINDS` lacks anvil, bellows, quench, wheel (potter's), press (common press 0.77 × 1.45 × 1.98 m), treadwheel, cresset, tiller wall.
15. `INDUSTRY_WATER_NEEDS` (six names) must grow: water forge/finery (§7-bis), salt works (§12), fish house/scalding house (§14, §13), dye house already; the smelter needs a POND and the lead bole needs WIND, not water (§7-bis).
16. Supply-state wear pilot: `COLD_FORGE` (§7) — the cleanest S7 demonstration; the empty granary floor and the dark exchange vaults are its siblings (§18, §20).
17. Catalog data observations for the owner: L1670 Auction house priorityCategory "military" (slip? — §2 note); L1112 "MUST be downstream and downwind" overstated (§9); L532 "Mill REQ" is a lordship rule (§11); L893 "Town granary REQ" is a continental import (§18); L929 "Craft guilds REQ" is a seat not a hall (§21); L1191 Chandler fuses two buildings (tallow/wax — §3-bis); L1717 Specialized metalworkers fans into ≥ 5 archetypes (§7-bis); L581/L1155 Sawmill is register-gated (§11); L1198 Glassblower is anachronistic in the English register before 1567 (§8); L104 Communal root cellar conflates granary and cellar and has no pre-1700 standing analogue (§18); L738/L1008 Cartographer's workshop did not exist as a building (§5); L1073/L1697/L1706 information entries have no historical interior (§20).

## OPEN-QUESTIONS LEDGER (final for this dossier — parent items, plus the nine sub-lane lists carried verbatim in the METHOD appendix: 106 items)

**Unverified (single-sourced or digest-only, flagged in place):** Chester Rows per-figure dimensions (digest of VCH, §3); Lady Row figures (aggregator, §3); the town smithy on a burgage plot — siting and footprint never measured this lane (§7); Pantin page numbers are read off the OCR running heads of `RINST2-pantin.txt` (pp. 202–235 as quoted) — verify against the printed volume before the grammar cites them; every sub-lane "CONFIRMED" label means "fetched by a subagent of this lane on 2026-08-23" — the parent did not re-open each URL, so P1c's verification pass is OWED on the grammar-consumed figures (the charter's PLAUSIBLE-may-not-be-consumed rule is extended here to: a sub-lane CONFIRMED figure may be consumed only after P1c re-opens its URL). Sub-lane headline figures most worth re-opening first: Koza Han court 45.9 × 37.5 m; Sultanhanı 44 × 58 m; Antwerp 40 × 51 m (digest); Lonja 35 × 21 m (travel guide); Ghent Vleeshuis 70 × 12.75 m (digest); Kenilworth 180 × 21 ft (1563 survey via two secondaries); Bothwell sheepcote 47 × 7.5 m; Meare 12.4 × 6.6 m; pakhuis 5–8 × 30 m (width digest-only); Leadenhall 11.17 m wall; the 1576 Royal Exchange paces.

**Superseded in this lane (recorded, not silently corrected):** the parent's brief to sub-lane IK attributed the 1566 "Miller be true" inscription to Sturminster Newton — it is Fiddleford Mill's (§11 note); the brief to sub-lane MNO asserted a 1239 Bristol dry dock — unverified, two fetched pages contradict it (§17 note); the brief's "Nuremberg Handwerkerhof" is a 1971 pastiche (§3-bis); "King John's Hunting Lodge, Axbridge" is a c. 1468 wool-merchant's house (§14); the brief's "Chatham 1,135 ft" and "Augsburg 1322" were not fetched (PLAUSIBLE in place).

**Negation coverage gaps (the session's shared WebSearch cap of 200 was reached during the sub-lane run):** families O, P, S, J, L and part of F/H lost their FORMAL negation queries; each sub-lane substituted targeted disconfirming fetches and says so. A P1c pass should issue the negation strings listed in their receipts (e.g. "no purpose-built warehouse before 1600", "quarry lodge lodging", "fish house medieval village").

**Deliberately not covered (with reason):** Liège/Siegerland 14th–15th-c. blast furnaces, the Venetian casa-fondaco, Goltho smithing, the Bologna Mercato di Mezzo (budget ceiling, one search away — §3-bis/§7-bis receipt); the Flemish weaver's house and the Winchester Lower Brook Street fullers/dyers (§10 receipt); Stuttgart Marstall, Iberian posada/corral, VOC Oostenburg, Thames revetment depths (§15–§17 receipt); Ottoman kapan and Venetian granai (§18 — both targets 404); the Champagne fair halles and Provins grange (§2); the magical/fantasy shelves (R-INST-5) and criminal fronts (R-INST-6) by charter; the inn's own brewhouse and stables (R-INST-4 — boundary stated in §13 and §15).

**Would most change this dossier:** (1) a measured Speicher/pakhuis SECTION (floor heights, loads, bay widths, hoist wheel) — the warehouse is the most-instantiated storage parti and has no measured interior; (2) Giles Appendix 6 (York/London hall dimensions) — the guildhall is room-rich and metric-poor; (3) a dated English ordinance expelling tanners, if one exists — it would turn the soft pull back into a law; (4) the Northampton tanyard plan (Shaw 1996 full text) and the Knightons glasshouse (Wood 1965/1982) — the two best-excavated wet/fire yards are behind paywalls/bot gates; (5) Schofield's London undercroft corpus for the vintner's vault.

**Weakest links (the parent's own judgment calls):** that the nine Pantin forms can be reduced to a `frontageBucket` switch (Pantin gives the threshold, not the weights); that the "NO BUILDING" census is ~30 (the count depends on where one puts the hamlet smith and the village brewer); that the hosted-institution relation is ONE capability rather than seven special cases; that `CORRIDOR`'s single 1648 occurrence licenses it at era ≥ 1650 — one Dutch plan is thin evidence for a class.

- Dyer, *Medieval Archaeology* 39 (1995) — sheepcote internals (§14); Kilby, *The Cooper and His Trade* (1971) (§6); Knoop & Jones, *The Mediaeval Mason* — lodge dimensions (§12); Salzman, *Building in England* (§6, §12).
